           Note. If the change in weight is greater than 0.1 percent, repeat steps 9 through 12 until the
           change in weight is equal to or less than 0.1 percent. Record the final weight of the dried soil and
           bowl.

            Step 13. When the change in weight is equal to or less than 0.1 percent the test is complete.

           Calculate the moisture content of the soil in terms of a percentage as follows—

                   𝑤(%) = �𝑊𝑖 − 𝑊𝑓� 100
                              �𝑊𝑓 − 𝑊𝑡�

           where—

           w = moisture content of the soil (as a percentage)

           Wi = weight of wet soil
           Wf = final weight of the bowl and dried soil
           Wt = weight of the tare

           Note. Software is available through the USACE, Engineer Research and Development Center,
           Vicksburg, Mississippi to assist with the conduction of this test in the field. The software
           provides a stepwise interface to input the microwave data and return the final moisture content
           when the test is finished. All calculations described earlier are internalized and the user only
           needs to be responsible for the proper weighing of the sample.

MICROWAVE PRECAUTIONS

     3-9. As with any electric heating element, care should be taken to protect hands and face when using a
     microwave. Gloves should be worn at all times to handle hot dishes or bowls as they are taken in and out of
     the microwave during testing. For sands and gravelly soils, eye goggles are recommended since potential
     overdrying of these soils can lead to bursting of the individual grains, posing a debris hazard when the
     samples are stirred.

     3-10. If a 700-watt microwave oven is not available, drying times may vary significantly. Higher-powered
     microwaves (1,000 watts or greater) will dry the soil much quicker, but also pose a greater risk to
     overdrying during the prescribed 1 minute intervals. This can damage the soil and the microwave much
     faster, so care should be taken with these devices. Thirty-second intervals would be better suited to these
     devices. Lower-wattage microwaves (500 watts or less) will require much longer drying times (up to 10
     times longer), 2- to 3-minute intervals would be better suited to these devices.

     3-11. Large sample sizes (in excess of 300 grams) may require very long dry times and may not provide
     consistent moisture contents. In this case, taking a smaller representative sample would be preferred to
     determine the final moisture content. If soil must be dried for other testing purposes, divide larger samples
     into portions that are 300 grams or less for microwave drying.

CALCIUM CARBIDE GAS PRESSURE TEST

                                            DANGER

           The chemical reaction of calcium carbide with water produces
           acetylene gas, which is extremely flammable. Exercise extreme
           caution to avoid open flame when releasing the gas from the
           calcium carbide pressure (speedy) moisture tester. Perform the
           test in a well-ventilated area, as asphyxiation could occur if
           performed in a confined area.

     3-12. Use the calcium carbide gas pressure test to determine the moisture content of a soil sample using the
     26-gram calcium carbide gas pressure test moisture tester to within ± 0.5 percent. If another tester is to be

3-6                TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034                        3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                         Moisture Content Determination

     used, consult the user’s manual for the tester before conducting the moisture content determination to
     obtain the specific soil sample size and quantity of calcium carbide reagent.

     3-13. Because of its small sample size, the device should not be used on soils that contain material greater
     than No. 4 sieve (4.75-millimeter diameter). Further, high plasticity clays or other soils not friable enough
     to break up may not produce representative moisture contents because the reagent cannot mix well with the
     soil sample.

EQUIPMENT

     3-14. Use the following items for the calcium carbide gas pressure test:
              A calcium carbide pressure moisture tester to hold a 26-gram soil specimen (approximately one
                  large tablespoon).
              A balance (readable to 0.1 gram).
              Two 1 1/4-inch steel balls.
              A cleaning brush and cloth.
              A scoop (for measuring calcium carbide reagent).
              Calcium carbide reagent.

STEPS

     3-15. Perform the following steps to determine the moisture content of the soil:
              Step 1. Weigh the soil sample to be tested, ensuring that it weighs exactly 26 grams. Place the
                  soil sample in the tester’s body, and add the two 1 1/4-inch steel balls.
              Step 2. Place three scoops (about 24 grams) of calcium carbide into the cap of the tester, and
                  with the pressure vessel in a horizontal position, insert the cap into the pressure vessel. Seal the
                  unit by tightening the clamp, taking care that no carbide comes in contact with the soil until a
                  complete seal is achieved.
              Step 3. Raise the moisture tester to a vertical position so that the reagent in the cap will fall into
                  the pressure vessel.
              Step 4. Shake the instrument vigorously in a rotating motion so that all lumps are broken up to
                  permit the calcium carbide to react with all available free moisture. Shake the instrument in a
                  rotating motion so that the steel balls will not damage the instrument or cause soil particles to
                  become embedded in the orifice leading to the pressure diaphragm. Continue shaking at least
                  1 minute for granular soils and up to 3 minutes for other soils to permit complete reaction
                  between the calcium carbide and the free moisture. Allow time for the dissipation of the heat
                  generated by the chemical reaction.
              Step 5. Hold the instrument in a horizontal position at eye-level. Read the dial when the needle
                  stops moving. Record the dial reading as the percent of moisture by wet mass.
              Step 6. Point the cap of the instrument away from the operator, and release the gas pressure
                  slowly. Empty the pressure vessel and examine the material for lumps. If the sample is not
                  completely pulverized, repeat the test using a new sample. Thoroughly clean the cap of carbide
                  and soil before running another test.

             Note. The limit of the tester is 12 percent moisture for aggregate or 20 percent moisture for soil.
             If the limit is exceeded, the test must be run again using a half-sized sample (13 grams), and the
             dial reading must be multiplied by 2.

CALCULATIONS

     3-16. Determine the percentage of moisture by dry mass of the soil from the calibration curve (figure 3-2,
     page 3-8) or from the conversion chart (table 3-2, page 3-9) as explained below. The calibration curves,
     moisture content determination by the calcium carbide gas pressure test, are used for materials that need
     pulverizing steel balls (table 3-2). Using the highest reading obtained during the test (direct reading), read

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3-7


---

Chapter 3

     over to the curve and then down to the oven-dry moisture percentage. The curve to be used will depend on
     the test time.

     Figure 3-2. Calcium carbide pressure moisture tester calibration chart for use with
                                                 1 1/4-inch steel balls

     3-17. Use the conversion chart (table 3-2) to determine oven dry moisture contents of materials that do not
     need the steel balls. If direct readings are not on the conversion chart, interpolate the difference between the
     two known direct readings.

EXAMPLE

     3-18. A calcium carbide gas pressure test is performed on a sand. The highest reading obtained is 3.5
     percent (wet weight). The difference between 3.0 percent (which has a known dry weight of 3.2 percent)
     and 4.0 percent (which has a known dry weight of 4.3 percent) must be interpolated for 3.5 percent.

     3-19. Using table 3-2, the values in table 3-3, are known. To find the value of x, place the differences in the

     table values into a ratio. The ratio of differences is—

     then—  3.5 − 3.0 𝑥 − 3.2 0.5 𝑥 − 3.2
            4.0 − 3.0 = 4.3 − 3.2 𝑜𝑟 1.0 = 1.1

            cross multiplied, 𝑥 − 3.2 = 0.55; therefore, leaving 𝑥 = 3.2 + 0.55 = 3.75.

     So, at 3.5 percent wet weight, 𝑥 = 3.2 + 0.55 = 3.75 percent comparable oven dry weight.

     3-20. The curves and charts are usually supplied with the moisture tester. However, check each moisture
     tester for the accuracy of its gauge and the accuracy of the conversion curve. Check the accuracy of the
     tester gauge by using a calibration kit (obtained from the tester’s manufacturer) equipped with a standard
     gauge. In case of discrepancy, adjust the gauge on the tester to conform with the standard gauge. For
     checking the accuracy of the conversion curve, make a calibration for meter readings versus oven-dry
     moisture contents using local soils. Also, additional testing may be necessary to extend the conversion
     curve beyond 44 percent moisture content.

                                                 Chapter 4

             Specific Gravity of Solids Determination

         The specific gravity of a solid substance is the ratio of the weight of a given volume
         of material to the weight of an equal volume of water (at 20°C). In effect, it tells how
         much heavier (or lighter) the material is than water. For exact analysis, the
         specifications require distilled or demineralized water and all measurements of water
         and solids should be made at stated temperatures. In dealing with soils testing, the
         value of specific gravity is necessary to compute the void ratio of the soil and for
         determining the grain size distribution in hydrometer analysis. The term specific
         gravity of soil actually refers to the specific gravity of the solid matter of the soil. The
         specific gravity of solids is normally only applied to that fraction of a soil that passes
         the No. 4 sieve. Generally, geotechnical engineers need the specific gravity of a soil
         to perform additional testing of that soil. The specific gravity of a soil largely
         depends on the density of the minerals making up the individual soil particles.
         However, as a general guide, some typical values for specific soil types are as
         follows:

          • The specific gravity of the solid substance of most inorganic soils varies between 2.60
                and 2.80.

          • Tropical iron-rich laterite and some lateritic soils usually have a specific gravity of
                between 2.75 and 3.00 but could be higher.

          • Sand particles composed of quartz have a specific gravity 2.65 to 2.67.
          • Inorganic clays generally range from 2.70 to 2.80.
          • Soils with large amounts of organic matter or porous particles (such as diatomaceous

                earth) have specific gravities below 2.60. Some range as low as 2.00.

SPECIFIC-GRAVITY TEST

     4-1. Perform the specific-gravity test to determine the specific gravity of solids (which will be used to
     assist in the hydrometer analysis test) and to calculate the zero air voids curve for compaction results. Take
     particular care to obtain representative samples for a specific-gravity test. It is easier to begin the test with
     an oven-dried sample. However, some soils (particularly those with a high organic content) are difficult to
     rewet. Test these at their natural water content, and determine the oven-dried weight at the end of the test.
     See ASTM C127 and ASTM C128.

EQUIPMENT

     4-2. Perform the specific-gravity test in a laboratory environment, using the following items (figure 4-1,
     page 4-2):

              A pycnometer; volumetric flask (500-milliliter capacity).
              A laboratory oven.
              Heat-resistant gloves.
              Balance scales sensitive to 0.01 gram.
              Pudding pans.
              A water bath.
              A thermometer.
              A battery filler syringe.

Step 2. Obtain a Soil Sample for Testing

     4-6. Obtain a soil sample for testing. Separate the given sample over a No. 4 sieve to obtain at least a 100-
     gram sample passing the sieve. Since this test is only concerned with the sample passing the appropriate
     sieve used, discard the material retained on the sieve.

Step 3. Prepare the Sample for Testing

     4-7. To determine the specific gravity of solids, the sample may be at its natural water content or oven-
     dried. Soils with a high organic content or with fines that are low compressible are difficult to rewet after
     having been oven-dried. These soils should be tested at their natural water content first and the oven-dried
     weight determined at the end of the test. Perform the following substeps to complete step 3:

              Step 3a. Record identifying information about the sample on the form.
              Step 3b. Place the -4 sample into the evaporating dish.
              Step 3c. Perform the following procedures for soil at natural water content or moisture. If soil is

                  not at natural water content or moisture, go to step 3d.
                   Step 3c(1). Add distilled water to the sample and mix to a slurry.
                   Step 3c(2). Transfer the slurry to the pycnometer, and add distilled water until the

                        pycnometer is about one-third to one-half its depth with water.
              Step 3d. Perform the following procedures for an oven-dried soil sample.

                   Step 3d(1). Oven-dry the sample to a constant weight at a temperature of 110°C ± 5°. Allow
                        the sample to cool, and weigh it to the nearest 0.01 gram. Record the weight on Block 6g as
                        the weight of the dish and dry soil.

                   Step 3d(2). Transfer the dried sample to the volumetric flask. Take care to avoid the loss of
                        any particles.

                   Step 3d(3). Fill the flask three-fourths full with distilled water, and allow it to soak for 12
                        hours.

                   Step 3d(4). Weigh the empty, dry evaporating dish. Record the weight on Block 6h as the
                        weight of the dish.

Step 4. Process the Sample Through the Test

     4-8. Process the sample through the test. Perform the following substeps to complete step 4:
              Step 4a. Remove entrapped air by bringing the solution to a slow, rolling boil for 10 minutes
                  while occasionally rolling the pycnometer to assist in the removal of the air (ensure that no loss
                  of material occurs while boiling). Cool the sample to room temperature.
              Step 4b. Fill the pycnometer with distilled water until the bottom of the meniscus is level with
                  the calibration mark.
              Step 4c. Dry the outside and thoroughly remove any moisture adhering to the neck of the
                  pycnometer.
              Step 4d. Weigh the pycnometer and its contents to the nearest 0.01 gram. Record this amount on
                  Block 6j as the weight of the flask, water, and immersed soil.
              Step 4e. Shake the flask immediately after weighing (putting its contents in suspension) and
                  determine the water temperature at middepth to the nearest whole degree. Record this amount on
                  Block 6k.
              Step 4f. Determine the dry unit weight for soil processed at natural moisture content.

               Step 4f(1). Transfer the soil solution from the flask to a preweighed pudding pan. Record
                   the weight in Block 6h. Use care when transferring all the grains of soil.

               Step 4f(2). Oven-dry the sample to a constant weight at a temperature of 110°C ± 5°. Allow
                   the sample to cool. Weigh and record the weight on Block 6g as the weight of the dish and
                   dry soil.

Step 5. Compute Results

                  step 4e. Using table 4-1, read across to the correction factor column and record it on the form as
                  the correction factor.

                  the form. Use the following formula to compute the specific gravity of solids:
                                                                                𝑊s𝐾

                                                                                             Specific Gravity of Solids Determination

SPECIFIC GRAVITY (APPARENT AND BULK)

     4-10. The specific gravity of solids is not applied to coarse particles because they normally contain voids
     from which air cannot be displaced unless the particles are ground into finer particles to eliminate the
     voids. Thus, when dealing with coarser particles, it is more convenient to work with the apparent specific
     gravity of the particle mass or to determine the bulk specific gravity.

     4-11. The apparent specific gravity is designated and is the ratio of the weight in air of a unit volume of the
     impermeable portion of aggregate to the weight in air of an equal volume of distilled water, both at a stated
     temperature. The impermeable portion of a porous material (such as most large soil grains) includes the
     solid material plus impermeable pores or voids within the particles. The bulk specific gravity is the ratio of
     the weight in air of a unit volume of aggregate (including permeable and impermeable voids in the
     particles, but not the voids between the particles) to the weight of an equal volume of distilled water at a
     stated temperature. These tests are applicable to testing soil with fine and coarse aggregates.

                                                 Chapter 5

                Grain Size Analysis and Distribution

         The accurate determination of grain size distribution or the gradation of coarse-soil
         fractions requires a grain size analysis. Grain size analysis, which is among the oldest
         of soil tests, is used in soils classification and as part of the specifications of soil for
         airfields, roads, earth dams, and other soil embankment construction. The standard
         grain-size analysis test determines the relative proportions of different grain sizes as
         they are distributed among certain size ranges. This test is referred to as particle size
         or grain size distribution. This is accomplished in two steps:

          • Step 1. A screening process (a sieve analysis, which is also called a mechanical analysis)
                for particle sizes retained on the No. 200 sieve.

          • Step 2. A sedimentation process (a hydrometer analysis) for particle sizes smaller than
                the No. 200 sieve.

             Note. Previous tests presented the sieve and hydrometer analysis as two separate tests. A
             combination of these analyses is a combined analysis. The ASTM employs a different test
             for particle size analysis which includes both tests (ASTM D422). These tests provide
             minor modifications to allow the end user to obtain results specific to the test purpose. The
             USMC uses a soil test set (see appendix D).

SIEVE ANALYSIS

     5-1. The accurate completion of the sieve-analysis test will produce the percent of gravel, sand, and fines
     of the material. The most accurate process for this test method is to wash the material over the sieves; this
     will give a more accurate percent of fines. It is possible the test will also provide sufficient information to
     calculate the coefficient of uniformity and the coefficient of curvature. Performing just the sieve analysis
     portion of this test may yield sufficient information to classify a soil type and therefore not require the
     hydrometer analysis. However, the hydrometer analysis will ensure a more accurate depiction of the soil
     gradation and provide necessary information required to determine the frost susceptibility of a soil.

PURPOSE

     5-2. Perform the sieve analysis test to determine the grain size distribution or the gradation of a soil or
     aggregate for the portion of the material that is larger than the No. 200 sieve. The results of this test should
     assist in the soil classification process.

                                                   Grain Size Analysis and Distribution

Note. The USACE RSAK (see appendix C) provides a method to closely approximate the
particle size distribution using fewer and smaller diameter sieves. In addition, it uses an
expedient dry sieving method versus wet sieving as prescribed below. In certain contingency
construction scenarios, this may be a preferable test for time savings allowing a test to be
completed in less than 1 hour. Case 1of the USMC soil test set includes all of the items required
for grain size analysis (see appendix D).

STEPS

     5-4. Perform the following steps to determine the grain size distribution:

             Note. All references to form in this paragraph refer to DD Form 1206 (figure 5-2, page 5-4)
             unless noted otherwise.

 Step 1. Prepare the soil sample.
      Step 1(a). Spread out and air-dry the soil sample.
      Step 1(b). Break up the aggregate particles thoroughly with fingers or with the mortar and
          pestle.
      Step 1(c). Obtain a representative sample for testing by using a sample splitter or by
          quartering. The sample size recommended for sieve analysis depends on the particle size.
          Obtain the required minimum sample as listed in table 5-1.

           Table 5-1. Representative soil samples for grain size analysis

              Maximum Particle Size                Minimum Sample Weight

              3/8 in (No. 4)       9.5 mm (No. 4)  500 g

Note. A field identification based on a visual or textural classification method as noted in Block
6 of figure 5-2, page 5-4 may differ from the actual engineering classification, depending on the
individual’s experience and level of detail obtained during the identification. In this example,
what appears to the user to be a red, fine sandy soil by quick observation actually classifies as a
sandy clay with gravel, illustrating the need to perform a sieve analysis for engineering design.

 Step 3. Oven-dry the material at 110°C ± 5° until a constant weight is obtained. Allow the
     sample to cool.

 Step 4. Weigh the oven-dried sample, and record the weight on the form (Block 8) to the nearest
     gram as the weight of the original sample.

 Step 7. Wash the sample over a No. 200 sieve into a 2-by-2 concrete pan until all -200 material
     has been washed through. If the sample contains an appreciable amount of coarse particles,
     combine the No. 4 and No. 200 sieves. Take care not to overload the No. 200 sieve. If necessary,
     transfer the sample in increments (this process may take up to 6 different pans and as long as
     8 hours).

 Step 8. Process the +200 material. Oven-dry the washed plus 200 material at 110°C ± 5° until a
     constant weight is obtained, and allow the material to cool. Record the weight to the nearest
     tenth of a gram (Block 10).

 Step 9. Process the -200 material.

      Step 9a. Allow the -200 material to settle in the pan until the surface water becomes clear
          (16 to 24 hours).

      Step 9b. Decant the surface water (using a siphon or a syringe), ensuring that the settled
          material is not disturbed.

      Step 9c. Use a trowel to transfer as much of the material as possible from the pan to the
          pudding pans.

      Step 9d. Rinse the remainder of the material from the 2-by-2 pans to the pudding pans with
          as little water as possible.

      Step 9e. Oven dry the washed -200 material, and determine the total -200 sample weight to
          the nearest .01 grams. Record this weight on Block 11. Retain this material for use in the
          hydrometer analysis.

 Step 10. Select a nest of sieves to accommodate the largest particle size of the soil being tested,
     ensuring that all material will pass through the largest sieve. As a minimum, the following sieves
     sizes will be used (up to the largest particle size): 2 inches; 1.5 inches; 1 inch; 3/4 inch; 3/8 inch;
     and Nos. 4, 10, 16, 30, 40, 50, 100, and 200.

 Step 11. Record the weight of each sieve selected on the form to the nearest tenth of a gram
     (Column 13), and arrange the sieves in a nest with the smallest sieve size on the bottom. Weigh
     the pan, and place it on the bottom.

 Step 12. Cover the sample. If the sample was prewashed, place only the +200 material onto the
     top sieve of the nest and place a cover over it. If the sample was not prewashed, place the entire
     sample on the top sieve of the nest and place a cover over it.

 Step 13. Place the nest of sieves and the sample in the sieve shaker, and shake for 10 to
     15 minutes (figure 5-3, page 5-6).

 Step 18. Weigh the pan with the material passing the No. 200 sieve. Subtract the weight of the
     pan (from step 11), and record this as the weight sieved through No. 200 (Block 20).

 Step 19. Complete Blocks 21 through 25 of the form using the formulas provided on the sheet. If

 Step 20. Compute the cumulative weight retained (Column 16) for each sieve by adding the
     weight retained to the previous cumulative weight retained with the starting point being 0.

 Step 21. Compute the percent retained (Column 17) for each sieve by dividing the weight
     retained by the total weight of fractions as follows:

                                            𝐶𝑜𝑙𝑢𝑚𝑛 15 × 100
                                             𝐵𝑙𝑜𝑐𝑘 23

 Step 22. Compute the percent passing for each sieve size by subtracting the cumulative weight

              follows:

                        𝐶𝑜𝑙𝑢𝑚𝑛 18 =         𝐵𝑙𝑜𝑐𝑘 23 − 𝐵𝑙𝑜𝑐𝑘 16  × 100

 Step 23. Determine the percentages for gravel, sand, and fines (Blocks 26, 27, and 28). Record

              the information on the form.

Notes.
1. Gravel is the material retained on the No. 4 sieve.
2. Sand is the material passing the No. 4 sieve and retained on the No. 200 sieve.
3. Fines are the material passing the No. 200 sieve.

 Step 24. Compute the decimal fines (Block 29) by dividing the percent of fines by 100 percent.
     This allows a smooth transition on the grain size curve between the soil sampled to conduct the
     hydrometer test and the total fine percentage in the sieved sample.

 Step 25. Complete DD Form 1207 (figure 5-5, page 5-8).
      Step 25a. Record the identifying information for the sample in the remarks block.
      Step 25b. Use the sieve analysis data to plot the sieve size and the percentage passing the
          sieve.
      Step 25c. Using a French curve, connect the plotted points to form a smooth, free-flowing
          curve (the grain size distribution curve).
      Step 25d. Determine the coefficient of uniformity.

Note. The grain size (in millimeters) which corresponds to 10 percent passing on the grain size
distribution curve, is called Hazen’s effective size (designated by the symbol D10). If the grain
size distribution curve extends to or below 10 percent passing, then the coefficient of curvature
can be determined. The coefficient of uniformity is the ratio between the grain diameter (in
millimeters), corresponding to 60 percent passing and 10 percent passing on the curve. Use the
following formula, and record the results on the form:

                                                            𝐷60
                                                     𝐶u = 𝐷10

where—
Cu = coefficient of uniformity
D10 = 10 percent passing on the grain size distribution curve
D60 = 60 percent passing on the grain size distribution curve

If D10 cannot be determined using the data from the sieve analysis, a hydrometer analysis may be
required to obtain information about the smaller size grains and to extend the distribution curve
to make it more complete.

              Step 26. Determine the gradation by using the abbreviated information listed below. Record the
                  information on the form. An indicator of a well-graded soil is a smooth curve plotted for grain
                  size distribution. The curve must not have any horizontal or vertical portions and must be
                  continuous. The soil is poorly graded if any of the above criteria is not fulfilled. The soil is well-
                  graded when all of the following apply:
                   The coefficient of uniformity is greater than 4 if the soil is predominantly gravel and greater
                        than 6 if the soil is predominantly sand.
                   The coefficient of curvature is at least 1.0 but not more than 3.0 for gravel and sand.

HYDROMETER ANALYSIS

     5-5. Hydrometer analysis is based on Stoke’s law, which relates the terminal velocity of a free-falling
     sphere in a liquid to its diameter—or, in simpler terms, the larger the grain size, the greater its settling
     velocity in a fluid. It is assumed that Stokes’ law can be applied to a mass of dispersed soil particles of
     various shapes and sizes. Larger particles settle more rapidly than smaller ones. The hydrometer analysis is
     an application of Stokes’ law that permits calculating the grain size distribution in silts and clays, where the
     soil particles are given the sizes of equivalent spherical particles.

     5-6. The density of a soil-water suspension depends on the concentration and specific gravity of the soil
     particles. If the suspension is allowed to stand, the particles gradually settle out of the suspension and the
     density decreases. The hydrometer is used to measure the density of the suspension at a known depth below
     the surface. The density measurement, together with the knowledge of specific gravity of the soil particles,
     determines the percentage of dispersed soil particles in suspension at the time and depth of measurement.

     5-7. The depth at which the measurement is made is found by calibrating the hydrometer. Stokes’ law is
     used to calculate the maximum equivalent particle diameter for the material in suspension at this depth and
     for the elapsed time of settlement. A series of density measurements at known depths of suspension and at
     known times of settlement give the percentages of particles finer than the diameters given by Stokes’ law.
     Thus, the series of readings will reflect the amount of different sizes of particles in the fine-grained soils.
     The particle diameter is calculated from Stokes’ equation using the corrected hydrometer reading.

PURPOSE

     5-8. Perform the hydrometer analysis to determine the grain size distribution of the -200 material in a soil
     to assist in determining the frost susceptibility of a soil and to provide data needed to calculate the
     coefficient of uniformity and coefficient of curvature. It is not recommended to run the hydrometer if the
     percent passing the No. 200 sieve is less than 5 percent.

EQUIPMENT

     5-9. Perform the hydrometer analysis in a laboratory environment using the following items:
              A hydrometer.
              A laboratory oven.

              A battery-filler syringe.
              Distilled water.

STEPS

     5-10. Perform the following steps for hydrometer analysis:

             Note. All references to form in this paragraph refer to DD Form 1794 unless noted otherwise.

              Step 1. Prepare the sample.

                   Step 1a. Obtain the -200 sample as prepared in the sieve analysis by collecting the material
                        passing the No. 4 sieve (to match that of the specific gravity test) or the No. 10 sieve. The
                        size of the sample varies according to the type of soil being tested. Approximately 100
                        grams are required for sandy soils, 50 grams for silty or clayey soils. Place the sample in a
                        dish, and add distilled water until the sample is submerged.

                   Step 1b. Determine the amount and type of dispersing agent that will be used during the test
                        as (table 5-2, page 5-12) with sodium hexametaphosphate being the most common. Record
                        it in Blocks 9 and 10. The dispersing agents shown in table 5-2 are listed in approximate
                        order of effectiveness. They have been found to be satisfactory for most types of soils. In
                        most instances, 15 milliliters of a dispersing agent is adequate to control flocculation (the
                        adherence of fine soil grains to each other in clusters while in suspension). An additional 15
                        milliliters can be added a second or third time if flocculation continues.

                   Step 1c. Add the predetermined amount of dispersing agent to the soaking soil sample, and
                        allow the sample to soak at least 16 hours.

            Step 2. Determine the type of hydrometer. If the hydrometer scale ranges from 1.000 to 1.038, it
                is Type 151H and measures specific gravity of the suspension. If the scale ranges from 0 to 60, it
                is Type 152H and measures grams per liter of the suspension. The dimensions for both
                hydrometers are the same.

            Step 3. Determine the composite correction. The first of these items needing correction is the
                meniscus reading. Hydrometers are graduated by the manufacturer to be read at the bottom of
                the meniscus formed by the liquid on the stem. Since it is not possible to secure readings of soil
                suspensions at the bottom of the meniscus, readings must be taken at the top and a correction
                applied. The second of these items needing correction is a result of using a dispersing agent in
                the water to control flocculation. This leads to errors in the analysis. While the dispersing agent
                assists in keeping the soil grains from adhering to each other, it also increases the specific
                gravity of the fluid used. The net amount of the correction for the two corrections required is
                designated as the composite correction.

           Note. Before performing the hydrometer test, a composite correction for hydrometer readings
           must be determined to correct items that tend to produce errors in the test.

                 Step 3a. Place about 500 milliliters of distilled water in a graduated cylinder.
                 Step 3b. Place the amount of dispersing agent that was used in step 1 in the cylinder, and

                     mix well.
                 Step 3c. Add additional distilled water to the cylinder to reach the 1,000-milliliter mark.
                 Step 3d. Place the hydrometer in the cylinder, and allow it to settle for 20 to 25 seconds.

                     Read the hydrometer at the top of the meniscus formed on the stem. For the Type 151H
                     hydrometer, the composite correction is the difference between the reading and 1. For the
                     Type 152H hydrometer, the composite correction is the difference between the reading and
                     0.
                 Step 3e. Record the composite correction in Block 11.
                 Step 3f. Remove the hydrometer from the dispersing-fluid cylinder, and place it in a second
                     cylinder filled with distilled water.

           Note. From this point forward, all hydrometer readings will be taken from the top of the
           meniscus.

            Step 4. Perform the hydrometer test.

                 Step 4a. Record identifying information for the sample, dispersing agent, quantity used, and
                     composite correction on the form.

                 Step 4b. Obtain the decimal fines from the original soil sample from DD Form 1206. Record
                     it in Block 12.

                 Step 4c. Obtain the specific gravity of solids of the soil sample from DD Form 1208. Record
                     it in Block 13.

                                                                                    Grain Size Analysis and Distribution

      Step 4d. Empty and thoroughly rinse the graduated cylinder containing the dispersing
          solution from step 3.

      Step 4e. Transfer the soaked sample to a dispersion cup, using distilled water to wash any
          residue from the dish into the cup. Add distilled water to the cup until the water surface is 3
          inches below the top of the cup. Place the cup in the dispersing machine, and mix silts and
          sands for 5 minutes, low-plasticity clay for 7 minutes, and high-plasticity clay for 9 minutes.

      Step 4f. Transfer the mixed solution to the clean 1,000-milliliter, graduated cylinder, using
          distilled water to wash any residue from the cup into the cylinder. Add distilled water until
          the 1,000-milliliter volume mark is reached.

      Step 4g. Place the rubber cap over the open end of the cylinder. Turn the cylinder upside
          down and back for a period of 1 minute to complete the agitation of the slurry.

Note. The number of turns during this minute should be about 60, counting the turn upside down
and back as two turns. If any soil remains at the bottom of the cylinder during the first few turns,
it should be loosened by vigorous shaking the cylinder while it is in the inverted position.

      Step 4h. After shaking the cylinder for 1 minute, place it on a level and sturdy surface where
          it will not be disturbed. Remove the cap, and start the timer. Remove any foam that has
          formed during agitation by lightly touching it with a bar of soap.

      Step 4i. Immerse the hydrometer slowly into the liquid 20 to 25 seconds before each
          reading. Take the actual hydrometer reading (R1) at 1 and 2 minutes of elapsed time. As
          soon as the 1- and 2-minute readings are taken, carefully remove the hydrometer and place
          it in the second cylinder of pure, distilled water using a spinning motion. Record the reading
          on the form (Block 16).

      Step 4j. Place a thermometer in the solution. Record the temperature reading, in centigrade,
          to the nearest whole degree. Record on the form (Block 18).

Note. It is extremely important to obtain accurate temperature readings. The soil hydrometer is
calibrated at 20°C. Variations in temperature from this standard temperature produces
inaccuracies in the actual hydrometer readings. These inaccuracies will be compensated for later
during the computations.

      Step 4k. Repeat steps 4i and 4j for the remainder of the required readings. Take readings at
          the following intervals: 5, 15, and 30 minutes and 1, 2, 4, and 24 hours. After each reading,
          remove the hydrometer, place it in the hydrometer of distilled water, and obtain the
          temperature reading. Record the information on the form for each reading.

 Step 5. Determine the dry weight of the sample by carefully washing all of the sample into a
     preweighed pudding pan or dish (Block 24). Oven-dry the sample, allow it to cool, and
     determine and record the weight of the sample and the pan or dish (Block 23).

 Step 6. Determine the weight of the dry soil by subtracting the weight of the pan from the
     weight of the pan and dry soil. Record this information on the form as the weight of the oven-
     dried soil used for hydrometer testing (Block 25).

 Step 7. Compute the results the form.

                                                 Chapter 6

         Liquid Limit, Plastic Limit, and Plasticity Index
                                Determination

         Clays and some other fine-grained soils exhibit plasticity if the proper amount of
         water is present in the soil. A plastic soil is one that can be deformed beyond the
         point of recovery without cracking or change in volume. Such soils can be remolded.
         The LL is the greatest water content that the material may contain and still remain
         plastic. More water causes it to become a thick liquid. The PL is the lowest water
         content that the material may contain for plastic behavior. With less water, the soil
         becomes brittle and breaks into fragments if remolding is attempted. The PI is the
         numerical difference between the LL and the PL (𝑃𝐼 = 𝐿𝐿 − 𝑃𝐿). A large PI
         indicates a very plastic soil; a small PI denotes a soil having little plasticity. As water
         content decreases below the PL, the soil mass shrinks and becomes stiffer. The
         shrinkage limit is the water content where, with further drying, shrinkage stops. Since
         there is no sharp distinction between the liquid, plastic, and brittle solid states of
         consistency, standardized procedures have been established for determining the LL
         and the PL. These consistency limits and the shrinkage limit are called the Atterberg
         limits. Research with large numbers of clay soils was used to establish the soil
         plasticity chart for laboratory classification of fine-grained soils. The LL and PI
         values are coordinates that locate a particular soil sample on the chart. The region on
         the chart in which the sample falls gives the classification based on the behavioral
         characteristics of the particular soil.

             Note. Take particular care when performing the test methods described below. Some soils,
             particularly those with a high organic content, can provide inconsistent readings or drastic
             differences between an oven-dried sample and a sample at natural moisture content. Conduct the
             tests on samples of natural moisture content. Determine the moisture content at the end of the
             test.

LIQUID LIMIT DETERMINATION

     6-1. The LL of a soil is the water content expressed as a percentage of the weight of the oven-dried soil at
     the boundary between the liquid and the plastic states and reported as a whole number. This boundary is
     arbitrarily defined by a standard test method. The test is performed on two halves of a prepared soil
     specimen in an LL device. The LL is determined when the soil halves flow together along a distance of
     13 millimeters when the cup is dropped or jarred exactly 25 times from a height of 1 centimeter. This rate
     of drop is 1.9 to 2.1 drops per second.

PURPOSE

     6-2. Perform the LL determination test to assist in classifying the soil by determining the LL from three
     moisture content samples.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  6-1

EQUIPMENT

     6-3. Perform the LL determination test in a laboratory environment using the following items (figure 6-1):

              A balance scale sensitive to 0.01 gram.
              A LL device (also known as a casagrande cup) and a grooving tool (figure 6-2).

              A No. 40 sieve.
              Pudding pans.
              A ground-glass plate (at least 30 square centimeters by 1 centimeter thick) for mixing soil and

                  rolling PL threads.
              A plastic bag.
              A calculator.
              Moisture-determination tares.
              Paper.
              A pencil.
              A grease pencil.
              DD Form 1209 (Liquid- and Plastic- Limits Determination).
              Gummed labels.
              A spatula.
              A straightedge.
              A mortar with a rubber-tipped pestle.
              A laboratory oven.
              Heat-resistant gloves.
              Distilled water.

STEP 1. PREPARE THE SOIL SAMPLE

     6-4. Perform the following substeps to complete step 1:
              Step 1a. Sieve the soil sample (at natural moisture content) over the No. 40 sieve to obtain a
                  sufficient quantity of at least 250 grams.
              Step 1b. Perform the following steps if little or no material is retained on the No. 40 sieve;
                  otherwise, go to step 1c:
                   Step 1b(1). Collect 200 to 250 grams of -40 material for testing.
                   Step 1b(2). Mix the material with distilled water until the water content is slightly below the
                        LL (peanut butter consistency). The goal is to have the material fall in the 25- to 35-blow
                        range for the first test.
                   Step 1b(3). Place the mixture in a plastic bag, making it airtight for at least 16 hours
                        (overnight) so that the moisture content can become consistent throughout the sample.
                        Remix the material thoroughly before testing.
              Step 1c. Perform the following steps if material is retained on the No. 40 sieve:
                   Step 1c(1). Place the –40 material in a plastic bag, making it airtight to maintain its natural
                        moisture content.
                   Step 1c(2). Soak the coarse material retained on the No. 40 sieve (the soaking time is
                        variable).
                   Step 1c(3). Rub the colloidal material from the surfaces of the large particles until they are
                        clean, placing the fines in suspension.
                   Step 1c(4). Pour off the suspended fines slowly into another pan, being careful not to pour
                        off the coarse material.
                   Step 1c(5). Add clean water to the coarse material, and repeat the wash process until the
                        water poured off is sufficiently clear to indicate that the majority of fines that were put in
                        suspension have been poured off.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  6-3


---

Chapter 6

                   Step 1c(6). Remove the excess water from the pan containing the suspended fines after the
                        fines have settled by decantation and evaporation. Do not oven-dry or add chemical
                        substances to speed-dry or hasten the settlement.

                   Step 1c(7). Oven dry the coarse material that has been soaked and washed.
                   Step 1c(8). Sieve the oven-dried coarse material over the No. 40 sieve.
                   Step 1c(9). Combine the -40 material obtained from steps 1c(1) and 1c(8) with the decanted

                        material from step 1c(6). If the combined material is too moist, air-dry it until the water
                        content is slightly below the LL. If the combined material is too dry, add small quantities of
                        water until the water content is slightly below the LL.
                   Step 1c(10). Place the combined mixture in a plastic bag, making it airtight for at least 16
                        hours (overnight) so that the moisture content can become consistent throughout the sample.
                        Remix the material thoroughly before testing.

STEP 2. INSPECT THE LIQUID LIMIT DEVICE BEFORE TESTING

     6-5. Perform the following substeps to complete step 2:
              Step 2a. Ensure that the pin connecting the cup is not worn (which would permit side play).
              Step 2b. Ensure that the screws connecting the cup to the hanger arm are tight.
              Step 2c. Check the cup for wear. If a groove has developed from use, replace it.
              Step 2d. Check the contact between the cup and the base. If a dent can be felt in the base or flat
                  on the cup, replace or repair it.
              Step 2e. Check the grooving tool for wear.
              Step 2f. Check the height of the drop of the cup so that the point on the cup that comes in
                  contact with the base (not the lowest point of the cup) rises to a height of 1 centimeter. Use the
                  gauge on the handle of the grooving tool to assist in this measurement. The height of the drop
                  must be 1 centimeter. Use the thumbscrew at the rear of the device to make an adjustment.
                   Step 2f(1). Place a piece of masking tape across the outside bottom of the cup parallel with
                        the axis of the cup hanger pivot. The edge of the tape away from the cup hanger should
                        bisect the spot on the cup that contacts the base. For new cups, place a piece of carbon paper
                        on the base and allow the cup to drop several times to mark the contact spot.

             Example. Step 2f(1) could be used to aid in checking and adjustments.

                   Step 2f(2). Attach the cup to the device, and turn the crank until the cup is raised to its
                        maximum height.

                   Step 2f(3). Slide the height gauge under the cup from the front, and observe whether the
                        gauge contacts the cup or the tape. If the tape and cup are contacted, the height of drop is
                        approximately correct. If not, adjust the cup until simultaneous contact is made.

                   Step 2f(4). Check the adjustment by turning the crank at two revolutions per second while
                        holding the gauge in position against the tape and cup. If a faint ringing or clicking sound is
                        heard without the cup rising from the gauge, the adjustment is correct. If no ringing is heard
                        or if the cup rises from the gauge, readjust the height of drop. If the cup rocks on the gauge
                        during this step, the cam-follower pivot is excessively worn and the worn parts should be
                        replaced.

                   Step 2f(5). Remove the tape after adjustments.

STEP 3. PERFORM THE LIQUID LIMIT TEST

     6-6. Perform the following substeps to complete step 3:
              Step 3a. Obtain about 50 grams of the 200- to 250-gram, prepared sample; and place in an
                  airtight container for use in the PL test.
              Step 3b. Record identifying information for the sample on DD Form 1209 (figure 6-3).

            Step 3e. Divide the soil sample in the cup with a grooving tool so that a clean, sharp groove is
                formed. Hold the cup with the cam follower upward and draw the grooving tool, with the
                beveled edge forward, through the specimen downward away from the cam follower (figure
                6-5). Use more than one stroke to make the groove, but no more than six, cleaning the grooving
                tool’s cutting edge after each stroke. Avoid tearing the side of the groove.

                                    Figure 6-5. Holding cup and grooving tool

            Step 3f. Replace the soil sample in the cup, and regroove it if the side tears. With some sandy
                and highly organic soils, it is impossible to draw the grooving tool through the specimen without
                tearing the sides of the groove. In such cases, the groove should be made with a spatula, using
                the grooving tool only for a final check of the groove (figure 6-6).

                            Figure 6-6. Cutting groove with spatula in sandy soil

            Step 3g. Attach the cup to the device, and ensure that the height of the drop is 1 centimeter.

            Step 3h. Turn the crank of the device at a rate of two revolutions per second. Count the blows
                until the two halves of the soil make contact at the bottom of the groove along a distance of
                13 millimeters (figure 6-7).

            Step 3i. Record the number of blows to close the groove for 13 millimeters.

6-6        TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                           Liquid Limit, Plastic Limit, and Plasticity Index Determination

                      Legend:              millimeter
                      mm

                      Figure 6-7. Soil coming into contact

 Step 3j. Obtain 5 to 10 grams of soil from the cup to determine the moisture content. Take the
     sample perpendicular to the groove from the edge of the cup and through the portion that has
     closed in the bottom of the groove. Place the sample in the preweighed moisture determination
     tare, and cover it with a lid. Weigh it, and record the weight on the form as the weight of the wet
     soil and the tare.

 Step 3k. Transfer the soil remaining in the cup to the mixing dish. Wash and dry the cup and the
     grooving tool.

Note. It is recommended that one of the trials be for a closure requiring 25 to 35 blows, one for a
closure between 20 and 30 blows, and one for a closure requiring 15 to 25 blows.

 Step 3l. Remix the entire soil specimen, adding a little water to increase the water content of the
     soil and decrease the number of blows required to close the groove. Repeat steps 3d through 3j
     for at least two additional trials, producing a successively lower number of blows to close the
     groove.

 Step 3m. Oven-dry the water content samples, allow them to cool, and reweigh them. Record
     the weight on the form as the weight of the dry soil and the tare.

 Step 3n. Compute the weight of the water by subtracting the weight of the dry soil and the tare
     from the weight of the wet soil and the tare. Record the weight on the form.

 Step 3o. Compute the weight of the dry soil by subtracting the weight of the tare from the
     weight of the dry soil and the tare. Record the weight on the form.

 Step 3p. Record the water content for each specimen by using the following formula:

              where—                             𝑊w
                                           𝑤 = 𝑊s × 100

              w = moisture content of the soil (as a percentage)

              Ww = weight of the water

              Ws = weight of the dry soil

Note. Weighing should be accurate to 0.01 gram, and water contents should be computed in
rounded to the nearest tenth percent.

 Step 3q. Plot the water content points on the semilog graph on DD Form 1209 (water versus
     number of blows), and draw a straight line (flow line) representative of the three or more points.

 Step 3r. Determine the LL by interpreting the graph where the flow line intersects the 25-blow
     line. Record the LL to the nearest whole number.

Chapter 6

FALL CONE

     6-7. The fall cone is provided as an alternative to the casagrande cup (LL device) technique for
     determining the LL. The fall cone is an international standard and is a common device used outside the
     United States. The fall cone also provides more reproducible results than the casagrande cup technique. It is
     easier to maintain in correct adjustment, involves easier calibration steps, and requires less judgment on the
     part of the operator to assess the soil state.

PURPOSE

     6-8. Perform this test to determine the moisture content of a soil at its LL to within ± 10 percent of the
     casagrande cup value.

EQUIPMENT

     6-9. Perform the fall cone test in a laboratory environment using the following items (figure 6-8):
              A balance scale sensitive to 0.01 gram.
              A fall cone device with stainless steel or duralumin cone tips (30° angle).
              A gauge plate for calibration.
              A flat, glass plate.
              One or more metal cups with a diameter of 55 ± 2 millimeters and a height of 40 ± 2 millimeters
              A wash bottle.
              A No. 40 sieve.
              Pudding pans.
              A calculator.
              Moisture-determination tares.
              Paper.
              A pencil.
              A grease pencil.
              DD Form 1209.
              A spatula.
              A straightedge.
              A suitable device for drying soil (oven, microwave).
              Heat-resistant gloves.
              Distilled water.

     Figure 6-8. Essential equipment for a fall cone test

6-8  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034     3 April 2015


---

                                                               Liquid Limit, Plastic Limit, and Plasticity Index Determination

STEP 1. PREPARE THE SOIL SAMPLE

     6-10. Perform the following substeps to complete step 1:
              Step 1a. Sieve the soil sample (at natural moisture content) over the No. 40 sieve to obtain a
                  sufficient quantity of at least 500 grams.
              Step 1b. Perform the following steps if little or no material is retained on the No. 40 sieve;
                  otherwise, go to step 1c:
                   Step 1b(1). Collect 300 grams of -40 material for testing.
                   Step 1b(2). Mix the material with distilled water until the water content is slightly below the
                        LL (a peanut butter consistency). The material falls in the 25- to 35-blow range for the first
                        test.
                   Step 1b(3). Place the mixture in a plastic bag, making it airtight for at least 16 hours
                        (overnight) so that the moisture content can become consistent throughout the sample.
                        Remix the material thoroughly before testing.
              Step 1c. Perform the following steps if material is retained on the No. 40 sieve:
                   Step 1c(1). Place the -40 material in a plastic bag, making it airtight to maintain its natural
                        moisture content.
                   Step 1c(2). Soak the coarse material retained on the No. 40 sieve (the soaking time is
                        variable).
                   Step 1c(3). Rub the colloidal material from the surfaces of the large particles until they are
                        clean, placing the fines in suspension.
                   Step 1c(4). Pour off the suspended fines slowly into another pan, being careful not to pour
                        off the coarse material.
                   Step 1c(5). Add clean water to the coarse material, and repeat the wash process until the
                        water poured off is sufficiently clear to indicate that most fines that were put in suspension
                        have been poured off.
                   Step 1c(6). Remove the excess water from the pan containing the suspended fines after the
                        fines have settled by decantation and evaporation. Do not oven-dry or add chemical
                        substances to speed-dry or hasten the settlement.
                   Step 1c(7). Oven-dry the coarse material that has been soaked and washed.
                   Step 1c(8). Sieve the oven-dried coarse material over the No. 40 sieve.
                   Step 1c(9). Combine the -40 material obtained from steps 1c(1) and 1c(8) with the decanted
                        material from step 1c(6). If the combined material is too moist, air-dry it until the water
                        content is slightly below the LL. If the combined material is too dry, add small quantities of
                        water until the water content is slightly below the LL.
                   Step 1c(10). Place the combined mixture in a plastic bag, making it airtight for at least 16
                        hours (overnight) so that the moisture content can become consistent throughout the sample.
                        Remix the material thoroughly before testing.

STEP 2. INSPECT THE CONE DEVICE BEFORE TESTING

     6-11. Perform the following substeps to complete step 2:
              Step 2a. Ensure that the tip angle has not been compromised (use the gauge plate provided with
                  the fall cone with the specifications given in figure 6-9, page 6-10. The compromise would
                  impede penetration of the cone resulting in too great a LL. Check for a worn tip, and if so,
                  replace with a new cone to ensure a smooth penetration into the soil, if necessary.

              Step 2b. Ensure that the cone drops freely when released from the locked position.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  6-9


---

Chapter 6

           Legend:  millimeter
           mm

                   Figure 6-9. British fall cone calibration tolerances

STEP 3. PERFORM THE LIQUID LIMIT TEST

     6-12. Perform the following substeps to complete step 3:
              Step 3a. Mix the 300-gram paste in (step 1) for 10 minutes, using the spatula to ensure that the
                  soil is well mixed.

             Note. Highly plastic clays may require longer mixing times (upwards of 40 minutes) to achieve
             repeatable results.

            Step 3b. Push a portion of the soil paste into the tin cup with a spatula or knife, taking care to
                not entrap air within the soil. Strike-off the excess soil above the rim of the cup with a
                straightedge.

            Step 3c. With the penetration cone locked and in the raised position, lower the supporting
                assembly until the tip of the cone touches the soil surface.

            Step 3d. Lower the stem of the dial gauge to contact the cone shaft, and take an initial height
                reading of the gauge to the nearest 0.1 millimeters.

            Step 3e. Release the cone for 4 to 6 seconds. Lock the cone in position, being careful not to jerk
                the apparatus and force the cone down further.

            Step 3f. Lower the stem of the dial gauge onto the cone shaft, and record the final height to the
                nearest 0.1 millimeters.

            Step 3g. Compute the change in height by using the following formula:

                                                   ∆𝐻 = 𝐻i − 𝐻f

           Where—

           ∆H = change in height
           Hi = initial height reading
           Hf = final height reading

            Step 3h. Lift the cone carefully to avoid scratching.

            Step 3i. Add a little more soil to the cup to fill in the void created by the cone, taking care to not
                trap air, and relevel the surface with a straightedge.

            Step 3j. Repeat steps 3d through 3h.

            Step 3k. Change in height is not more than 0.5 millimeters if the difference between the two
                height readings record the average of the two penetrations. Go to step 3n.

            Step 3l. Conduct a third test if the difference between the two height readings is between 0.5 and
                1.0 millimeter. If the difference between the lowest and highest readings between all three tests
                is still between 0.5 and 1.0 millimeter, take the average of the three tests and continue to step 3n.

6-10               TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034                3 April 2015


---

                                                 Liquid Limit, Plastic Limit, and Plasticity Index Determination

 Step 3m. Remove the soil from the cup, and remix the soil paste with remaining soil until a
     better consistency is created if the difference between the two height readings is greater than 1.0
     millimeter. It may be necessary to add additional distilled water at this time. Rerun the test
     beginning at step 3b.

 Step 3n. Take approximately 10 grams of soil from the penetrated area of the sample cup, and
     dry it to obtain the moisture content.

 Step 3o. Remove the soil from the cup, remold it with remaining soil, and dry it by spreading it
     across a flat surface.

 Step 3p. Repeat the test (steps 3b through 3o), trying to obtain a smaller penetration value
     indicating a stiffening of the soil after removing some of the moisture. Continue drying the soil
     and repeating the test until a value of 15 millimeters or less penetration is achieved. Ideally, four
     moisture content penetration points will have been obtained.

 Step 3q. Plot the data (figure 6-10).

              Legend:  liquid limit
              LL       millimeter

               Step 3q(1). Identify whether the apparatus used has British or Chinese origins.
               Step 3q(2). Create an arithmetic plot with the y-axis being values of penetration and the x-

                   axis being values of moisture content for the British fall cone device.
               Step 3q(3). Create a logarithmic plot with the y-axis being values of penetration and the x-

                   axis being values of moisture content for the Chinese fall cone device.
               Step 3q(4). Draw a best-fit, straight line through the data points plotted on the graph.

Note. The moisture content at the LL is the point at which the penetration reaches 20 millimeters
for the British cone or 17 millimeters for the Chinese cone.

               Step 3q(5). Record the moisture content at the LL to the nearest whole percentage value.

Chapter 6

PLASTIC LIMIT DETERMINATION

     6-13. The PL of a soil is the water content (as a percentage) of the weight of oven-dried soil at which the
     soil begins to crumble when rolled into a thread 3.2 millimeters (1/8 inch) in diameter. About 50 grams of
     material is required for the PL test. Prepare the sample, and set it aside while preparing for the LL test.

PURPOSE

     6-14. Perform the PL test to assist in classifying the soil by determining the PL moisture content to within
     ± 1 percent.

EQUIPMENT

     6-15. Perform the PL test in a laboratory environment using the same equipment listed in the LL
     determination test (paragraph 6-3).

STEPS

     6-16. Perform the following steps to determine the PL:
              Step 1. Label and preweigh two empty moisture determination tares. Record the weight on DD
                  Form 1209 as the weight of the tare.
              Step 2. Obtain the 50-gram sample that was set aside during the LL test. Reduce the water
                  content, if required, to obtain a consistency with which the soil can be rolled without sticking to
                  the hands by spreading or mixing continuously on the glass plate. The drying process may be
                  accelerated by air-drying only.
              Step 3. Select a portion of about 2 grams (marble-size) from the 50-gram mass, and form the test
                  specimen into an ellipsoidal mass. Roll it on a finely ground glass plate with the fingers or palm
                  of the hand to a uniform thread diameter of 3.2 millimeters (1/8 inch), taking no more than 2
                  minutes (figure 6-11).

                                   Figure 6-11. Rolling a soil specimen

      Note. The rate of rolling should be between 80 and 90 strokes per minute, counting a stroke as
      one complete motion of the hand forward and back to the starting position. The rate of rolling
      may have to be decreased for very fragile soil.

       Step 4. Remold the sample, and roll it again to 3.2 millimeters in diameter, repeating the rolling
           and remolding process until the sample crumbles before reaching the 3.2-millimeter thread
           (figure 6-12).

6-12  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                 Liquid Limit, Plastic Limit, and Plasticity Index Determination

Note. All of the sample may not crumble at the same time. If the thread breaks into smaller
lengths, roll each of these lengths to 3.2 millimeters. Continue the rolling and remolding process
until the sample can no longer be remolded and rolled to the 3.2-millimeter thread without
totally breaking up.

                            Figure 6-12. Rolled threads, crumbled and uncrumbled

              Step 5. Collect and place the crumbled portions into a preweighed moisture determination tare
                  and cover it with the lid.

              Step 6. Repeat steps 3 through 5 until the crumbled threads in the moisture determination tare
                  weigh at least 6 grams.

              Step 7. Repeat steps 3 through 6 to obtain a second moisture determination tare of at least
                  6 grams of material.

              Step 8. Weigh the moisture-determination tares with the crumbled threads, and record the
                  weight on the form as the weight of the wet soil and the tare.

              Step 9. Determine the water content by following steps 3l through 3o (paragraph 6-6) of the LL
                  test.

              Step 10. Determine the average water content of the samples and record to the nearest tenth as
                  the PL. When determining the average water content, the individual tests must be within
                  ± 1 percent of the mean. Any individual tests that do not meet this requirement will not be used.
                  If none of the individual tests meet this requirement, then additional testing is required.

PLASTIC LIMIT DEVICE

     6-17. As an alternative to the manual hand-rolling method of conducting a PL, an ASTM-accepted test
     device is available from commercial manufacturers, known as the PL device (figure 6-13). This device
     streamlines the test process by using specific size soil samples to roll a thread and a calibrated box with a
     lip that ensures that a 3.2-millimeter-diameter thread is rolled.

              Figure 6-13. PL device components

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  6-13

Chapter 6

EQUIPMENT

     6-18. Use the following items in addition to the those specified in paragraph 6-3:
              PL device.
              Self-adhesive PL paper.

             Note: The plastic limit roller alleviates the need for a glass plate and the use of hands to conduct
             the physical rolling of the soil sample. The self adhesive paper provides friction for rolling the
             soil masses and a drying effect to help decrease moisture content with continued rolling.

STEPS

     6-19. Perform the following steps to determine the PL with the PL device:
              Step 1. Place one sheet of adhesive-backed PL paper on the bottom plate and one on the top
                  plate of the PL device.
              Step 2. Reduce the moisture content of the soil to the approximate PL (where the soil is
                  nonsticky).
              Step 3. Select four or five, 1.5-gram to 2-gram portions of soil.
              Step 4. Roll each portion of soil into an ellipsoidal mass.
              Step 5. Place one or two ellipsoidal masses about 25 to 40 millimeters apart on the bottom plate.
                  Experience has shown that best results occur with one or two masses.
              Step 6. Lower the top plate gently onto the top of the soil masses, and begin gently rolling down
                  the soil portions using a back-and-forth motion as shown in figure 6-14.

                                   Figure 6-14. Operation of PL device

       Step 7. When the top plate comes into contact with 3.2-millimeter rail, check to see if the soil is
           at the PL.

       Step 8. Collect the threads and knead them together into a ball and repeat steps 3 through 7 if the
           soil is not at the PL. Repetitive rolling on the paper will also help dry out the soil if it is too wet.

       Step 9. Pour the threads off the bottom plate into a moisture tare container when the soil is at the
           PL, and weigh it immediately.

       Step 10. Determine the average water content of the samples, and record it to the nearest tenth as
           the PL. When determining the average water content, the individual tests must be within
           ± 1 percent of the mean. Individual tests that do not meet this requirement will not be used. If
           none of the individual tests meet this requirement, additional testing is required.

      Note. To ensure smooth rolling of the threads, replace the PL paper if it becomes torn or if
      significant amounts of soil become stuck to the paper as to prevent.

6-14  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015

PLASTICITY INDEX DETERMINATION

     6-20. Compute the PI and record it on DD Form 1209 using the following formula:

                                                          𝑃𝐼 = 𝐿𝐿 − 𝑃𝐿
     6-21. Classify the soil by plotting the LL versus the PI on the plasticity chart as follows (figure 6-15):

              The material plotted on or above the A line is classified as clay, and the material plotted below
                  the A line is classified as silt.

              The material plotted on or to the right of the 50 percent line has a high LL, and the material
                  plotted to the left of the 50 percent line has a low LL.

              The upper line is an approximate upper boundary. Any results plotted above this line should be
                  considered suspect, and the tests should be rechecked.
