                                                 Chapter 7

    Laboratory Compaction Characteristics of Soil Using
                               Modified Effort

         Compaction is one of the basic construction procedures involved in building
         subgrades and bases for roads and airport pavements, embankments, earthen dams,
         and similar structures. Compaction is the process of increasing the amount of solids
         per unit volume of soil by mechanical means. This increase in density has an
         important effect in improving such soil properties as strength, permeability, and
         compressibility. The amount of compaction is quantified in terms of the density (dry
         unit weight) of the soil. Usually, soil can be compacted best and a greater density
         achieved if only a certain amount of water is added. In effect, water acts as a
         lubricant, allowing soil particles to be packed together better. However, if too much
         water is added, a lesser density will result because the excess water separates the soil
         particles. Therefore, for a given compactive effort, there is a particular moisture
         content at which dry density is greatest and compaction is best. This moisture content
         is the OMC, and the associated dry density is called the maximum dry density
         (MDD).

COMPACTION TEST

     7-1. In the field, compaction is accomplished by rolling or tamping with special equipment or by the
     passing of construction equipment. Laboratory compaction is usually accomplished by placing the soil in a
     cylinder of known volume and dropping a tamper of known weight onto the soil from a known height for a
     given number of blows. The amount of work done to the soil per unit volume of soil in this dynamic
     compaction procedure is called compactive effort. Each compactive effort for a given soil has its own
     OMC. As the compactive effort is increased, the maximum density usually increases and the OMC
     decreases.

     7-2. Testing standards exist for varying compaction energies, but to simplify the procedure, only the
     compaction effort known as modified proctor is discussed. This is considered a high-energy compaction
     effort suitable for airfield and highway horizontal constructions and embankment and foundation
     preparation. ASTM D1557 defines the criteria for this procedure and is summarized in the following text.

     7-3. A lower-compaction energy procedure can be found in ASTM D698, which requires slightly lighter
     laboratory equipment. It is not discussed here and, in some cases, may be suitable for less critical structures
     or when the equipment or environment prohibit the compaction of soil to high densities.

     7-4. Before performing the compaction test, the grain size analysis must be determined (see chapter 5).
     This test method provides three alternative testing procedures. The procedure used is indicated in the
     project specifications for the type of material being tested. If no procedure is clearly specified, the selection
     should be based on follow-on testing requirements (such as the CBR test [see chapter 9]) and the material
     gradation.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  7-1


---

Chapter 7

PURPOSE

     7-5. Perform compaction tests in the laboratory to determine such soil properties as the effect of varying
     percentages of water on dry density, the maximum density obtainable under a given compactive effort, and
     the OMC. Use the following procedures:

              Procedure A. This procedure uses a 4-inch mold on only the soil passing the No. 4 sieve when
                  the overall representative sample has no more than 20 percent of the material by weight retained
                  on the No. 4 sieve. The number of blows per layer for this procedure is 25, and the number of
                  layers is 5.

             Note. Materials that meet these gradations may also be tested using Procedures B or C.

              Procedure B. This procedure uses a 4-inch mold on only the soil passing the 3/8-inch sieve
                  when the overall representative sample has more than 20 percent of the material by weight
                  retained on the No. 4 sieve and 20 percent or less is retained on the 3/8-inch sieve. The number
                  of blows per layer for this procedure is 25, and the number of layers is 5.

             Note. Materials that meet these gradations may also be tested using Procedure C.

              Procedure C. This procedure uses a 6-inch mold on only the soil passing the 3/4-inch sieve
                  when the overall representative sample has more than 20 percent of the material by weight
                  retained on the 3/8-inch sieve and less than 30 percent retained on the 3/4-inch sieve. The
                  number of blows per layer for this procedure is 56, and the number of layers is 5.

             Note. Previous testing methods incorporated a mix of standards that have since been rescinded.
             The current standard for this test is ASTM D1557. This test procedure standardizes the use of
             the 4-inch (Proctor) and 6-inch (CBR) molds. The compaction effort at 56 blows is about the
             same as used in previous test methods (compaction effort [CE] 55). This test method is
             applicable only to soils containing 30 percent or less by weight of material retained on the
             3/4-inch sieve. If greater than 30 percent of the soil weight is retained on the 3/4-inch sieve, refer
             to ASTM D4718 for preparing the sample and correcting the measured moisture content and dry
             density.

EQUIPMENT

     7-6. The following items are used for the compaction test:
              Cylinder molds (which one depends on the soil sample being processed).
                   Protctor mold. The proctor mold should be 4 inches with a 4.0-inch inside diameter, 4.584-
                        inch inside height, 0.0333-cubic-feet internal volume, and 2.375-inch extension collar, and
                        detachable metal baseplate.
                   CBR mold. The CBR mold should be 6 inches with a 6-inch inside diameter, 7-inch inside
                        height, 2-inch extension collar and detachable metal baseplate. The mold should also have a
                        metal spacer disk that is 2.416 inches thick with a 5.94-inch inside diameter and. The metal
                        spacer disk is used as a false bottom in the mold during testing. When the spacer disk is in
                        place in the bottom of the mold, the internal volume of the mold (excluding extension
                        collar) is 0.075 cubic feet.
              A compacting hammer or tamper. This hammer should be a sliding-weight type compacting
                  tamper, with a 2-inch diameter steel-striking face, 10-pound mass, and 18-inch fall.
              A No. 4 sieve.
              A 3/8-inch sieve.
              A 3/4-inch sieve.
              A balance scale sensitive to 0.01 gram.
              A balance scale sensitive to 1.0 gram.

7-2  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                    Laboratory Compaction Characteristics of Soil Using Modified Effort

              Moisture tares.
              A soils oven.
              Filter paper.
              A large spoon.
              A large knife.
              A steel straightedge.
              A calculator.
              DD Form 1210 (Laboratory Compaction Characteristics of Soil [Compaction Test]).
              DD Form 1211 (Laboratory Soil-Compaction Test Graph).

     7-7. The amount of material (field sample) required for the compaction test depends on the test procedure
     being used and the field sample’s moisture content. The following are guidelines for the amount of soil
     required for:

              Procedures A and B. Use about 35 pounds of dry soil or at least 50 pounds of moist soil
                  (approximately one 5-gallon bucket would be a sufficient volume).

              Procedure C. Use about 75 pounds of dry soil or at least 100 pounds of moist soil.
                  (approximately two 5-gallon buckets would be a sufficient volume).

STEPS

     7-8. Perform the steps in the following paragraphs to complete the compaction test:

Step 1. Determine the Test Procedure to be Used

     7-9. Perform the following substeps to complete step 1:
              Step 1a. Do not use this method if the CBR design and tests are to be developed for the project
                  (see chapter 9).
              Step 1b. Determine the test procedure by evaluating the gradation criteria of the procedures
                  listed in the preceding paragraph.

Step 2. Prepare the Soil Sample

     7-10. Perform the following substeps to complete step 2:
              Step 2a. Dry the sample until it can be easily crumbled under a trowel. Drying may be done by
                  air-drying or by using a drying apparatus, if the temperature of the sample does not exceed 60°C.
              Step 2b. Break up the sample thoroughly, but not in such a manner as to reduce the size of the
                  individual particles.
              Step 2c. Sieve the sample over a No. 4 (paragraph 7-4, Procedure A), 3/8-inch (paragraph 7-4,
                  Procedure B), or 3/4-inch sieve (paragraph 7-4, Procedure C). When preparing the material by
                  passing it over the 3/4-inch sieve for compaction in the 6-inch mold, break up aggregates
                  sufficiently to at least pass the 3/8-inch sieve. This facilitates the distribution of water
                  throughout the soil in later mixing.
              Step 2d. Separate five equal portions from the sample representing each point desired on the
                  compaction curve. The size of each sample for one mold is about 2,700 grams for Procedures A
                  and B or 6,800 grams for Procedure C. Retain all excess soil from the sample.

Step 3. Adjust the Water Content

     7-11. Perform the following substeps to complete step 3:

             Note. The water content adjustments in this step are designed to provide approximations of the
             OMC. In no way should these approximations be used for, or be interpreted as, the actual
             moisture content. Exact moisture determinations will be conducted in a later step.

              Step 3a. Establish the assumed or approximate OMC.
                   Step 3a(1). Place exactly 100 grams of the excess soil sample in a dish.

                        OMC is typically achieved so that when the soil is squeezed in the palm, it will adhere
                        together on its own, but it will break cleanly into two separate pieces without pieces
                        shattering when bent. Usually, this will be slightly less than the PL.

                        the actual moisture content of the soil, which will be determined in a later step. For purposes
                        of conducting the test method, the approximate OMC will be the amount of water (in
                        milliliters) added to the sample. For example, if 8 milliliters of water was added to achieve
                        the approximate OMC, the approximate OMC is 8.0 percent. This works in estimating
                        because 1 milliliter of water is equal to about 1 gram. By adding weight to an original

Chapter 7

Step 6. Place Soil in the Mold

     7-14. Place sufficient soil in the mold (about 1 1/2 to 2 inches) to obtain a 1-inch (approximate) compacted
     layer.

Step 7. Apply Compactive Effort

     7-15. Perform the following substeps to apply compactive effort:
              Step 7a. Hold the 10-pound compaction tamper within 5° of vertical, placing its face on top of
                  the soil.
              Step 7b. Raise the handle until it reaches the top (18 inches), and release it, allowing the weight
                  to fall freely onto the soil.
              Step 7c. Change the position of the guide and tamper, and repeat the process until the soil layer
                  has received the prescribed 25 blows for Procedures A and B or 56 blows for Procedure C.
                  Apply the blows at a uniform rate of about 25 blows per minute. The height of fall of the tamper
                  must be controlled carefully and the blows distributed evenly over the specimen’s surface (figure
                  7-2).

                                        Legend:
                                        lb pound
                                         Figure 7-2. Soil compaction in a mold

Step 8. Trim the Compacted Layer

     7-16. Use a knife to trim any soil adjacent to the mold walls that has not been compacted or that extends
     above the compacted surface. Include the trimmed soil with the additional soil for the next layer. Use the
     knife to roughen the compacted surface by striking shallow lines across its surface to simulate field
     scarification, creating a stronger bond between lifts.

Step 9. Repeat Steps 6, 7, and 8

     7-17. Repeat steps 6, 7, and 8 until five layers have been compacted in the mold. Each compacted layer
     should be about equal in thickness (just under 1 inch). Adjust each layer accordingly to ensure that the fifth
     compacted layer will slightly extend into the collar, but will not exceed 1/4 inch above the top of the mold.

             Note. Do not perform step I on the fifth layer.

Step 10. Remove the Collar From the Mold

     7-18. Perform the following steps to complete step 10:
              Step 10a. Cut around the inside edge of the collar to prevent shearing the compacted soil when
                  removing the collar.
              Step 10b. Trim and smooth the compacted soil flush with the top of the mold (figure 7-3). Use a
                  sawing motion with the straightedge to trim the excess soil. Start at the center of the mold, and
                  work outward, first to one side and then to the other. Fill any holes with unused or trimmed soil
                  from the specimen, press in with the fingers, and scrape the surface with the straightedge.

                                 Figure 7-3. Trimming the compacted soil sample

             Note. If the soil surface is indented below the lip of the mold (embedded hammer imprints), the
             recorded density will be too low and the test should be rerun. If rocks or materials are left above
             the surface of the mold, the recorded density will be too high.

Step 11. Weigh and Record Data

     7-19. Weigh the complete mold with the baseplate and compacted specimen (including the spacer disk for
     the 6-inch mold) to the nearest gram. Record the weight on the form as the weight of the mold and wet soil
     (Block 12). Do not include the collar.

Step 12. Prepare the Specimen for Moisture Content Determination

     7-20. Perform the following substeps to complete step 12:
              Step 12a. Remove the specimen from the mold.
              Step 12b. Slice the compacted specimen axially through the center, remove about 250 grams of
                  material from one side of the cut, place it in one of the moisture determination tares, and cover
                  it. Remove about 250 grams from the other side of the cut, place it in the other moisture
                  determination tare, and cover.

Step 13. Repeat Steps 5 through 12

     7-21. Repeat steps 5 through 12 for the remaining molds at the different moisture contents described in
     step 3a(5).

GRAPH PRESENTATION OF RESULTS

     7-24. DD Form 1211 is an important part of presenting the data from the compaction test. It is used to plot
     a compaction curve. This curve is needed to determine the MMD and OMC as part of the compaction test
     procedures. It also includes a zero air voids curve and a compaction specification block.

COMPACTION CURVE

     7-25. The compaction curve is obtained by plotting moisture content versus dry unit weight for each test on
     the soil compaction test graph (figure 7-4). To construct an acceptable curve, at least two of the plotted
     points should fall on each side of the OMC. It is important to remember that during the testing period, the
     only density that can be determined is the wet soil density or wet unit weight. To compute the dry unit
     weight, the moisture content must be determined. This can take up to 24 hours.

     7-26. For a typical cohesive soil, dry density increases to a certain point (the OMC) as the moisture in the
     soil increases. Once the OMC is achieved, the dry density begins to decrease with increasing moisture
     content. The primary reason for performing the compaction test is to determine the moisture content at
     which the MDD can be obtained. After plotting the compaction curve, it is possible to determine the
     moisture content, which will provide the MDD for that particular soil directly from the plotted curve.

     7-27. The compaction curve shows the OMC or the moisture content at which the MDD is obtained for a
     given compaction effort. By determining the highest point on the compaction curve (apex) and dropping
     vertically to the horizontal moisture scale (or line), the OMC for this particular soil is found to be 8.8
     percent.

     7-28. The compaction curve also shows the MDD (100 percent compaction). The MDD of 100 percent
     effort may be obtained by running a tangent from the highest point on the compaction curve for the
     particular soil to the vertical dry-density scale (figure 7-4); in this case, 123.2 pounds per cubic foot.

     7-29. A compaction curve is not complete without the zero air voids curve, which acts as a control to the
     compaction curve.

ZERO AIR VOIDS AND SATURATION

     7-30. The zero air voids curve represents theoretical values that are practically unattainable—no matter
     what degree of compactive effort—because it is not possible to remove all of the air contained in the voids
     of a soil by compaction alone. Typically, at moisture contents beyond optimum, the actual compaction
     curve closely parallels the theoretically perfect compaction curve. Any values of dry density that plot to the
     right of the zero air voids curve are in error. The error may be in the test measurement, the calculations, or
     the specific gravity.

7-8                        TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                              Laboratory Compaction Characteristics of Soil Using Modified Effort

7-31. At complete saturation, the voids in the solid mass are completely filled with water. That is, no air is
present and the degree of saturation is equal to 100 percent. The zero air voids curve (100 percent
saturation) for the soil tested is shown on the graph in figure 7-4. This curve is obtained by plotting dry
densities that correspond to complete saturation at different moisture contents using the following formula:

                                                                   𝛾w 1
                                                     𝑤 = 𝑆 × �𝛾d − 𝐺s�

     w = moisture content of the soil (as a percentage)
     S = degree of saturation (as a percentage)

     γd = dry unit weight of soil (in pounds per cubic foot, grams, or centimeters)
     Gs = specific gravity of solids for this soil (DD Form 1211, Block 6) (chapter 4)

PERCENT MOISTURE

     7-33. To obtain the maximum dry unit weight or density in the field, it is necessary to maintain the
     construction moisture content of the soil as close as possible to the optimum determined from the
     laboratory compaction test. If the moisture content is not close to the OMC, it will require extra time and
     equipment effort to obtain the MDD. The limits for moisture content should be outlined in the
     specifications for each job. If not specified, the limits should be established as ± 2 percent of the OMC.
     Using figure 7-4, where the OMC is 8.8 percent, the moisture limits would range from 6.8 to 10.8 percent.
     This provides the limits for a workable and practical specification block.

PERCENT COMPACTION

     7-34. Some soils will not or cannot be compacted to 100 percent at a reasonable equipment effort,
     regardless of the combination. In those cases, it is not mandatory to compact to 100 percent. For each job,
     the specifications will state the percent of compaction required for the particular loads. Assume that the
     specifications require 90 percent of MDD. To find the dry density required, multiply the MDD
     (100 percent), regardless of its value, by 0.90. This will give the density limit. If the specifications state
     between 90 and 95 percent, the 90 percent density will constitute the lower limit with 95 percent as the
     upper limit. A specification block can now be constructed.

COMPACTION SPECIFICATION BLOCK

     7-35. The compaction specification block shows a determination range based on the project specifications.
     If no specifications are given, refer to the minimum compaction specification requirements as listed in TM

7-10           TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                    Laboratory Compaction Characteristics of Soil Using Modified Effort

     3-34.64/MCRP 3-17.7G; FM 5-430-00-1/AFJPAM 32-8013/MCRP 3-17.7A, Volume I; or FM 5-430-00-
     2/AFJPAM 32-8013/MCRP 3-17.7B, Volume II. Once the range is determined, the specification block is
     plotted on the compaction curve, covering the specified compaction range requirement in percent for the
     dry unit weight within a 4 percent range of the OMC (± 2). The block is then lightly cross-hatched so as to
     not interfere with the compaction curve. If the field results fall within this block, the job is meeting
     specifications.

Equipment

     7-36. Use the following items to complete the compaction specification block:
              DD Form 1211 with compaction curve and zero air voids curve.
              A calculator.
              The proposed use of the soil.
              The project’s compaction specifications.

Steps

     7-37. Perform the following steps to complete the soil compaction specification block (DD Form 1211):
              Step 1. Determine the compaction specification requirements as discussed above.
              Step 2. Draw the compaction specification block. Establish the upper and lower limits of the
                   block as the specification range (in percent) of the dry unit weight. Establish the left and right
                   limits of the block as ± 2 percent of the OMC. The example in figure 7-4, page 7-9, represents
                   the compaction data from DD Form 1210, the zero air voids curve plotted from a specific
                   gravity value of 2.62, and a compaction specification of 90 to 95 percent. Notice the compaction
                   specification block ranges vertically from 117.0 to 110.9 pounds per cubic foot and horizontally
                   from 6.8 to 10.8 percent in moisture content. The computation used to achieve the upper and
                   lower limits is based on the MDD (in this case, 123.2 pounds per cubic foot) and the
                   specification range of 90 to 95 percent by using the following formula:
                                       123.2 𝑥 0.95 = 117.0 123.2 𝑥 0.90 = 110.9
                   Once the block has been established and drawn, the inside of the block can be lightly cross-
                   hatched to easily identify the range but do not to interfere with the curve or any other data
                   plotted on the chart.

WATER DENSITY EFFECT

     7-38. Figure 7-5 demonstrates that as the moisture content is varied, the dry density also varies. As water is
     added to an oven-dried soil, the dry density increases until the OMC is reached. The dry density then
     begins to decrease using a constant compactive effort.

                                    Figure 7-5. Effect of water on density

7-39. After adding small increments of water to a completely air-dry soil, subsequent compaction with a
constant compactive effort causes a small increase in the dry unit weight of the soil. During the initial

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  7-11


---

Chapter 7

     hydration phase, the water being added to the soil is absorbed on the surface of the soil grains. This water
     does not aid compaction by acting as a lubricant since it is firmly attached to the surface of the soil
     particles. Adding additional water brings the soil to a point where a slight change in moisture begins to
     produce a large increase in density. This rapid increase indicates that the lubrication phase of the
     compaction curve has been reached.

     7-40. The first portion of the compaction curve to the right of OMC is known as the swell phase. The
     addition of water increases the film around the soil particles, forcing the soil particles apart and decreasing
     the dry density. With further increases in moisture content, free water added to the soil will fill the void
     spaces. This is known as the saturation phase. In the swell and saturation phases, the water begins to take
     the place of the solids, thus decreasing the dry density.

EFFECTS OF COMPACT EFFORTS ON DENSITY

     7-41. The mass-per-unit volume of a soil varies directly with the amount of energy expended to compact
     that soil. Therefore, the greater the compactive effort, the greater the amount of solids per unit volume. This
     results in a stronger and more stable soil.

     7-42. As the compactive effort is increased, the dry density of the soil increases. This means that if more
     energy is used to compact a soil, the increased energy will cause the particles to be rearranged to a greater
     extent, thus increasing the mass of soil particles per unit volume. If the compactive effort is decreased, the
     particles will not be rearranged as much, thus decreasing the dry density. Figure 7-6 shows how the dry
     density varies with the compactive effort.

     7-43. The OMC varies inversely with respect to compactive effort. If the compactive effort is increased,
     the soil does not have to be as wet to obtain the MDD. In other words, the OMC will be decreased with
     increasing compactive effort (figure 7-6).

      Legend:  pound per cubic foot
      pcf

      Figure 7-6. Effect of different compactive effort on density

EFFECTS ON SOIL DENSITY

     7-44. Different soils have varying compactive characteristics. Gravelly and sandy soils have a lower OMC
     and higher densities under the same compactive effort compared with silty and clay soils (figure 7-7). The
     sharpness of the curves indicates that moisture content is much more critical in obtaining maximum density
     for coarse-grained soils than for fine-grained soils.

7-12  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034             3 April 2015


---

                       Laboratory Compaction Characteristics of Soil Using Modified Effort

              Legend:  pound per cubic foot
              pcf

              Figure 7-7. Effect of different soil types on density

COMPACTION EQUIPMENT

     7-45. Equipment normally available to the military engineer for soil compaction includes sheepsfoot,
     pneumatic-tired, and steel-wheeled rollers. Other construction equipment and load-hauling units may also
     be used. Crawler type tractor units are efficient in compacting free-draining sands and gravels that should
     be kept wet during the compaction process. This equipment is not efficient for compacting cohesive soils
     (TM 3-34.62/MCRP 3-17.7I).

     7-46. For compaction equipment to be used efficiently, the moisture content at which maximum
     compaction can be obtained (the OMC) and the maximum density to which the soil can be compacted are
     required. This data is obtained by performing the laboratory compaction test. The stability or strength of the
     base course in the field can only be obtained if the moisture content allows proper compaction and if
     compaction is obtained at or above the amount specified.

OTHER COMPACTIVE EFFORTS

     7-47. Under some circumstances, it may be necessary to use a compactive effort in the laboratory. Usually,
     this is done to study the effect of variation in density on some property of the soil (such as the CBR). In this
     case, samples are compacted using the procedures previously described, except that the variation in density
     is achieved by varying the number of blows applied to each layer as described in ASTM D1883. In unusual
     circumstances, the laboratory compaction procedure may be changed to produce a compactive effort that
     more closely resembles the energy that can be put into the soil using available rolling equipment. In the
     CBR test, the compaction procedure calls for 10, 25, or 56 blows per layer.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034      7-13

                                        Chapter 8

             In-Place Density Determination

Proper field control is essential in earthwork construction. The control tests are
conducted on the soil at the jobsite as construction proceeds. If at any time a test
indicates that operations are not producing a soil condition specified by the design
tests, take immediate action to remedy the situation. Stress deformation
characteristics of the soil are directly related to the moisture content and density of
the soil, allowing specifications to be set for a given soil as construction proceeds.
Densities obtained are compared with minimum density requirements established for
the particular job. Moisture contents are compared with a specified range of moisture
bounding the OMC, previously established, to see that compaction is taking place
within the desired range or to permit its adjustment. An undisturbed sample of known
or measurable dimensions provides information for computing the density and
moisture content of the soil. If the soil is not in proper condition during construction
to remove an undisturbed sample, the density may be determined by measuring the
volume of the hole after the sample is removed. The procedure consists of filling the
hole with a measured quantity of a known density material (sand, ball bearings, oil,
water) and computing the volume of soil removed. The moisture content and density
of the soil are then determined. The method for in-place density depends on the type
of soil encountered and the equipment available. On moist, cohesive, fine-grained
soils, undisturbed samples taken by samplers may be sufficient. Coarse-grained or
cohesionless soils make it difficult to obtain an undisturbed sample. In these soils,
density determination may require the displacement method. Sand displacement may
be used on any type of base course or subgrade material. Oil displacement cannot be
used on highly pervious soils, crushed stone, or slag base courses. If the pavement to
be used is asphaltic concrete, the residual oil and spillage will tend to soften the
asphalt. Displacing water requires the use of a balloon to contain the water and can
be used on any type of soil. If the density determined by the methods described in the
following paragraphs is equal to or greater than that required, compaction may be
judged to be satisfactory and the placing of another lift may proceed. If the density is
lower than that required, additional rolling may be necessary or the moisture content
may have to be adjusted. If these methods fail, the weight of the roller may have to be
increased, the thickness of lift reduced, or other methods used to obtain adequate
compaction. The possibility that the soil being compacted in the field is not the same
as the one tested in the laboratory should never be overlooked. Under normal field
conditions (the work is proceeding smoothly and uniform soils are being compacted),
the number of density and moisture checks required should be limited after the initial
period of compacting. If adequate densities are being obtained and the proper
moisture content is being maintained, inspections may be performed to determine and
verify the number of passes and the combination of rollers to achieve the desired
result with minimum effort. Where conditions are more variable, density and
moisture checks may be needed more often for a fill of even moderate length. The
exact number of checks needed should be determined by the engineer in charge of the
job.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034                           8-1


---

Chapter 8

SAND CONE OR SAND DISPLACEMENT METHOD

     8-1. The sand cone or sand displacement method may be used in fine- or coarse-grained materials.
     Calibrated sand is used to determine the volume of the hole from which a sample has been taken.

     8-2. The sand cone or sand displacement test consists of digging out a sample of the material to be tested,
     determining the volume of the hole, and determining the dry weight of the sample. The volume of the
     sample should be as close to the same volume at which the sand was calibrated; however, the sample’s
     maximum particle size will determine the volume of sample to be tested. Sample requirements are as
     follows:

              Material with a maximum aggregate size of 1/2 inch requires a minimum test hole volume of
                  0.05 cubic foot.

              Material with a maximum aggregate size of 1 inch requires a minimum test hole volume of
                  0.075 cubic foot.

              Material with a maximum aggregate size of 2 inches requires a minimum test hole volume of
                  0.10 cubic foot.

     8-3. A double-cone cylinder must be used. This permits calibrating the sand for each test performed. The
     sand must be clean, dry, and free-flowing with a constant moisture content during the test. The sand should
     pass the No. 10 sieve and should have less than 3 percent passing the No. 60 sieve. The ideal solution to
     ensure that the gradation requirements are met is to obtain Ottawa sand, which generally ranges from the
     No. 20 to No. 40 sieve sizes.

PURPOSE

     8-4. Perform the sand cone or sand displacement test to determine the in place density of a soil or to
     validate other in-situ density test devices.

EQUIPMENT

     8-5. Use the following items to perform this test in a field environment (figure 8-1):

              A template with a 6-inch hole.
              A 6-inch soil density tester.
              A CBR mold.
              A 2 1/2-inch spacer disk.
              Filter paper.
              A knife.
              A hammer.
              Nails, twenty-penny.
              A steel straightedge.
              A varnish brush.
              A balance scale sensitive to 1.0 gram with a 20-kilogram capacity.
              A balance scale sensitive to 0.01 gram with a 500-gram capacity.
              A ruler.
              A calcium carbide pressure moisture test set.
              A laboratory oven.
              Heat-resistant gloves.
              A spoon.
              A chisel.
              Friction top cans.
              Sand, well rounded, passing the No. 10 sieve with less than 3 percent passing the No. 60 sieve.
              A sand recovery bucket (such as a 5-gallon bucket)

8-2  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015

STEPS

     8-6. Perform the following steps for the sand cone or sand displacement method:

             Note. All references to form in this paragraph refer to DD Form 1215 (figure 8-2, page 8-4)
             unless noted otherwise.

              Step 1. Determine the sand’s density.
                   Step 1a. Weigh the mold, baseplate, spacer disk, and filter paper. Record this weight (in
                        grams) on Line 8.
                   Step 1b. Attach the collar to the mold and place the filled sand cone apparatus, with the
                        valve closed, on top of the mold.
                   Step 1c. Open the valve, and allow sand to fall at its own rate into the mold. Do not jar the
                        apparatus while the sand is falling.
                   Step 1d. Close the valve when the sand stops running into the mold and the collar.
                   Step 1e. Remove the sand cone and collar carefully from the mold.
                   Step 1f. Use a straightedge to strike off the excess sand remaining in the top of the mold.
                   Step 1g. Brush off any sand adhering to the outside of the mold.
                   Step 1h. Weigh the mold full of sand (in grams), and record it on Line 7.
                   Step 1i. Determine the weight (in grams) of the material by subtracting Line 8 from Line 7,
                        and record the weight on Line 9.
                   Step 1j. Enter the known volume of the mold (in cubic feet) on Line 10.

      Step 1k. Determine the unit weight of the material. Convert the weight of the materials
          (Line 9) from grams to pounds, divide this quotient by the known volume (Line 10), and
          record the weight on Line 11. Determine the unit weight of material by using the following
          formula:
                                                                    �𝑤𝑒𝑖𝑔ℎ𝑡 𝑜𝑓 𝑠𝑎𝑛𝑑 453.6 �
                  𝑢𝑛𝑖𝑡 𝑤𝑒𝑖𝑔ℎ𝑡 𝑜𝑓 𝑚𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑟𝑖𝑎𝑙 (𝐿𝑖𝑛𝑒 11) = 𝑣𝑜𝑙𝑢𝑚𝑒 𝑜𝑓 𝑚𝑜𝑙𝑑

      Step 1l. Repeat steps 1a through 1k at least two more times. The unit weight of sand used in
          the calculations is the average of at least three determinations. Record the average unit
          weight of material on Line 12. The maximum variation between any one determination and
          the average should not exceed 1 percent.

 Step 2. Prepare the site for sand-cone testing.
      Step 2a. Clear the overburden, and seat the template tray flush on the surface. Fasten it in
          place with nails.
      Step 2b. Seal the spaces on the inside edge under the template using soil from the
          preparation site.

 Step 3. Determine the surface calibration.
      Step 3a. Weigh the sand cone apparatus filled with sand. Record the weight (in grams) on
          Line 13.
      Step 3b. Turn the sand cone apparatus over with the valve closed, and place it on the
          template.
      Step 3c. Open the valve and allow the sand to fall at its own rate. Do not jar the apparatus
          while the sand is falling. Close the valve when the sand stops running.
      Step 3d. Remove the sand cone apparatus from the template, and weigh it. Record this
          weight (in grams) on Line 14.
      Step 3e. Determine the weight of the sand in the cone by subtracting Line 14 from Line 13.
          Record the difference on Line 15.

 Step 4. Recover as much sand as possible from the template and brush the remaining sand
     lightly from the hole, being careful not to disturb the soil surface.

 Step 5. Dig the sample.
      Step 5a. Predetermine the weight of a friction top can. Record this weight on Line 29.
      Step 5b. Dig a hole through the center of the template. The hole should be 6 inches deep or
          to the bottom of the lift and about the same diameter as the hole in the template. The sides
          should be as smooth as possible.
      Step 5c. Place all soil particles from the hole into the preweighed can, keeping the lid on the
          can as much as possible to prevent excessive moisture loss.
      Step 5d. Weigh the wet soil and can. Record the weight on Line 28.

 Step 6. Determine the volume of the hole.
      Step 6a. Refill the jar if it appears there is not enough sand to fill the hole and record the
          weight of the jar on Line 16; otherwise, transfer the weight from Line 14 to Line 16.
      Step 6b. Turn the sand cone apparatus over with the valve closed, and place it on the
          template.
      Step 6c. Open the valve, and allow the sand to fall at its own rate into the prepared hole. Do
          not jar the apparatus while the sand is falling.

Note. If additional sand is needed due to the requirements for hole volume, ensure that the jars
with sand have been properly weighed will be on hand during the testing procedures. Enter the
initial weight of the additional jar plus sand is included on Line 16 and the final weight of the
additional jar on Line 17.

STEEL SHOT VOLUME REPLACEMENT METHOD

     8-7. The steel shot volume replacement method is appropriate for expeditionary type military operations
     where the shipment of nuclear density gauges is prohibitively difficult or where the sand cone method is
     prohibitively slow, or requires too much user training and experience.

     8-8. The steel shot volume replacement method may be used in fine-grained and sandy soils with a
     maximum particle size of 1/2 inches or less. The soil must have physical and mechanical properties that
     permit the excavation of a stable cavity with a volume on the order of 0.04 to 0.05 cubic feet.
     Consequently, this test method is not applicable for granular soils that are saturated or loosely deposited.
     The relatively conservative limitation on particle size is necessary because the volume of the cavity from
     which soil is removed is smaller than the volume of cavities for the sand cone test.

     8-9. The steel shot volume replacement test consists of digging out a sample of the soil for which density
     must be estimated, determining the volume of the cavity, and determining the wet and dry weights of the
     sample of soil. Stainless steel balls, all with the same diameter of 3/16 inches are used to estimate the
     volume of the cavity from which a sample of soil has been taken. The balls are uniform in size in order to
     leave no opportunity for changing gradations, which could occur with segregation. The recommended balls
     are American Iron and Steel Institute (AISI) Type 440C as specified by ASTM A276.

     8-10. The steel shot replacement method is simpler and quicker than the sand cone method because the
     steel shot is much less susceptible to changes in densification with changes in methods of placement.
     Therefore, the procedure can be more lenient in terms of how the balls are placed. In addition, the packing

8-6        TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034                   3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                          In-Place Density Determination

     of the balls in a graduated cylinder is similar to the packing of the balls in a soil cavity. Therefore, the
     volume of balls used to fill a soil cavity is estimated directly from the volumetric marks on a graduated
     cylinder.

PURPOSE

     8-11. Perform this test to determine the in-place density of a soil to within 2 percent, or ± 2.5 pounds per
     cubic foot for soils with an average density of 125 pounds per cubic foot. There is no apparent bias in the
     steel shot test so the average measured soil density over multiple replicates will approach the same average
     measured soil density as found by the sand cone method.

EQUIPMENT

     8-12. Use the following items to perform this test in a field environment (figure 8-3):
              Straightedge, 12-inch length (to smooth the ground surface for testing).
              A 12-inch-square aluminum template with a 4-inch-diameter hole, raised edges (0.5 inches), and
                  a 3/16-inch hole near each corner for the purpose of using nails to secure the plate to the ground.
                  The plate should be 1/8 inch thick.
              Nails, twenty-penny four or more (for securing the aluminum template).
              80 pounds of steel shot with a loose unit weight of 284 pounds per cubic foot.
              Balance scale sensitive to 1.0 gram with a 5-kilogram capacity (not included in the photo).
              Hot plate for drying steel shot (not included in the photo).
              Plastic, graduated cylinders with a capacity of 1,000 milliliters and markings each 10 milliliters.
              Chisel, 9-inch (for digging the hole).
              Hammer, 3-pound (to be used with the chisel).
              Stainless-steel scoop, flat bottom, 3-inch scoop length, 9 inches overall (for digging the hole).
              Metal spoon, serving style, 13-inch (for digging the hole).
              Spatula, 3/4- by 4-inch blade (for trimming edges of cavity in the soil).
              Paint brush, 2 1/2 inches, soft bristle (for cleaning the hole and template surface).
              Metal bowl, 9 1/2-inch-diameter (for collecting and weighing soil).
              Stainless-steel scoop, round bottom, 5-inch scoop length, 9 inches overall (for pouring balls into
                  the graduated cylinder).

              Straightedge, 8-inch (for leveling balls with the top surface of the aluminum template).
              Magnetic wand for retrieving steel shot from the cavity in soil.
              No. 8 sieve (for cleaning balls).
              Metal pan, 9-inch-diameter (for drying balls).
              Data sheets.

              Figure 8-3. Steel shot volume replacement equipment with traveling case

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034                        8-7

Chapter 8

STEPS

     8-13. Perform the following steps for the steel shot volume replacement method:
              Step 1. Select a location/elevation that is relatively level and representative of the soil to be
                  tested.
                   Step 1a. Use a flathead shovel or the 12-inch straightedge to prepare the surface of the soil
                        at the location to be tested. The surface of the soil should be a level plane.
                   Step 1b. Seat the baseplate on the plane surface, ensuring that there is contact with the
                        ground surface around the edge of the center hole. Secure the plate against movement by
                        pushing or hammering a nail through each of the four 3/16-inch holes.
              Step 2. Dig the soil cavity.
                   Step 2a. Ensure that the test cavity volume is on the order of 0.04 to 0.05 cubic feet, which
                        equates to digging a 4-inch-diameter cavity that is 6 inches deep. For most expedient
                        construction operations, this depth should approximate the thickness of a compacted lift.
                   Step 2a. Dig the test cavity through the center hole in the baseplate, being careful to avoid
                        disturbing or deforming the soil that will bound the cavity. The sides of the cavity should
                        slope slightly inward, and the bottom should be reasonably flat or concave. The cavity
                        should be kept as free as possible of pockets, overhangs, and sharp obtrusions since these
                        affect the accuracy of the test. Place all excavated soil and any soil loosened during digging
                        in a moisture-tight container that is marked to identify the test number. Take care to avoid
                        losing any materials. Protect this material from any loss of moisture until the mass has been
                        determined and a specimen has been obtained for a water content determination.
              Step 3. Estimate the volume of the cavity in the soil.
                   Step 3a. Use the round-bottom scoop to fill the graduated cylinder with steel shot up to the
                        1,000-milliliter mark (figure 8-4). All measurements of steel shot bulk volume will involve
                        flattening the surface of balls in the cylinder and estimating the volume at the top surface of
                        the balls. While filling the cylinder, it may sit vertically or be tilted slightly. The scoop
                        should be held near the cylinder opening. The cylinder should not be subjected to excessive
                        shaking or vibration.

Figure 8-4. Schematic drawing of the graduated cylinder filled with balls up to the 1000 milliliter
                                                                 mark

                   Step 3b. Overfill the cavity slightly by pouring the steel shot from the graduated cylinder.
                        This process will take filling the graduated cylinder twice with steel shot because the
                        volume of the cavity should be on the order of 1,100 to 1,400 milliliters. While pouring, the
                        open end of the graduated cylinder should be held within 3 inches of the top of the cavity.
                        Use the 8-inch straightedge to strike off the overfilled cavity (figure 8-5) and pick up the
                        excess steel shot with the magnet. Place these balls back into the graduated cylinder.

8-8  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015

                          Figure 8-5. Striking off excess steel shot

          volume of the remaining balls to the nearest 5 milliliters (figure 8-6). Assuming the cavity is
          of sufficient size and the graduated cylinder had to be filled twice use the following
          formula:

                                        𝑉𝑡 (𝑖𝑛 𝑐𝑢𝑏𝑖𝑐 𝑐𝑒𝑛𝑡𝑖𝑚𝑒𝑡𝑒𝑟𝑠) = 𝑉𝑡 (𝑖𝑛 𝑚𝑖𝑙𝑙𝑖𝑙𝑖𝑡𝑒𝑟𝑠)
           where—
           Vt = total volume of the cavity
           Vb = bulk volume of the remaining balls to the nearest 5 milliliters
Note. The 25.7 milliliters is subtracted to account for the volume within the thickness of the
plate.

           described in the last section of this procedural document.

            Step 4. Determine the mass of soil removed from the cavity, and determine its moisture content.

            Step 4a. Ensure that the measured mass of soil (in grams) removed from the cavity excludes
                the mass of the container used to transport the soil.

            Step 4b. Mix the soil thoroughly to obtain a representative specimen for water content
                determination. The representative specimen should have a mass of at least 250 grams.

            Step 4c. Determine the moisture content of the representative specimen according to ASTM

           D2216, ASTM D4643, ASTM D4944, or ASTM D4959. Correlations to ASTM D2216 will

           be performed when required by other test methods.

                   𝑤(%) =  𝑤𝑒𝑡 𝑠𝑜𝑖𝑙 − 𝑑𝑟𝑦 𝑠𝑜𝑖𝑙              × 100%

           where—                                       𝑀s

                content (as a percentage), calculate the dry density of soil (in grams or cubic centimeters):
                                                                      ρs
                                                            ρd = 1 + 𝑤
                                                                           100

            Step 5c. To convert wet or dry density to unit weight of soil use the following formula:

           𝑢𝑛𝑖𝑡 𝑤𝑒𝑖𝑔ℎ𝑡 𝑜𝑓 𝑠𝑜𝑖𝑙 = 𝑠𝑜𝑖𝑙 𝑑𝑒𝑛𝑠𝑖𝑡𝑦 × 𝑤𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑟 𝑢𝑛𝑖𝑡 𝑤𝑒𝑖𝑔ℎ𝑡 𝑎𝑡 𝑟𝑜𝑜𝑚 𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑢𝑟𝑒

           ρd = dry density of soil (in grams or cubic centimeters)
           γs = wet unit weight (in pounds or cubic feet)
           γd = dry unit weight (in pounds or cubic feet)

                 Step 6c. Report in-place wet density or wet unit weight.
                 Step 6d. Report in-place dry density, dry density of the soil, or dry unit weight.
                 Step 6e. Report in-place moisture content of the soil.

 Step 7. Steel balls that do not fall into contact with soil can be reused. However, any balls that
     do fall into contact with the soil must be cleaned prior to reuse. Cleaning the balls simply
     involves rinsing them with water while they rest on a sieve; a No. 8 sieve has been found to
     work well for this purpose (figure 8-7). A slight agitation of the balls may be required to
     dislodge some soil particles. Once the balls appear to be clean, they can be dried by sitting them
     in the sun or by using a direct heat source.

                                  Figure 8-7. Rinsing steel shot in the No. 8 sieve

DRIVE CYLINDER METHOD

     8-14. The drive cylinder density method may be used in fine- or coarse-grained materials and is especially
     useful in weaker soils and soils where a sand cone or steel shot replacement test cannot be used because a
     cylindrical hole cannot retain vertical side walls. The test method is not recommended in very hard, natural
     soils; heavily compacted soils; or organic soils. Soils with appreciable amounts of gravel (particles larger
     than 3/16 inch) may introduce significant error in the final density measurement, owing to large voids in
     the final extruded sample.

     8-15. The drive cylinder density method consists of tamping a steel cylinder of known volume into the
     ground, digging the cylinder out of the ground and determining the dry weight of the sample.

PURPOSE

     8-16. Perform this test to determine the in-place density of a soil to within ± 2 pounds per cubic foot.

EQUIPMENT

     8-17. Use the following items to perform this test in a field environment (figure 8-8, page 8-12):
              A 3-, 4-, or 6-inch diameter drive cylinder.
              A drive head of suitable diameter to drive the cylinder.
              A drive hammer (4.5-kilogram sliding weight).
              A ruler.
              A knife.
              A steel straightedge.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034                   8-11


---

Chapter 8

              A shovel.
              A brush.
              A balance scale sensitive to 1.0 gram with a 20-kilogram capacity.
              Drying equipment for obtaining moisture content.
              A sealable container for the soil specimen.

                                        Figure 8-8. Drive cylinder components

STEPS

     8-18. Perform the following steps for the drive cylinder density method:
              Step 1. Prepare a level surface.
                   Step 1a. Where a density test is required, obtain a fairly level surface using a shovel,
                        straightedge, or on-site construction equipment (figure 8-9).

                        Figure 8-9. Preparing surface for drive cylinder test

            Step 1b. Brush away any loose particles on the surface prior to inserting the drive cylinder.
       Step 2. Assemble the apparatus.

            Step 2a. Obtain and record the mass of the empty drive cylinder.
            Step 2b. Screw the drive head onto the drive hammer.
            Step 2c. Insert the drive cylinder into the drive head with the sharpened edge facing the

                surface to be sampled.

8-12  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034                            3 April 2015


---

                                                                                            In-Place Density Determination

 Step 3. Obtain the sample.
      Step 3a. Drive the cylinder into the ground by raising the drop hammer and allowing it to
          fall or by applying a uniform force via a jack or similar device until the top of the cylinder is
          1/2 inch below the original surface. Keep the drive rod steady and in a vertical position at all
          times (figure 8-10).

                       Figure 8-10. Inserting drive cylinder into soil

Note. Care should be taken to only push the cylinder as deep as its measured height (2.5 to
4.5 inches). Overdriving the sample will compress the soil within the drive cylinder and provide
a false density reading. If overdriving is suspected, discard the sample and retest it.

      Step 3b. Remove the drive head, and dig the cylinder out of the ground using a shovel,
          digging the soil from around the sides of the cylinder and undercutting several inches below
          the bottom of the cylinder before lifting it out of the ground (figure 8-11).

                    Figure 8-11. Removing the drive cylinder sample

Note. If the soil is noncohesive or saturated, a plate or similar level surface may need to be slid
on the underside of the cylinder to prevent the leakage or soil prior to removing it from the
ground.

      Step 3c. Trim the excess soil from the end of the sample using a knife and straightedge until
          the sample is flush and plane with the ends of the cylinder (figure 8-12, page 8-14). Care
          should be taken to avoid cutting into soil contained within the cylinder.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  8-13


---

Chapter 8

                                 Figure 8-12. Trimmed sample ready for weighing

             Note. If the cylinder is not full or representative of the surrounding material, discard the sample
             and take another sample. If the drive cylinder is deformed or otherwise damaged while driving
             or removing the sample, obtain a new cylinder for subsequent tests and discard the current
             sample.

                   Step 3d. Weigh the full drive cylinder and soil immediately upon completion of trimming
                        and record the weight as mass.

                   Step 3e. Extrude the soil sample, and place at least 100 grams of it into a sealable container
                        to obtain the moisture content. Use a drying technique (oven, microwave, calcium carbide
                        pressure moisture test) to obtain the moisture content.

                   Step 3f. Calculate the dry mass of the drive-cylinder sample as follows:
                                                    𝑀3 = [(𝑀1 − 𝑀2)/(100 + 𝑤)] 𝑥 100

                  where—
                  M1 = mass
                  M2 = mass of the empty drive cylinder
                  M3 = dry mass of the drive cylinder sample
                  w = moisture content of the soil (as a percentage)
                   Determine the volume of the drive cylinder based on its height and internal diameter.
                   Calculate the dry density of the drive-cylinder sample as follows:

                                                                      𝑝d = 𝑀3/𝑉
                  where—
                  pd = dry density of the drive cylinder
                  M3 = dry mass of the drive cylinder sample
                  V = volume of the drive cylinder

     8-19. If the sample must be taken below the ground surface, auger a hole to the required depth, clean loose
     material from the bottom of the hole, lower the drive cylinder into the hole with sufficient rod to permit
     driving with a sledgehammer, and drive the cylinder until it is approximately 1 inch below the bottom
     surface of the augured hole. Remove the cylinder from the hole and trim it and weigh as above.

NUCLEAR MOISTURE AND DENSITY TESTER

     8-20. Use this method to determine the dry density and moisture content of the soil. Individual models of
     equipment vary in the specific procedures, and radiation protection programs vary from Service to Service.
     It is for this reason that the procedures are not discussed here. The procedures for this test method must be

8-14       TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                          In-Place Density Determination

     as prescribed by the equipment manufacturer’s manual, the requirements prescribed by the Service holding
     the piece of equipment, and the Nuclear Regulatory Commission licensing agreement with that Service.

     8-21. The testers contain sources of radioactive material, typically cesium and a combination of americium
     mixed with beryllium powder. The cesium emits gamma radiation that the detector in the tester can count
     when it is passes through the soil. This count can be translated into density. The americium/beryllium emits
     neutrons following collisions with hydrogen which are moderated and detected by the tester. The moisture
     content can be determined by counting the hydrogen atoms in the soil.

WATER DISPLACEMENT METHOD

     8-22. Measure the volume of the hole from which a soil density sample is taken by placing a rubber
     balloon in the hole and observing the volume of water required to fill the balloon. A water balloon device is
     a watertight container with a float attached to a calibrated scale, graduated directly in cubic feet. A balloon
     is attached to the bottom of the device to make the test. Fill the cylinder in the device with water, and place
     the apparatus over the area where the sample is to be removed. Allow the balloon to fill with water and take
     an initial reading. Remove the sample from the ground and replace the device over the hole in the original
     position. Allow the water to flow by gravity into the balloon in the hole. Blow through the hose attached to
     the device to increase the air pressure on the water surface and force the water-filled balloon in the hole to
     conform to all the contours of the hole. Observe the scale attached to the float for a reading of the water
     volume left in the device. Subtract this value from the original reading. The result is the volume of the hole
     (in cubic feet).

NONNUCLEAR DENSITY DEVICES

     8-23. Currently, several manufacturers produce nonnuclear devices that are capable of measuring moisture
     content and soil density by measuring the resistance of electrical impulses within the soil or correlating
     electromagnetic field response to known soil properties.

     8-24. No formal military guidance has been provided to compare the effectiveness of these nonnuclear
     devices to the known test methods described previously nor the limitations these nonnuclear devices have
     in certain soil classifications. Care should be taken by the operator when using these devices as calibration
     is a critical issue in obtaining useful data. It is recommended that these devices only be used for quality
     control when large-scale soil construction is ongoing using a uniform soil to minimize field calibration.
     These devices are not suitable for spot-checking density in random soils at a level sufficient for quality
     assurance.

                                                 Chapter 9

                     California Bearing Ratio Tests

         The CBR test is a relatively simple test used to obtain an indication of the strength of
         a subgrade soil, subbase, and base course material for use in road and airfield
         pavements. The test is used primarily to determine empirically required thicknesses
         of flexible pavements for highways and airfield pavements. The test was developed
         by the California Division of Highways in 1929 and adopted by the Corps of
         Engineers for use in the design of flexible pavements for airfields in locations where
         frost action is not the controlling factor. The test procedure determines the CBR of
         pavement subgrade, subbase, and base course materials from laboratory-compacted
         specimens that can be used in the design of a specific airfield. It consists of—

          • Preparing the soil test specimens.
          • Performing the penetration test on the prepared soil samples.

         Although one standardized procedure has been established for the penetration portion
         of the test, it is not possible to establish one procedure for the preparation of test
         specimens since soil conditions and construction methods vary widely. The soil test
         specimens are prepared to duplicate the existing (or those expected to occur later) soil
         conditions in the field. The method of preparing the test specimens and the number of
         specimens depend on the type of airfield, the soils encountered at the site, and other
         factors. Test the soil sample in the laboratory at a density that is comparable to the
         density required on the construction site. There are situations where moisture
         conditions are favorable and the subgrade will not accumulate moisture approaching
         a saturated condition. Test samples at a moisture content that approximates the actual
         moisture conditions expected during the time the road or airfield is used. In all other
         conditions, samples are laboratory tested in a saturated condition.

             Note. The current employed standard for CBR testing is ASTM D1883.

CALIFORNIA BEARING RATIO OF LABORATORY-COMPACTED
SOILS

     9-1. The basic operations for conducting the CBR test are the same regardless of variations in soil
     conditions and types of construction. The test essentially measures the shearing resistance of the soil under
     controlled moisture and density conditions. The CBR for soil is the ratio obtained by dividing the
     penetration stress required to cause a 3-inch square area piston to penetrate 0.10 inch into the soil by a
     standard penetration stress of 1,000 pounds per square inch. This standard penetration stress is roughly
     what is required to cause the same piston to penetrate 0.10 inch into a mass of crushed rock (limestone).
     The CBR value may be thought of as the strength of the soil relative to that of crushed rock.

     9-2. Minor variations in the CBR test will cause wide variations in the results. For this reason, step-by-
     step procedures are detailed. Difficulties may still arise. Material with gravel or stones does not yield
     entirely satisfactory results. A number of tests must be conducted to establish a reasonable average value.

     9-3. CBR values range from 0 to more than 100, depending on the type of soil and the moisture content in
     which they are measured. A misconception is that CBR cannot exceed 100, but in reality, some field soils
     in the right environment can have values exceeding 100. For the purposes of design and evaluation, the
     CBR will be capped at 100 even if field values exceed this.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  9-1


---

Chapter 9

     9-4. CBR is often reported as a soaked CBR value, meaning that a prepared sample has been allowed to
     saturate prior to testing, providing a worst-case scenario value in case of water inundation in the field. Fine-
     grained soils typically have a lower CBR than coarse-grained soils, but moisture plays an important role in
     the final value. A wet clay can have CBRs in the 0 to 10 range, whereas a dry clay can have a CBR greater
     than 50. Soaked micaceous or diatomaceous silts and sands have CBRs of 15 or greater. The sand-silt-clay
     coarse-grained combinations range from 10 for clayey mixtures to 40 for gravelly and silty sands. Gravelly
     soils range from 20 for the clayey group to 80 for well-graded gravels and gravel-sand mixtures which are
     much less susceptible to inundation by moisture. See appendix B for the typical range for soils classified
     under the USCS over a wide range of compaction moisture contents.

PURPOSE

     9-5. Perform this test to determine the CBR of a soil over a moisture content range of ± 4 percent of OMC
     in nonswelling soils and ± 8 percent of OMC in swelling soils.

EQUIPMENT

     9-6. Use the following items in a laboratory environment to perform this test (figure 9-1):

              A 225-pound soil sample with known classification, PI, and OMC.
              A CBR laboratory test set consisting of—

                   Five CBR molds for each compaction effort desired.
                   Surcharge weights.
                   A penetration piston.
                   Two dial gauges reading to 0.001 inch.
              Pails.
              Plastic bags.
              A compaction tamper.
              Filter paper.
              A recorder.
              Moisture determination tares.
              Balance scales sensitive to 0.01 gram and 0.1 gram.
              A steel straightedge.
              A mixing pan.
              A spoon.
              A spatula.
              A graduated cylinder (100-milliliter).
              A laboratory oven.
              Heat-resistant gloves.

             Note. Asbestos gloves should not be used for any materials testing procedures. If the unit has
             asbestos gloves, turn them in through the supply system for proper disposal. Order heat resistant
             gloves to replace them.

              A pencil.
              Paper.
              A grease pencil.
              A calculator.
              DD Form 1210, DD Form 1211, and DD Form 1212 (Laboratory California Bearing Ratio

                  [CBR] Test Data).
              A 3/4-inch sieve.
              A No. 4 sieve.

9-2  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                             California Bearing Ratio Tests

 Pudding pans.
 A stopwatch.
 Plans and specifications.

                                   Figure 9-1. Laboratory CBR test-set apparatus

STEPS

     9-7. Perform the following steps for the CBR test.

Step 1. Determine the Moisture Content Range of Investigation

     9-8. Determine the moisture content range of investigation (± 4 percent of OMC) for the CBR test. If the
     OMC has not yet been definitively established for this soil type, perform the laboratory compaction test as
     described in chapter 8 to obtain the OMC.

Step 2. Prepare the Soil Sample

     9-9. The size of the total sample will be about 225 pounds, which should provide enough material for the
     required 6,800 grams for each of the 15 molds.

              Step 2a. Dry the soil sample until it can be easily crumbled under a trowel. Drying may be done
                  by air-drying or by using a drying apparatus, if the temperature of the sample does not exceed
                  60°C.

              Step 2b. Break up the sample thoroughly, but not in such a manner as to reduce the size of the
                  individual particles.

              Step 2c. Sieve the sample over a 3/4-inch sieve. If all material passes the 3/4-inch sieve, use the
                  entire gradation without modification. If there is material retained on the 3/4-inch sieve, remove
                  it and replace it with an equal amount (by weight) of material passing the 3/4-inch sieve, but
                  retained on the No. 4 sieve, by separation from portions of the sample not otherwise used for
                  testing. The CBR test is not conducted on any material retained on the 3/4-inch sieve.) the
                  amount not retained on the 3/4-inch sieve will be recorded later on DD Form 1212 (figure 9-2,
                  page 9-4).

              Step 2d. Divide the entire sample into 15 smaller samples of about 6,800 grams each and place
                  them into separate plastic bags. Seal the bags to maintain the current (or floor) moisture content.

              Step 2e. Determine the moisture content from a 20-gram sample of the remaining material.
                  Record this on a sheet of paper as the moisture content of the floor sample.

Chapter 9

Step 3. Prepare and Label a 6-Inch Compaction Mold

     9-10. Label a 6-inch compaction mold for each water content to be used (-4 percent, -2 percent, OMC, +2
     percent, and +4 percent) at the compactive effort of 56 blows per layer as described in chapter 7.

             Note. If required, prepare additional molds for each water content, using other compactive
             efforts of 10 and 25 blows per layer (use five molds per effort). Prepare a DD Form 1212 for
             each mold and record the weight of the mold with the baseplate (to the nearest gram) on the form
             before continuing.

Step 4. Prepare the Samples at the Adjusted Water Contents

              Step 4a. Determine the amount of water to add to each sample using the following formula:
                                                        𝑤𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑟 𝑡𝑜 𝑎𝑑𝑑 (𝑖𝑛 𝑚𝑖𝑙𝑙𝑖𝑙𝑖𝑡𝑒𝑟𝑠)

                                   = 𝑤𝑒𝑖𝑔ℎ𝑡 𝑜𝑓 𝑠𝑎𝑚𝑝𝑙𝑒 (𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚𝑠) 𝑥 (𝑑𝑒𝑠𝑖𝑟𝑒𝑑 %– 𝑓𝑙𝑜𝑜𝑟 𝑚𝑜𝑖𝑠𝑡𝑢𝑟𝑒 𝑐𝑜𝑛𝑡𝑒𝑛𝑡)

             Example. To determine the amount of water to add to a sample see the following formula:
                                  6,800 𝑥 (0.08 – 0.02) = 6,800 𝑥 0.06 = 408.0 𝑚𝑖𝑙𝑙𝑖𝑙𝑖𝑡𝑒𝑟𝑠

             where—
             sample weight = 6,800 grams
             OMC = 8% (0.08)
             floor moisture content = 2% (0.02)

             To determine the water to add for the remaining samples for the required moisture content range,
             perform the same calculation. The example below illustrates this calculation for the remaining
             samples, taking into consideration that not all of the sample weights will be exactly the same
             (6,800 grams):
             6.0% 𝑚𝑜𝑖𝑠𝑡𝑢𝑟𝑒 𝑓𝑜𝑟 𝑎 𝑠𝑎𝑚𝑝𝑙𝑒 𝑜𝑓 6,800 𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚𝑠: 6,800 𝑥 (0.06 – 0.02) = 272.2 𝑚𝑖𝑙𝑙𝑖𝑙𝑖𝑡𝑒𝑟𝑠
             4.0% 𝑚𝑜𝑖𝑠𝑡𝑢𝑟𝑒 𝑓𝑜𝑟 𝑎 𝑠𝑎𝑚𝑝𝑙𝑒 𝑜𝑓 6,800 𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚𝑠: 6,815 𝑥 (0.04 – 0.02) = 136.3 𝑚𝑖𝑙𝑙𝑖𝑙𝑖𝑡𝑒𝑟𝑠
             10.0% 𝑚𝑜𝑖𝑠𝑡𝑢𝑟𝑒 𝑓𝑜𝑟 𝑎 𝑠𝑎𝑚𝑝𝑙𝑒 𝑜𝑓 6,800 𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚𝑠: 6,822 𝑥 (0.10 – 0.02) = 545.8 𝑚𝑖𝑙𝑙𝑖𝑙𝑖𝑡𝑒𝑟𝑠
             12.0% 𝑚𝑜𝑖𝑠𝑡𝑢𝑟𝑒 𝑓𝑜𝑟 𝑎 𝑠𝑎𝑚𝑝𝑙𝑒 𝑜𝑓 6,800 𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚𝑠: 6,810 𝑥 (0.12 – 0.02) = 681.0 𝑚𝑖𝑙𝑙𝑖𝑙𝑖𝑡𝑒𝑟𝑠

              Step 4b. Add the water figured from the formulas for each of the five desired moisture contents
                  (-4, -2, OMC, +2, and +4), and mix thoroughly to ensure an even distribution of water
                  throughout the sample.

              Step 4c. Place each sample in an airtight container, and allow it to stand for the minimum of
                  time indicated as follows:
                   For GW, GP, SW, and SP soil types, there is no minimum standing time.
                   For GM and SM soil types, a minimum of 3 hours standing time is required. For all other
                        soil types, a minimum of 16 hours standing time is required.

Step 5. Mark and Weigh Two Moisture Determination Tares for Each Mold Prepared

     9-11. For the CBR test on laboratory-compacted soil, mark and weigh two moisture determination tares for
     each mold prepared. Record each on DD Form 1212 as the weight of tare.

Step 6. Remove a Small Quantity of Material

     9-12. Remove a small quantity of material (20 grams) from each sample, and place it in one of the marked
     tares upon completion of the standing time. Weigh it in preparation for moisture determination, and record
     the results on the form.

Step 7. Place Sufficient Soil in the Mold

     9-13. Place sufficient soil in the mold (about 1 1/2 to 2 inches) to obtain a 1-inch compacted layer. After
     compaction of all five layers, each layer should be about equal in thickness. The fifth compacted layer will
     slightly extend into the collar, but will not exceed 1/4 inch above the top of the mold.

9-6  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                            California Bearing Ratio Tests

Step 8. Apply Compactive Effort

     9-14. Perform the following steps to complete step 8:
              Step 8a. Hold the 10-pound compaction tamper within 5°, vertically, placing its face on top of
                  the soil.
              Step 8b. Raise the handle until it reaches the top (18 inches) and release it, allowing the weight
                  to fall freely onto the soil.
              Step 8c. Change the position of the guide and tamper and repeat the process until the soil layer
                  has received the prescribed number of blows for the compactive effort required. Apply the blows
                  at a uniform rate of about 25 blows per minute. The height of fall of the tamper must be
                  controlled carefully, and the blows must be distributed evenly over the specimen’s surface.

Step 9. Trim the Compacted Layer

     9-15. After compacting each layer (except the fifth layer), use a knife to trim soil adjacent to the mold
     walls that has not been compacted or that extends above the compacted surface. Include the trimmed soil
     with the additional soil for the next layer.

Step 10. Repeat Steps 7, 8, and 9

     9-16. Repeat steps 7, 8, and 9 until five layers have been compacted in the mold. Each compacted layer
     should be about equal in thickness (just under 1 inch). Adjust each layer accordingly to ensure that the fifth
     compacted layer extends slightly into the collar, but does not exceed 1/4 inch above the top of the mold.

Step 11. Remove the Collar From the Mold

     9-17. Perform the following steps to complete step 11:
              Step 11a. Cut around the inside edge of the collar to prevent shearing the compacted soil when
                  removing the collar.
              Step 11b. Trim and smooth the compacted soil flush with the top of the mold. Use a sawing
                  motion with the straightedge to trim the excess soil. Start at the center of the mold, and work
                  outward, first to one side and then to the other. Fill any holes with unused or trimmed soil from
                  the specimen, press in with the fingers, and scrape the surface with the straightedge.

Step 12. Take Another Small Sample

     9-18. Take another small sample from the remaining material after compacting each mold. Place this
     sample in the other moisture determination tare, and weigh it in preparation for moisture determination.
     Record the results on DD Form 1212.

Step 13. Place a Disk of Filter Paper on Top of the Compacted Specimen

     9-19. Release the mold from the baseplate; and while slightly lifting, slide the mold with the spacer disk
     off the baseplate and onto the edge of the table or countertop. Invert the baseplate, and place it on top of the
     mold against the filter paper and reattach it. Raise the mold and baseplate to allow the spacer disk to slide
     out. Invert the mold with the baseplate, and place it flat on the table or countertop.

Step 14. Weigh and Record Data

     9-20. Weigh the complete mold with the baseplate and compacted specimen (without the spacer disk) to
     the nearest gram. Record as the weight of the mold and wet soil (DD Form 1212, Block 20). Do not include
     the collar.

Step 15. Repeat Steps 7 through 14

     9-21. Repeat steps 7 through 14 for each of the compactive efforts required (10, 25, and 56 blows per
     layer). Compact a minimum of five molds for each compactive effort.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  9-7
