Chapter 9

Step 16. Soak the Samples and Measure the Swell

     9-22. Perform the following steps to complete step 16 (figure 9-3):

                                          Figure 9-3. Soaking the CBR sample
              Step 16a. Place surcharge weights on the perforated plate and adjustable stem assembly, and

                  carefully lower into the mold onto the filter paper and compacted soil specimen. Ensure that the
                  surcharge applied is equal to the weight of the base material and pavement within
                  2.27 kilograms, but never use a total weight of less than 4.54 kilograms. If no pavement weight
                  is specified, use 4.54 kilograms. Record the surcharge weight on the form.
              Step 16b. Immerse the mold in water.
              Step 16c. Calibrate the adjustable stem so that the tripodal dial reads 100 and can then travel in
                  direction. Obtain the initial dial reading, and record it on the form.
              Step 16d. Soak the sample for 96 hours.
              Step 16e. Assemble the penetration apparatus (figure 9-4).

                    Figure 9-4. Assembled CBR test penetration apparatus

      Step 16f. Attach the jack to the frame.
      Step 16g. Attach the proving ring and dial indicator to the frame. Record the proving-ring data

          on the form.
      Step 16h. Take the dial reading at 96 hours, and record it on the form.

              Step 16i. Determine the amount of swell (in inches) by subtracting the initial dial reading from
                  the final dial reading. Record the data on the form.

              Step 16j. Determine the percent of swell, and record it on the form using the following formula:
                                                    𝑠𝑤𝑒𝑙𝑙 (𝑖𝑛 𝑖𝑛𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑠) (𝑎𝑡 96 ℎ𝑜𝑢𝑟𝑠) × 100
                                                         𝑜𝑟𝑖𝑔𝑖𝑛𝑎𝑙 𝑠𝑎𝑚𝑝𝑙𝑒 ℎ𝑒𝑖𝑔ℎ𝑡

Step 17. Drain the California Bearing Ratio Mold

     9-23. Perform the following steps to complete step 17:
              Step 17a. Remove the immersed mold from the water, and remove the free water. Allow the
                  specimen to drain downward for 15 minutes. Do not disturb the specimen’s surface while
                  removing the water. It may be necessary to tilt the specimen to remove surface water.
              Step 17b. Remove the surcharge weights, the perforated plate, and the filter paper from the
                  mold. Do not disturb the specimen’s surface while removing these items.
              Step 17c. Determine the mass, and record it on the form as the weight of the soaked sample.
              Step 17d. Return a 5-pound surcharge weight to the specimen.

Step 18. Perform the California Bearing Ratio Penetration

     9-24. Perform the following steps to complete step 18:
              Step 18a. Prepare the components.
                   Step 18a(1). Place the mold on the jack.
                   Step 18a(2). Attach and adjust the piston to the jack, and zero the dial indicator.
              Step 18b. Lower the penetration piston until it is in contact with the sample with sufficient
                  pressure to cause the load dial to register a load of 1 pound.
                   Step 18b(1). Replace the remainder of the surcharge weights required for the mold.
                   Step 18b(2). Attach the adjustable arm with the dial indicator to the jack assembly, adjusting
                        the position until the dial-indicator plunger is resting on the mold’s projecting rim.
                   Step 18b(3). Turn both dial indicators to 0.
              Step 18c. Apply the load. Perform the following steps to complete step 18c:
                   Step 18c(1). Crank the jack to lower the piston at a rate of 0.05 inch per minute.
                   Step 18c(2). Read the proving-ring dial indicator when the piston has reached the
                        penetration depths of 0.025, 0.050, 0.075, 0.100, 0.125, 0.150, 0.175, 0.200, 0.300, 0.400,
                        and 0.500 inch. Take the first eight readings at 30-second intervals and the remaining three
                        at 2-minute intervals.
                   Step 18c(3). Record the proving-ring dial readings on the form.
              Step 18d. Determine the average moisture of the soaked samples.
                   Step 18d(1). Remove the top 1 inch of soil from the mold. For fine-grained soils, place at
                        least 100 grams of soil in a moisture-determination tare. For granular soils, place at least
                        500 grams of soil in a moisture determination tare.
                   Step 18d(2). Weigh the tare, and record on the form.
                   Step 18d(3). Remove an additional sample from the remaining contents of the mold for
                        moisture determination. For fine-grained soils, place at least 100 grams of soil in a moisture
                        determination tare. For granular soils, place at least 500 grams of soil in a moisture
                        determination tare.
                   Step 18d(4). Weigh the tare and record on the form.
                   Step 18d(5). Perform the moisture content determination of the tares. Record the results and
                        average on the form.

Chapter 9

Step 19. Solve the Computations for Each Reading

     9-25. Perform the following steps to complete step 19:

              Step 19a. Determine the total load (in pounds) by multiplying the proving-ring dial reading
                  (Block 19c) by the proving-ring constant (Block 13). Enter this number in Block 19e of the
                  form. The corrected ring dial readings need not be determined if the dial indicators have been
                  zeroed before penetration. If the dials were not adjusted to 0 before penetration, determine the
                  corrected ring dial readings and enter them in Block 19d. Calculate the total load from the
                  corrected reading instead of the observed reading.

              Step 19b. Determine the unit load (in pounds per square inch) by dividing the total load by 3.
                  Enter this number in Block 19f of the form. The value of 3 is determined by the area of the
                  penetrating piston in square inches.

              Step 19c. Determine the corrected unit load (in pounds per square inch) by plotting (on the
                  reverse side of the form) the unit load (in pounds per square inch) against the depth of
                  penetration (in inches). If the curve has an initial concave upward shape between 0 and 0.1, then
                  the zero point must be adjusted. This occasionally happens with some soil types under certain
                  conditions and it is necessary to obtain true penetration loads. Adjust the zero point of the curve
                  as indicated in the example in figure 9-3. Once the zero-point correction has been made, the
                  0.100- and 0.200-inch points are moved to the right on the curve the same distance as the zero
                  point. Obtain corrected unit-load values from the corrected graphs at 0.100- and 0.200-inch
                  penetrations and enter in Block 19g of the form. If no corrections were made, the numbers
                  entered into Block 19g will be the same as Block 19f.

              Step 19d. Calculate the CBR (in percent) for penetration at 0.100 and 0.200 inches using the
                  following formula:
                                                            𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑡𝑒𝑑 𝑢𝑛𝑖𝑡 𝑙𝑜𝑎𝑑 × 100
                                                            𝑠𝑡𝑎𝑛𝑑𝑎𝑟𝑑 𝑢𝑛𝑖𝑡 𝑙𝑜𝑎𝑑

           Example. Since the standard unit load for each penetration is given (Block 19b), use the
           following formulas for each penetration:

           𝐶𝐵𝑅 𝑓𝑜𝑟 0.100 =  𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑡𝑒𝑑 𝑢𝑛𝑖𝑡 𝑙𝑜𝑎𝑑  × 100

                            1,000

           𝐶𝐵𝑅 𝑓𝑜𝑟 0.200 =  𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑡𝑒𝑑 𝑢𝑛𝑖𝑡 𝑙𝑜𝑎𝑑  × 100

                            1,500

           Note. The CBR value of the mold is computed at 0.100- and 0.200-inch penetrations. The
           bearing ratio normally reported is that of the 0.100-inch penetration. When the ratio at the
           0.200-inch penetration is greater, the test must be verified by another test. If the test is verified
           with similar results, use the bearing ratio at the 0.200-inch penetration.

Step 20. Complete Block 33

     9-26. Ensure that information concerning the soil sample is indicated in Block 33.

UNDISTURBED SAMPLE TESTING

     9-27. Tests on undisturbed samples are used when the base design calls for uncompacted soil (such as
     highly compressible clay that loses strength upon remolding) or when correlating field in-place tests to the
     design moisture condition. For the latter condition, duplicate samples should be tested to determine the
     correction necessary for the in-place tests. The reduction that occurs from 4 days of soaking is applied as a
     correction to the field in-place test.

     9-28. Care and patience are necessary to maintain the relatively undisturbed samples in this condition. If
     proper lateral support is not given on the sides of the samples, erroneous CBR values will result. In fine-
     grained materials, molds or metal jackets are satisfactory. With samples are cut or trimmed from a pedestal,

use a mixture of 10 percent resin and 90 percent paraffin to fill the annular space and offer support. For
gravelly soils, the box method is desirable. Use wax paper or paraffin to cover the sample and prevent
moisture loss while transporting it to the laboratory.

9-29. Perform soaking and penetration tests after removing the paper or paraffin from the end of the
specimen and after leveling the surface (use a thin layer of sand if necessary).

CALIFORNIA BEARING RATIO DATA PRESENTATION AND
ANALYSIS

     9-30. The CBR value, molding water content, and dry density for each specimen can be presented in
     several ways that facilitate analysis. The individual test programs used to present this data are relative to
     the type of soil encountered and are discussed in the following paragraphs.

TEST PROGRAM FOR NONSWELLING SOILS

     9-31. The test program for nonswelling soils applies to the majority of soils used in construction. Soils that
     fall into this group might be used as compacted subgrade, select, or subbase materials depending on their
     strengths and location regarding the construction site. The compaction requirements can then be determined
     as listed in table 9-1.

                                 Table 9-1. Summary of compaction requirements

                                    design CBR values below 20

              subgrade in fills     95% of CE 56 maximum density. Cohesive fill
                                    will not be placed at less than 90% of CE 56

Example

     9-32. To illustrate the methods of evaluating the design CBR, the data given on the DD Form 2463
     (California Bearing Ratio [CBR] Analysis) for the Engineer Center expansion road (figure 9-5, page 9-12)
     will be used. The data was taken from the subgrade along a proposed road alignment. The object of the
     following analysis is to determine a soil placement moisture content range for a specified level of
     compactive effort which gives the greatest assured design CBR. This technique for determining a design
     CBR provides for a strength measure of at least 15.0 when the associated density and moisture content
     ranges are followed. Greater strengths will be realized within the specified limits, but the value obtained
     allows the engineer to size the structure for the worst condition. Notice that for this soil and the limits used,
     the greatest assured strength occurs for the 4 percent moisture content range centered on the OMC. This
     may not always be the case. Also note that the analysis is based on an initial selection of density limits. It is
     better to evaluate other density limits that meet the minimum requirements to see if an adjustment to these
     limits yields greater strengths.

                                                             California Bearing Ratio Tests

Steps for California Bearing Ratio Testing of a Nonswelling Soil

     9-33. Perform the following steps for CBR testing of a nonswelling soil:

              Step 1. Establish the optimum moisture content. Establish the OMC of the soil at 56 blows
                  per layer by using the data collected from the compaction test (see chapter 7). For this example,
                  OMC equals 8.8 percent.

              Step 2. Establish a moisture range for CBR investigation. For step 2 of CBR testing of a
                  nonswelling soil, Establish a moisture range for CBR investigation. The moisture range
                  generally used for nonswelling soils is OMC ± 4 percent. This is a time-saving guide, as
                  experience shows that the maximum CBR normally occurs at compaction moisture contents
                  within this range and that testing soils beyond these limits is wasted effort. For this example, the
                  moisture-content investigation range is 5 to 13 percent.

              Step 3. Compact the samples within the moisture content investigation range. For step 3 of
                  CBR testing of a nonswelling soil, compact the samples within the moisture-content
                  investigation range at different levels of compactive effort. This allows for evaluation of soil
                  strength when field placement is other than 100 percent of the compaction test’s maximum
                  density. The levels of compactive effort selected in the laboratory are based on the compaction
                  requirements. If the soil is to be used as select material or subgrade in fills (CBR less than 20), it
                  may be placed at less than 100 percent of the compaction test’s maximum density for CE 56, as
                  indicated in table 9-2. Additionally, laboratory compactive efforts of 10, 25, and 56 blows per
                  layer are usually selected for this soil. If the soil is a very high-quality subgrade or a subbase
                  (CBR greater than 20), the laboratory tests should include samples compacted in excess of 56
                  blows per layer. Normally, 25, 56, and 72 blows per layer are adequate. The only stipulations
                  placed on the levels of compactive effort are that a 56-blow-per-layer compaction curve be
                  obtained. If desired, data can be developed at two other levels of compactive effort,
                  encompassing the specified placement densities. In the example on DD Form 1211 the soil type
                  is SC and the DD Form 1211 displays a bell-shaped compaction curve. This is indicative of a
                  swelling or a nonswelling soil. A U-shaped compaction curve indicates a free-draining soil, so
                  an assumption is made that this is a nonswelling soil.

                        Table 9-2. Summary of remolded CBR laboratory test programs

Test Program    Type of Soil Normally  Compaction Blows      Probable Use of Test
                          Tested*            per Layer                Results

Swelling soils  CH, MH, and OH         10, 25, and 56        Low-quality compacted
                                                             subgrades

Free-draining soils GW, GP, SW, and SP 25, 56, and 72        Compacted subgrade,
                                                             select, and subbase
                                                             materials

Other soils     All except CH, MH,     For CBR >20: 25, 56,  Compacted subgrade,
                OH,                    and 72                select, and subbase
                                       For CBR <20: 10, 25,  materials
                GW, GP, SW, and SP     and 56

*This categorization is intended to serve as a guide for planning laboratory activities. Deviations may be noted in the
initial stages of a test program, which will dictate adjustments.

CBR

 Step 4. Soak the samples and measure the well. Soak the samples and measure the swell.

 Step 5. Perform CBR tests and determine the corrected CBR. Perform CBR penetration tests
     and determine the corrected CBR for each sample.

Note. Accumulating the required data can involve a considerable amount of work. For this
example, 21 molds (7 for each of three compactive efforts) were soaked and then penetrated.

Chapter 9

              Step 6. Record the test results on the DD Form 2463. Transfer the test results from each DD
                  Form 1212 to the data summary section of DD Form 2463 (figure 9-5, page 9-12).

              Step 7. Plot the data on graphs. Plot the data on the graphs of dry density versus molding
                  moisture content and corrected CBR versus molding moisture content on DD Form 2463.

              Step 8. Determine the moisture range. Determine the moisture range for the CBR family of
                  curves. For a nonswelling soil, the moisture content that corresponds to the MDD is the OMC.
                  The moisture range is OMC ± 4 percent. In this example, the OMC discovered from DD Form
                  2463 is 9 percent; therefore, the range for the family of curves is 5 to 13 percent.

              Step 9. Transcribe data points to DD Form 2463. Transcribe the data points to the graphs on
                  DD Form 2463 for each whole moisture percentage in the range determined from step 8. This
                  step is performed to identify values of dry density and CBR for whole integer moisture contents
                  within the range of investigation at each level of compactive effort. Some of the data may be
                  transferred directly from the data summary table. The remainder must be interpolated from the
                  CBR/dry-density graphics versus molding moisture content graphs.

              Step 10. Plot the CBR family of curves. Plot the CBR family of curves on DD Form 2463,
                  page 3. This step places the laboratory data in a form that lends itself to analysis. The trends of
                  strength variation are determined as the moisture content and dry-density change. Drawing a
                  CBR family of curves involves considerable practice, numerous attempts, and subsequent
                  adjustments. The sample DD Form 2463 (figure 9-5, page 9-12) shows that the available data
                  has been plotted on a graph of corrected CBR versus molded, dry density for whole moisture
                  contents. For low-molding water contents (from 5 to 8 percent), there was an increase in strength
                  with dry density. At high moisture contents (12 and 13 percent) the reverse was true. For the
                  intermediate moisture contents, there was an increase in strength to some point and then a
                  decrease as the dry density increased.

              Step 11. Proceed with an engineering analysis. Proceed with an engineering analysis. After all
                  testing has been completed, it is the engineer’s responsibility to ensure that the CBR data is
                  properly obtained and presented. The engineer must analyze the data and understand how the
                  results affect the design and economic factors. Before considering the details of the analysis, it is
                  possible to observe the following points of interest about the soil being used for the sample:

                   The maximum strength of this soil does not occur at OMC, but at 1 percent drier (see
                        corrected CBR versus molding water content).

                   The CBR family of curves shows that for some moisture contents, the soil loses strength as
                        the dry density or level of compactive effort increases.

                  The impact of the two preceding factors is important. If maximum strength from this soil is
                  desired, may be achieved at a moisture content less than OMC and at some level of compactive
                  effort less than CE 56. The reasons for these two phenomena are highly speculative. Soil
                  placement at OMC and the most compactive effort possible are not always the answers to good
                  construction.

              Step 12. Establish a density range. Establish a density range at which soil will be placed in the
                  field. The theater of operations standard compaction range is 5 percent, unless otherwise stated.
                  The flow chart in figure 9-6 provides the information necessary for determining the density and
                  moisture ranges for nonswelling, swelling, and free-draining soils. To facilitate construction, it is
                  common to specify a reasonable range of densities that can be economically obtained and
                  examine the strength values that would occur without that range. Establishing the density range
                  depends greatly on economics. The more latitude given to the builder, the better the chances of
                  placing the soil within established limits. However, if an extreme range is stipulated, the CBR
                  value allowed for design might be reduced and thicker pavement structure could be required.
                  Another factor is the actual field compaction experience obtained from a test strip or prior
                  construction. Such data can be accumulated by measuring the in-place dry densities for different
                  numbers of passes with the available compaction equipment and determining the point where
                  additional passes give little increase in density. For this example, the soil type was determined to
                  be SC with a PI of 10, as shown on DD Form 1209, figure 6-3, page 6-5. With this information
                  and the following figure 9-6, the soil must be placed to at least 90 percent of MDD. This would

9-18  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015

                                                                                                     California Bearing Ratio Tests

              provide a density range (in pounds per cubic foot) from 110.3 (at 90 percent MDD) to 116.4 (at
              95 percent MDD). This is calculated with the following formula:

                                  0.90 𝑥 122.5 = 110.3 𝑝𝑜𝑢𝑛𝑑𝑠 𝑝𝑒𝑟 𝑐𝑢𝑏𝑖𝑐 𝑓𝑜𝑜𝑡
                                  0.95 𝑥 122.5 = 116.4 𝑝𝑜𝑢𝑛𝑑𝑠 𝑝𝑒𝑟 𝑐𝑢𝑏𝑖𝑐 𝑓𝑜𝑜𝑡

Chapter 9

              Step 13. Determine the assured CBR values between the specified density limits. Determine
                  the assured CBR values between the specified density limits. If the builder is allowed to place
                  the soil between 110.3 and 116.4 pounds per cubic foot, this step involves determining the CBR
                  values obtained for each moisture content. The change in CBR for any specific moisture-content
                  line between the two density limits shows that a range of strengths is possible. Since the builder
                  will be allowed to place the soil anywhere between the established density limits, the CBR value
                  selected as a potential design strength should represent the worst case. Using 8 percent moisture
                  as an example, the CBR at 110.3 pounds per cubic foot is 15.6. At 116.4 pounds per cubic foot,
                  the CBR is a maximum of 18.0. Of the two, 15.6 is the minimum strength for the specified
                  density range. This procedure was followed for the remaining moisture contents in the example,
                  and the results are recorded on page 4 of DD Form 2463.

              Step 14. Determine the CBR values for potential moisture content specification ranges.
                  Determine the CBR values for potential moisture-content specification ranges. Like the density
                  range, a moisture content range that can be economically achieved in the field is desired. Within
                  the overall range of investigation (OMC ± 4 percent), a smaller specification range giving the
                  greatest assured CBR will be determined. Experience shows that a 4 percent range ( ± 2 percent)
                  is a reasonable requirement; however, this span is not intended to represent an absolute rule. A
                  smaller range may be specified to achieve a larger design CBR and a reduction in pavement
                  thickness requirements. This savings in pavement materials may be offset by increased costs
                  associated with the difficulties in meeting the more stringent requirements. Conversely, for some
                  soils, an expanded moisture content specification may have little effect on the design CBR. The
                  sample problem uses a 4 percent specification range. One possible range is 6 to 10 percent. If the
                  engineer specified that the soil be placed within these limits, the worst possible strength would
                  be a CBR of 14.4. A continued analysis can be done for the other possible 4 percent ranges as
                  shown on DD Form 2463.

              Step 15. Select the moisture content range. For step 15 of CBR testing of a nonswelling soil,
                  select the moisture content range that gives the greatest assured CBR. In steps 13 and 14, CBR
                  values were selected assuming that the builder will be allowed to place the soil anywhere
                  between potential moisture content and density specification limits. Now the desired set of limits
                  is selected. The tabulation in step 14 shows that the soil, if placed between 7 and 11 percent
                  moisture, will give the largest of the possible CBR values. Thus, 15.0 becomes the design CBR.
                  To ensure that a CBR of 15.0 is achieved, compaction must be 90 to 95 percent MDD or 110.3
                  to 116.4 pounds per cubic foot.

TEST PROGRAM FOR SWELLING OR EXPANSIVE SOILS

     9-34. There is a small group of soils, that expand objectionably after being compacted and saturated. This
     presents a problem in pavement design as this expansion or swell can damage the structure through reverse
     settlement. The measure of swell is expressed as a percentage of the initial sample height. Objectionable
     swell is defined as that in excess of 3 percent of the initial sample height. The expansive nature of the soil
     is mainly due to the type of clay minerals present. For example, montmorillonitic clay consists of the
     smallest and most highly charged particles found in nature. The combination of large surface-area-per-unit
     volume and high surface charge causes a tremendous affinity for water and the ability to expand or shrink
     as water is taken in or removed from the soil.

     9-35. Experience shows that the PI is an excellent indicator of expansive soils. Although a high PI does not
     guarantee that the soil is expansive, critical soils should be checked more closely for swell tendencies (table
     9-3). The test procedure to determine a design CBR for an expansive soil is similar to that discussed for
     nonswelling soils, but the objective is different. For nonswelling soils, the object is to find the greatest
     assured CBR value for some range of densities and moisture contents. The object of the test program for
     expansive soils is to find the moisture content ranges that will prevent objectionable swell and provide the
     highest-soaked CBR. Generally, the minimum swell and the highest-soaked CBR occur at a molding
     moisture content higher than the OMC.

9-20  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                              California Bearing Ratio Tests

              Table 9-3. Swell potential

                       PI      Expansiveness
              0 to 14      Not expansive
              14 to 25     Marginal
              25 to 40     Critical
              40           Highly critical
              Legend:
              PI           plasticity index

Example

     9-36. The following is an example of such a test for a CH soil. As most of the steps are similar to those
     developed for the case of nonswelling soils, only differences will be discussed.

Steps

     9-37. Perform the following steps to complete tests on swelling or expansive soils.
              Step 1. Establish the Optimum Moisture Content of the Soil. Establish the OMC of the soil.
                  Determine a moisture range for CBR investigation. The OMC ± 4 percent range of
                  investigations as used for the nonswelling soils may not apply. It will be necessary to prepare
                  samples over a wider range of moistures with most of the work being done on samples higher
                  than the OMC. Prepare samples over a range of the OMC ± 8 percent. As illustrated later, much
                  of the laboratory work done on samples dry of the OMC is not essential.
              Step 2. Compact the samples within the moisture content range of investigation. Compact
                  the samples within the moisture-content range of investigation at different levels of compactive
                  effort. As the type of soil being tested will generally be cohesive and will have a CBR less than
                  20 (table 9-2, page 9-13) indicates that compaction must be at least 90 percent MDD. An upper
                  limit can be established as expansive soils are very difficult to compact at levels greater than 100
                  percent of MDD. Laboratory compactive efforts of 10, 25, and 56 blows per layer are adequate
                  for nearly all cases.
              Step 3. Soak the samples, and measure the swell. Soak the samples and measure the swell.
                  This step deviates from the same step for the nonswelling soil. For each sample, measure the
                  expansion, compute the percent of swell, and plot it against the molding water content. The
                  triangular data point at 10 percent moisture on the swell data curve (figure 9-6, page 9-19) was
                  obtained from the following:
                   Initial sample height equals 4.60 inches.
                   Final dial reading equals 0.025 inches.
                   Molding water content equals 10.0 percent.
                   Level of compactive effort equals blows per layer.
                   Free-swell index or percentage of swell equals (0.025/4.60) x 100 = 0.5 percent.
              Step 4. Plot the Points on DD Form 2463. Plot the points on DD Form 2463. Once the points
                  are plotted, a curve is then usually fit to the CE 56 data points only. The curve for a swelling soil
                  shows that this soil, if placed and compacted at molding moisture contents of 14 percent or
                  greater, will swell 3 percent or less.

             Note. The sample DD Form 2463 in figure 9-6 is based on data from the nonswelling soil type as
             explained in the nonswelling program section. A plot of data for a swelling soil may look
             slightly different than this example.

 Step 5. Perform CBR rests. Perform CBR penetration tests and determine the corrected CBR
     for each sample.

              Step 6. Plot the data on graphs of dry density. Plot the data on graphs of dry density versus
                  molding moisture content and corrected CBR versus molding moisture content.

              Step 7. Reformat the data on DD Form 2463. Reformat the data on DD Form 2463.

              Step 8. Plot the CBR family of curves. Plot the CBR family of curves on DD Form 2463.

              Step 9. Establish a density range. Establish a density range at which soils will be placed in the
                  field. If no prior experience or benefit of a test strip is available, the flow chart in figure 9-6,
                  page 9-20, may be used. In this case, the minimum level of compaction is 90 percent of MDD.
                  Assuming a reasonable specification range of 5 percent, the upper limit will be set at 95 percent
                  MDD. The actual density range is calculated as discussed in the previous example for
                  nonswelling soils. These calculated limits are then placed on page 3, DD Form 2463, by drawing

                  two vertical lines on the CBR family of curves. For example, if the MDD was determined to be
                  110, use the following calculation to establish the actual density limits:

              Step 10. Determine the assured CBR values detween the specified density limits. Determine

                                                                                                            California Bearing Ratio Tests

              Step 13. Analyze the results. This technique does not provide for drying the soil to a moisture
                  content less than the amount of placement. Such extreme drying takes place, excessive shrinkage
                  and pavement failure might be expected. However, it takes considerable effort to remove water
                  from expansive soils and such soils are normally protected from drying by the overlying
                  pavement. The first thing to consider when encountering an expansive soil is testing another
                  location. However, this is not always feasible and this technique does not allow for
                  determination of a design CBR at which swell is not excessive.

             Note. This design value was obtained at the expense of strength.

     9-38. Expansive soils can be chemically stabilized to allow building. Adding small amounts of lime
     considerably reduces the potential for shrinkage and swell. Soils stabilization is further discussed in
     chapter 5; FM 5-430-00-1/AFJPAM 32-8013/MCRP 3-17.7A, Volume I; FM 5-430-00-2/AFJPAM 32-
     8013/MCRP 3-17.7B, Volume II; and TM 5-822-14/AFJMAN 32-1019.

TEST PROGRAM FOR FREE-DRAINING SOILS

     9-39. Determining a design CBR for free-draining soils requires the least testing of the three remolded
     laboratory test programs.

     9-40. The ease in testing is due to the free-draining characteristics or lack of fines in the soil. The CBR
     analysis sheet for borrow pit A shows that the density-versus-moisture-content curves have a concave,
     upward shape and show maximum densities between 7 and 9 percent moisture, depending on the level of
     compactive effort. For each curve, there is a limiting or minimum moisture content (MMC) at which
     moisture above that required to fill the voids after compaction is squeezed or drained from the soil. The
     dashed lines to the right of the MMC represent attempts to compact the soil in a saturated condition, but the
     results after compaction are densities and moisture contents at the limiting condition. This means that field
     placement is relatively easy for such soils. To ensure the MDD for any level of compactive effort, the only
     control measure necessary is to have more water available than that required for the MDD at the
     appropriate level of compactive effort. The corrected CBR-versus-molding-water-content curves show the
     same pattern in relation to moisture content as the dry density. Soils placed with a moisture content above
     the minimum moisture content achieve the maximum CBR possible for that level of compactive effort. In
     other words, moisture contents of loose soils above the limiting values have little bearing on the strength of
     a soil after compaction. This makes laboratory testing, field placement, and field control relatively easy
     matters.

Example

     9-41. To arrive at a design CBR, the steps outlined in the previous examples are followed.

Steps

     9-42. Perform the following steps for the test program for free-draining soils:
              Step 1. Establish the MMC of the soil at 56 blows per layer. It is obtained by locating the
                  moisture content at which the MDD is achieved on the compaction curve. For this example, the
                  MMC is 8 percent with an MDD of 120 pounds per cubic foot. To ensure that a free-draining
                  soil is being tested, this curve should display an MDD at a limiting or minimum moisture
                  content.
              Step 2. Compact the samples at different levels of compactive effort. Compaction curves must
                  be made for three levels of compactive effort up to the MMC. As free-draining soils are
                  frequently represented by well-graded and poorly graded sands and gravels with CBR values
                  above 20, table 9-2, page 9-17, indicates a compactive effort in excess of 100 percent of
                  maximum CE 56 dry density. Therefore, 25, 56, and 72 blows per layer are usually used.
              Step 3. Soak the samples, and measure the swell. Swell measurements are not required, and
                  soaking can be eliminated when it is determined that saturation does not affect the strength.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  9-23


---

Chapter 9

              Step 4. Perform CBR penetration tests. Only the samples at the limiting moisture contents for
                  each level of compactive effort need to be tested. Normally, more than one sample at the limiting
                  conditions will be made for each level of compactive effort and all should be tested.

              Step 5. Plot the data on graphs (figure 9-7) of dry density corrected CBR versus molding
                  moisture content. Only the plot of dry density versus molding moisture content is required. The
                  corrected graph of CBR versus molding moisture content graph is presented only for discussion.

              Step 6. Plot the CBR family of curves. This graph can be condensed into a single line. The three
                  data points are obtained by plotting the corrected CBR against the associated dry density at the
                  limiting moisture content.

           from table 9-2, page 9-12 and noting that the CBR value for this soil is usually greater than 20,
           the minimum level of compaction allowed is 100 percent of maximum CE 56 dry density.
           Because no additional information is provided, specify 100 to 105 percent of maximum CE 56

           shows that the minimum CBR value achieved between the density range is 70, which is achieved
           at 120 pounds per cubic foot. The placement moisture content necessary to ensure that this
           strength is obtained is 8 percent or greater.

      Note. Considerably greater CBR values can be achieved if more field compaction is applied to
      the soil. If this is not too costly, it may be advantageous to specify greater densities.

IN-PLACE FIELD CALIFORNIA BEARING RATIO TEST WITH THE
DYNAMIC CONE PENETROMETER

     9-43. To overcome some of the shortfalls associated with older, in-place field CBR test methods, the
     ERDC has developed the dual-mass, DCP for evaluating the load-carrying capability of military roads and
     airfields. The results from using the DCP are reported in terms of index values which can be converted to
     CBR values. The CBR is useful in determining strength values for the operation of aircraft and military
     vehicles on unsurfaced soils and in the design of soil in pavement systems. Three correlations currently
     exist for this conversion, and each depends on the soil type being tested.

             Note. The procedures for testing with the DCP and the correlation of CBR values can be found
             in greater detail in the DCP user’s manual or in FM 5-430-00-2/AFJPAM 32-8013/MCRP 3-
             17.7B, Volume II.

EQUIPMENT

     9-44. Figure 9-8 shows a dual-mass DCP kit (national stock number [NSN] 6635-01-436-6095) designed
     for military engineer use. The kit includes the following items:

              Case assembly.
              Top rod, threaded and welded to the handle.
              Bottom rod, threaded and welded to the anvil.
              Dual-mass hammer.
              Vertical scale, in centimeters and inches.
              Go/no-go gauge.
              Six hardened, 60°, fixed cones.
              Three hardened cone adapters and 200 disposable cones.
              Two channel lock pliers.
              One can of light, lubricating oil.
              Thread-locking compound.
              Hexagonal wrench set (5/64 to 1/4 inch).

                                              Figure 9-8. Dual-mass DCP kit

DESCRIPTION

     9-45. The dual-mass DCP consists of a 5/8-inch-diameter, steel rod, approximately 36 inches in length,
     with a steel cone attached to one end. The rod is driven into the soil by a sliding, dual-mass hammer. Figure

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  9-25


---

Chapter 9

     9-9, page 9-26, shows an assembled DCP with vertical scale for measuring the cone penetration depth. The
     DCP is driven into the soil by dropping the 17.6-pound (dual-mass) or 10.1-pound (single-mass) sliding
     hammer. The 17.6-pound hammer is converted to 10.1 pounds by removing the hexagonal setscrew and the
     outer steel sleeve. The cone penetration caused by one blow of the 17.6-pound hammer is essentially twice
     that caused by one blow of the 10.1-pound hammer. The 10.1-pound hammer is more suitable for use and
     yields better test results in weaker soils having a CBR value of 10 or less. The 17.6-pound hammer
     penetrates high-strength soils quicker and may be preferred when these type soils are encountered.
     However, the 10.1-pound hammer can be used on soils up to CBR 80.

Legend:  degree
deg      diameter
dia      inch
in       pound
lb

Figure 9-9. Assembled dual-mass DCP with vertical scale and disposable cone and adapter

9-46. The depth of cone penetration is measured at selected penetration or hammer drop intervals, and the
soil shear strength is reported in terms of DCP index. The DCP index is based on the average penetration
depth resulting from one blow of the 17.6-pound hammer. The average penetration per hammer blow of the
10.1-pound hammer must be multiplied by two in order to obtain the DCP index value. The DCP is
designed to penetrate soils up to a depth of 36 inches (limit of the rod and scale). Individual DCP index
values are reported for each test depth resulting in a soil strength with depth profile for each test location.

9-47. The disposable cone is for use in soils where a standard cone is difficult to remove. The disposable
cone mounts on an adapter. At the conclusion of the test, the disposable cone easily slides off the cone
adapter, allowing the operator to remove the DCP device from the soil. The disposable cone remains in the
soil. Using the disposable cone approximately doubles the number of tests per day that can be run by two
operators.

9-26     TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                            California Bearing Ratio Tests

STEPS

     9-48. Perform the following steps to conduct a DCP test in soil:
              Step 1. Ensure that two people operate the DCP. One person holds the device by its handle in a
                  vertical position and taps the device with the hammer until the base of the cone is flush with the
                  surface of the soil. The second person then checks the device for a zero reading by holding the
                  vertical scale between the soil surface and bottom of the hammer.
              Step 2. Ensure that the bottom of the 4-inch-diameter portion of the hammer reads 0 millimeters
                  on the vertical scale. In weak soils, the weight of the DCP device will sink the cone past its zero
                  reading. In this case, a zero-blow penetration reading is recorded, in millimeters, at the actual
                  measured pretest depth.
              Step 3. Raise the hammer to the bottom of the handle and then drop it. Care should be exercised
                  when raising the hammer to ensure that the hammer is touching the bottom of the handle but not
                  lifting the cone before it is allowed to drop. The hammer must be allowed to fall freely with its
                  downward movement not influenced by any hand movement. The operator should also be
                  careful not to exert any downward force on the handle after dropping the hammer.

              Step 4. Ensure that the recorder and the operator should keep track of the number of hammer
                  drops (blows) applied to the soil, with the recorder writing the number down between
                  measurements on the DCP data sheet shown in figure 9-10, page 9-28. The recorder is also
                  responsible for measuring and recording the penetration after each set of hammer blows.
                  Penetration measurements are recorded to the nearest 5 millimeters. As an example of how to
                  read the penetration depth, figure 9-11, page 9-29, shows a penetration depth of 150 millimeters.

              Step 5. Ensure that the cone penetrates at least 25 millimeters between recorded measurements.
                  Data taken at less than 25-millimeter penetration increments are unnecessary and sometimes
                  result in inaccurate strength determinations. The number of hammer blows between
                  measurement recordings will generally be 20, 10, 5, 3, 2, or 1, depending on the soil strength and
                  the cone penetration rate. The operator and recorder should be alert to sudden increases in the
                  cone-penetration rates during the test. Any noticeable increase in the penetration rate indicates a
                  weaker soil layer. The operator should stop and allow the recorder to record the blow count and
                  penetration depth whenever a weaker soil layer is encountered.

              Step 6. Extract the cone from the soil by driving the hammer upward against the top handle,
                  after the cone has been driven to the desired test depth (maximum 36 inches). Caution must be
                  exercised during this operation to prevent damage to the DCP device. The hammer must be
                  raised in a vertical direction (rather than in an arcing motion), or the rod can be bent or broken
                  where it connects to the anvil. In soils where great difficulty is encountered in extracting the
                  DCP device, disposable cones should be used.

              Step 7. Calculate the CBR from the recorded DCP values using table 9-6, page 9-29. The
                  correlation of DCP index with CBR requires some knowledge of the soil condition. Three
                  correlations exist depending on whether the soil is—

                   A fat clay, CH.

                   A weak clay, CL with a CBR below 10.
                   All other soils (table 9-6 shows the tabulated correlation of DCP index with CBR for the

                        three soil conditions).

Dynamic Cone Penetrometer Maintenance

     9-49. The DCP should be kept clean, and all soil should be removed from the penetration rod and cone
     before each test. A light application of spray lubricant or oil should be applied to the hammer slide rod
     before each day’s use. All joints should be constantly monitored and kept tight. Loose joints will lead to
     equipment failure. Any problem joints should be treated with a joint locking compound. The lower
     penetration rod should be kept clean and lubricated with oil when clay soils are tested.

     9-50. Using disposable cones will save wear and tear on the device and the operator. In soils with large
     aggregate, the DCP may try to penetrate the soil at a slant rather than from a vertical direction. The operator
     should not apply force to the handle of the DCP in an attempt to force it to penetrate the soil vertically.
     Lateral force on the handle in an attempt to make the DCP penetrate the soil vertically will cause the upper
     handle rod to fatigue and break at the point where it screws into the anvil. Instead, the test should be
     stopped when the handle deviates laterally 6 inches or more from the vertical position and a new test should
     be attempted at another location.

     9-51. The go/no-go gauge is used to ensure the cone’s base diameter is within proper tolerance. Each new
     cone should be checked before use and at selected usage intervals to ensure that the cone’s base diameter is
     within a proper tolerance of 0.78 to 0.80 inch. The cone must be replaced if its base diameter fits into both
     ends or neither end of the go/no-go gauge. The cone is within proper tolerance when it fits into only one
     end of the gauge

SOIL STRENGTH EVALUATION WITH THE DYNAMIC CONE PENETROMETER

     9-52. The following are important aspects of soil strength evaluation with the dynamic cone penetrometer:

              Number of measurements. The number of measurements to be made, location of the
                  measurements, depth of measurements required, and frequency of recording data with depth vary
                  with type of road or airfield pavement operation and with the time available for conducting the
                  tests. For this reason, hard-and-fast rules for the number of tests required in evaluating roads and
                  airfields are impracticable. Soil conditions are extremely variable. The strength range and
                  uniformity of the soils or existing pavement materials will generally control the number of
                  measurements necessary. In all cases, it is advisable to first test those spots that appear to be
                  weakest. Since the weakest condition controls the pavement evaluation, penetrations in areas that

9-30               TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034                       3 April 2015


---

                                                                                             California Bearing Ratio Tests

     appear to be firm and uniform may be few and widely spaced. In areas of doubtful strength,
     penetration tests should be more closely spaced.

Note. No less than three penetration tests should be made in each area having similar type soil
conditions.

 Reading depths in soil. Soil strength usually increases with depth; but in some cases, a thin,
     hard crust will overlay a soft layer or the soil will contain thin layers of hard and soft material.
     For this reason and the fact that many aircraft and some military vehicles will affect the soil to
     depths of 36 inches or more, it is recommended that each penetration be made to a depth of 36
     inches unless prevented by a very hard condition at a lesser depth. Soil test depths may be
     reduced when required traffic operations are known and the thickness requirements indicate that
     a reduced thickness above the subgrade controls the evaluation.

 Data tabulation. The data can be tabulated in spreadsheet format with the number of hammer
     blows, hammer weight, and cone penetration recorded to the nearest 5 millimeters after each set
     of hammer blows.

Note. Figure 9-10, page 9-28 shows a printout from the Excel spreadsheet program for use in
analyzing the DCP test data. The Excel DCP program may be included in the DCP kit or is
available from ERDC-WES or the DCP manufacturer. To obtain the latest software, contact
ERDC-WES.

Example. The DCP data shown in figure 9-12 was obtained on a test road consisting of 10
inches of crushed gravel over a very weak CH subgrade. A geogrid over geotextile was installed
over the soft subgrade to separate and reinforce the aggregate layer. The CBR versus depth plot
shows a 10-inch layer of base material with an 80 to 100 CBR over a very weak subgrade soil
having a CBR of 0.6 to 0.7. The stair step increase in subgrade strength below the 15-inch depth
is typical in plastic clay soils. This occurs due to the plastic clay sticking to the DCP penetration
rod at test depths below 12 inches. The strength of the CH soil may not actually be increasing as
indicated at the 17-inch depth. The geogrid/geotextile layer was easily located by the DCP as it
took several hammer drops to penetrate the geotextile at the 10-inch depth.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  9-31

                          Note. Corrected curve may lie below or above actual curve.

                          psi pound per square inch

                                Figure 9-12. Correction of load deformation curve

SPECIAL CONSIDERATIONS

     9-53. The following are special considerations for in-place field CBR testing with the DCP:
              Weather. Because soil conditions are immediately and significantly affected by weather, an
                  evaluation is valid only for the period immediately after measurements are made for unsurfaced
                  pavements. However, it can usually be assumed that the evaluation will remain constant as long
                  as no rain occurs. Gravel-surfaced pavements will be affected to a much lesser extent by rain.
              Clay soils. DCP tests in highly plastic clays are generally accurate for depths to approximately
                  12 inches. At deeper depths, clay sticking to the lower rod may indicate higher CBR values than
                  the actual values. Oiling the penetration rod will help prevent the clay from sticking to the
                  penetration rod; however, it will not significantly improve the test results. A 2-inch-diameter (or
                  larger) auger can be used to open the test hole after each 12-inch DCP test penetration. This will
                  eliminate lower-rod friction problems and allow the test to accurately measure the clay soil
                  strength for an additional 12 inches.
                  Sands. Many sands occur in a loose state. When relatively dry, loose sands show no DCP index
                  values for the top few inches and may show increasing DCP index values with depth. The
                  confining action of aircraft tires will increase the strength of the sand. Generally, dry sand or
                  gravel will be adequate for aircraft in the C-130 class, regardless of the DCP index values. All
                  sands and gravels in a quick condition (water percolating through them) must be avoided. The
                  evaluation of moist sands should be based on the DCP tests as described above.

9-32       TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                            California Bearing Ratio Tests

              Cone penetration refusal. If the cone does not penetrate 25 millimeters after 10 blows with the
                  17.6-pound hammer of 20 blows with the 10.1-pound hammer, the test should be stopped and
                  the CBR noted at that depth as 100. If this firm material is a stabilized soil or high-strength
                  aggregate base layer, it should be cored or drilled with an auger to allow access of the DCP cone
                  to underlying layers. The DCP test can then proceed through the access hole after the depth of
                  the material layer has been recorded. The drilled material layer is assigned a CBR value of 100 if
                  penetration was possible. However, if a core or auger drill is not available, the 17.6-pound DCP
                  hammer might still be used to drive the lower rod and cone through the firm material. If a large
                  rock or other object stopped the cone penetration, the DCP should be extracted and another
                  attempt made within a few feet of the initial test.

             Note. The DCP is generally not suitable for soils having significant amounts of aggregate greater
             than a 2-inch sieve.

PLATE BEARING TEST FOR DETERMINING MODULUS OF SOIL
REACTION

     9-54. This test is used to determine the modulus of reaction of soils by means of the plate-bearing test and
     for determining the corrections to be applied to the field test values by means of laboratory tests. The
     modulus of soil reaction is required in rigid pavement design and evaluation.

STEP 1. STRIP AN AREA OF SOIL TO BE TESTED

     9-55. Strip an area of soil to be tested to the proposed elevation of the subgrade surface. The stripped area
     should be twice the diameter of the largest bearing plate. If the subgrade is to be fill material, construct a
     small embankment at least 30 inches in height, using the desired soil compacted at the moisture and density
     conditions specified in the field. Clear the area to be tested of any loose material and make it level. Apply
     and level a thin layer of sand or plaster of Paris, not to exceed 1 inch in thickness on the test area.

             Note. Care should be taken to not disturb the soil around the test, especially in granular soils.

STEP 2. SEAT THE 30-INCH-DIAMETER BEARING PLATE

     9-56. Seat the 30-inch-diameter bearing plate on the sand or plaster of Paris. Turning or working the plate
     back and forth will help to provide uniform seating of the plate. Center the 24-inch- and 18-inch-diameter
     plates on the 30-inch-diameter plate, and center the hydraulic jack on the 18-inch-diameter plate. If
     cribbing is needed, crib between the top plate and the jack. A steel proving ring, if used to measure load,
     should be placed on top of the jack; and the ball joint should be used between the proving ring and load
     reaction device. The load reaction device must be long enough so that its supports will be at least 8 feet
     from the bearing plate. A steel beam between two loaded trucks provides a good load reaction device. Place
     the three micrometers so that the dial stems rest on the bottom 30-inch-diameter plate not more than 0.25
     inches from the outer edge, spaced 120° apart. Fasten the micrometers to a frame whose supports are at
     least 4 feet from the edge of the 30-inch-diameter plate. A typical apparatus setup is shown in figure 9-13.
     Place the loading plate and the sensors so that they are resting on a firm and stable test surface.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  9-33


---

Chapter 9

           Legend:  pound
           lb       minimum
           min

           Figure 9-13. Plate bearing test equipment and set-up

STEP 3. SEAT THE LOADING SYSTEM AND BEARING PLATE

     9-57. Seat the loading system and bearing plate by applying a load of 707 pounds (1 pound per square
     inch) when the thickness of the pavement will be less than 15 inches or a load of 1,414 pounds (2 pounds
     per square inch) when the design thickness of the pavement is 15 inches or more. Allow the seating load to
     remain in place until nearly all deformation has taken place. Then, take a reading on all three micrometers,
     which will be used as the zero reading. The seating load is also considered to be the zero load. Cyclic
     loading under the seating load may be used to assure good seating of the apparatus and bearing plate.

              Step 3a. Cohesive Soils (PI greater than 0). Do not release the seating load. Apply two load
                  increments of 3,535 pounds (5 pounds per square inch) each with each load increment being held
                  until the rate of deformation is less than 0.0001 inch/minute. Read all three micrometers at the
                  end each load increment. Following the completion of the 7,070-pound (10 pounds per square
                  inch) load increment, determine the average deflection (in inches) by averaging the total
                  movement between the 0- and 10-pounds per square inch increment for each dial.

              Step 3b. Noncohesive Soils (PI equals 0). Proceed after the seating load deformation has
                  practically ceased and the zero dial readings taken. Do not release the seating load; apply load
                  increments of 3,535 pounds (5 pounds per square inch) each until a total load of 21,210 pounds
                  (30 pounds per square inch) is reached, allowing each load increment to remain until the rate of
                  deformation is less than 0.0001 inch per minute. Read all three micrometers at the completion of
                  each load increment (in inches).

9-34       TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034     3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                            California Bearing Ratio Tests

STEP 4. COMPUTE A VALUE FOR UNCORRECTED MOLDULUS OF SOIL REACTION

     9-58. Compute a value of k’u (uncorrected modulus of soil reaction) using the following formula:

                                                 𝑘′u = 10 𝑝𝑜𝑢𝑛𝑑𝑠 𝑝𝑒𝑟 𝑠𝑞𝑢𝑎𝑟𝑒 𝑖𝑛𝑐ℎ
                                                             𝑎𝑣𝑒𝑟𝑎𝑔𝑒 𝑑𝑒𝑓𝑙𝑒𝑐𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛

     where—
     k'u = uncorrected modulus of soil reaction

     9-59. If the value of the uncorrected modulus of soil reaction is less than 200, the test is considered
     complete and the load is released. If the value of the uncorrected modulus of soil raction is 200 or greater
     (in cohesive soils), apply additional load increments of 3,535 pounds (5 pounds per square inch) until a
     total load of 21,210 pounds (30 pounds per square inch) is reached, allowing each load increment to remain
     until the rate of deformation is less than 0.0001 inch per minute. Read all three micrometers at the
     completion of each load increment (in inches).

STEP 5. OBTAIN AN UNDISTURBED SAMPLE

     9-60. Obtain an undisturbed sample of the foundation material for laboratory testing to determine the
     saturation correction to apply to the field test value. The undisturbed sample must be large enough to obtain
     two consolidometer specimens side by side (at least 5 inches across). Take the sample in a container
     suitable for sealing to preserve the moisture content until the laboratory correction tests can be performed.
     When the plate-bearing test is performed directly on cohesive subgrade material, obtain the undisturbed
     specimen from the foundation at the same elevation at which the test is performed, but alongside rather
     than under the plates. When the test is performed on a granular base course material that is underlain by a
     cohesive material and when the base course is less than 75 inches in thickness, take the undisturbed sample
     from the cohesive material at the bottom of the base course.

STEP 6. DETERMINE THE CORRECTED VALUE OF THE MOLDULUS OF SOIL REACTION

     9-61. To determine the corrected value of the moldulus of soil reaction from the higher load (30 pounds
     per square inch), plot a load deformation curve being the unit load (pounds per square inch) on the plate
     plotted against the average deflection for each load increment. The average deflection is the average of the
     three dial readings between the zero and the end of each load increment. If the load deformation relation
     plots as a straight line passing through the origin, no correction is necessary. However, if the load-
     deformation relation results in a curve or a straight line not passing through the origin, the curve is
     corrected as shown in figure 9-12. Generally, the load deformation curve will approximate a straight line
     between the unit loads of 10 and 30 pounds per square inch. The correction consists of drawing a straight
     line, parallel to the straight-line portion of the plotted curve, and through the origin. When correcting the
     load deformation curve, good engineering judgment will be required. If the curve is nonlinear throughout
     its length, the straight-line correction will be based on the average slope of the curve through at least three
     points in the region of the curve having the least curvature.

STEP 7. RECOMPUTE THE UNCORRECTED MODULUS OF SOIL REACTION

     9-62. Using the same equation in step 4, take the average deflection from the corrected curve from step 6 at
     a load of 10 pounds per square inch. The value of uncorrected modulus of soil reaction computed must then
     be corrected for bearing plate bending and soil saturation as outlined in steps 8 and 9.

STEP 9. CORRECT FOR SOIL SATURATION

     9-63. The design of pavement is generally based on the modulus of soil reaction when the soil is saturated.
     It is not feasible to saturate the soil in the field prior to the field test, and seldom will the soil be in a
     saturated condition in its natural state. Therefore, the field test value must be corrected to reflect the value
     that will be obtained when the soil becomes saturated. For cohesionless soils, no correction is necessary.
     The correction is obtained for cohesive soils through the use of the consolidation test. The correction test
     will be made on undisturbed specimens of the soil from the location of the field test taken in step 5. If the

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  9-35


---

Chapter 9

     field test is performed on the surface of a cohesionless base course material that is underlain by a cohesive
     soil, the saturation correction will be determined by tests on the underlying cohesive material (ASTM
     D2435 and EM 1110-2-5027).

STEP 8. CORRECT FOR BEARING PLATE BENDING

     9-64. Bending in the bearing plates results in a greater deflection at the center of the plate than at the rim
     where the deflections are measured. The correction of The value of uncorrected modulus of soil reaction is
     made by entering figure 9-14, page 9-36, with the computed value of uncorrected modulus of soil reaction
     on the ordinate and projecting horizontally to the intersection of the plotted curve. The corrected value for
     the modulus of soil reaction, is then determined by projecting vertically to the abscissa of the plot and
     reading the value as shown.

STEP 10. CONDUCT CONSOLIDATION TESTING

     9-65. The saturation correction factor is the ratio of the deformation of the consolidation specimen at the
     natural moisture content to the deformation in a saturated specimen under a 10-pounds-per-square inch
     load. Two specimens of the undisturbed material are placed in a consolidometer. One specimen will be
     tested at the in-situ moisture content, and the other specimen will be saturated after the seating load has
     been applied. Each specimen is then subjected to the same seating load (1 or 2 pounds per square inch) that
     was used for the field test (step 5). The seating load is allowed to remain on the in-situ moisture content
     specimen until all deformation occurs, at which time a zero reading is taken on the vertical deformation
     dial. The other specimen is allowed to soak in the consolidometer under the seating load (1 or 2 pounds per
     square inch). After the specimen is saturated, a zero dial reading is obtained.

     9-66. Without releasing the seating load, an additional 10-pounds-per –square-inch load is applied to each
     specimen and allowed to remain until all deformation has occurred. A final reading is then taken on the
     vertical deformation dial. Record the final deflection of the saturated specimen.

 Figure 9-14. Chart for correction of uncorrected modulus of soil reaction for bending
                                                      of the plate

9-67. For soils of certain types, the specimen may swell under the seating load as it becomes saturated.
Swelling of the material will result in extrusion of material above the top of the consolidometer ring, so that
when the 10-pounds per square inch load is applied, the material may squeeze out over the ring rather than
consolidate, which will lead to erroneous results. To prevent this when dealing with a swelling type soil or
one that is suspected of being a swelling type soil, the consolidometer ring will not be completely filled

9-36  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                            California Bearing Ratio Tests

     with soil. This can be accomplished by trimming the top of the specimen a sufficient amount, generally
     0.06 inch, to allow for the swelling. When the specimen for saturation is trimmed to allow for swelling, the
     specimen to be tested at the in-situ moisture content will also be trimmed an equal amount so that the
     heights of the specimens will be equal at the beginning of the test.

STEP 11. COMPUTE THE CORRECTED MOLDULUS OF SOIL REACTION

     9-68. The correction for saturation will be applied in proportion to the deformation of the two specimens
     under a unit load of 10 pounds per square inch as follows:

                                                                    𝑑𝑏 𝑑
                                                         𝑘 = 𝑘u �𝑑s + 75� − 𝑑s
     where—
     k = corrected modulus of soil reaction (in pounds per cubic inch)
     ku = modulus of soil reaction uncorrected for saturation (in pounds per cubic inch)
     d =deformation of a consolidometer specimen at in-situ moisture content under a unit (in pounds per
     square inch)
     ds = deformation of a saturated consolidometer specimen under a unit load of 10 pounds per square inch
     b = thickness of base course material (in inches)

             Note. In no instance, will a value of d/ds greater than 1.0 be used in the above formula. This
             formula is applicable for the computation of the corrected k value, whether there is a granular
             base course or not. However, if the base course is 75 inches or more in thickness, a saturation
             correction for the cohesive subgrade material will not be made.

PURPOSE

     9-69. The plate bearing test is used to obtain the modulus of soil reaction required for rigid airfield
     pavement design and evaluation. figure 9-15, page 9-38, displays the typical setup of the plate bearing test.
     The test is designed to incrementally load soil up to 25,000 pounds of force on top of a rigid plate and
     measure the resulting deflection under the plate. The test measures the stiffness of the soil under loading
     that is designated as the modulus of subgrade reaction.

EQUIPMENT

     9-70. The essential components used for this test are (figure 9-15)—
              Load reaction equipment. A truck or trailer, a tractor-trailer, an anchored frame, or another
                  structure having a dead load of at least 25,000 pounds.
              Hydraulic jack capable of applying 25,000 pounds of force with a spherical bearing attachment.
              Bearing plates. These should be 1-inch-thick steel or 1.5-inch-thick aluminum plates of 18, 24,
                  and 30 inch diameters to be stacked in a pyramid fashion.
              Load-measuring device, consisting of a hydraulic gauge on the jack, a steel proving ring, or a
                  load cell.
              Three dial micrometers reading 1/10,000 inches.
              Deflection beam to mount the micrometers.
              Cribbing, consisting of short pieces of hardwood or steel H- or I-beams.
              Sand or plaster of Paris for ground leveling.
              Stopwatch.
              Containers to collect soil samples.
              Cutting tools for extracting soil samples.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  9-37

Chapter 9

SOIL MODULUS TESTING WITH A LIGHT WEIGHT
DEFLECTOMETER

     9-71. This test method covers the use of a light weight deflectometer (LWD) also known as a portable
     falling weight deflectometer (PFWD). This device is used to measure small deflections occurring on
     compacted soil layers and bound paving materials. These deflections are converted to stiffness by back-
     calculation analysis techniques to determine the bearing capacity of the soil for vehicle and airfield
     trafficking.

     9-72. This device acts as a miniature plate-bearing test. A weight is manually lifted to a prescribed height
     and dropped onto a plate resting on top of the soil or pavement (figure 9-15). The peak vertical deflection
     of the soil surface and the peak force occurring at the center of the plate are recorded on the LWD as the
     falling weight strikes the plate and stored in a laptop or handheld computer.

                                  Figure 9-15. PFWD with data acquisition device

PURPOSE

     9-73. The purpose of soil modulus testing is to estimate the soil or pavement layer stiffness for use in
     assessing bearing capacity. The device will provide an assessment of the soil modulus for depths of 280 to
     380 millimeters in weak soil layers and less than 280 millimeters in strong soil layers.

9-38  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                           California Bearing Ratio Tests

EQUIPMENT

     9-74. The essential components of this test are (figure 9-16):
              Transport case.
              PWFD equipped with a load cell and deflection gauge.
              Laptop or handheld computer for data acquisition.
              Rubber base pad for hardened pavement surfaces.
              Sand to provide a level surface on rocky or gravelly soil.

                     Figure 9-16. Operation of the PFWD, with laptop data acquisition

STEPS

     9-75. Perform the following steps to operate an LWD:
              Step 1. Position the PFWD over the desired test point. Ensure that the test surface is as clean and
                  smooth as possible, with loose granules and protruding material removed. If the soil surface is
                  uneven, the placement of sand may be required to provide a smooth solid surface on which to
                  rest the baseplate.
              Step 2. Place the loading plate and the sensors to ensure that they are resting on a firm and stable
                  test surface (figure 9-16).
              Step 3. Raise the falling weight to the desired height, and allow it to fall freely.
              Step 4. Record the resulting peak surface deflections and peak load.
              Step 5. Repeat at least once to provide two falling weight sequences for comparison. If the
                  difference is greater than ± 3 percent for any sensor, note the variability. The variability of
                  stiffness measurements depends on soil type. As the soil becomes more fine-grained, the
                  accuracy of the stiffness measurement decreases. This suggests that this device is best suited for
                  granular soils, particularly gravels that are commonly found in base layers of pavement systems.
                  The reported coefficient of variation for typical field conditions or the major soil types is as
                  follows:
                   GM, GC and GP soils: 10 to 20 percent.
                   SM, SW and SP soils: 15 to 35 percent.
                   ML and CL soils: 40 to 60 percent.

     9-76. Data from this device is correlated to the modulus obtained from a large-scale falling weight
     deflectometer. Published studies differ on the relationship of the modulus correlations, but USACE
     provides the following guidance as best current practice:

                        𝐸𝐹𝑊𝐷 = 0.97 𝑥 𝐸𝑃𝐹𝑊𝐷 𝑓𝑜𝑟 12.5 𝑀𝑃𝑎 < 𝐸𝑃𝐹𝑊𝐷 < 865 𝑚𝑒𝑔𝑎𝑝𝑎𝑠𝑐𝑎𝑙𝑠

     9-77. It is difficult to conduct this test on weak soils, those with a recorded modulus of less than
     12.5 megapascals. The test works best with stiffer soils, primarily those materials that are being improved
     through compaction effort.

                                                Chapter 10

                           Technical Soil Report

         A good program for soil testing not only requires that careful and complete tests be
         performed, but also that the tests be completed as quickly as possible and that the
         data be presented in a clear, logical, consistent manner.

REQUIRED TESTS

     10-1. The tests required by any program depend on the type of construction being planned. However, there
     are a number of tests that are consistently run on road and airfield construction programs. A complete
     testing program should include the following tests:

              Soils exploration (see chapters 1 and 2).
              Compaction (see chapter 7).
              Plasticity index (see chapter 6).
              Particle size analysis (see chapter 5).
              CBR (see chapter 9).
              Trafficability (see FM 5-430-00-1/AFJPAM 32-8013/MCRP 3-17.7A, Volume I and FM 5-430-

                  00-2/AFJPAM 32-8013/MCRP 3-17.7B, Volume II).

     10-2. The soil tests listed above are specific tests that are used to gain knowledge about the control of soils
     during construction, including the—

              Suitability of subgrade and borrow materials.
              Degree to which soil can be compacted.
              Bearing value of the subgrade and borrow material under projected future conditions.
              Location of ledge rock and groundwater table.
              Susceptibility to detrimental frost action.

     10-3. Each test supplies the necessary data to answer questions based on the engineering evaluation of
     scientific data rather than a meaningless guess.

     10-4. Because of the number of tests to be performed for a particular project, careful planning may avert
     considerable delays in the presentation of the results. List the tests required and their sequence in a manner
     that will permit running the tests continuously, without delays due to the time needed for soaking or drying
     samples.

     10-5. An example of the daily activities in a complete soil-testing program are listed in figure 10-1, page
     10-2. Some testing programs may not include all of the tests listed in the example, and soil types or test
     results may change the activity list. An activity schedule for each unit may be different due to equipment
     and mission priority.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  10-1

      (hydrometer method)

      PL                liquid limit
