                     Figure 10-1. Sample schedule for soils laboratory testing

10-6. The following cite some considerations that may be helpful in setting up a continuous soil-testing
program:

        Exploration. Before any tests can be performed, representative samples of the soil involved in a
             given project must be obtained. This, however, is only one objective of soil exploration. Other
             considerations include plotting a profile of boring results, locating ledge rock, determining the
             depth to the groundwater table, determining field moisture content, and field-identifying the soils
             sampled.

        Particle/grain size analysis. The mechanical analysis is another test that should be used to
             occupy periods between other tests. The test is an evaluation of the grain size distribution used to
             establish the gradation of the soil sample. The sieve analysis and hydrometer analysis may be
             used in obtaining the required information.

        LL and PL tests. One use of LL and PL test results is to predict how the fine-grained portion
             (No. 200 fraction) will affect the engineering value of a particular soil sample. This evaluation,
             obtained through soil classification, is to ensure that the sample being tested meets specifications
             set on the LL and PL for the particular project for which it will be used. These tests should be
             performed while waiting for compaction test moisture content samples to dry and CBR samples
             to soak or anytime between other tests.

        Compaction test. The compaction test indicates the MDD that can be obtained practically in the
             field. It also indicates the OMC at which this dry density can be obtained. Since this will be a
             test used for control purposes, it should be run as soon as possible. While moisture content
             samples from this test are drying, tests for plasticity and particle size analysis can be started.

        Laboratory CBR test. The CBR test is determined by an arbitrary penetration procedure to
             obtain a modulus of shearing resistance of a subgrade or base course soil. This value is used to
             determine the required thicknesses of the various base courses through its application to
             empirically derived design curves. Because the procedure for this test may involve a 4-day
             soaking requirement, CBR samples should be prepared as soon as the necessary information

10-2                       TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                                        Technical Soil Report

                  from the results of the compaction test is available. This information may be obtained from
                  approximated values of the OMC.
              In-place, field CBR test. The latest addition to equipment used for determining the CBR value
                  of a soil is the dual-mass DCP (FM 5-430-00-2/AFJPAM 32-8013/MCRP 3-17.7B, Volume II).

SOIL TECHNICAL REPORT

     10-7. In writing technical reports, one of the most important aids is a clear, logical outline of the subject.
     Outlines will vary according to the program conducted and the data required, but the suggested format that
     follows should help organize a report.

     10-8. Frequently, portions of the information shown in the outline will be required at different times. For
     this reason, a preliminary report and several supplementary reports may actually be made before the project
     is completed. However, if all of the information provided follows the same basic outline, filing the data and
     assembling the final report will be simplified.

     10-9. Use the following items and information to write a soils technical report:
              Soil boring logs.
              The profile of the soil.
              The field identification of soil.
              In-place moisture data.
              Particle/grain size analysis data.
              LL and PL data.
              The USCS laboratory soils classification.
              CBR test data.
              In-place density data (as required locally).
              In-place CBR data.
              A topographic map of the site.
              Aerial photographs (if available).
              Pencils.
              Pens.
              Paper.
              The project directive.
              FM 5-430-00-1/AFJPAM 32-8013/MCRP 3-17.7A, Volume I.

     10-10. Use the following recommended outline to organize a technical soils report:
              Project.
                   Description. What type of construction is being performed (Class D road, base camp,
                        airstrip)?
                   Purpose and scope. Who is requesting construction? What is the extent and why is it being
                        performed?
              Site description.
                   Location. All map references and directions, including county or province (if available).
                   Existing facilities. Old logging roads, dirt paths, patrol roads, buildings, overhead power
                        lines.
                   Topography, cultivation, and drainage. Forests, farmland, well-drained areas, swampy
                        areas, hills.
                   Climate. Temperature extremes, seasonal precipitation, average wind speed.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  10-3


---

Chapter 10

              Geology.
                   Overburden. Main depositing force (river, glacial,), rock classes (sedimentary, igneous,
                        metamorphic), and depth of overburden.
                   Bedrock. Average depth to bedrock and state of bedrock (faulted, fractured, folded).

              Site conditions.
                   Field explorations. Details of performed tests, location of test sites, and explanation of
                        sample tags.
                   Field tests. Type of field tests performed and field sample results. Enclose copies in
                        Annex G.
                   Laboratory tests. Type of laboratory tests performed. (Do not include MDD or laboratory
                        CBR results.)
                   Test results. Synopsis of test results and classifications. (Refer to forms in Annex F.)

              Fill and borrow materials.
                   Field explorations. Details of performed tests, location of test sites, and explanation of
                        sample tags.
                   Field tests. Type of field tests performed and field sample results. Enclose copies in Annex
                        G).
                   Laboratory tests. Type of laboratory tests performed. (Do not include MDD or laboratory
                        CBR results.)
                   Test results. Synopsis of test results and classifications. (Refer to forms in Annex F.)

              Conclusions and recommendations.
                   Final site selection. Exact location of finished project. (Refer to plans enclosed in Annex
                        C.)
                   Economical design. Low-cost or resource alternatives to current suggestions (soil cement,
                        asphalt binder, chemical stabilization methods, mats, or fabrics).
                   Minimum specifications. Design CBR, MDD, and OMC specifications. (Refer to laboratory
                        forms in Annex F.)

              Annexes.
                   Project directive and all directives involving this project.
                   General plan drawings (geology should be indicated).
                   Location plan drawings (existing and proposed features).
                   Profiles and cross sections.
                   Boring logs.
                   Laboratory testing data.
                   Field testing data.

             Note. Not every subject will apply to every report. In many reports, some of the items may be
             covered in several sentences, while others may require a page of discussion. Some items may be
             repeated for each runway in a major airfield. All laboratory test forms will be included in Annex
             C. If a specific test form is not necessary or not conducted, write Not Applicable across the form.

                                            PART TWO

                  Bituminous Mixtures

Part Two provides information on the materials used in constructing bituminous
surfaces, the methods of testing these materials, the design considerations and the
mixes prepared from them. Mixing and placing operations and considerations when
preparing the mixes, are described in UFC 3-250-03.

                                                Chapter 11

               Bituminous Pavements and Surfaces

         Bituminous pavements and surfaces are a mixture of mineral aggregates, mineral
         filler, and a bituminous material or binder. This mixture is used as the top portion of a
         flexible pavement structure to provide a resilient, waterproof, load-distributing
         medium that protects the base course from the detrimental effects of water and the
         abrasive action of traffic.

AGGREGATES

     11-1. Mineral aggregates may consist of crushed rock, crushed or uncrushed soils (including gravels and
     sands although their content may be limited), slag, mineral filler, or a combination of these materials. Other
     materials that may be used as aggregate in certain geographical areas include vesicular lava and coral.
     Another source of aggregate that is sometimes used is reclaimed asphalt pavement (RAP). Aggregates
     normally constitute 90 percent or more by weight of bituminous mixtures, and their properties greatly
     affect the finished product. The aggregate provides three basic functions when used in bituminous surfaces:

              It transmits the load from the surface to the base course. In pavement, this is accomplished
                   through the mechanical interlock of the aggregate particles.

              It withstands the traffic’s abrasive action. If a wearing surface were laid consisting of binder
                   alone, it soon would be worn away by the abrasive action of tires.

              It provides a skid-resistant surface. A portion of the aggregate extends slightly above the normal
                   surface of the wearing mat, thereby providing texture for tires to grip.

BITUMINOUS MATERIALS

     11-2. A bituminous material is the adhesive agent or binder in a bituminous mixture. This material
     provides two functions:

              It binds the aggregate together, holds it in place, and prevents displacement.
              It fills approximately 75 to 80 percent of the voids in the mineral aggregate, they providing a

                   waterproof mixture that keeps surface water from seeping into and weakening the base material.

     11-3. The binder’s functions require it to be a waterproof substance having the ability to bind aggregate
     particles together. All bituminous materials possess these qualities due to being composed mainly of
     bitumen—a black semisolid that provides the black color, cementing ability, and waterproofing properties.
     Bituminous materials are classified into two main groups—asphalts and tars. Due to environmental issues,

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  11-1


---

Chapter 11

     tar is seldom used. The asphalt binder is available in several forms that are suitable for different procedures
     of mixing or application under wide variations in temperature. Some bituminous materials are solid or
     semisolid at room temperature. Other grades are a relatively viscous (thick) liquid at room temperature.
     Mixing bituminous materials with solvents or water produces cutbacks or emulsions that are liquid at
     atmospheric temperatures. Such liquid asphalts are used for cold mixes or are applied as sprays in building
     pavements. Cutback asphalts are no longer used in some locations due to environmental issues as a result of
     the added solvent. Prior to considering the use of cutback asphalts, confirm that it is allowed to be used in
     the location.

ASPHALTS

     11-4. Asphalt is normally obtained from crude petroleum, but may also occur as a natural asphalt. There
     are two general classes of asphalt—natural and manufactured. Natural asphalts occur in lakes (as lake
     asphalt), pits, or rock structures (as rock asphalt). Manufactured asphalt is produced by distilling crude
     petroleum (figure 11-1). A military engineer is seldom concerned with natural asphalts because they are not
     usually available in areas of interest. Therefore, this chapter discusses the uses and testing of manufactured
     asphalts. All asphalt cements (ACs) are solid or semisolid at room temperature (77°Fahrenheit [F]) and,
     prior to use, must be converted to a fluid state by heating, emulsifying, or dissolving in a petroleum solvent.

Grading

     11-5. There are three classification systems that can be used for identifying AC—penetration graded,
     viscosity graded, and performance graded. The penetration grade is determined by the distance that a
     standard needle under a standard load will penetrate a sample in a given time, under a given temperature
     condition. The viscosity grade is determined at 140°F using a standard viscometer under standard
     conditions. The performance-graded (PG) asphalts are graded by determining the maximum and minimum
     temperature °C at which the binder is expected to perform satisfactorily. Table 11-1, page 11-4, lists some
     typical grades for each of the three classification systems, along with the relative consistency.

Cutback Asphalts

     11-6. When heating equipment is not available or is impractical to use, AC can be made fluid by adding
     solvents (called cutter stock or flux oils). Cutter stock may be any one of the more volatile petroleum
     distillate products. The resulting combination is called asphalt cutback. Exposure to air causes the solvents
     to evaporate and leave the AC to perform its functions. In many areas, cutback asphalts are no longer used
     due to the environmental issues caused by the solvents. In these areas that do not use cutback asphalt, use
     asphalt emulsion instead. Asphalt emulsions do not cause any particular environmental issues so they are
     often preferred. Prior to using cutback asphalts, check with the state or other local government
     environmental authorities to determine if the use of cutback asphalts are allowed and to see if there are any
     rules that must be followed when using these materials.

     11-7. The classification of the cutback is based on the evaporation rate of the distillate in the mixture.
     Gasoline or naphtha (high volatility) produces a rapid-curing (RC) cutback; kerosene (medium volatility)
     produces a medium-curing (MC) cutback; and fuel oil (low volatility) produces a slow-curing (SC)
     cutback. Road oils, referred to occasionally, are heavy petroleum oils in the SC grade of liquid asphalt.
     Table 11-2, page 11-4, shows the percentage of components by grade for the three types of asphalt
     cutbacks.

11-2  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015

11-8. As more cutter stock is mixed with a given amount of AC, a thinner liquid results. In practice,
different amounts of cutter stock are added to a given amount of AC to obtain various viscosities, or grades
of cutbacks. The ASTM has specified a set of specifications for cutbacks based on kinematic viscosity
(ASTM D2026, ASTM D2027, and ASTM D2028). The number assigned to each grade, as shown in figure
11-2 corresponds to the lower limit of kinematic viscosity as determined by a standard test. The upper limit
of each grade is equal to twice the lower limit or grade number. The units used in the test are centistokes.

11-9. Thus, a number 70 cutback refers to a viscosity range of 70 to 140 centistokes. The other grades and
their limits are 250 (250 to 500 centistokes), 800 (800 to 1,600 centistokes), and 3,000 (3,000 to
6,000 centistokes). In addition, the MC has a 30 grade (30 to 60 centistokes). Figure 11-2 shows the scale
of viscosity grades. The higher the viscosity, the thicker the liquid, and the less amount of solvent in the
cutback.

11-10. Emulsions are classified according to the setting or breaking rate, which is the speed at which the
emulsion breaks or the asphalt and water separate. This rate usually depends on the emulsifier used and the
proportion of water to asphalt. Emulsions are described as rapid-setting (RS), medium-setting (MS), and
slow-setting (SS) and also by viscosity numbers (table 11-2). Because of this breaking rate, emulsions can
also be grouped according to their ability to mix with damp aggregate. The RS emulsion breaks so fast that
it cannot be mixed; therefore, it is called a nonmixing type. The MS and SS emulsions break slowly enough
to permit good mixing until each particle of the aggregate is uniformly coated. Emulsions are often used as
a tack coat and can be specifically designed to be used as a prime coat for bituminous pavements. In fact,
most state DOTs use asphalt emulsions for prime coats due to their environmental advantage over cutback
asphalts. The grades of asphalt emulsions are found in ASTM D977 and ASTM D2397. These documents
provide a list of the various grades of asphalt emulsion and the specifications for the different grades.

SAFETY PRECAUTIONS

     11-11. Be extremely cautious when handling bituminous materials. AC, which is solid at room
     temperature, is heated to high temperatures to make it workable as a binder material. Heated asphalt can
     cause severe burns if it comes in contact with the skin. The vapors emitted by heating bituminous materials
     may be harmful if inhaled. Use care during heating to ensure proper ventilation. Asphalt cutbacks contain
     highly flammable volatiles. The vapors will ignite at relatively low temperatures. The lowest temperature at
     which they will ignite is called the flash point. The minimum flash point for RC-250, RC-800, and RC-
     3,000 is 80°F; for MC-30 and MC-70, about 100°F; for MC-250 to MC-3,000 and SC-70, about 150°F; and
     for the remaining SC grades, about 25° higher per grade up to 225°F for SC-3,000.

             Note. The spraying and mixing temperatures in many cases are above the flash point (table
             11-3). Use extreme caution when handling these mixtures. Do not expose their vapors to an open
             flame. Cutbacks may also be classified as an environmentally hazardous material. Check with
             unit and installation environmental representatives for further guidance as to the proper storage,
             use, and disposal of these substances. If the unit’s environmental representative is unknown,
             consult the commander for assistance. Emulsified asphalts can replace cutback asphalts for
             almost any purpose, and there are no significant environmental issues with emulsions.

Chapter 11

ADVANTAGES AND DISADVANTAGES

     11-12. Advantages and disadvantages of the bituminous materials used in construction are as follows:

              Cutback asphalts are flammable. Asphalt pavements are susceptible to damage by the blast
                  from jet planes, and they can be softened by petroleum products that may be spilled on them
                  (such as during refueling at an airfield). Tars, on the other hand, resist damage from petroleum
                  derivatives since they are products of coal. Tars are not used much anymore due to their
                  environmental issues and, especially, the emissions during placement that may be harmful to the

                  workers. About the only place that tars are used is for sealers of asphalt pavements to provide
                  some resistance to fuel and oil spillage. For this reason, tars are not discussed in any detail in
                  this document.

              Asphalt emulsions are not flammable and are liquid at normal temperatures. Since they are
                  mixed with water, they can be used with more damp aggregate than that allowed for cutbacks.
                  Additional water may be added to the emulsion (maximum 1:3) for use in slurry seal coats.
                  Because emulsions contain water, they have certain disadvantages. During freezing weather, the
                  emulsions can freeze and the components separate. Emulsions are difficult to store for extended
                  periods because they tend to break even in unopened drums. When shipped, the water in the
                  emulsion takes up valuable space, which could be used to transport hard-to-obtain materials. In
                  recent years, the use of emulsions has far exceeded the use of cutback asphalts due to the
                  environmental issues with cutbacks. Table 11-4 gives typical uses of bituminous materials.

                                                Chapter 12

                             Material Sampling

         When conducting tests, use the materials that represent those that will be used in
         construction; otherwise, test results will be misleading. The sampling of materials for
         testing should receive close attention. When large samples must be subdivided into
         small units for the actual tests, take care to keep the sample representative of the
         original mass. Reduce aggregate samples to the proper size for testing by means of
         quartering or with a sample splitter. The methods for sampling natural deposits of
         sands and gravels are discussed in soil surveys and are applicable here. Take samples
         of bituminous materials at the place of manufacture or at the delivery point. (This
         manual assumes that sampling is done at the point of delivery.) Testing is done by the
         refineries; hence, it is good to get a copy of the certified test results for the binder
         material being produced. It is common practice to use the test results provided by the
         supplier and to take additional samples to be tested in case there is a problem. Take
         samples for analysis to identify properties of bituminous materials if records are not
         available. Obtain sufficient quantities of materials at the time of sampling to
         determine if materials meet specification requirements and to provide sufficient
         material for laboratory mix design. Normally, aggregates that produce 150 pounds of
         the desired gradation and 2 gallons of bituminous material are sufficient to conduct
         the mix design.

BITUMINOUS MATERIAL SAMPLING

     12-1. Use clean, dry containers for sampling bituminous materials. Keep the containers tightly closed and
     properly marked. A sample for a routine laboratory examination should be at least one quart.

LIQUID MATERIALS

     12-2. When sampling liquid bituminous materials from nonagitated vertical tanks, take samples from near
     the top, middle, and bottom. Consider testing the samples from the three levels separately to detect
     stratification. However, this additional testing may significantly increase the test time and cost. To reduce
     testing time and cost, combine the three samples and conduct one set of tests. Materials shipped in tank cars
     may be sampled from valves and taps. Take samples from drain cocks on the side of the tank or car. If
     cocks are not present, take the samples by lowering weighted bottles or cans into the material (figure 12-1,
     page 12-2). Fit the bottle or can with a stopper that can be removed by a string or wire after it has been
     lowered to the proper depth.

SOLID AND SEMISOLID MATERIALS

     12-3. When sampling solid or semisolid materials in drums, barrels, cartons, or bags, take samples at least
     3 inches below the surface and at least 3 inches from the side of the container. Use a clean hatchet on hard
     material and a stiff putty knife on soft material.

AGGREGATE SAMPLING

     12-4. Aggregate particles vary in size from large stones and rocks to gravels and sands and even to dust-
     size materials. These materials for paving may still be in their natural deposits or may be in individual
     stockpiles.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  12-1


---

Chapter 12

         Figure 12-1. Sampling liquid bituminous materials from a tank car or storage tank

LEDGE AND QUARRY STONE

     12-5. Obtain separate samples of unweathered stone, weighing at least 50 pounds, each from all strata that
     appear to vary in color and structure. Prepare a sketch plan, with elevation, showing the thickness, length,
     width, and location of the different layers so that the quantity available can be estimated.

NATURAL DEPOSITS OF SAND AND GRAVEL

     12-6. Select samples that represent the different materials available in the deposit. Sketch the area and
     indicate the approximate quantities of different materials. If the deposit is an open-face bank or pit, take the
     sample by channeling the face so that it, represents material that visual inspection indicates may be used.
     Cut the face immediately before sampling, and discard any material that has fallen from the surface along
     the face. Do not include in the sample any overlying material (overburden) that is not suitable for use as an
     aggregate, since this material would be stripped away when the aggregate is removed from the pit. It may
     be necessary to make test borings or dig test pits to determine the approximate extent of the material. If test
     pits are dug, they must be adequately shored to prevent material from caving in on personnel working in the
     pit. Obtain samples for each change in strata from the pit representative. If the material being sampled is all
     sand, about 25 pounds is sufficient for tests. If it consists of sand and gravel, a larger sample (about 75
     pounds) is required for preliminary tests. The coarser the gravel portion, the larger the sample required.

STOCKPILES

     12-7. If the material has been stockpiled previously, take care in obtaining samples. There is a natural
     tendency toward segregation of materials in the stockpile. The material near the outer edges and near the
     base of the pile is likely to be coarser than the average material. Cut a face into the stockpile about halfway
     up the stockpile, and take a sample there. (Use care to ensure that material from above this point does not
     flow into the sampling area. This may require that a plate be used immediately above the sampling areas.)
     Do this at several points around the stockpile, and combine all of these samples into one large sample. This
     process should provide a representative sample of the stockpile. Combine samples from at least three
     different sections of the pile. Always follow good, safe practices when working around stockpiles.

12-2        TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                                             Material Sampling

COMMERCIAL AGGREGATES

     12-8. It is preferable to obtain samples of commercial aggregates at the plant, during loading, from
     stockpiles or bins. Obtain separate samples at different times while the material is being loaded, to
     determine variations in the grading of the material. Take bin samples from the entire cross section of the
     flow of material as it is being discharged. Testing separate samples gives a better idea of variations that
     occur, but samples should be mixed and reduced by quartering when the average condition is desired.
     When it is not practicable to visit the plant to obtain samples, the next preferred method is to sample the
     material in cars or trucks or while it is being unloaded. Take railroad car samples from three or more
     trenches dug across the car, at points that appear (on the surface) to be representative of the material. When
     obtaining the sample, remember that segregation of the different sizes has probably taken place choose
     samples that are representative.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  12-3

                                                Chapter 13

                             Field Identification

         Laboratory tests conducted on bituminous materials to check compliance with
         specifications are not considered field tests. They are described in this section for
         information purposes only. The field tests discussed in this section are limited to the
         bitumen identification procedures, flash point tests, and penetration tests. These tests
         are conducted to determine safe uses for a material. Field identification enables the
         military engineer to determine the type of surface that can be constructed with the
         type and grade of material available. With the type of surface known, the construction
         procedure may be outlined and scheduled. This procedure determines the proper
         equipment and the necessary safety procedures. The aggregate materials must also be
         tested for acceptable bituminous construction use.

BITUMEN FIELD-IDENTIFICATION TESTS

     13-1. Perform field tests to identify the bituminous paving materials as AC, asphalt cutback, or asphalt
     emulsion. In addition, identify the viscosity grade of the bitumen. To distinguish among the several asphalt
     products, it is necessary to know something of their origin, physical properties, and the manner in which
     they are normally used. The identification procedure outlined in figure 13-1, page 13-2, is based on a
     consideration of the physical properties of these materials.

     13-2. Ensure that all tests are performed away from open flames and in well-ventilated areas. Also ensure
     that all materials are properly disposed of according to local environmental policy.

ASPHALTS

     13-3. The first procedure in identifying an unknown bituminous material is to determine, by a solubility
     test, whether it is an asphalt product. Attempt to dissolve an unknown sample (a few drops, if liquid, or
     enough to cover the head of a nail, if solid) by stirring it in any petroleum distillate. Kerosene, gasoline,
     diesel oil, or jet fuel is suitable for this test. Since asphalt is derived from petroleum, it will dissolve in the
     petroleum distillate. Tar products will not dissolve. If the sample is an asphalt, the sample distillate mix
     will consist of a dark, uniform liquid. ACs or cutbacks dissolve completely.

     13-4. Asphalt in emulsions is also distinguishable as it dissolves and forms black beads or globules in the
     bottom of the container of distillate. A road tar sample will be a dark, stringy, undissolved mass in the
     distillate. A check can be made by spotting a piece of paper or cloth with the mix. Asphalt dissolved in
     distillate will produce a brown to black stain. The clear distillate above the settled tar will not cause a stain.
     The solubility test provides a positive method of identification. While tars have been used in the past for
     paving surfaces, it is not recommended that they be used, due to environmental issues and the effects of
     emissions on the workers.

ASPHALT CEMENT AND CUTBACKS

     13-5. Perform the following procedure to determine if the specimen is an AC or a cutback. The various
     grades of AC are solid at room temperature, while cutbacks are liquid; a pour test will distinguish between
     them. Place a sample of the material in a small container and attempt to pour it. If the material does not
     pour, it is an AC. If it pours, it is a cutback or an emulsion.

             Note. At 77°F the softest AC will not pour or deform if the container is tilted.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  13-1


---

            med      medium setting

            RC       rapid-setting
            RS       road tar
            RT       road tar cutback
            RTCB     slow curing
            SC       slow setting
            SS

            Figure 13-1. Identification of unknown bituminous materials

13-6. The various grades of AC are distinguished principally by their hardness, as measured by a field
penetration test. The information obtained may be sufficient for planning for or starting emergency
construction. The exact penetration grade is not determined, but the field test will distinguish between hard,
medium, or soft groups of AC. Perform the test by pushing a sharpened pencil or nail into the container of
asphalt (at about 77°F) using about 10 pounds of force. If only a slight penetration is made with
considerable difficulty, a hard AC is present. If penetration is made with some difficulty, a medium AC is
present. If penetration is made with ease, it is a soft AC in the high-penetration scale. Even the highest
penetration will not pour or deform at 77°F if the container is tilted (table 13-1).

13-2                 TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034   3 April 2015

                      Penetration    Viscosity  Performance

CUTBACK ASPHALT TESTS

     13-7. The following tests are used to determine the grade of a cutback asphalt:
              Pour test. As stated previously, an AC will not pour at 77°F, but a cutback will. The pour test
                  can be used to determine whether the unknown material is a cutback asphalt. If the material
                  pours, it is a cutback asphalt. The approximate viscosity grade number of the cutback is found by
                  comparing the flow to well-known materials (water, syrup). If this test is made at a temperature
                  below 77°F, the materials will appear more viscous (stiff) than at 77°F and the opposite if tested
                  when warmer than 77°F. The cutbacks of a given viscosity grade will pour in a manner similar
                  to the following:
                   Water: 30.
                   Light syrup: 70.
                   Syrup: 250.
                   Molasses: 800.
                   Barely deform: 3,000.

Note. After the pour test, the approximate viscosity grade of the cutback is known, but the type
(RC, MC, or SC) is not.

              Smear test. The smear test is used to determine if a cutback is an RC. This is done by making a
                  uniform smear of the substance on a piece of glazed paper or other nonabsorbent surface.
                  Volatile materials, if present, will evaporate. Since RC materials are cutback with a very volatile
                  substance, most of the volatiles will evaporate within 10 minutes. The surface of the smear then
                  becomes extremely tacky. This is not true of the lighter grades (MC and SC), which remain fluid
                  and smooth for some time. An MC will not result in a tacky surface for a matter of hours. SC
                  materials may require several days. A prolonged smear test is used to identify an 800- or 3,000-
                  grade MC or SC cutback. This is necessary because these grades contain such small quantities of
                  cutter stock that they may become tacky in the 10-minute period specified above. Place a thin
                  smear of the material on a nonabsorbent surface, and let it cure for at least 2 hours. By the end of
                  that time, if the material being tested is not an MC or SC, the smear will be hard or just slightly
                  sticky. However, if the material being tested is an MC or SC, the smear will be uncured and still
                  quite sticky. If the material is RC-3,000, it will cure completely in 3 hours, whereas RC-800 will
                  take about 6 hours to cure. Even after 24 hours, an MC or SC will still be sticky.

ASPHALT EMULSION (ANIONIC) TESTS

     13-8. Another asphalt material used in paving is asphalt emulsion, which is a mixture of asphalt, water, and
     an emulsifying agent. The anionic emulsion specifications cover three types of asphalt emulsion—RS
     grades 1 and 2; MS grades, 1, 2, and 2h; and SS grades 1 and 1h. There are other grades, but these are the
     grades that are most frequently used.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034                                   13-3


---

Chapter 13

SOLUBILITY TEST

     13-9. The solubility test will make an emulsion’s identity known by forming into globules or beads that fall
     to the bottom of the container of petroleum distillate. During this test, the emulsion will present a
     distinctive dark brown color while other bituminous materials are black. The following are solubility tests:

              Water-mixing test. If mixed with water, an emulsion will accept the extra water and still remain
                  a uniform liquid. The sample and water will mix uniformly if the material is an emulsion. This
                  test is positive since no other bituminous material will mix with water. However, mixing with
                  excessive water may cause the emulsion to break, resulting in the asphalt and water separating.
                  This can cause significant problems when trying to use an emulsion.

              Flame test. Since an emulsion contains water, a small piece of cloth saturated with it will not
                  burn if a flame is applied. The other bitumens will burn or flame.

              Stone-coating test. After establishing that the material is an emulsion, determine whether the
                  emulsion is a mixing grade (MS or SS) or a nonmixing grade (RS). Use a metal spoon to mix a
                  small amount (6 to 8 percent by weight) with damp sand do not saturate it. An RS emulsion will
                  break so quickly that it will not be possible to mix it with sand. It breaks immediately, gumming
                  up the spoon with the relatively hard original AC. If the sample is an MS or SS emulsion, the
                  material will mix easily and coat the particles completely (including the mixing spoon) with a
                  uniform coating of asphalt.

AGGREGATE IDENTIFICATION AND SELECTION

     13-10. Identify the aggregate by shape or roughness, hardness, cleanliness, hydrophobicity, gradation, and
     particle size. Select aggregate with the best combination of these characteristics. Also, consider the
     availability, length of haul, and overburden.

SHAPE AND ROUGHNESS

     13-11. The aggregate in a pavement must transmit the traffic load to the base, usually by interlocking of
     the particles. This interlocking is much more pronounced when the particles are angular in shape and rough
     in surface texture. If angular pieces of aggregate are in a pavement, individual particles will not slip or slide
     over one another, but will lock together. However, more binder may be required since angular aggregate
     will not pack as closely together as rounded aggregate, hence requiring more asphalt to provide the desired
     air void level in the mixture. Although angular particles are desired, the aggregate should not contain an
     excessive number of flat or elongated particles, as these particles cause bridging and make compaction
     difficult.

     13-12. Angular aggregates very seldom occur in nature, so it is necessary, in most cases, to crush the
     aggregate to obtain the desired angular particles.

HARDNESS AND DURABILITY

     13-13. The aggregate must be able to withstand the applied loads without cracking or being crushed.
     Resistance to weathering is also a function of the durability. An aggregate’s resistance to wear can be
     determined by the Los Angeles abrasion test, which is the preferred method if it is available. The Mohs
     hardness scale may be used to determine the hardness of the aggregate. This scale is fully explained in TM
     3-34.64/MCRP 3-17.7G. It ranges from 1 for talc or mica to 10 for diamond. By trying to scratch the
     aggregate or the common material, it is possible to establish which is harder; this determines the hardness
     of the aggregate. If both are scratched, the hardness of both is the same. Rub the scratch mark to see that it
     is really a scratch and not a powdering of the softer material. Some common materials and their
     approximate level of hardness are as follows:

              Fingernail: 2.0.
              Copper coin: 3.5.
              Knife blade: 5.0.

13-4  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                 Field Identification

         Window glass: 5.5.
         Steel file: 6.5.

CLEANLINESS

     13-14. The bituminous binder must penetrate into the pores of the aggregate and also adhere to the surface
     of the particles. Aggregates coated with clay or dust or having water-filled pores prevent the penetration
     and adherence of bitumen and result in stripping of the binder. If aggregate particles are coated with silt or
     clay particles, the aggregate should be washed before being used to produce an asphalt mixture. The
     aggregate should be made as clean as possible with the equipment and manpower available. Some dust-size
     particles are needed in the mixture, but this dust should not coat the aggregate. The dust should come from
     the crushing of aggregate, and it should not stick to the coarser aggregate. Typically, most mixtures will
     need at least 3 percent dust in the mix and can have up to 6 to 8 percent dust.

MOISTURE SUSCEPTIBILITY

     13-15. Affinity for water can make an aggregate undesirable. If the aggregate is porous and absorbs water
     easily, the binder can be forced out of the pores, the bond between the aggregate and binder can weaken
     and break, and stripping can occur. Stripping is the loss of bituminous coating from the aggregate particles
     due to the action of water, leaving exposed aggregate surfaces. The most common test for the detrimental
     effect of water on a bituminous mix is ASTM D4867.

     13-16. A rough estimate of the stripping potential can be determined by following ASTM D3625. This
     test involves placing a sample of asphalt mixture in water, boiling it, and then making a visual examination
     to determine the percentage of exposed aggregate surface.

     13-17. Asphalt mixtures containing fines of doubtful quality are sometimes measured for swell as a basis
     for judging the possible effects on a pavement. This test is more frequently used with dense-graded
     mixtures using emulsified and cutback asphalts. Compact a sample of the mix in a metal cylinder (usually
     100 millimeters in diameter), and cool it to room temperature. Obtain a height measurement for the
     specimen. Place the specimen and mold in a pan of water, and mount a dial gauge above the sample in
     contact with the surface. Take an initial reading. Allow the specimen to soak for a specified period (usually
     24 hours) or until there is no further swelling. Take another dial reading. The difference in readings,
     divided by the original height and expressed in percent, is the swell of the mixture. Experience has shown
     that bituminous pavement made with clean, sound stone; slag; or gravel aggregate and mineral filler
     produced from crushed stone will show test values of swell of less than 1.5 percent of the original specimen
     thickness.

GRADATION

13-18.        The following designations help identify aggregates:

               Uniform gradation occurs when all particles are about the same size, normally less than 1
                   inch. These mixtures tend to have high void levels, and permeable, and mixtures tend to be
                   weak.

               Macadam gradation consists of uniformly sized particles, 1 inch or larger.

               Gap-graded mixtures have a small amount of certain-size materials. For instance, most gap-
                   graded mixtures have a deficiency of material on the middle sieve sizes. These mixtures
                   typically result in open mixtures that may be permeable to air and water.

               Dense gradation occurs when there is a good representation of all particle sizes, including
                   coarse, fine, and mineral fillers. These mixtures tend to be strong, with little permeability.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  13-5


---

Chapter 13

PARTICLE SIZE

     13-19. In bituminous construction, it is common practice to designate aggregates according to particle
     size. There are three types of designations under this system, based on two sieve sizes—No. 4 and No. 200.
     The following are particle sizes:

              Coarse aggregate. Coarse aggregate is material larger than a No. 4 sieve.
              Fine aggregate. Fine aggregate passes the No. 4 sieve, but is retained on the No. 200 sieve.

                  However, fine aggregates always contain some percentage of material passing the No. 200 sieve.
                  In bituminous paving, the fine aggregate may consist of natural sand or crushed fine aggregate
                  (ASTM D1073).
              Mineral filler or mineral dust. Mineral filler or mineral dust is nonplastic materials which pass
                   the No. 200 sieve. Most clays are too plastic and aggregates containing clay particles are
                   normally unacceptable. Generally, crushed rock dust, agricultural mineral filler, lime, or
                   portland cement may be used as mineral filler (ASTM D546). Generally, fine aggregates will
                   contain sufficient mineral filler and no additional filler has to be added.

13-6  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                Chapter 14

                              Bitumen Testing

         The field identification tests for bitumen identify the material during expedient
         conditions or until more detailed tests can be performed. The identification
         determines whether the material is an asphalt or a tar and whether it is an AC,
         cutback, or emulsion. Bituminous materials are manufactured to meet specifications
         established by the American Association of State Highway and Transportation
         Officials (AASHTO) and the ASTM. These specifications define the limits permitted
         in the manufacture of the material and assure the user that the material will possess
         definite characteristics and fulfill the project requirements. The tests used to specify
         AC vary considerably depending on the grading system used. These tests are not
         normally conducted in the field laboratory but there may be some lab equipment
         available that will allow an estimate of the asphalt grade. The equipment for
         performing these tests is not included in the asphalt test set and is not normally
         available to the materials technician. However, the test procedures are discussed
         below. Specific gravity of a material is defined as the ratio of the weight of a given
         volume of the material at 77°F to the weight of an equal volume of distilled water at
         the same temperature. The results of a bitumen’s specific-gravity test are used to
         convert weight to volume. Space is provided on DD Form 1216 (Specific Gravity of
         Bituminous Mix Components) to make this determination (figure 14-1, page 14-2).
         The specific gravity of ACs will usually be in the range of 1.00 to 1.06, with most
         ACs being around 1.03. The specific gravity of an asphalt has little bearing on quality
         or other properties of the asphalt. However, the specific gravity is needed for other
         tests and computations. In acceptance and control testing on a job, it is used as a
         check on the uniformity of succeeding shipments of asphalt.

CLEVELAND OPEN-CUP AND TAG OPEN-CUP FLASH POINT AND
FIRE POINT TESTS

     14-1. These tests are applicable to asphalt materials and are of some use in identifying these materials.
     Their greatest usefulness, however, is in determining safe heating temperatures. Material heated above its
     flash point presents a real danger, particularly, if it is exposed to an open flame. The following are flash
     point and fire point tests—

              ASTM D92. Perform the ASTM D92 test on all petroleum products, except fuel oils and those
                  having open-cup flash points above 175°F.

              ASTM D1310. Perform the ASTM D1310 test on RC- and MC-asphalt cutbacks having a flash
                  points below 325°F.

             Note. Either test can be used when the flash point is between 175° and 325°F.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  14-1

               SAMPLE


---

                                                                                                                               Bitumen Testing

PENETRATION TEST

     14-2. The penetration test is sometimes used to specify the grade of an AC. Penetration is defined as the
     distance that a standard needle vertically penetrates a sample of the material under standard conditions of
     time (5 seconds), temperature (77°F), and loading (100 grams). The units of penetration are in one
     hundredths of a centimeter. Other conditions of temperature, load, and time are sometimes used for special
     testing purposes, but these are exceptions and should only be used in special cases.

DUCTILITY TEST

     14-3. In the ductility test, dumbbell-shaped specimens of asphalt are molded under standard conditions.
     The dumbbell-shaped specimens are conditioned in a water bath to standard temperature (77°F) then
     extended at the rate of 5 centimeters per minute until the threads connecting the two ends break. The
     difference in centimeters between the final length at the break and the original length is the ductility.

     14-4. The ductility test is helpful in estimating an asphalt’s ability to resist cracking and raveling. High-
     ductility asphalts have greater flexibility and tenacity. Conversely, low-ductility asphalts are considered
     more likely to crack under heavy loads or severe changes in temperature. Ductility is affected by various
     factors (such as the method of refining and consistency). Blown asphalts (asphalts that have been hardened
     by blowing air through them, which causes oxidation) have low ductility. This is one reason why they are
     not used as paving asphalts. Within the group of asphalts produced by steam and vacuum distillation,
     ductility will vary according to the consistency at a given temperature. Note that the ductility test is also
     sensitive to other factors (specimen imperfections or impurities).

SOFTENING-POINT TEST

     14-5. For the softening-point test (ASTM D61), position a 3/8-inch-diameter steel ball on a brass ring filled
     with asphalt. Place the assembly in beaker containing freshly boiled, distilled water; and heat it slowly. As
     the asphalt becomes warmer, it begins to soften and the weight of the ball forces the asphalt out of the ring.
     Record the temperature at which the asphalt touches the bottom of the beaker as the softening point.

     14-6. The softening point is another consistency test and varies inversely with the penetration test. Like the
     penetration test, the softening-point test can be used to determine changes in an asphalt due to excessively
     high or prolonged heating. An abnormal increase in the softening point is an indication of excessive
     heating. The softening-point test is used in studies on asphalts recovered from pavements after extended
     service to determine the effects of aging. If asphalt shows an unusual increase in the softening point,
     considerable aging and hardening have occurred.

VISCOSITY TESTS

     14-7. There are two viscosity tests for identifying the qualities of bitumen:
              Saybolt-Furol test. This test measures the time (in seconds) required to pass 60 cubic
                  centimeters of asphalt emulsion material, at a given temperature, through a tube of standard
                  dimensions. Its purpose is to determine the viscosity of the material from which the spraying
                  temperatures are established for the field application of the bitumen.
              Capillary tube viscometer. This test, like the Saybolt-Furor test, measures the time that a given
                  amount of liquid asphalt will flow through a tube of standard dimensions under rigidly
                  controlled conditions of temperature and pressure (or vacuum). At higher temperatures, the flow
                  through the tube is caused by gravity and no vacuum is needed. This test is good from 6 to
                  100,000 centistokes so it can be used for liquid asphalts and ACs at higher temperatures (such as
                  275°F). At lower temperatures (140°F) a vacuum has to be used to create flow in the material
                  (ASTM D2171).

PERFORMANCE-GRADED TESTS

     14-8. There are four tests that are primarily conducted as a part of the PG system. These tests include the
     pressure aging vessel, dynamic shear rheometer, bending beam rheometer, and direct tension tests. These

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  14-3


---

Chapter 14

     tests are conducted at various temperatures to establish the grade of the asphalt being used. These tests are
     fairly complicated and are almost certainly not used in the field. The standard specification for PG asphalts
     is provided in ASTM D6373. The pressure aging vessel test is described in ASTM D6521. The dynamic
     shear rheometer test is described in ASTM D7175. The bending beam rheometer test is described in ASTM
     D6648. The direct tension test is described in ASTM D6723.

SOLUBILITY TESTS

     14-9. The solubility of AC can be determined using trichloroethylene. Trichloroethylene is toxic; therefore,
     protective equipment (including a ventilator, protective goggles, and protective gloves) must be worn while
     using it. Trichloroethylene waste is a hazardous waste and must be disposed of as such. Consult the
     installation environmental office for further guidance as to the safe handling and disposal of
     trichloroethylene.

     14-10. Dissolve 2 grams of the sample in 100 milliliters of solvent. Pour the mixture into a tared Gooch
     crucible, and wash it through. Dry and weigh the crucible. The increase in the crucible’s weight is the
     portion of the sample that is insoluble in the solvent.

     14-11. The solubility test is a quality control test used in specifications to ensure that the AC is not
     contaminated with mineral matter or is not improperly refined.

     14-12. The thin-film oven test was developed to overcome the deficiencies of the standard loss-on-heating
     test. The test uses the same oven as the loss-on-heating test, except for a modification of the rotating shelf.
     A penetration, viscosity, or one or more of the PG grading tests may also be conducted on the sample after
     the heating period to evaluate changes in the asphalt. The primary purpose of the thin-film oven test is to
     estimate the property changes of the asphalt binder that will take place when it is mixed with the hot
     aggregates during production of the asphalt mixture.

14-4  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                Chapter 15

                      Aggregate and Filler Testing

         The aggregate transmits the load, takes the abrasive wear of traffic, and provides a
         nonskid surface. Desired aggregate characteristics include angular shape, rough
         surface, hardness, and gradation. Some of these characteristics (shape, surface,
         cleanliness) are determined visually. Durability and hardness cannot be seen, but
         require knowledge based on experience or some form of abrasion testing. Rocks that
         soak up water will eventually reach the condition where the binder is forced from the
         surface pores and the cementing action breaks down. Gradation is typically
         established by the specification requirements. In bituminous paving, the aggregate
         constitutes the bulk of the mixture. Common practice subdivides the bituminous
         aggregates into coarse aggregate, fine aggregate, and mineral filler. The No. 4 sieve
         separates coarse from fine aggregate, and the No. 200 sieve is the lower limit for fine
         aggregate. The distribution of the different sizes determines how many voids will
         remain in the aggregate and helps determine how much bitumen will be needed.
         Bituminous pavement specifications define acceptable gradation limits. The bitumen
         content for the mix is then determined from the trial mix properties that are defined in
         the specifications.

SIEVE ANALYSIS

     15-1. A sieve analysis of the aggregates to be used in a paving mixture is required to determine the particle
     size distribution.

MINERAL FILLER

     15-2. In bituminous paving, particles finer than the No. 200 sieve are referred to as a mineral filler. To
     measure the amount of filler and percentages passing each sieve in a selected sample, perform a washed
     sieve analysis (ASTM C136).

FINE AND COARSE AGGREGATE

     15-3. Specific size designations of coarse and fine aggregates are specified as shown in ASTM D448.
     Materials are generally ordered from the aggregate supplier using the designated numbers. For example, a
     No. 6 aggregate would have 90 to 100 percent passing the 3/4-inch sieve and 0 to 15 percent passing the
     3/8-inch sieve. The sieve analysis with prewashing must be made using suitable sieves. When conducting
     gradation tests, record the results on DD Form 1206 and plot the results as a gradation curve on
     DD Form 1207. When testing aggregates, obtain a sample as described in ASTM D75 and reduce the
     sample down to the proper size by using a sample splitter (if available) or by quartering if necessary. The
     minimum required size of the sample depends on the maximum size of particles as specified in the testing
     methods.

SPECIFIC GRAVITY

     15-4. The bulk specific gravity of aggregates and mineral filler used in bituminous paving mixtures is
     required to compute the percent of voids in the mineral aggregate. The air voids in the asphalt mixture are
     determined from the density of a compacted mixture and the theoretical maximum specific gravity (ASTM
     D2041) of the mixture.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  15-1


---

Chapter 15

APPARENT AND BULK SPECIFIC GRAVITY OF COARSE AGGREGATE

     15-5. Apparent and bulk specific gravity can be determined using the method described for apparent and
     bulk specific gravity in ASTM C127. The apparent specific gravity is much easier to measure, and it can be
     determined using the Dunagan apparatus furnished with the concrete test set.

Equipment

     15-6. Use the concrete test set to determine the apparent specific gravity. The test set includes—
              The Dunagan apparatus (figure 15-1).
              Sieves (2-, 1 1/2-, 1/2-, and 3/8-inch and Nos. 4, 10, 40, 60, 80, 100, and 200).
              Evaporating dishes.
              An electric oven.
              Pans.

                                             Figure 15-1. Dunagan apparatus

Steps

     15-7. Perform the following steps to determine the apparent specific gravity:
              Step 1. Select (by splitting or quartering) about 5,000 grams of aggregate from the sample, do
                   not include particles smaller than the 3/8-inch sieve.
              Step 2. Wash the aggregate to remove dust or other coatings, and dry it to a constant weight in
                   the oven. Record the total weight of the oven-dried aggregate on DD Form 1216 (figure 14-1,
                   page 14-2).
              Step 3. Immerse the aggregate in water at 59° to 77°Farenheit for 24 hours.
              Step 4. Soak the sample, and place it in a copper bucket filled with water. Turn the bucket and
                   aggregate sharply back and forth to remove any air.
              Step 5. Suspend the bucket from the brass hanger, and bring the water level to the overflow
                   pipe.
              Step 6. Determine the submerged weight of the sample. Record the weight.

Calculations

     15-8. The calculations required to determine the apparent specific gravity of coarse aggregate are shown on
     DD Form 1216 and are self-explanatory (figure 14-1).

15-2  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                              Aggregate and Filler Testing

BULK AND APPARENT SPECIFIC GRAVITY OF FINE AGGREGATE (CALIBRATED FLASK)

     15-9. The fine aggregate bulk and apparent specific gravity can be determined according to ASTM C128.
     It is much easier to determine the apparent specific gravity than the bulk specific gravity. The procedure
     discussed below can be used to determine the apparent specific gravity when a calibrated flask is available.

Equipment

     15-10. Use the following items (figure 15-2) to determine the apparent specific gravity of fine aggregate
     (calibrated flask):

              A balance, 2,000-gram, sensitive to 0.1 gram.
              An evaporating dish.
              A battery filler.
              A volumetric flask, 500-milliliter.
              An electric oven.
              A pan.
              A thermometer (0° to 300°F, in 1°F gradations).
              An absorbent paper or cloth.

     Figure 15-2. Calibrated-flask apparatus for determining apparent specific gravity of fine
                                                             aggregate

Steps

     15-11. Perform the following steps for particles finer than the No. 4 sieve (the data is recorded on
     DD Form 1208 [figure 4-2, page 4-3]):

              Step 1. Calibrate a 500-milliliter volumetric flask.
              Step 2. Dry a representative sample, weighing about 500 grams, to a constant weight in the

                   electric oven.
              Step 3. Determine the oven-dried weight of the cooled sample, and record in on DD Form 1208

                   (figure 4-2)
              Step 4. Transfer the sample to the 500-milliliter flask, being careful not to lose any of the

                   material.
              Step 5. Add clean water until the level reaches the neck of the flask. Allow the sample to soak

                   for 24 hours.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  15-3


---

Chapter 15

              Step 6. Hold the flask containing the soaked sample by the neck, and roll it back and forth on a
                   smooth surface until air bubbles stop coming from the sample (figure 15-3).

                          Figure 15-3. Manipulation of calibrated flask to remove air

              Step 7. Use the battery filler to bring the water level up in the neck of the flask until the bottom
                   of the meniscus coincides with the calibration mark on the flask. Use absorbent paper or cloth to
                   remove any drops of water from the inside of the neck and outside of the flask.

              Step 8. Determine the weight of the flask, aggregate, and water. Record the weight on
                   DD Form 1208.

              Step 9. Measure and record the temperature.

Calculations

     15-12. The calculations for apparent specific gravity of fine aggregate are the same as those indicated for
     soil in figure 4-2, page 4-3.

APPARENT SPECIFIC GRAVITY OF FINE AGGREGATE (UNCALIBRATED FLASK)

     15-13. Perform the procedure below to determine the apparent specific gravity when a calibrated flask is
     not available.

Equipment

     15-14. Use the same equipment as for the calibrated-flask test, with the addition of a water bath
     maintained at 68°F. Do not use this procedure unless the temperature can be maintained.

Steps

     15-15. Perform the following steps to determine the apparent specific gravity:
              Step 1. Obtain a representative sample of aggregate passing the No. 4 sieve, weighing about
                   500 grams. Dry it to constant weight in the electric oven maintained at a temperature of 230°F
                   plus 9°F.
              Step 2. Obtain and record the dry weight after the sample has air cooled.
              Step 3. Transfer the sample to the flask, being careful not to lose any of the material.
              Step 4. Add clean water until its level reaches the neck of the flask. Allow the sample to soak
                   for 24 hours.
              Step 5. Roll the flask back and forth on a smooth surface until air bubbles stop coming from the
                   sample.
              Step 6. Use the battery filler to bring the water level up in the neck of the flask to slightly above
                   the calibrated mark.

15-4  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                              Aggregate and Filler Testing

              Step 7. Place the flask with water and aggregate in the water bath, maintained at 68°Farenheit.
                   Use the glass thermometer to check the temperature of the water in the flask from time to time.

              Step 8. Bring the water in the flask to a uniform temperature of 68°F. Use the battery filler and
                   absorbent paper to adjust the bottom of the meniscus to coincide with the calibration mark.
                   Remove any drops of water inside the neck of the flask.

              Step 9. Remove the flask from the bath, and dry the outside thoroughly. Determine and record
                   the weight of the flask plus the aggregate and the water at 68°F. It does not matter if the level of
                   the water in the neck of the flask changes after removal from the bath. The proper adjustment
                   was made at 68°Farenheit, and the total weight is not affected by the subsequent change in
                   volume.

              Step 10. Repeat the procedure in steps 6 through 9 above, using water only. Enter the weight of
                   the flask filled with water at 68°Farenheit on DD Form 1216. This needs to be done only once
                   for a given flask, and the value can be tabulated and used in subsequent tests. The weight of the
                   flask filled with only water at 68°Farenheit must be known.

Calculations

     15-16. Indicate the calculations necessary to determine the apparent specific gravity of fine aggregate
     using an uncalibrated flask on DD Form 1216.

SPECIFIC GRAVITY OF MINERAL FILLER

     15-17. The specific gravity of mineral filler used in bituminous mixes is required for calculation of the
     voids in the mineral aggregate. Most of the time the mineral filler will come from the fine aggregate. In this
     case there is no additional mineral filler added so this test is not needed. The methods described in the
     specific-gravity test apply (including procedures, calibration, testing, and calculations). Details can be
     found in ASTM D854.

LOS ANGELES ABRASION TEST

     15-18. The Los Angeles abrasion test requires a special machine consisting of a revolving drum rotated at
     the rate of 30 to 33 revolutions per minute by an electric motor. Inside the drum, there is a shelf that picks
     up the aggregate sample and a charge of steel balls and drops them together on the opposite side of the
     drum. Details can be found in ASTM C131. The LA abrasion test is used to evaluate the ability of the
     aggregate to resist degradation during handling and when exposed to traffic after the pavement is
     constructed. Materials with a higher LA abrasion will tend to break down more than aggregates with lower
     abrasion values.

     15-19. Job specifications usually require that the loss in weight as determined in the Los Angeles abrasion
     test is not greater than 40 percent for pavement aggregates and 50 percent for base and subbase aggregates.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  15-5
