                                                Chapter 16

                         Bituminous-Mix Design

         Hot-mix bituminous concrete for pavements is a mixture of blended aggregate that is
         coated with bituminous cement binder. The materials are heated when mixed so that
         the bitumen becomes fluid and thoroughly coats the aggregate particles. The design
         of a bituminous concrete mix is performed to determine an economical blend of
         aggregates meeting the specification requirements and to identify the proper
         bituminous cement content. This produces a mixture that is durable, has the stability
         to withstand traffic loads, and is workable for placement and compaction with the
         construction equipment available. The procedures described in this section are
         performed during the design of a hot-mix bituminous concrete. They include testing,
         plotting the results on graphs, and checking the readings against values from the
         design tables. The testing of ingredients and the mix is started before and continued
         throughout the paving operations. (FM 5-430-00-1/AFJPAM 32-8013/MCRP 3-
         17.7A, Volume I covers the design considerations in more detail. The paving
         operations, blending, and bitumen content criteria are explained in UFC 3-250-03.
         The testing phases are described in this manual.) The selection of the mix ratios of
         materials is tentative and will likely need to be changed during mixture production.
         The aggregate and bitumen used for mix design should be the same as that used
         during construction. The aggregates and binder must meet definite requirements. At
         times, it is necessary to shorten the design procedures to expedite military
         construction. Suggestions for expediting design mix are given at the end of this
         chapter. The final step is the preparation of a job mix formula to be furnished to the
         construction unit.

HOT-MIX DESIGN CONSIDERATIONS

     16-1. The objective of designing a hot mix is to determine the most economical blend of components that
     will produce a final product that meets specifications.

STEPS

     16-2. Perform the following steps to determine the most economical blend of components:
              Step 1. Sample the available aggregates and binder materials.
              Step 2. Perform a sieve analysis of the available aggregates.
              Step 3. Determine the aggregate blend that will achieve the specified gradation (UFC 3-250-03).
                   Plot the selected blended gradation on a graph, with the allowable limits, to see that the blend
                   conforms to the specification requirements.
              Step 4. Determine the specific gravity of the components.
              Step 5. Use selected percentages of bitumen (UFC 3-250-03), make trial mixes, and determine
                   the test properties.
              Step 6. Plot the test properties on individual graphs, using the selected bitumen percentages.
                   Draw smooth curves through the plotted points.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  16-1


---

Chapter 16

              Step 7. Select the bitumen content that provides 4 percent air voids in the mixture.
              Step 8. Determine mixture properties at the selected bitumen content. If the properties are

                   within the specification requirements, this is the optimum asphalt content. If one or more
                   properties do not meet the specification requirements, adjust the binder content slightly so that
                   the mixture meets the specification requirements. If the properties do not all meet with some
                   slight binder content adjustment, the aggregate proportions or sources must be changed and the
                   mix design performed again.

VARIABLES

     16-3. Gradation specifications are based on the limits established as satisfactory by USACE. Within these
     limits, the following variables will affect the final mix design:

              The use of the mix (surface course, binder course, or road mix).
              The binder grade (AC).
              Loading (low tire pressure [100 pounds per square inch and under] or high tire pressure [more

                   than 100 pounds per square inch]).
              The maximum size of the aggregate to be used on the project.

BLENDS

     16-4. Once the gradation specifications have been selected, check the available materials to determine how
     to proportion the blend to meet these specifications. Study the sieve analysis of the available aggregates,
     and compute a series of trial blends. Make any necessary adjustments of the blend after testing the design
     and prepared mix. The considerations for establishing and adjusting the blend are explained in UFC
     3-250-03.

OPTIMUM BITUMEN CONTENT

     16-5. The determination of optimum bitumen content is based on the Marshall test method or some other
     method of mix design (such as Superpave) (ASTM D6925).

MARSHALL TEST

     16-6. The purposes of the Marshall test are to—
              Prepare specimens by drop-hammer compaction. The number of drop-hammer blows used
                   on the specimens is based on empirical correlations with two different traffic conditions: 50
                   blows on each end of the specimen for tires with less than 100 pounds per square inch of
                   pressure and 75 blows on each end of the specimen for tires with greater than 100 pounds per
                   square inch of pressure.
              Find the optimum bitumen content by determining the asphalt content that produces 4
                   percent air voids in the laboratory-compacted mixture. This method of picking the optimum
                   asphalt content does not work very well for sand asphalt mixtures, but does work well for dense
                   graded asphalt mixtures having maximum aggregate sizes of approximately 1/2 inch and larger.
                   Typically, sand mixtures will require a higher void level in the mixture.
              Measure maximum breaking load (stability) and corresponding deformation (flow) for
                   specimens prepared according to the compaction procedure. These values of stability and
                   flow are empirically correlated for conditions outlined in the paragraph above.
              Obtain the unit weights of specimens from calculations based on weighing the specimens in
                   air and in water. Porous specimens are coated with paraffin before weighing them in water.

     16-7. The Marshall test is applicable for hot mixes containing penetration, viscosity, or performance
     graded of AC and containing aggregate with not more than 10 percent of the aggregate larger than the
     1-inch sieve. The procedure for handling large aggregate and for cold mixes is described later in this
     chapter.

16-2  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                                     Bituminous-Mix Design

Steps

     16-8. The following paragraphs are procedures and examples to determine the optimum asphalt content for
     one particular blend of aggregates.

Step 1. Select the Bitumen Content

     16-9. Start the laboratory tests by estimating the optimum amount of bitumen for the aggregate to be
     tested. Continue the tests until the results show at least two bitumen contents above and two below what
     would be the optimum content. Since the optimum is not determined until after the results are plotted,
     specimens are usually prepared for each of six different contents. Prepare at least three specimens at each
     bitumen content. One percent incremental changes of bitumen content may be used for preliminary work.
     However, use increments of 1/2 percent for final tests. Express the percent of bitumen as a percent of the
     total weight of the batch of paving mix. The procedure for establishing the estimated optimum content is
     explained in UFC 3-250-03.

Step 2. Prepare the Aggregate

     16-10. The procedures for determining the particle size distribution and blending to meet specification
     requirements have already been presented. Dry 50 pounds of the selected blend to a constant weight at 221°
     to 230°F. This amount of material provides for 1,220 grams per specimen for 18 specimens (three in each
     of bitumen contents), with allowance for some loss. The Marshall method uses a 4-inch-diameter mold and
     is not applicable, without special handling, when more than 10 percent of the aggregate is larger than 1
     inch. The previously given total amounts of aggregate required assume less than 10 percent of the particles
     will exceed 1 inch. (Special handling of oversize aggregates is covered later.)

Step 3. Prepare the Hot Mixture

     16-11. Heat the bitumen and aggregates to specified temperatures for mixing. Bitumen should not be held
     at the mixing temperature for more than 1 hour before using. Therefore, plan the preparation so that the
     mixing will be done within this time limit. Preheat the mixing pans to a temperature about 50°F above the
     mixing temperature. Pour the heated dry aggregate fractions into the pans, and mix thoroughly. Form a
     crater in the mixed aggregate, and pour the required amount of bitumen at the proper temperature into the
     crater. At this point, the temperature of the ingredients should be within the limits specified above. Mix the
     aggregates and bitumen as rapid and thoroughly as possible to ensure that the bitumen is uniformly
     distributed throughout the aggregate.

Step 4. Compact the Hot Mixture

     16-12. Prepare three specimens at each bitumen content, and prepare the molds to receive the specimens
     as soon as they are mixed. Thoroughly clean and heat the striking face of the compaction hammers and the
     compaction molds to 200° to 300°F. Oil the mold and other metal in contact with the mix before the
     mixture is introduced to facilitate removing the specimen after compaction (a silicone spray is convenient
     for this use). Wipe the parts with a rag or paper towel before using. Place the mix in the mold, rodding the
     material as it is added. Remove the collar, and with a trowel, smooth the top surface of the mix to a slightly
     rounded shape. The thickness of the compacted specimen should be 2.5 + .05 inches. One or two trials will
     indicate the quantity of mix required to produce such a specimen. Replace the collar, and place the mold
     assembly on the compaction pedestal. The temperature at the time of compaction should be no more than
     25°F lower than the mixing temperatures provided in table 11-3, page 11-5.

     16-13. Apply the required number of blows with the compaction hammer (figure 16-1, page 16-4).
     Remove the base plate and collar, and reverse and reassemble the mold. Apply the required number of
     blows to the other side of the specimen. For example, roads, streets, and facilities for an airfield designed
     for aircraft whose tires carry pressure of 100 pounds per square inch or less should be compacted by 50
     blows on each end of the specimen. If the pavement is being designed for aircraft, whose tires carry
     pressure greater than 100 pounds per square inch, the compactive effort should be 75 blows per side.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  16-3


---

Chapter 16

                         Figure 16-1. Compaction of bituminous trial mix specimens

Step 5. Cool the Hot Specimen

     16-14. After compacting, remove the baseplate and collar; air-cool the mold and specimen (normally
     overnight), or place them in cold water for at least 2 minutes for fast cooling. Remove the cooled specimen
     from the mold with an extension jack or by placing the collar on the floor (with the mold and specimen on
     top) and forcing the specimen out with blows from the compaction hammer. The specimen is easier to
     remove if the mold is placed in a 140°F oven for a few minutes just before ejecting the specimen. Place the
     specimen (carefully handled and suitably identified) on a smooth surface until it is ready for testing.

Step 6. Weigh the Specimen in Air and in Water

     16-15. Weigh each specimen in air and in water to obtain the weight and volume measurements used in
     calculating the unit weight of the compacted mix (figure 16-2). These tests should be conducted according
     to ASTM D2726.

                Figure 16-2. Weighing compacted bituminous specimen in water

16-16. DD Form 1218 (Marshall Method–Computation of Properties of Asphalt Mixtures) (figure 16-3)
provides space for recording the measurements made at room temperature. A direct weight in water of
open-textured or porous specimens will give erroneous results because of water penetration and absorption.
For such specimens, other means must be used to determine the volume. Two means of measuring the
volume of the porous specimens include the use of paraffin (ASTM D1188) and the use of automatic
vacuum sealing methods (ASTM D6752).

16-4        TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                                     Bituminous-Mix Design

Step 7. Measure Stability and Flow
     16-17. The purpose of the stability and flow test is to determine the potential for the rutting or shoving of
     the mixture under traffic. If the stability is too low or the flow is too high, this is an indication of a potential
     for rutting or shoving. The data is recorded, and calculations are made as shown in table 16-1, page 16-7.

              Figure 16-3. Sample DD Form 1218

Step 8. Calculate and Present Results

     16-18. DD Form 1218 is used to summarize the measured and calculated Marshall test properties. The
     specimen numbers, placed on each specimen with a marking crayon, are given for identification.

      Note. There are four duplicate tests for each bitumen content and these four test values are
      averaged in each instance. Note also that the specimen’s thickness is not indicated, since the
      volume can be used to find the stability correlation ratio.

16-19. The theoretical specific gravity is determined according to ASTM D2041 and is used to calculate
the voids. Note that the stability value is shown directly in pounds. The stability value varies directly with
the specimen’s thickness. Therefore, it is necessary to correct the stability values for specimens of a

3 April 2015          TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034                16-7


---

Chapter 16

     thickness greater or less than the standard 2 1/2 inches as presented in the standard. All other calculations
     are indicated directly on the form.

Step 9. Complete a Graphical Presentation
     16-20. The average Marshall test properties from the tabulation for each bitumen content are shown
     graphically on DD Form 1219 (Bituminous Mix Curves) (figure 16-4, page 16-8). The average values for
     each property are plotted on their respective graphs, using the bitumen content as ordinates. A smooth
     curve is drawn through the plotted points in each instance.

                                                                    Bituminous-Mix Design

Step 10. Apply the Marshall Test

     16-21. Table 16-1, page 16-7, lists the criteria for determining the optimum bitumen content along with
     the Marshall specifications for a satisfactory mix. Once the optimum bitumen content is obtained, the
     Marshall properties at this average are read from curves representing the mix. These values are compared
     with the specification limits to evaluate the mix.

Step 11. Apply the Modified Marshall Test for Cold-Mix Pavement

     16-22. This method is used as an aid in determining the asphalt content for cold-mix design of light-duty
     pavement. It can be used where asphalt cutbacks will be the binder. The procedure follows those used for
     hot-mix design in general, with the following modifications:

              Aggregates. These are dried to a moisture content expected during construction (up to a
                   maximum of 2 percent by weight).

              Asphalt. The selected bitumen is mixed with the aggregates, but at the temperature
                   recommended for field application. The aggregates remain at room temperature.

              Curing. Before compaction, the mixture is cured at least 12 hours in an oven, set at 140°F ± 5°.
              Compaction. After curing, the mixture is compacted at 140°F, using 50 blows of the hammer at

                   each end of the specimen.
              Cooling. After molding, the specimens are cooled to room temperature in the molds. Care must

                   be taken to remove the specimens, undisturbed and undamaged, from the molds.
              Testing. The specimens are heated in an oven to 100°F ± 2° and tested in the Marshall machine.

                   Heating will normally take about 2 hours.
              Design amount of asphalt. The asphalt contents at maximum density and maximum stability,

                   after averaging, are used as the design amount.

TEST VARIATIONS

16-23. These variations apply to aggregates with 10 percent or more larger than 1 inch. The procedure
previously described is applicable where the amount of aggregate larger than the 1-inch sieve is less than
10 percent of the total. When the +1-inch material exceeds 10 percent of the total, the following variations
are made in the procedure:

 Mix bitumen at the selected content with the entire aggregate, including the + 1-inch portion.

 Pass the mixed hot batch through a 1-inch sieve. Discard the +1-inch portion.

 Make compacted specimens from the portion that passes the 1-inch sieve, and perform the
     Marshall test. Do not calculate the voids of the compacted specimens at this time.

 Determine the bulk specific gravity of the +1-inch aggregate, and with the specific gravity of the

              compacted specimens, compute the adjusted specific gravity as follows:

where—                                      100
                                𝐺A = �𝐴 + 𝐵� × 𝑓

                                        𝐶𝐷

GA = adjusted specific gravity

A = weight of dry, 1-inch material (as a percentage) of the total batch weight (bitumen + aggregate)

B = portion of the total batch remaining after the dry, +1 -inch portion is removed (100 percent – A

percent)

C = bulk specific gravity of the +1-inch aggregate

D = actual specific gravity of the compacted specimen

F = empirical factor = 0.995

 Calculate the voids by using the adjusted specific gravity, and apply the design criteria for this
     value.

 Use stability and flow values as measured on the compacted specimens.

Chapter 16

     16-24. When using emulsions, it is better to design the mix with some compactive effort and select the
     asphalt emulsion for some void level, typically 4 to 6 percent. However, if samples cannot be compacted,
     use the following formula to determine the amount of asphalt emulsion, based on the surface area of the
     aggregate:

                                                  𝑃 = 0.05𝐴 + 0.1𝐵 + 0.5𝐶
     where—
     P = percent by weight of asphalt emulsion, based on weight of graded mineral aggregate
     A = percent of mineral aggregate retained on the No. 8 sieve
     B = percent of mineral aggregate passing the No. 8 sieve and retained on the No. 200 sieve
     C = percent of mineral aggregate passing the No. 200 sieve

             Note. Express all percentages as whole numbers. Absorptive aggregate (slag, lime rock,
             vesicular lava, coral) will require additional asphalt to fill the voids in the aggregate particles.

SUPERPAVE MIX DESIGN

     16-25. Superpave mix design is used by most state departments of transportation and many locations
     outside the United States The Surperpave mix design method has also been used some for airfields. This
     procedure for preparing and compacting samples is described in ASTM D6925. The mix design procedure
     is the same as described for the Marshall test, but the method of compaction is different. A gyratory
     compactor is used for compaction as shown in figure 16-5. The Superpave gyratory compactor compacts
     samples that are 6 inches in diameter; hence, the aggregate can be up to 1.5 inches without causing any
     problem.

                               Figure 16-5. Superpave gyratory compactor

16-26. The samples are compacted in the gyratory compactor and tested for volumetric properties similar
to that for Marshall test. All volumetric testing of the Superpave samples is the same as for Marshall;
however, there are no Marshall stability and flow tests conducted on the Superpave gyratory-compacted
samples. The asphalt content for the Superpave designed mixtures is selected to provide 4 percent air voids

16-10  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                                     Bituminous-Mix Design

     just like with the Marshall procedure. The number of gyrations to be used for compaction has to be
     determined and provided in the specifications. Mixtures designed for high traffic will need a higher number
     of gyrations, and mixtures designed with lower traffic will need a lower number of gyrations. Ninitial and
     Nmaximum requirements are normally part of the Superpave design procedure, but these properties are not
     used as part of the procedure for designing military airfield mixtures. Only Ndesign is used for military
     airfields.

JOB MIX FORMULA

     16-27. When the necessary laboratory tests have been completed and the optimum bitumen content has
     been determined, the job mix formula must be established for use by plant personnel producing the paving
     mix. Setting up the job mix formula involves the relative percentages of the available aggregate and
     bitumen. In the mix-design test already illustrated, the optimum content was found to be 4.7 percent of the
     total mix. Accordingly, the aggregate portion of the mix will be 95.3 percent of the total mix. Referring to
     the aggregate blend calculations on DD Form 1217 (Bituminous Mix Design–Aggregate Blending) (figure
     16-6) and DD Form 1207 (figure 16-7, page 16-12), the portions were 45 percent coarse aggregate, 30
     percent fine aggregate, 20 percent fine river bar sand, and 5 percent limestone dust or mineral filler.
     Usually, there is enough dust in the crushed aggregates so that mineral filler does not have to be added to
     the mixture. So, adding mineral filler separately is not typically done. Also, the amount of natural sand is
     20 percent, which is satisfactory for road projects, but exceeds the 15 percent that is allowed for airfields.

                                                Chapter 17

                                 Plant Control

         Aggregate is mixed in correct proportions for specified construction in high-type
         bituminous paving plants and intermediate-type plants. The type of plant used
         depends on proximity and the requirements for the aggregate. Laboratory procedures
         must be done to obtain correct mixes and must be correlated with production
         procedures. Proper control over all procedures and equipment must be exercised to
         ensure quality for each aggregate specified.

PLANT TYPES

     17-1. Figures 17-1 and 17-2 are schematic drawings of a batch plant and a drum-mix plant.

              Figure 17-1. Bituminous hot-mix batch plant

              Figure 17-2. Drum mix plant

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034                                 17-1


---

Chapter 17

BATCH PLANT

     17-2. In the operation of a high-type bituminous paving plant, aggregates from two or more sources
     (typically at least three) are fed into the aggregate dryer in the approximate proportions required to produce
     the desired gradation. This initial proportioning is usually accomplished by a hopper-type feeder, operating
     from individual bins for each aggregate being used in the mixture. The aggregates are fed onto a collector
     belt, which feeds the aggregates into a cold elevator that delivers them to the dryer. The mechanical feeder
     is typically loaded with a front-end loader that picks up the material from the stockpile and loads the feeder
     bin.

     17-3. The aggregates are heated to the desired temperature. Upon leaving the dryer, the combined
     aggregate passes over vibrating screens, where they are separated according to size. The usual screening
     equipment for a three-bin plant consists of a rejection (scalping) screen for eliminating oversized material
     and screens for dividing the coarse aggregate into two separate sizes (bins). The fine aggregate is screened
     into the third bin. An additional screen is provided for separating the coarse aggregate into another
     component in a four-bin plant. Additional mineral filler, if required, is usually stored and weighed or
     proportioned into the mix separately. It may be obtained from the plant’s dust collector system or an
     external source. Plant screens may be changed to provide a variation in the size of openings. The sizes used
     depend largely on the type of mixture being produced. In some cases, it may be necessary to change the
     size of the screens to obtain a proper balance of aggregate sizes in each bin.

     17-4. The aggregates must be fed into the plant uniformly, preferably with a mechanical feeder, to obtain
     efficient plant operation and to produce a desired mixture. It is necessary that the portion of material taken
     from each hot bin be selected so that the combined gradation of the material coming from the hot bins be
     approximately the same as the gradation of the material going into the plant. If the gradations are not
     approximately the same, the plant will be unbalanced and result in one or more bins running full of material
     and one or more bins running low on material, leading to an overflow of the full bins.

DRUM MIX PLANTS

     17-5. Drum mix plants have not been available for use as long as batch plants. In fact, drum mix plants
     have only been around since the late 1970s. These plants have the ability to produce mixtures of equal
     quality to that of a batch plant. However, these plants are much more portable and easier to use. There is no
     rescreening of the aggregates; hence, the gradation of the aggregate entering the plant should be the same
     as the gradation of the aggregate entering the plant, except for some breakdown of materials that will
     generally increase the amount of material passing the No. 200 sieve and some of the larger sieves. It is
     estimated that more than 90 percent of the new plants being sold in the United States are drum mix type
     plants.

INITIATING PLANT PRODUCTION

     17-6. The heaviest demands on laboratory facilities arise at the beginning of plant production. Preliminary
     computations, which provide the gradation for the mixture design, may be made to determine the weight of
     material from each aggregate feeder. The gradation of aggregate supplied by the plant may not precisely
     reproduce the desired gradation. The gradation of the plant-produced aggregates approximates the one used
     in design, within reasonable tolerances, if initial sampling were performed properly and if the plant were
     operated efficiently. Certain steps should be taken, however, to ensure that satisfactory mixtures are
     reproduced from the beginning and throughout the period of plant production. Procedures outlined in this
     section will ensure satisfactory paving mixtures.

SIEVE ANALYSIS

     17-7. A sieve analysis is made on material recovered from the asphalt mixture and material fed into the
     plant prior to mixing with AC. Aggregate samples that are recovered from the hot-mix asphalt (HMA)
     mixtures for sieve analyses are never obtained at plant start-up. One should always wait for a couple of
     trucks to load out before testing begins since there are typically some minor issues with the first mix

17-2  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                                                    Plant Control

     produced at start up. Final adjustments to the mixture are based on these sieve analyses and other testing
     that is performed.

MIX REDESIGN

     17-8. Sometimes, significant changes have to be made in the mixture design to produce acceptable
     properties. It is then necessary to redesign the mix, using plant-produced aggregates. Specimens are
     prepared and tested for the new design in the same manner as for the original design. This allows the
     determination of optimum asphalt content and ensures that a satisfactory mix is designed and produced by
     the plant. Sufficient additional tests should be performed to establish optimum asphalt content requirements
     and to ensure that the mix meets applicable criteria.

PLANT PRODUCTION CONTROL

     17-9. When production begins, obtain samples from one of the first few truckloads of asphalt mixture, but
     typically not the first two trucks. Prepare three or four specimens from this sample, and compact and test
     them according to standard procedures described previously. On the first day of construction, the operation
     should be stopped until the test results are completed and adjustments in the mix are made as needed. After
     the mix has been verified to meet the requirements, additional work can be continued and testing done
     during production. When samples are taken, conduct the tests as rapidly as possible so that the results are
     available prior to production of too much additional asphalt mixture. Be careful in making adjustments
     since there is always variability in testing, and this normal variability may make it appear that the test
     results are slightly different than desired when this is actually a result of normal testing variability and not
     material or mixture issues. It is always good to look at the last few tests that were conducted when making
     changes in the mixture components.

     17-10. Probable causes of paving-mixture deficiencies for batch and drum mix plants are shown in figure
     17-3, page 17-4. These deficiencies are observed at the plant. Other imperfections and their causes that may
     be encountered in placing the mix in the pavement are shown in figure 17-4, page 17-4.

MARSHALL TEST CONTROL

     17-11. The flow value of the individual test specimens should never exceed the specification limits and
     should not vary more than two points between specimens. If a polymer-modified asphalt is used, the flow
     test will not provide reasonable values so, in this case the flow is not part of the criteria. The average value
     of the other test properties for the test specimens from any given truckload should be within the
     specification limits and not vary from the final design values by more than the following amounts:

              Unit weight total mix, ± 1.5 pounds per cubic foot.
              Marshall stability, ± 10 percent.
              Voids total mix, ± 1 percent.

             Note. No limit is placed on voids filled since control is ensured by the limitations on the voids
             total mix.

PLANT CONTROL EXECUTION

     17-12. Stop plant production if the measured test properties fail to comply with the limitations cited
     above. Resume production on a trial basis until the problem has been isolated and corrective measures have
     been taken. Small variations in aggregate quantities do not usually change test properties significantly,
     while small changes in bitumen content (a few tenths of a percent) can have a very significant effect on test
     properties. If trouble is indicated, check all calculations as a first step. Check the total of the batch weights
     dumped into the truck against the total load on the truck. These total weights should not vary from each
     other by more than 2 percent.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  17-3


---

Chapter 17
            Figure 17-3. Probable causes of paving-mixture deficiencies detected at plant

     Figure 17-4. Probable causes of imperfections encountered in laying pavement

17-13. Improper weighing or faulty scales may be detected readily. Take corrective measures by
maintaining a close check on load weights. Check plant scales and gate openings and recalibrate them as
necessary. Adjust scales and proportioning devices found to be inaccurate. After checking the plant
components and making the necessary adjustments, obtain additional samples for testing. The amount of
sampling and testing required depends on the specifications for the project. If there is no guidance available

17-4  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                                                    Plant Control

     on the amount of testing required, then two sets of tests should be conducted per day of operation. The
     testing should include, as a minimum, sampling the asphalt mixture from a loaded truck; compacting
     samples in the laboratory; and measuring the air voids, stability, and flow. Additional testing includes
     asphalt content measurement and gradation measurement.

     17-14. It is very important that the mixture be controlled during production so that the air voids in
     laboratory-compacted samples is approximately 4 percent.

ASPHALT CONTENT DETERMINATION

     17-15. The procedure for controlling plant production (based on the test properties obtained using
     Marshall or Superpave procedures) may be supplemented by the determination of asphalt content using a
     centrifugal extraction apparatus or the ignition test. The measured asphalt content is compared with the
     optimum asphalt content previously established. Discrepancies may be corrected by plant adjustments, if
     necessary. After the asphalt has been extracted or ignited, the remaining aggregates may be subjected to a
     sieve analysis for comparison with the previously selected aggregate gradation. Corrections again may be
     made in the operation of the plant as necessary.

     17-16. The ignition test is much more user-friendly since it does not require the use of solvents that are
     difficult to obtain, store, and discard. Both tests, if correctly conducted, will provide a reasonably accurate
     measure of the asphalt content and allow for the gradation of the aggregate to be determined. Perform
     calculations on DD Form 1793 (Determination of Asphalt Content [Dullin - Rotarex Extractor]) (figure 17-
     5, page 17-6) for the solvent extraction test.

     17-17. The clean aggregate recovered from the solvent extraction test or ignition test may be subjected to
     sieve analysis. When using the solvent extraction test, the amount of material passing the No. 200 sieve
     (mineral filler) in the sieve analysis must be increased by the weight of filler retained in the filter-paper
     gasket and in the solvent as determined in the calculations.

DENSITY TESTS

     17-18. Density test samples should be taken and tests performed as often as conditions require, but at least
     once for every 400 tons of mix placed. To obtain a satisfactory specimen, take the samples early in the
     morning when the pavement is cool. Take the sample for testing from a random location on any portion of
     the bituminous pavement.

     17-19. A coring machine or concrete saw may be used for cutting out samples. Avoid chopped or jack-
     hammered samples, if possible, as these are likely to develop cracks or other disturbances which would
     lead to erroneous results. Cut samples completely through the thickness of the pavement, and remove them
     carefully to avoid damage. In hot weather, it may be necessary to chill the area with ice for 15 to
     30 minutes before cutting out the sample. Using a coring machine is the preferred method most often used
     for obtaining samples for density measurement.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  17-5


---

Chapter 17

     17-20. When density samples are taken from a surface course placed on a binder course, it is practically
     impossible to remove the specimen from the tacked binder course surface. It usually requires that a sharp
     object and hammer be used to remove the top layer. The two layers can be separated by sawing. Determine
     the bulk specific gravity for each specimen, and use the following formula to calculate the specimen’s
     density:

                                                          𝐷 = 𝐺m 𝑥 62.4
     where—
     D = density of specimen (in pounds per cubic foot)
     Gm = bulk specific gravity of specimen
     62.4 = density of water (in pounds per cubic foot)

      Legend:  April
      Apr      Department of Defense
      DD       February
      Feb      number
      No.      specialist four
      SP4
                Figure 17-5. Sample DD Form 1793

17-6           TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                                                    Plant Control

     17-21. Compare the calculated density of the bituminous pavement with the densities obtained during the
     laboratory compaction of samples of the same mixture. Normally, the field density must be at least 95 to
     98 percent of the laboratory-compacted density. However, individual project specifications do vary, and the
     acceptable density ranges must be verified for each project. When the density test on samples from the
     bituminous pavement show that the minimum specified field density has not been obtained, correct the
     compaction procedures so that satisfactory density is obtained. When constant difficulty is experienced in
     meeting the specified density, check the capability of the rollers and the acceptability of the rolling pattern.

     The most common method used to specify density requirements is to specify it in terms of theoretical
     maximum density (TMD) (ASTM D2041). The typical requirements when using this requirement is
     approximately 4 percent lower than that required for the percent of laboratory density. A typical
     requirement is 91 to 94 percent of TMD.

EXPEDITING THE DESIGN

     17-22. When military expediency demands it, the preliminary laboratory mix design can be eliminated
     and the mix designed directly from plant-produced aggregates. In such cases, the engineering officer will,
     on the basis of sieve analysis or other information or judgment, select the most promising of the available
     aggregates and start the plant using this aggregate. As pointed out previously, in nearly all cases, the
     aggregate will show some breakdown of the particles because of heating and screening. Consequently, the
     grading curve for the material that has passed through the plant will differ from the grading curve of the
     material before entering the plant. Laboratory tests using aggregate from the plant bins combined in the
     most desirable proportions must be conducted to determine the optimum asphalt content (OAC). The most
     important requirement to meet is to have 4 percent air voids in the laboratory compacted mixture.

     17-23. The manufacture of the paving mix can then be initiated. If the available aggregate must be used,
     regardless of whether it produces a mix that meets all of the design criteria at OAC, the asphalt content
     should be selected to give a mix that meets the criteria for stability, flow, and percent of voids in the total
     mix.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  17-7
