                                          PART THREE

                          Concrete

Part Three discusses the testing of both fresh and hardened concrete and the
materials from which it is made. Fresh concrete properties are performed to ensure
that the concrete mixture proportions meet design requirements before it is placed.
Hardened properties tests are performed to ensure that the concrete meets basic
design performance criteria. Specifications are developed around results of standard
test methods; therefore, it is important to comply with the details of these test
methods which are based on ASTM standards. These methods may not necessarily
represent the placing environment or conditions. Information on contingency field
testing and troubleshooting are also included. Further information on concrete types,
components, design, and uses in military construction can be found in TM 3-
34.44/MCRP 3-17.7D.

                                                Chapter 18

      Description, Terminology, Materials, and Concepts

         Concrete is one of the most economical, versatile, and universally used construction
         materials. It is one of the few building materials that can be produced by the user
         directly on the job to meet the specific requirements of the construction. When first
         mixed, fresh concrete varies from thick fluid to a putty-like material, depending on
         the mixture proportions. The mixture can then be placed in a form or extruded
         (usually in the case of pavements), where it is allowed to harden until it reaches the
         required strength for service loads. The finished and cured concrete is a hard, stone-
         like material. It is used for pavements, foundations, dams, retaining walls, bridges,
         and buildings of all types.

TERMS

     18-1. The following are some useful terms, some of which are commonly misunderstood:
              Air. In the context of concrete, air refers to the air-filled voids that exist in concrete. Air is
                  classified as two types: entrapped air and entrained air. Field tests indicate total air, which
                  includes both entrained and entrapped air.
                   Entrapped air. Entrapped air refers to air-filled voids that occur unintentionally, but as a
                        natural part of the mixing, transporting, placing, and consolidation of concrete. Values of
                        1 to 2 percent are common.
                   Entrained air. Entrained air refers to air-filled voids that are intentionally introduced in
                        fresh concrete by using air-entraining admixtures. Properly entrained air voids range in
                        diameter from 0.01 to 1.00 millimeter. Whether concrete is air entrained or not is difficult to
                        determine without magnification.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  18-1


---

Chapter 18

              Cement. Cement is one of the components of concrete. When it reacts with water, it functions to
                  bind the other components of concrete together. Concrete structures are often incorrectly
                  identified as made of cement (a cement runway).

              Concrete. Concrete is a mixture of cement, sand, coarse aggregate, and water.
              Grout. Grout is a material that is used to fill spaces. Grouts normally have a high level of

                  fluidity so that they can be pumped into spaces. They may be cement paste, mortar (commonly
                  called sanded grout), or concrete. Grouts are commonly used in repair applications and in some
                  new construction where there are voids or gaps that need to be filled.
              Hydraulic cement. Hydraulic cement is any cement whose chemical basis for cementing
                  depends on reacting with water.
              Mortar. Mortar is a mixture of cement, sand, and water. Mortars are most commonly used in
                  masonry construction.
              Paste. Paste is a mixture of cement and water. Cement pastes are sometimes used for repair and
                  grouting of very small cracks and features and is the binder phase of concretes and mortars.

MATERIALS

     18-2. Materials used in making concrete are discussed in the following paragraphs.

CEMENT

     18-3. In the United States, cements are sold for general construction under three specifications: ASTM
     C150, ASTM C595, and ASTM C1157. ASTM C150 covers portland cements; ASTM C595 covers
     blended cements; and ASTM C1157 covers a series of hydraulic cements that may have a wide variety of
     chemical compositions that are significantly different from portland cements. All three specifications
     require similar performance properties. Some cements comply with more than one of these specifications,
     so bags will sometimes be marked as complying with more than one specification.

     18-4. Portland cements are cements that are made primarily of calcium silicates that conform to a relatively
     narrow set of requirements on composition and performance. ASTM C150 recognizes the following types:

              Type I. Type I is for general-purpose use. This is the predominant cement used in general
                  construction.

              Type II. Type II is for application where moderate amounts of sulfate are found in soil or
                  groundwater, and for use when lower heat evolution than typical of Type I cement is needed.

              Type III. Type III is for use when more rapid strength gain than offered by Type I cement is
                  needed.

              Type IV. Type IV is for use when very low heat evolution is required (mostly in structures with
                  thick sections).

              Type V. Type V is for use when high levels of sulfate are present in soils and groundwater.

     18-5. Optional limits for early workability, air entraining, and chemical compatibility with certain
     aggregates can be invoked for dealing with special conditions.

     18-6. Blended cements consist principally of portland cement, but will have significant amounts of
     supplemental cementing materials (SCM) blended in with them. Typically, coal fly ash and slag are the
     most common materials used. The amounts of these blended SCMs is large enough that the material is no
     longer called a portland cement. Specification ASTM C595 recognizes two basic types based on whether
     the cement uses pozzolan (coal fly ash, clay, or volcanic ash) as the SCM or uses slag from steel
     manufacture as the SCM. There are a number of options that deal with the same performance properties
     that the 5 types of portland cement described in ASTM C150 are designed to address.

     18-7. ASTM C1157 on generic hydraulic cements recognizes six types of hydraulic cements similar
     problems to those covered by ASTM C150:

              Type GU (general use).
              Type MS (moderate sulfate exposures).

18-2  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                              Description, Terminology, Materials, and Concepts

              Type HS (high sulfate exposures).
              Type MH (moderate heat evolution).
              Type LH (low heat evolution).
              Type HE (high early strengths).

     18-8. Many nationalities have standard specifications that cover the cements available in their locality. The
     names and type designations will usually differ considerably from U.S. specifications, but performance
     properties are designed to deal with the same issues that are behind the various requirements of U.S.
     cements. Specific requirements are typically designed around strength, time of setting, length of
     workability, amount of heat evolved, and resistance to certain chemical degradation problems (exposure to
     sulfates, chemically unstable aggregates).

     18-9. Testing hydraulic cements for specification compliance requires more in the way of laboratory
     capability than usually exists in field construction laboratories. Hydraulic cements are normally supplied
     with a mill certificate that describes the relevant properties. Mill certificates often represent typical
     properties and do not always accurately represent the cement in hand. Under critical construction
     conditions, these properties may need to be verified by an independent laboratory that is competent to
     execute the relevant tests.

SUPPLEMENTAL CEMENTING MATERIALS

     18-10. SCM is a class of materials that are not usually used alone as cements, but rather as a partial
     replacement for portland cement. Most only SCM develop cementing properties in the chemical
     environment of portland cement. When blended with cement at a cement manufacturing facility, these are
     called blended cements and covered by ASTM C595. Blending often occurs during the batching of
     concrete, which allows the user to control the proportions.

     18-11. Typical materials and their associated specification are fly ash from coal combustion in coal-fired
     electric power plants (ASTM C618), blast furnace slag from steel manufacture (ASTM C989), volcanic
     ash, some clays and shales (ASTM C618), and silica residues from the manufacture of silicon metal, called
     silica fume (ASTM C1240). Other national specifications recognize these same materials, but names and
     designations will differ from U.S. specifications.

     18-12. The testing of SCMs requires a capability that is not usually available in a construction laboratory.
     Manufacturer’s certificates can normally be secured for these materials, but they usually represent typical
     properties rather than specific properties of the material on hand.

AGGREGATES

     18-13. Aggregates are the sand (fine aggregate) and stone (coarse aggregate) components of concrete and
     mortar. Both are specified in ASTM C33. Aggregates may be derived from natural deposits or by crushing
     quarried rock. Fine aggregate normally contains particle sizes ranging from about 0.2 to 5.0 millimeters in
     diameter. Coarse aggregate particle diameters range from about 1 to 75 millimeters for paving, and up to
     150 millimeters for other applications. In general, the maximum size used depends on the dimensions of the
     structure and on the spaces between reinforcing steel. General guidance is that the smallest dimension of a
     structure and the minimum openings in reinforcing steel should be at least three times larger than the
     maximum aggregate size.

     18-14. Several aggregate properties have significant impact on concrete. These include absorption of
     mixing water, particle shape, particle strength and durability to freezing, and absence of any tendency to
     chemically react with cement paste in a damaging way (alkali aggregate reaction). Aggregate sources are
     normally evaluated for these properties before construction begins and the source approved for use.

     18-15. Aggregate grading (distribution of particle sizes) has major effects on workability and the amount
     of cement and water required to produce good concrete, consequently, is an important test regularly
     executed during construction. Other properties that must be frequently verified are moisture content,
     absorption, density, and the amount of extremely fine material (dirt).

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  18-3


---

Chapter 18

WATER FOR MIXING AND CURING

     18-16. Properties of water that is used for mixing and curing concrete can be critical. In general, however,
     water that is suitable for drinking should work well for making and curing concrete. Most natural water
     sources that are not used for drinking water can also be used without a problem. Even seawater can be used
     to make concrete as long as there is no steel reinforcement that can be corroded by the chlorides present in
     seawater. When problems develop from a particular water source, they are usually related to chemical
     contaminations. Specifically problematic are significant contaminations with organic materials and salts.
     ASTM C1602 is the standard specification for mixing water. This specification basically uses strength
     development and time of setting as indicators of major water quality problems. There are also limits on
     certain chemical properties that may have implications for long-term durability and staining.

     18-17. Full ASTM C1602 testing of water is beyond the capability of most construction laboratories;
     however, the strength and time of setting requirements for mixing water can usually be determined in field
     construction laboratories. Chemical properties testing must be done in a specialized laboratory.

CHEMICAL ADMIXTURES

     18-18. Chemical admixtures are used to generate properties in concrete that usually cannot be achieved
     without them. Chemical admixtures are often an optional ingredient for ordinary concrete applications.
     Through much of the 20th century, most concrete was made without chemical admixtures. One exception is
     air entraining admixture for use in concrete that is exposed to cycles of freezing and thawing when wet.
     ASTM C260 and ASTM C494 are the common specifications for admixtures used in the United States.
     Commonly used chemical admixtures are:

              Water-reducing admixtures (WRAs). WRAs are normally used to achieve better workability
                  without increasing the mixing water amount or allowing mixing water reduction to obtain higher
                  strengths without loss of workability. Materials that allow major reductions of mixing water are
                  called high-range WRAs (or superplastizers).

              Accelerators. These materials cause concrete to set more quickly and to achieve higher early
                  strengths than normal. These are usually used to compensate for the slow setting times and
                  strength gain caused by cold weather.

              Retarders. These materials cause a retardation of setting time. These are usually used to
                  compensate for the accelerated setting time caused by hot weather. Sugars are common and
                  strong retarders. Some concrete operations use table sugar as a retarder. This is effective, but it is
                  easy to overdose the concrete and to retard the setting time for many days.

              Air-entraining admixtures (AEAs). These materials cause a system of fine (less than 1
                  millimeter) air bubbles to form in the cement paste fraction of concrete. The primary purpose is
                  to protect against the effects of freezing and thawing cycles of concrete while in a water-
                  saturated condition. Concretes are sometimes air-entrained to enhance workability.

CONCEPTS

     18-19. The following paragraphs discuss concepts involved in concrete.

AIR ENTRAINING

     18-20. Air entraining is the development of a system of very fine air bubbles in the cement paste fraction
     of concrete and mortar. Bubble sizes range from about 0.01 to 1.00 millimeter. All but the largest sizes are
     not easily seen without the use of magnification. The primary purpose is to protect concrete from cycles of
     freezing and thawing when saturated with water, so air entraining is usually a requirement in environments
     in which the concrete will experience these conditions. The practice was first developed in the early 1940s,
     so structures in cold and wet environments constructed earlier than this often suffer significant
     deterioration.

18-4  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                              Description, Terminology, Materials, and Concepts

ALKALI AGGREGATE REACTION

     18-21. AAR refers to a process in which the pore fluid of concrete reacts chemically with certain
     aggregates resulting in the formation of an expansive material that can destroy concrete. The most common
     form of AAR involves silica-containing aggregates and is called alkali silica reaction (ASR). Most
     aggregates are not reactive; but for those that are, the visible evidence of the damage does not appear for
     many years. Reactive aggregates can only be determined through detailed analysis and testing by a
     laboratory that is specifically qualified for that purpose.

BLEEDING

     18-22. Bleeding is the accumulation of mixing water on the surface of concrete as a result of the solid
     particles settling. Bleeding starts immediately after the end of consolidation and ends no later than the time
     of initial setting. The maximum rate of bleeding typically occurs in the first hour after finishing. A small
     amount of bleeding is normal and sometimes useful for buffering concrete against early age drying.
     Excessive bleeding (more than a few millimeters) often indicates a problem with the proportions of
     materials in the concrete. Excessive bleeding is often recognized by the presence of small mounds of fine
     material that is brought to the surface along bleed channels. This can result in a weak surface.

CRACKING (PLASTIC SHRINKAGE)

     18-23. Plastic shrinkage cracking is a form of drying shrinkage cracking that sometimes occurs while
     concrete is in the plastic state (before the time of initial setting). This is likely to occur when the rate of
     evaporation of mixing water is higher than the rate at which the bleed water forms. This is typically caused
     by windy conditions and/or high concrete temperatures. Air temperature and relative humidity also
     contribute but are of less importance than wind speed and concrete temperature. The exposed surfaces of
     fresh concrete are particularly prone to this because water is easily evaporated. Concrete is more resistant to
     loss of water to evaporation after time of setting. Plastic shrinkage cracks vary in depth from a few
     millimeters to full depth, depending on the conditions.

CRACKING (DRYING SHRINKAGE)

     18-24. After the time of setting, concrete may continue to dry out and shrink, although at a slower rate
     than before setting. This shrinkage may eventually cause cracking. It is important to cure the concrete for
     several days after setting to allow for most of the water to be consumed by a reaction with the cement so
     that it will not be available to evaporate. Also, the concrete will be stronger and better able to withstand the
     shrinkage without cracking. Long-term drying shrinkage is very difficult to completely prevent.

CURING

     18-25. Curing is the maintenance of moisture and temperature conditions necessary for concrete to
     develop physical properties. Concrete gains strength because of the chemical reaction between cement and
     water. This reaction stops if the concrete dries out. Project specifications will normally specify a time
     period (usually a few days) during which concrete must be kept wet by the direct application of water
     (misting, wet burlap, wet sand or soil) or by preventing the evaporation of the water already in the concrete
     (mixing water). The latter is accomplished by using waterproof sheeting or by applying a suitable paint-like
     membrane called curing compound.

     18-26. Concrete only gains strength when reasonably warm. Strength gain effectively stops with
     temperatures approach the freezing point of water. If the concrete freezes during the first 24 hours,
     irreversible damage usually occurs. Temperatures between 10° and 40°C are ideal for proper strength gain
     rates. Strength gain is faster at higher temperatures. Temperatures of fresh concrete are normally limited to
     a maximum of about 35°C because there is a tendency for premature loss of workability and setting at
     higher temperatures and because of plastic shrinkage cracking issues.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  18-5


---

Chapter 18

DURABILITY

     18-27. Durability refers to any number of conditions that cause concrete to deteriorate. In the absence of
     aggressive chemical or overloading physical conditions, concrete will slowly change chemical composition
     due to the interaction with atmospheric conditions and groundwater, but will last for centuries. Conditions
     that cause deterioration include physical overload, abrasion, cycles of freezing and thawing while saturated
     with water, and chemical reactions within the concrete that cause expansion (cracking) or cause
     decomposition of cement and resultant loss of strength.

SHRINKAGE

     18-28. Shrinkage is a normal phenomenon in concrete due to large temperature changes, drying, and
     chemical reactions with atmospheric carbon dioxide. Excessive shrinkage will cause cracks to form.
     Shrinkage is minimized if concrete is properly proportioned and cured and if standard design and
     construction practices are followed. Excessive shrinkage is a common field problem.

STRENGTH

     18-29. Concrete strength starts to develop after the time of initial setting and continues until all of the
     cement in the concrete has reacted with the water or until the hydration consumes all of the mixing water.
     Low temperatures will also essentially stop strength gain. Most modern concretes reach a large fraction of
     their ultimate strength between 28 and 90 days (depending on materials, mixture proportions, and
     temperature) but may continue to slowly gain strength for years.

TEMPERATURE EFFECTS

     18-30. Temperature has the following major effects on concrete:
              Rate of strength gain and the time of setting. Strength gain is severely limited when
                  temperatures drop below 5°C for a long time, although no permanent damage occurs and normal
                  strength ultimately results. Setting time is also significantly extended at such low temperatures.
                  Significant damage occurs to concrete if it freezes during the first 24 hours.
              Damage from freezing. If concrete is not properly air-entrained, even strong concrete will
                  suffer damage from repeated freezing and thawing cycles when wet.
              High temperature. The major problem with high placing temperatures is that working time can
                  become severely restricted, and it may contribute to excessive evaporation of mixing water and
                  subsequent shrinkage cracking. Very high temperatures in newly hardened concrete can cause
                  cracking if the concrete cools rapidly. This is particularly a problem when wet concrete is
                  exposed to extreme drying conditions, resulting in the rapid cooling of the concrete surface.

TIME OF SETTING

     18-31. The time of setting is determined as the point at which concrete ceases to be a fluid and starts to
     take on properties of a solid. Two setting conditions are defined: time of initial setting and time of final
     setting. These are not sharply distinguishable events, but the time of initial setting is usually taken as the
     time at which concrete will start to support some load, while time of final setting is the time at which
     concrete can tolerate light service loads (foot traffic) without damage. The final finishing and application of
     final curing measures (plastic sheeting, wet burlap, water, or curing compound) can be applied at about the
     time of initial setting without damaging the surface of the concrete.

WATER-CEMENT RATIO

     18-32. The water-cement ratio is the ratio of the mass of water to the combined mass of cement and
     SCMs. The water-cement ratio is a critical parameter affecting workability, setting time, and strength
     development of concrete. Workability and setting time decrease with the decreasing water-cement ratio;
     strengths increase with the decreasing water-cement ratio. Unless water-reducing admixtures are used, a
     lower practical limit on the water-cement ratio is 0.45 to 0.50 for most concrete mixtures.

18-6  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                              Description, Terminology, Materials, and Concepts

WORKABILITY

     18-33. The workability of concrete is a vague term that refers to the ease with which concrete is
     distributed during placement and consolidated. The property is loosely related to the results of the slump
     test, but concretes of equal slump will often show different workability properties. The serious loss of
     workability typically occurs when the concrete is more than an hour or so old or when some unusual event
     happens with the hydration of the cement. This is normally indicated by a rapid loss in slump.

UNITS OF MEASURE AND CONVERSIONS

     18-34. The units of measure used collecting data and for reporting results can be somewhat confusing, but
     it is important to follow a consistent practice. Two systems of measure are primarily used. One is the
     System Internationale (SI), often called metric in common practice. The other is the English Inch-Pound
     System, sometimes called non-SI.

     18-35. Much of the world uses only SI-based unit systems for the measurement and reporting of test
     results. For much of its history, the United States has non-SI Systems. Currently there is a transition in
     process from the English Inch-Pound System to the SI System, and both systems of units will likely be
     encountered by the operator for some years to come. A common problem occurs when specifications are in
     SI units and equipment readout is in non-SI units. Following are procedures for working with units of
     measure in data collection and in reporting.

DATA COLLECTION

     18-36. As a general practice, data should be recorded in the units of measure of the instrument from
     which the data are obtained. The test methods will instruct which SI units or non-SI units to use. Data will
     then be converted to units of measure that are consistent with the units of measure required for the report.

CONVERSIONS AND REPORTING

     18-37. When data to be reported are simply the data recorded from measurement and the units are
     appropriate, then no conversions are required. If the data are not in the correct system of units, a conversion
     table or conversion factor is used to convert them into the required reporting format. For example, slump
     might be measured in inches, but required to be reported in millimeters. In this example, the data are simply
     converted from inches to millimeters by multiplying the inches by 25.4 to obtain the millimeters per inch.

     18-38. Some reported properties are determined by a calculation using several measured properties. If all
     of the measurements are recorded in the same system of measurement, calculate the property in those units
     as instructed in the test method and then convert the result to the required reporting system if it is different
     from the units used during data collection. For example, concrete strength is calculated by dividing the load
     required to crush the specimen by the surface area of the end of the specimen. If these are both in the
     English system (pound and inches), calculate the strength as pounds per square inch. If the reporting
     requirement is SI, convert the measurements to SI units of megapascals using an conversion factor of
     0.00689 megapascals per pounds per square inch.

     18-39. A more complicated example occurs when individual measurements used in a calculation are in
     different (mixed set) units. In this case, before doing any calculations, convert the measurements that are
     not in the proper system of units for reporting into those units and then calculate the property and report.
     For example, if the load is in pounds, but the surface area of the specimen is in square millimeters and the
     reporting convention is the English system (pounds per square inch), convert square millimeters to square
     inches, and then execute the calculation and report.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  18-7


---

This page intentionally left blank.


---

                                                Chapter 19

                         Field Aggregate Testing

         Aggregates used in making concrete are a blend of fine and coarse material, usually
         sand with natural gravel or crushed rock. The aggregates serve as an inert filler to
         provide the bulk material used in concrete. Well-graded aggregates contain particles
         of all sizes, from the largest permitted by the dimension of the structural member to
         be formed to the finer sand particles. The smaller sand particles fill the spaces
         between the larger particles, thus providing a dense material that requires a minimum
         amount of cement paste as a binder. The aggregate materials must be clean and hard,
         resist weathering, and have no unfavorable reaction with the cement. Aggregates
         must provide maximum strength and durability in a concrete mixture. Fineness,
         coarseness, and aggregate gradation are factors considered when deriving the correct
         concrete mixture for a specific construction purpose. Relative density (specific
         gravity), absorption, and moisture content also affect the aggregate’s ability to bind
         well with cement and water in a concrete mixture. The components of the final
         mixture (cement, water, and aggregate) must bond adequately for structural strength
         and must resist weathering and applied loads. Correct aggregate selection also
         minimizes the project’s overall cost. An engineering analysis determines the
         aggregate best suited for a particular purpose. Laboratory testing supports the best
         selection analysis. Field testing includes executing a few of the laboratory tests used
         to initially qualify the aggregate source. The purpose of this testing is to verify that
         these critical properties do not vary excessively during construction.

STOCKPILE SAMPLING

     19-1. It is difficult to ensure that unbiased samples are obtained from stockpiles. This is due to the
     segregation that often occurs when materials are stockpiled, allowing coarser particles to roll down to the
     outside base of the stockpile. For coarse or mixed coarse and fine aggregates, every effort should be made
     to enlist the services of power equipment to develop a separate, small sampling stockpile composed of
     materials drawn from several increments.

     19-2. When power equipment is not available, take samples from at least three increments, from the top
     third, the middle and the bottom third of the stockpile. Shove a board vertically into the stockpile just above
     the sampling point to prevent further segregation. When sampling fine-aggregate stockpiles, remove the
     outer surface before taking the sample. The best method of sampling fine-aggregate stockpiles is to use a
     tube or pipe (1 1/2 to 3 inches in diameter) inserted into the stockpile at five random locations to form the
     sample.

     19-3. Pit deposits are sources of natural sand and gravel. Sample them by channeling exposed faces or
     drilling test holes in the pits if exposed faces are not available. Ensure that the samples include only
     materials that are below the overburden or disturbed material.

GRADATION DETERMINATION

     19-4. The gradation of aggregate refers to the distribution of particles of aggregate among various sizes. A
     set of sieves is used in a mechanical shaker that shakes the sample of aggregate for a prescribed period of
     time allowing each particle an opportunity to pass through any number of sieves until that particle is
     retained on the sieve slightly smaller than the particle itself. The mass of each material retained on each

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  19-1


---

Chapter 19

     sieve is determined and calculated as a percentage of the initial sample mass. Aggregates having a smooth
     grading curve, no deficiency, and excess of any one particle size usually produce mixtures with fewer voids
     between particles. A higher proportion of coarse aggregate leaves voids that require more cement paste to
     fill. This affects the economy of the mixture. Too much fine aggregate increases the amount of surface area
     that must be coated with cement paste. This may weaken the concrete and is also uneconomical. Proper
     gradation results in—

             • A dense mass of concrete with a minimum of voids.
             • An economical mixture.
             • A strong structure.

     19-5. Optimum strength, water tightness, and durability in the hardened concrete require careful control of
     aggregate gradation.

     19-6. A gradation or sieve analysis conducted according to ASTM C136 indicates whether an aggregate
     particle size distribution meets the project’s requirements. Dense volumes of aggregate can result in a
     concrete that is denser, stronger, and more economical, watertight, and resistant.

MATERIAL FINER THAN 75-MICROMETER SIEVE

     19-7. Within the gradation, very fine material becomes an important factor in the development of a
     concrete mixture. Extremely fine material (clay, silt, dust, loam) occurring in most aggregates requires
     relatively large increases in the amount of mixing water because of the increased surface area associated
     with finer materials. Fine particles also tend to work to the surface of concrete and cause cracking upon
     drying, referred to as drying shrinkage cracks. If fine particles adhere to larger aggregate particles, they
     tend to interfere with the bond between the aggregate particles and cement-water paste. Specifications limit
     the amount of such material to a small percentage. ASTM C117 gives the standard test method for fine
     materials. Some fine material, not exceeding 3 percent of the total aggregate mass, is generally not harmful
     to concrete; and a small amount of such fines may improve the workability of the mixture.

FINENESS MODULUS

     19-8. After conducting the gradation, the particle size distribution of an aggregate can be monitored with a
     single value—the fineness modulus. The fineness modulus is an empirical factor that gives a relative
     measure of the proportional particle size distribution of an aggregate, fine, coarse, or a combination of fine
     and coarse particles. The fineness modulus represents the gradation of a particular sample by converting the
     cumulative percentage retained from the gradation into this empirical factor. Following the calculations for
     a gradation on an aggregate sample, the fineness modulus is calculated based on a series of sieves,
     beginning with the Nos. 100, 50, 30, 16, 8, 4, 3/8 inch, 3/4-inch, 1 1/2 inch, 3 inch, and ending as high as
     the 6-inch sieve size. The series of sieves used in the calculations depends on the nominal maximum size
     aggregate in the sample, fine aggregate generally includes the 3/8-inch sieve sizes, and therefore the
     calculations would only those percentages retained up to the 3/8-inch particles. An aggregate for a large
     mass concrete project using 6-inch nominal maximum size aggregate would include each of the sieves up
     to the 6-inch sieve. The fineness modulus values range from 2.3 for fine aggregate to 7.5 for very large,
     coarse aggregate.

SURFACE MOISTURE

     19-9. The specific gravity or relative density refers to the ratio of the mass of the aggregate to the mass of
     equal volume of distilled water. The absorption refers to the percentage of water absorbed into the pores of
     the aggregate, but does not involve the water on the outer surface of the aggregate. The outer surface water
     is referred to as the surface moisture. Tests for relative density, absorption, and surface moisture and make
     the necessary calculations to design the concrete mixture. For aggregates used in portland cement concrete,
     determine the relative density of the aggregates in a saturated, surface-dry (SSD) condition. This is the
     condition in which the pores in each aggregate particle are filled with water, and no excess water is on the
     particle surface. When used in concrete, the moisture condition of an aggregate can be defined as “not
     absorbing water from” or “contributing water to” the concrete mixture. Relative density is thus based on

19-2  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                                     Field Aggregate Testing

     determining the total volume occupied by the aggregate particles, including the permeable pore space.
     Absorption and surface-moisture determinations are necessary to calculate the amount of mixing water
     used in a concrete mixture. Adjustments to the mixing water are made based on the percentage of surface
     water calculated from the percentage of the total moisture content of an aggregate, minus the percentage
     associated with the absorption for that aggregate.

ORGANIC IMPURITIES, LOS ANGELES ABRASION, AND SULFATE
SOUNDNESS

     19-10. Natural fine aggregates may occasionally possess some unwanted materials that affect the strength
     of the mortar to bond the aggregate particles together. Organic impurities found in natural fine aggregate
     must be held to a minimum to satisfactorily provide sufficient strength in the mortar. Natural fine aggregate
     recovered from pits and from dredging operations often contain organic matter whose presence and levels
     can be determined by using ASTM C40. Sodium hydroxide reacts with organic matter and changes color
     with certain levels of organic materials present. This test uses a sodium hydroxide solution applied to a
     sample of the natural fine aggregate and allowed to stand for 24 hours as the reaction, if any, takes place.
     The color of the liquid above the settled sample is compared to a standard color solution or with a color
     chart containing five standard colors. Dark liquids indicate the presence of organic matter; a darker liquid
     indicates higher levels of organic matter. Samples of natural fine aggregates may be further tested for
     compressive strength loss in the mortar due to the amount of organic matter in the aggregate.

     19-11. Coarse aggregate must be hard and able to resist fracturing, wear, and abrasion during normal
     handling operations and in the hardened concrete. The Los Angeles abrasion resistance test evaluates the
     relative strength and abrasion resistance of coarse aggregate for use in concrete that may be subjected to
     high abrasion (high traffic areas, areas of erosion). ASTM C131 and ASTM C535 are similar tests with
     exceptions to sample size, number of steel ball bearings, and number of drum rotations. Both tests uses a
     Los Angeles abrasion test machine, which is a rotating steel drum with a fixed, steel shelf that picks up the
     aggregate sample and steel ball bearings and drops them onto the rolling sample below. The abrading of the
     rotating steel drum and impacting and crushing from the falling sample and steel ball bearings determines
     the aggregate’s resistance to degradation.

     19-12. The durability of the aggregate to cycles of freezing and thawing when wet affects the durability of
     the concrete. The sulfate soundness test, which evaluates aggregates for this susceptibility, uses a saturated
     sulfate salt solution that is absorbed into the pores of the aggregate and then heated to recrystallize the salt,
     which expands internally, seeking out the weak and less durable aggregate. ASTM C88 describes the
     procedures, sample sizes, sodium or magnesium sulfate salt solutions, heating stages, number of cycles,
     washing process, and calculations.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  19-3
