                                                Chapter 20

                          Field Concrete Testing

         A laboratory provides an ideal setting for generating test results used in the field.
         However, when tests are conducted partially or entirely in the field, conditions may
         not be ideal and can have significant effects on test results. The vibration of samples
         from nearby heavy equipment and extremes in temperature are two examples of such
         conditions. Proactive measure must be taken to protect samples from the natural
         environmental effects placed on them.

FRESH CONCRETE TESTS

     20-1. After concrete is mixed, a slump test and an air content test are performed and compared to project
     requirements. These tests are normally used for quality control.

SAMPLING

     20-2. Take samples of concrete for test specimens at the mixer by repeatedly passing a receptacle through
     the entire discharged stream until sufficient concrete is collected in the pan. For ready-mixed concrete, take
     samples from the transporting vehicle while it is discharging the concrete (figure 20-1).

                                Figure 20-1. Sampling concrete from a truck mixer

     20-3. Transport the samples to the testing site. To counteract segregation, mix the concrete with a shovel
     until the concrete is uniform in appearance. Note the type of truck, the time, and the location of the
     concrete placement for future reference. For paving concrete, take samples from the batch immediately
     after it is deposited on to the subgrade. Take at least five samples from different portions of the pile, and
     mix these samples thoroughly to form the test specimens.

TEMPERATURE

     20-4. The temperature of fresh concrete is measured according to ASTM C1064. This property is usually
     determined to verify that concrete meets the delivery temperature requirements of the project specification.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  20-1


---

Chapter 20

     It may also be used to determine whether unexpected chemical reactions in the cement are occurring that
     affect early workability properties. These reactions result in temperature increases of 5°C or more during
     the time between completions of mixing and time of setting. The concrete sample should be protected from
     high ambient temperatures during such testing.

     20-5. This test requires a thermometer capable of reading temperatures of 0°C through 50°C. The
     thermometer may be of a liquid in glass type, dial type, or digital type. The thermometer should be
     calibrated at least annually, or when readings are suspect. Calibration is accomplished by measuring the
     temperature of an ice and water slurry, containing mostly ice. Field measurements of concrete temperature
     are then corrected by the amount of error noted in this calibration. Liquid in glass thermometers tend to
     develop errors when the column of liquid develops a gap somewhere in the length of the column. Dial
     thermometers are particularly noted for developing errors in readings. Deficient dial-gage thermometers
     will not usually appear to be defective on simple visual inspection.

     20-6. The temperature may be determine on a sample of concrete, taken as described above, or directly in
     the mixer or transport vehicle. Submerge the tip of the thermometer at least 75 millimeters into the concrete
     and compact the concrete into any void around the thermometer to prevent air from affecting the reading.
     Allow at least 2 minutes for the temperature to stabilize. Record to the nearest 0.5.

SLUMP TEST

     20-7. When the mixture appears to have reached the desired consistency or when directed by project
     specifications, perform a slump test (ASTM C183). The method of testing covers the procedures to be used
     in the laboratory and in the field to determine concrete consistency, which is a characteristic of workability.
     It is not an exact method, but it gives sufficiently accurate results.

     20-8. Use the slump test to determine the consistency of a concrete mix by measuring the vertical distance
     that the concrete settles to the nearest 5 millimeters. Slump testing is a quick way to determine whether the
     concrete has been batched accurately or whether the concrete may be too old to be used in construction.

     20-9. The test is applicable to concretes with a slump over 13 millimeters, but under 225 millimeters.
     When slump is less than 13 millimeters, the material is no longer plastic and the test is invalidated, except
     to verify that a specified value has not been met. Slumps over 225 millimeters are more fluid than plastic,
     and again, the test does not accurately represent the concrete properties. However, most concretes designed
     for production work are designed to have a slump between 25 and 200 millimeters.

     20-10. Contractors usually push the upper end of slump limits because it makes the concrete easier to
     place. Owners usually prefer slumps in the lower range because they indicate higher strength.
     Specifications usually cite a slump value as a specific value with a tolerance, for example 100 millimeters
     ± 25 millimeters.

Equipment

     20-11. Use the following items to perform this a slump in a field or simulated field environment:
              A ruler.
              A scoop.
              A trowel.
              A water source.
              A level, flat, smooth surface made of nonabsorptive material.
              A slump cone with a tamping rod.
              Concrete mix datasheet as required in project specifications.

20-2  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                                      Field Concrete Testing

Steps

     20-12. Perform the following steps to determine the slump:
              Step 1. Moisten the inside of the slump cone; and place it on a rigid, flat, moist, and
                  nonabsorbent surface. Hold it in place during filling by standing on the two foot pieces.

             Note. From steps 2 to 10, a total time of no more than 2-1/2 minutes should elapse.

              Step 2. Fill the slump cone to one-third of its volume (about 70 millimeters high).
              Step 3. Rod the concrete by applying 25 evenly distributed strokes, penetrating the full depth of

                  the first layer in the slump cone.
              Step 4. Add a second layer of concrete to the slump cone until two-thirds of its volume is filled

                  (about 150 millimeters).
              Step 5. Rod the second layer in the same manner as the first, with the rod just penetrating the

                  underlying layer.
              Step 6. Add the third and last layer of concrete, overfilling if possible.
              Step 7. Rod the third layer, following the procedure in step 5. If the concrete height subsides

                  below the top of the cone, add additional concrete to keep it above the top of the mold.
              Step 8. Strike off the excess concrete with a screening and rolling motion of the tamping rod so

                  that the cone is completely filled.
              Step 9. Remove the slump cone from the concrete.

                   Step 9a. Place hands on the handles, and press downward.
                   Step 9b. Step off the footholds.
                   Step 9c. Raise the cone carefully and quickly in a vertical direction. Raise the cone a

                        distance of 12 inches within 3 to 7 seconds (5 ± 2), using a steady, upward lift with no
                        lateral or twisting motion.
                   Step 9d. Place the cone directly beside the slumped concrete. At this point, about 2
                        1/2 minutes should have elapsed since the start of filling in step 2.
              Step 10. Measure and record the slump immediately (figure 20-2).
                   Step 10a. Place the tamping rod along the top of the cone so that it projects over the
                        concrete.
                   Step 10b. Measure the slump with a ruler, from the bottom of the rod to the top center of the
                        concrete.
                   Step 10c. Record the slump to the nearest 5 millimeters.

              Figure 20-2. Measuring the slump of fresh concrete

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034   20-3


---

Chapter 20

Supplementary Test Procedure

     20-13. After completing the slump test, gently tap the side of the specimen with the tamping rod. The
     behavior of the concrete under this treatment is a valuable indication of the cohesiveness, workability, and
     placeability of the mix. A well-proportioned, workable mixture will gradually slump to lower elevations
     and retain its original identity. A poor mix will crumble, segregate, and fall apart. Slump is usually
     indicated in the project specifications as a range (such as 2 to 4 inches) or as a maximum value not to be
     exceeded. Concretes may be designed to have slumps that range from 1 to about 8 inches, depending on the
     application. When it is not specified, an approximate value can be selected from the list in table 20-1.

                   Table 20-1. Recommended slumps for various types of construction

            Types of Construction                                 Slump (in inches)
                                                              Maximum1 Minimum

            Reinforced foundation walls and footings          3                                 1

            Plain footings, caissons, and substructure walls  3                                 1

            Beams and reinforced walls                        4                                 1

            Pavements and slabs                               4                                 1

            Mass concrete                                     2                                 1

            Pumped concrete                                   8                                 5

            1 May be increased 1 inch for consolidation by hand methods (rodding and spading).

AIR CONTENT TEST

     20-14. ASTM C231 measures total air and is affected by any air voids in the aggregate, which is not
     useful air for solving the problems for which air entraining is intended. If aggregates have significant air
     voids, a correction factor must be determined so that the air content measured only reflects the air in the
     cement paste fraction of the concrete.

     20-15. The air measured in fresh concrete is not necessarily the same air content that will exist in the
     hardened concrete. Air can be lost during transportation and consolidation. It is often specified that air
     content be measured for acceptance testing purposes at the site of placement.

     20-16. ASTM C231 describes two types of air meters. The two meter types are differentiated by the type
     of measuring hardware on top. Type A meters have both a pressure gage and a transparent standpipe with
     calibrated markings that allow the user to measure the level of water in the instrument under different
     pressure loads. Type A meters are rare in the industry today. Type B meters have only a single dial-type
     pressure meter, marked in units of percent air. The meter may be on a tall standpipe, but it will not have a
     mechanism for viewing water levels (no window and measuring scale).

     20-17. There are many different brands and versions of Type B air meters currently fielded, and
     replacements of old equipment may not be the same in details of operation. For this reason, it is
     recommended that the steps outlined in the manufacturer’s user manual be followed. They are general steps
     of a typical determination using a Type B meter, including calibration and determination of the aggregate
     correction factor.

Equipment

     20-18. Use the following items to perform the air content test in a field or simulated field environment:
              An air entrainment meter with 5 percent calibration vessel and instructions for calibration.
              A trowel.
              A tamping rod that is 15 millimeters in diameter and about 600 millimeters long, with a rounded
                  ends or a concrete vibrator.
              A sample of plastic (fresh) concrete.
              Water.

20-4        TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034                                      3 April 2015


---

                                                                          Field Concrete Testing

 Oil.
 Rags.
 A pail.
 A kitchen scoop.
 A rubber mallet.

Steps

     20-19. Measure the air content according to the following steps.

Step 1. Prepare the Sample

     20-20. Secure a sample of fresh concrete that is sufficient to fill the bowl of the apparatus. If the concrete
     contains aggregate larger than 50 millimeters, sieve the material using a 37.5-millimeter sieve to remove
     the large aggregate. Do not try to remove the adhering paste from the aggregate in the sieve and add it to
     the sieved material.

Step 2. Prepare and Fill the Bowl

     20-21. Dampen the interior of the measuring bowl; and place the bowl on a flat, level, rigid surface. Place
     concrete in the bowl and consolidate according to the procedures for rodding or vibrating consolidation.
     Only use rodding for concretes with a slump greater than 75 millimeters. Rod or vibrate concretes with a
     slump of 25 to 75 millimeters. Use only vibration for concretes with a slump less than 25 millimeters.

Step 3. Consolidation

20-22.        Consolidate by rodding or vibrating.

              Step 3a. Rodding procedure. Place concrete in the measuring bowl in three approximately
               equal layers. The final layer should slightly overfill the container. Consolidate each layer by
              rodding 25 times over the surface. When rodding the first layer, avoid forcibly striking the
               bottom of the bowl. When rodding the second and third layers, penetrate the underlying layer by
               about 25 millimeters. After rodding each layer, tap the sides of the bowl sharply with a rubber
               mallet to close any voids left by the rod and to remove any air entrapped by the placing process.

               Step 3b. Vibrating procedure. Place the concrete in the measuring bowl in two approximately
               equal layers. The second layer should slightly overfill the container. Consolidate each layer by
               inserting a vibrator at three locations over the surface. Cease vibrating when the surface of the
               layer is smooth and large air bubbles cease to come out. Do not vibrate to the point of frothing.
               Do not let the vibrator rest on the bottom or sides of the bowl during vibration.

Step 4. Strike Off

     20-23. Strike off the excess concrete with a rigid straight edge (strike-off bar), using a sawing motion.
     Ideally, there will be about 3 millimeters of overfill material to strike off.

Step 5. Prepare for Test

     20-24. Clean the rim of the bowl and lid so that an airtight seal results when the lid is put on. Put the lid
     on and fasten it down. Close the valve between the air chamber and the bowl, and open the two petcocks on
     the top of the lid. Using a rubber syringe or bulb, inject water through one petcock until water flows out of
     the other petcock. Jar the apparatus, and continue injecting water until no air comes out of the second
     petcock.

Step 6. Conduct the Air Content Test

     20-25. Close the air bleeder valve on the air chamber, and pump air into the chamber until the needle on
     the pressure meter reaches the initial-pressure line. Allow a few seconds for the air to cool, and thump the
     gauge to ensure that the needle is working freely. Adjust the air with the pump or bleeder valve to get the

3 April 2015           TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  20-5


---

Chapter 20

     needle back on the initial reading line if it has drifted off. Check the petcocks for evidence of air leakage.
     Air leakage at this point would indicate a problem with the valve between the air chamber and the bowl,
     which would cause faulty readings.

     20-26. The initial-pressure line is constructed differently among brands of meters. Some have an
     adjustable line whose position is established during calibration. Others have a numbered scale called test
     mark below the zero reading. The number on this scale for the initial-pressure setting is determined during
     calibration.

     20-27. Close both petcocks on the lid, and open the valve between the air chamber and the bowl. Tap the
     sides of the bowl gently with a rubber mallet. Read the percent air on the pressure gauge. Record this
     number. Subtract the aggregate correction factor, and report this as the air content.

Calibration

     20-28. The exact steps in calibration vary among brands, so the manufacturer’s user manual should be
     consulted for this step. Meters should be calibrated at least every 3 months.

Aggregate Correction Factor

     20-29. Aggregate correction factors range from a few tenths of a percent for dense and low absorptive
     aggregates to as much as 1 percent for low-density and highly absorptive aggregates. Correction factors
     should be established for all aggregates used in concrete, but these do not need to be repeated with great
     frequency. Correction factors are normally relatively stable for a given source, but changes in density and
     absorption indicate the possible need for a check on the correction factor.

     20-30. Determine the aggregate correction factor (percent) according to the following steps.

Step 1. Make a Sample

     20-31. Assemble a sample of coarse and fine aggregate that represents the amounts in a sample of
     concrete used for an air content test. These are calculated from the following formulas:

                                                         𝐹s = (𝑆/𝐵) × 𝐹b
                                                          𝐶s = (𝑆/𝐵) × 𝐶b
     where—
     Fs = weight of fine aggregate in concrete sample under test (in kilograms or pounds)
     S = volume of concrete sample (same as volume of bowl) (in cubic millimeters or cubic feet)
     B = volume of concrete in a batch
     Fb = total weight of fine aggregate in the moisture condition used in a batch (in kilograms or pounds)
     Cs = weight of coarse aggregate in concrete sample under test (in kilograms or pounds)
     Cb = total weight of coarse aggregate in the moisture condition used in batch (in kilograms or pounds)

             Note. Use mass units of kilograms or pounds, but be consistent throughout the calculation.

Step 2. Place Aggregate in the Bowl

     20-32. Mix samples of fine aggregate and coarse aggregate, and place in the measuring bowl filled about
     one-third full of water. Prevent the entrapment of air by putting in small amounts of aggregate at a time.
     Add additional water to completely cover the aggregate if needed. Stir and rod the aggregate sample, and
     tap the sides of the bowl with a rubber mallet to remove any entrapped air. Remove foam, and let the
     aggregate stand in water for approximately the same time that would elapse in the making and testing of a
     normal batch of concrete.

Step 3. Conduct the Test Procedure

     20-33. Completely fill the bowl with water, and execute an air determination as described above for a
     complete concrete sample. The measured air content is the aggregate correction factor, and the number is
     subtracted from the air content determination made on a sample of concrete.

20-6  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                                      Field Concrete Testing

FLEXURAL-STRENGTH TEST (MODULUS OF RUPTURE)

     20-34. ASTM C78 covers the determination of flexural strength. The flexural strength of hardened
     concrete is measured by using a simple concrete beam and third-point loading mechanism. The flexural
     strength is determined by calculating measured breaks of the beam and is expressed as a modulus of rupture
     in megapascals (pounds per square inch). Perform this test to determine the flexural strength (modulus of
     rupture) of the test specimen to the nearest 0.1 megapascals or 10 pounds per square inch. Record the
     specimen identification, modulus of rupture, any defects noted, and specimen’s age. Measurements may be
     in metric or English units. If it is not practical to be consistent, appropriate conversions to one system or the
     other will be required before final calculations are made.

TEST BEAMS

     20-35. Test beams may be cast in suitable molds or sawed from hardened concrete. ASTM C192 covers
     the fabrication and handling of beams in a laboratory setting, ASTM C31 covers the fabrication and
     handling of beams in field conditions, and ASTM C42 covers the sawing beams from hardened concrete.
     Beam molds for casting test beams from fresh concrete are available in many sizes. The most commonly
     used size is 150 by 150 by 530 millimeters. Limits on specimen size are—

              The smallest beam dimension is at least three times the maximum aggregate size.
              The length of the beam is at least 50 millimeters longer than three times the depth.
              The ratio of beam width to depth is less than or equal to 1.5.

Samples

     20-36. Make test specimens at least once for each 100 cubic yards (or fraction thereof) for each class of
     concrete placed in any 1 day or as directed in the project specifications. Make at least three specimens for
     each test age and mixture design being evaluated in the laboratory. Additional specimens may be made for
     future testing. Test ages are normally 14 and 28 days for flexural-strength tests. For testing field-placed
     concrete, a minimum of two specimens for each test age is required.

Specimens

     20-37. The steps for making cast test specimens are discussed the following paragraphs.

Step 1. Location of Specimen Molding

     20-38. Mold specimens on a flat, rigid surface that is free of vibration and other disturbances at a place as
     near as practicable to the place that they will be stored during the first 24 hours. If specimens must be
     moved or if it is desired that specimens be put into the curing room the same day as they are molded, move
     them to the place of storage as soon as possible after molding. Avoid vibrations, jarring, and other physical
     disturbances as much as possible.

Step 2. Placing and Consolidating Concrete in Molds

     20-39. Assemble the concrete beam mold and lightly oil the inside. Fill the mold with two layers of
     concrete from the production batches, each about 3 inches deep. Consolidation is accomplished by rodding
     or by internal vibration. Internal vibration is required when the slump is less than 25 millimeters. Rodding
     or internal vibration is allowed when slump is greater than 25 millimeters.

Step 3. Rodding Option

     20-40. Consolidate each layer by rodding, using one stroke per 2 square inches (63 per layer), evenly
     distributed over the layer’s surface. Tap the sides lightly 10 to 15 times with a rubber mallet to close the
     voids left by rodding. Lightly spade the concrete along the mold’s sides with a trowel to help remove
     surface voids. When rodding the second layer, penetrate the first layer about 1/2 inch. Strike off the top
     surface with a straightedge, and finish it with a wood or magnesium float.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  20-7


---

Chapter 20

Step 4. Internal Vibration Option
     20-41. Vibrate each of the two layers at four equally spaced locations along the specimen centerline.
     Insert the vibrator slowly, and do not allow the tip to touch the mold. Vibrate at each location for 5 to
     10 seconds. The shorter time is suitable for concrete with a slump over 3 inches, while lower slump mixes
     should be vibrated for longer periods, but not usually more than 10 seconds. Normally, enough vibration
     has been applied when the surface becomes relatively smooth and large air bubbles cease to break through
     the surface.

Step 5. Strike Off and Finishing
     20-42. After consolidation, strike off excess material and finish the test according to any special
     instructions in the project specifications. If there are no special finishing instructions, finish with a wood or
     magnesium float.

Curing Beams

     20-43. Leave the specimens in the molds for the first 16 to 24 hours after molding. Do not move the
     specimens during this period. Cover the exposed surface with wet burlap or plastic to prevent drying; or if
     specimens are in a curing room, do not allow water to drip on the specimen surface. After the initial curing
     period, specimens may be moved and demolded. Keep the specimens wet in a curing tank or fog room after
     demolding. Standard curing temperature is 23 ± 2°C. Curing tanks contain saturated lime water, and
     specimens are totally submerged. Specimens protruding from the tank may suffer evaporation atmosphere
     and consequent drying and cooling.

Equipment

     20-44. Use the following items to perform this test in a laboratory environment:
              A calibrated load testing device.
              A concrete beam.
              A measuring tape.
              A stopwatch (if using a testing machine that does not control load rate)
              Safety goggles.
              A calculator.
              A 16-millimeter diameter tamping rod, if using the tamping form of consolidation.

Steps

     20-45. The steps for performing the flexural-strength tests are discussed in the following paragraphs.

                                                 WARNING

                                      Wear safety goggles throughout this test.

20-8  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                                      Field Concrete Testing

Step 1. Assemble the Test Appartus
     20-46. Assemble the test apparatus, and check for functional operation (figure 20-3).

              Legend:  distance
              d        inch
              in

              Figure 20-3. Apparatus for flexural-strength test

Step 2. Measure and Record the Length of Span
     20-47. Measure and record the length of span, in millimeters or inches, depending on the measuring
     device. The length of span is determined by measuring the distance from center to center of the two loading
     points (or supports) on the base of the apparatus. The normal length of specimens in the United States is
     530 millimeters and the normal length of span is 450 millimeters.

Step 3. Place the Specimen in the Load Tester
     20-48. Place the specimen in the load tester and bring the loading surface into contact with the test
     specimen.

Step 4. Zero the Gauge
     20-49. Zero the gauge. Some apparatuses are equipped with an automated hydraulic pump and
     corresponding gauge, while others are equipped with a loading jack and proving ring.

Step 5. Apply a Load
     20-50. Apply a load at a minimum continuous rate of 6,500 to 9,000 newtons or 1,500 to 2,100
     pounds/minute. The choice of units will depend on the instrument setup configuration.

Step 6. Record the Total Load at Failure
     20-51. Record the total load at failure in newtons or pounds, depending on the readout of the instrument.

Step 7. Determine and Record the Specimen Width and Depth
     20-52. Determine and record the width and depth of the specimen (in millimeters or inches) at the point of
     failure. Standard U.S. specimens are 150 by 150 millimeters.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  20-9


---

Chapter 20

Step 8. Determine the Point of Failure

20-53. Determine the point of failure in the specimen, and calculate the modulus of rupture. If the
specimen fails outside the middle third of the span length by more than 5 percent of the total span length,
the specimen is considered unusable and should be discarded (not more than 23 millimeters for 450-
millimeter span).

20-54. Use the following formula to calculate the modulus of rupture if the specimen fails within the

middle third of the span length:

where—                                                   𝑃×𝐿
                                                   𝑅 = 𝑏 × 𝑑2

R = modulus of rupture (in pounds per square inch or megapascals)

P = applied load (in pounds or newtons)

L = length of span (in millimeters or inches)

b = width of specimen at failure point (in millimeters or inches)

d = depth of specimen at failure point (in millimeters or inches)

20-55. Use the following formula to calculate the modulus of rupture if the specimen fails outside the

middle third of the length of span by not more than 5 percent of the span length:

where—                                                  3𝑃 × 𝑎
                                                   𝑅 = 𝑏 × 𝑑2

R = modulus of rupture (in megapascals or pounds per square inch)

P = applied load (in newtons or pounds)

b = width of specimen at failure point (in millimeters or inches)

d = depth of specimen at failure point (in millimeters or inches)

a = distance between the failure point and the nearest support, measured along the centerline of the bottom

       of the specimen (in millimeters or inches)

Step 9. Record Information

     20-56. Record the following information about the test (some information may be unavailable at the time
     of the test):

              Specimen identification number.
              Average width to the nearest millimeter or 0.05 inch.
              Average depth to the nearest millimeter or 0.05 inch.
              Span length (in millimeters or inches).
              Maximum applied load (in newtons or pounds).
              Modulus of rupture (to the nearest 0.1 megapascals or 10 pounds per square inch).
              Curing history (how the specimen was cured) and apparent moisture condition of the specimen

                  at the time of the test (surface wet or surface dry).
              Defects noted in the specimen.
              The age of the specimen (in days).

COMPRESSIVE-STRENGTH TEST

     20-57. The compressive strength of hardened concrete, as measured by compression tests on standard
     forms of cylindrical specimens, is used in the design of structures. ASTM C39 is the basic test method for
     determining strength of concrete. ASTM C42 covers preparation of test specimens from in place concrete.
     ASTM C192 covers preparation of specimens from laboratory concrete. Compressive-strength tests are
     made on concrete trial mixtures to evaluate the performance of available materials and to establish mixture
     proportions that give the required strength. Strength tests are also used to control the quality of concrete
     being manufactured in the field. Compressive strength is defined as the average of the strengths of all
     cylinders of the same age made from a sample taken from a single batch of concrete. At least two cylinders
     are required to constitute a test. Therefore, a minimum of four specimens are required if tests are to be

20-10       TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034                      3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                                      Field Concrete Testing

     made at 7 and 28 days. The test results will be the average of the strengths of the two specimens.
     Compressive strength is measured in units of megapascals or pounds per square inch.

CASTING A CONCRETE CYLINDER

     20-58. The standard test specimen is a cylinder that is 150 millimeters in diameter by 300 millimeters
     long. Smaller cylinders (100 by 200 millimeters) are also often used. The governing rule on cylinder size is
     that the diameter needs to be at least three times the maximum aggregate size. Length-to-diameter ratios
     should be 2:1. For testing, cylinders are capped with a suitable material to provide smooth, bearing surfaces
     on each end. Load is applied to the end surfaces through metal platens on the testing machine (cylinder
     breaker), causing compressive stress in the longitudinal direction of the cylinder.

     20-59. Fill the cylinders as near as possible to the place where they will be stored for the first 16 to
     48 hours. Sufficient concrete, about 70 kilograms for the desired number of cylinders, must be available in
     the trial mixture or field sample. Material from the air-content test must not be reused since it may be
     contaminated with excess water. Use appropriate sampling procedures for procuring the sample as
     described for fresh concrete testing.

Equipment

     20-60. Use the following items and information to perform the compressive-strength test in a field or
     simulated field environment (figure 20-4):

              A tamping rod, about 15 millimeters in diameter and 600 millimeters long, with a rounded end.
              Oil for releasing the forms (if using reusable molds)
              Rags.
              Cylinder molds (reusable or single use).
              A sheet of plastic or burlap.
              A kitchen scoop or trowel.
              A grease pencil or other permanent marking device (note that many permanent markers fade in

                  prolonged exposure to direct sunlight).
              Water for cleanup.
              A curing tank or room.
              Calipers that are capable of measuring at least 150 millimeters, to within 1 millimeter.
              A carpenter’s square that is at least 250 millimeters on one side and 150 millimeters on the other

                  side.

Figure 20-4. Hand-operated compressions tester (shown testing a concrete cylinder)

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  20-11


---

Chapter 20

Steps

     20-61. Perform the following steps to produce and label a concrete cylinder for testing:
              Step 1. Prepare the mold if using reusable molds. (Single-use molds (usually plastic) require no
                  preparation, but they should be inspected to ensure that they are not physically distorted.
                  Common distortions are of round at the top edge or convex bottom surfaces.)
                   Step 1a. Clean and dry the mold.
                   Step 1b. Oil reusable molds lightly.
                   Step 1c. Assemble the mold.
              Step 2. Make the cylinder.
                   Step 2a. Fill the mold one-third full with fresh concrete.
                   Step 2b. Consolidate the concrete by applying 25 evenly distributed strokes over the mold’s
                        surface area with the tamping rod. The tamping rod must totally penetrate the layer of
                        concrete.
                   Step 2c. Tap the side of the mold 8 to 10 times with the tamping rod.
                   Step 2c. Add concrete to the mold to fill it two-thirds full.
                   Step 2e. Apply 25 evenly distributed strokes to the mold’s surface area using the rounded
                        end of the tamping rod, which must pass entirely through the second layer of concrete and 1
                        inch into the preceding layer.
                   Step 2f. Tap the side of the mold 8 to 10 times with the tamping rod.
                   Step 2g. Add concrete to the mold to overfill it slightly.
                   Step 2h. Repeat step 2e. The tamping rod must pass entirely through the top layer and 1 inch
                        into the preceding layer.
                   Step 2i. Tap the side of the mold 8 to 10 times with the tamping rod.
                   Step 2j. Trowel off the concrete so that it is flush with the top of the mold and smoothly
                        finished.
              Step 3. Label the mold. The label should include, as a minimum, the following information:
                   Step 3a. The specimen number.
                   Step 3b. The date the cylinder was made.
                   Step 3c. The project or placement where the concrete was obtained.

             Note. The system of labeling is optional, but the specimen is labeled on the mold with a
             permanent label. Do not rely on a specimen lid label (if used).

              Step 4. Cover the cylinder with plastic or wet burlap to maintain moisture in the sample if a
                  tight-fitting lid is not used. The covering should be tight around the cylinder, but should not
                  make contact with the fresh concrete.

              Step 5. Allow the cylinder to cure undisturbed for 24 hours.
              Step 6. Remove the covering and the mold from the cylinder after 24 (plus 8) hours.
              Step 7. Label the concrete cylinder. The label itself may be transferred, or the information may

                  be recorded directly on the cylinder with a grease pencil or permanent marker.
              Step 8. Cure the cylinder as directed by the project requirements.

Specimens

     20-62. The number of specimens tested depends on the job specifications. If no requirement is listed in
     the specifications, a minimum of two will be molded for each test age for each 100 cubic yards, or fraction
     thereof, of each class of concrete placed in any 1 day. A third specimen may be taken to assist in
     determining when forms may be removed. The test specimens must remain on site and undisturbed for an
     initial curing period (the first 16 to 48 hours after molding). Normally, the test ages are 7 and 28 days for
     compressive-strength tests.

20-12  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                                      Field Concrete Testing

CURING AND STORING CYLINDERS

     20-63. After an initial curing period of 16 to 48 hours, remove specimens (from the jobsite) that are
     intended for checking the strength of laboratory trial mixtures or to serve as the basis for acceptance or
     quality control of field concrete. Take them to the testing laboratory and moist-cure them at 23 ± 2°C. Store
     them in moist rooms, in damp sand or sawdust, or in limewater to maintain free water on all surfaces of the
     specimen at all times. ASTM C511 covers moist rooms and curing tanks.

     20-64. Occasionally, test specimens are made in the field to determine when forms may be removed.
     Make these in addition to the specimens made for quality control or specification compliance testing. Give
     these specimens (as much as possible) the same protection from the elements on all surfaces as is given to
     the portions of the structure that they represent. Store them in or on the structure as near as possible to the
     point of use. Test them in the moist condition resulting from the specified curing treatment. Specimens
     intended for testing to determine when a structure may be put into use are removed from the molds at the
     same time the forms are removed from the structure.

     20-65. When shipping specimens from the field to the laboratory for testing, pack them in a sturdy
     wooden box or other suitable container, surrounded by wet sawdust, wet sand, or a closed plastic bag
     containing a small amount of water (a water-soaked rag is effective). Provide protection from freezing and
     physical damage during storage or shipment. Moist curing is continued when the specimens are received in
     the laboratory.

PREPARING TEST SPECIMENS FOR TESTING

     20-66. The preparation of test specimens is discussed in the following paragraphs.

Specimen Condition

     20-67. The physical dimensions and features of test specimens have important effects on strength
     measurements (ASTM C39). Important details are given in the following paragraphs.

     20-68. Measure (to the nearest 0.25 millimeter) the diameter at several points on the circumference, at
     midheight of the specimen. If any diameter is more than 2 percent different from any other diameter, do not
     test the specimen. This sometimes occurs when single-use molds become distorted into an oval shape
     during storage or handling. The average of this diameter, if within tolerance, is used to calculate the cross-
     sectional area of the specimen that is required input for final calculations.

     20-69. The ends of the specimens are required to be plane (no depressions and projections) to within
     0.050 millimeter. The ends of the test specimen must also be perpendicular to the axis of the cylinder
     within 0.5°. This angle is approximately equivalent to a 1.5-millimeter gap across a 150-millimeter
     diameter test specimen. This can be determined holding a carpenter’s square against the side of the
     specimen and measuring the maximum gap under the side of the carpenter’s square that crosses the end of
     the specimen.

     20-70. If specimens fail to meet the above requirements, they may be ground or sawed to achieve these
     tolerances or capped if the defects can be overcome with caps no thicker than 8 millimeters for concrete
     strengths less than or equal to 35 megapascals or 5 millimeters for concrete strengths between 35 and 50
     megapascals. Specimens for concretes stronger than 50 megapascals must be ground to the above
     tolerances. (See figure 20-5, page 20-14.)

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  20-13

                                        Square

                                                           Gap  Top as molded
                                                Long axis           Midpoint

                    End condition      Perpendicularity
            projections and planeness     to long axis

                                                                                                                                                   Bottom

                               Figure 20-5. Important specimen-condition features

Cylinder Capping

     20-71. If capping is required, the following methods are acceptable: capping with neat cement, high-
     strength gypsum, or sulfur (ASTM C617), or using unbonded caps (ASTM C1231). The basic requirement
     for capping materials is that they be stronger than the concrete to which they are applied. Ordinary plaster
     of paris, compressible rubber, or fibrous materials are not suitable for caps. Strength must be verified by
     fabricating and testing 50- by 50-millimeter cubes made of the capping material. During capping, protect
     moist-cured specimens against drying by covering them with wet burlap.

Neat Cement and High-Strength Gypsum

     20-72. Neat cement caps and high-strength gypsum caps are somewhat impractical options because of the
     difficulties in forming the material onto the ends of the test specimens within the tolerances of thickness,
     planeness, and perpendicularity to the axis of the cylinder. An advantage of neat cement caps is that
     portland cement is normally an easily obtained material, so no specialty products need to be procured. The
     strength of both materials must be higher than the concrete for which they are testing. This must be
     determined by qualification tests on the strength of the capping material using 50- by 50-millimeter cubes
     or 50- by 100-millimeter cylindrical specimens. Neat cement caps must also be cured for several days to
     gain sufficient strength to qualify as a capping material. A water-cement ratio of about 0.35 is usually
     sufficient to give adequate strength. High-strength gypsum will gain strength to levels of approximately
     5,000 pounds per square inch within 1 to 2 hours if water-gypsum ratios are kept in the range of 0.26 to
     0.30. Gypsum caps cannot be stored more than 4 hours in a moist room.

     20-73. Capping with sulfur is a commonly used technique in testing laboratories. Hardware (capping jig)
     is available to hold test specimens in rigid vertical positions while forming the cap, and sulfur sets very
     rapidly. Sulfur caps require at least 2 hours of curing time before testing. Molten sulfur must be heated to at
     least 129°C to ensure adequate strength development. Since the melting point of sulfur is only 113°C, the
     entire mass of capping material must be melted before it can be heated beyond the melting point to the
     required temperature. Ensure that sulfur vapors are not inhaled while heating the capping compound.
     Ensure that there is adequate ventilation and that respiratory protection is used. Used sulfur capping
     compound is a hazardous material and must be properly discarded.

20-14       TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034                  3 April 2015


---

                                                                 Field Concrete Testing

Unbonded Caps

     20-74. Unbonded caps are covered by ASTM C1231. The use of unbonded caps is a very practical option
     compared to the other capping procedures described above if equipment is locally available. When using
     unbonded caps, there are no capping materials that must be mixed, formed, and cured prior to determining
     strength. The following are potential disadvantages:

              The rubber pads must be replaced periodically, so a supply of these must be available.
              The caps must be qualified by comparing results with another capping method for strengths over

                  7,000 pounds per square inch.

     20-75. Unbonded caps are applicable for determining strengths greater than 1500 pounds per square inch
     and less than 12,000 pounds per square inch. Test-specimen condition requirements include the following:

              Diameter less than 2.0 percent variation.
              Planeness of ends less than or equal to 5 millimeters (gaps under straight edge).
              Perpendicular to axis of cylinder 0.5° (3 millimeters in 300 millimeters).
              Rings and pads must be certified for C1231/C1231M use (table 20-2). Rings and pads are sized

                  for 4- and 6-millimeter test specimens. Pad hardness must conform to the requirements in table
                  20-2.

                                   Table 20-2. Requirements for unbonded caps

DETERMINING COMPRESSIVE STRENGTH OF A CYLINDRICAL SPECIMEN

     20-76. Perform the compressive strength test to determine the compressive strength of the concrete
     cylinder to within 10 pounds per square inch. The form of the fracture pattern at failure gives information
     about possible errors in test conditions.

Equipment

     20-77. Use the following items to perform the compressive strength test in a well-ventilated laboratory:
              A cylindrical concrete test specimen.
              A load-testing machine, capable of measuring the largest load anticipated for the concrete under
                  test. Normally, a machine of at least 1,000 kilonewtons (about 200,000 pounds) is sufficient for
                  most applications using 150 by 300 millimeter test cylinders. A lower loading capacity is
                  suitable for smaller specimens.
              Heat-resistant gloves (if using sulfur caps).
              Capping compound materials and capping jigs (sulfur or cement caps) or unbonded cap devices
                  of the proper size for the test specimens.
              A ruler, readable to 0.25 millimeter.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034         20-15


---

Chapter 20

             Note. Conventional, commercially available rulers are usually readable to only 1 millimeter.
             Errors in strength determinations associated with measuring the diameter of a 150-millimeter test
             specimen to the nearest 0.25 millimeter is about ± 0.3 percent. Errors associated with
             measurements taken to the nearest 1 millimeter result in an error of about ± 1.3 percent.

              Calipers with an opening larger than the diameter of the largest test specimen.
              Safety goggles.
              A protective apron.
              A face shield.
              Oil (if using capping compound).
              Rags.
              A calculator.

Steps

     20-78. The following paragraphs discuss the steps to determine the concrete cylinder’s compressive
     strength.

Step 1. Determine and Record the Average Diameter

     20-79. Determine and record the average diameter of the concrete cylinder. The average diameter is the
     average of two diameters taken perpendicular to each other at midheight of the cylinder.

Step 2. Prepare the Concrete Cylinder

     20-80. Follow the procedures described below for each capping type used.
              Sulfur capping material.
                   Step 1. Completely melt the capping compound in the melting pot to a temperature of 130
                        to 145°C. Ensure that enough compound is melted to make several caps.
                   Step 2. Clean and lightly oil the baseplate of the capping apparatus.
                   Step 3. Set the baseplate into the stand of the capping-apparatus.
                   Step 4. Pour a small amount of the heated (liquid) capping compound into the baseplate.
                   Step 5. Position the cylinder at midheight against the backrest of the capping apparatus.
                        Slowly lower the cylinder into the baseplate while keeping the cylinder flush against the
                        backrest. If the cylinder is not kept flush with the backrest while capping, the caps and the
                        cylinder will not be perpendicular and a proper break will not occur.
                   Step 6. Remove the cylinder from the capping apparatus when the capping compound has
                        solidified.
                   Step 7. Inspect the cap for uniformity and defects. If any defects are seen, remove the cap,
                        recap the cylinder, and return to step 1.
                   Step 8. Repeat steps 1 through 7 for the uncapped end of the cylinder.
              Neat portland or hydraulic cement caps and high-strength gypsum caps.
                   Step 1. Mix the paste at the water-cement ratio used for the qualification of the material.

             Note. Forming the caps can be tricky (ASTM C617).

                   Step 3. Cure for the length of time required to get adequate strength, as determined in the
                        material qualification.

              Unbonded caps.
                   Step 1. Clean and examine the bearing surface of the steel cap for cracks or splits exceeding
                        10 millimeters in length. Replace them if they are in poor condition or if the maximum

20-16  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                                      Field Concrete Testing

                        number of serviceable uses has been exceeded. Place the steel cap firmly on the cylinder’s
                        ends.
                   Step 2. Center caps on the cylinder, and place the test specimen on the lower bearing block
                        of the testing machine. Carefully align the axis of the cylinder with the center of thrust of
                        the testing machine by centering the upper retaining ring on the spherically seated bearing
                        block.
                   Step 3. Rotate the moveable portion gently by hand, as the spherically seated block is
                        brought to bear on the upper retaining ring, so that uniform seating is obtained. After
                        application of less than 10 percent of the anticipated strength, ensure that the test specimen
                        is vertical and centered in the retaining rings. If the specimen is misaligned, release the load,
                        recenter, and reload.
                   Step 4. Complete the loading of the specimen as described in step 5.

Step 3. Prepare the Compression Tester
              Step 3a. Clean the tester’s bearing plates (loading surfaces).
              Step 3b. Check the tester for proper operation.
              Step 3c. Set the gauge to zero.

Step 4. Place the Capped Cylinder in the Compression Tester

     20-81. Place the capped cylinder into the compression tester, and center it on the bearing plates. Secure
     the protective cage around the cylinder.

Step 5. Apply the Test Load

     20-82. Apply the test load at a rate of 0.15 to 0.35 megapascals/second (20 to 50 pounds per square
     inch/second).

Step 6. Read the Gauge and Record the Load Applied

     20-83. Read the gauge, and record the load applied at the time of failure.

Step 7. Inspect the Broken Cylinder and Record Information

     20-84. Inspect the broken cylinder, and record the following information:
              Identification number.
              Diameter (in units of the measuring device [millimeters or inches])
              Cross-sectional area, in square inches, 𝐴 = π(d/2)2
                  where—
                  A = cross-sectional area
              Record the maximum load applied, in the units reported for the machine (in newtons or pounds).
              Compressive strength (calculated to the nearest 0.1 megapascal).
              Type of break (figure 20-6, page 20-18). The types of breaks are as follows:
                   Type A. Type A is expected under normal operating conditions.
                   Types B through E. Types B through E may indicate problems with adherence to test
                        procedures.
              Defects in specimen or caps.
              Age of specimen.

NONDESTRUCTIVE TESTING

     20-86. The portable seismic pavement analyzer (PSPA) is a portable, nondestructive device that rapidly
     measures the physical integrity of hydraulic-cement concrete and asphalt concrete pavements. All
     measurements are made on the pavement surface, but information on properties at depth can be derived
     from the output. Figure 20-7 shows two PSPA units and the portable computers that control the instruments
     and log the data.

                                        Figure 20-7. PSPA instruments

20-87. The principle of the PSPA operation depends on ultrasound. Ultrasound waves are transmitted into
the concrete through the rubber feet under the electronics box on the left side of the instrument. The waves
radiate down and out through the concrete from this point. The three cylindrical components mounted on
the extension arm to the right of the electronics box are the receivers that capture the ultrasound as reflected
and/or transmitted waves after they have moved through the concrete. The spacing of the three receivers
allows focusing data on various depths of the structure.

20-88. The output data gives information on the modulus of elasticity, which can be related to strength;
and it also detects irregularities in the pavement (layers of delamination, pavement thickness).

20-18       TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034                      3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                                Field Concrete Testing

20-89. Training in the operation of the PSPA takes about 1 day. Details of the operation are outside the
scope of this document, but are included with the manual that accompanies the instrument. If the manual is
missing, operational instructions can be found in ERDC/GSL SR-06-9.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  20-19
