                                                Chapter 21

        Contingency Field Testing and Troubleshooting

         Under some conditions, operations may require troubleshooting fresh and early
         hardened properties of concrete without the benefit of normal laboratory facilities.
         The purpose of this chapter is to present some approaches to isolating the causes of
         these problems, and to present some nonstandard procedures that can help gain some
         insight into problems with concrete in the event standard testing facilities are not
         available. These ad hoc test methods are not suitable for official acceptance testing
         purposes, but can help determine the source of some problems. The most common
         field problems that sometimes cause interruptions in operations include low strength,
         early loss in workability, and large irregularities in the time of setting, the loss of air,
         and early cracking. Sometimes, a combination of these will exist.

NONSTANDARD TESTS

     21-1. Nonstandard tests are as follows:

              Strength. It is difficult to measure strength numerically without the benefit of a load tester.
                  However, approximate conditions can be estimated by simply impacting concrete with a
                  hammer. Concrete with serious strength problems will crumble fairly easily under the impact of
                  a hammer, with paste and aggregate easily separating out. The concrete actually feels somewhat
                  soft under the impact. Of course, this is the condition that is expected in concrete in the first few
                  hours or first 24 hours after setting, but should not exist after several days of curing. Concrete
                  that has attained normal strength will normally require significantly more impact energy (unless
                  the impact is very close to an edge or corner, it is fairly easy the chip the edge off of concrete).
                  The concrete will feel brittle when fractured, and fractures will often break through the coarse
                  aggregate particles.

              Loss of workability. The loss of workability is normally evaluated in the field by measuring
                  changes in slump (ASTM C143) at intervals of about 15 minutes. In the absence of a slump
                  cone, relative behavior can be assessed with homemade slump cones (such as a small bucket
                  with the bottom cut out). A reasonable facsimile of a slump cone can be fabricated from sheet
                  metal. Data from these ad hoc devices will not correctly reflect executed slump tests, but
                  changes in properties can be reasonably assessed using such devices. In extreme cases, the loss
                  of workability can be assessed simply by trying to move the concrete around with a shovel.

              Time of setting. Standard laboratory equipment for measuring the time of setting according to
                  ASTM C403 is relatively complicated. However, this behavior can be assessed in the field with
                  simple ad hoc techniques. The time of initial setting normally corresponds to the time when the
                  paste in the concrete shows some perceptible resistance to penetration with a pointed stick
                  (pencil, ballpoint pen). It is also closely indicated by the loss of sheen on the surface of the
                  concrete. To see this behavior, cover the surface of the concrete to prevent evaporation of the
                  bleed water. As the concrete starts to set, it also starts to consume some or all of the bleed water
                  on the surface. Unless bleeding is excessive, all of the bleed water will be absorbed, leaving a
                  dull appearance on the surface. The time of final setting can be approximated as the point at
                  which there is significant resistance to penetration of a pointed stick with moderate force (for
                  example, the concrete can be walked on).

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  21-1


---

Chapter 21

              Bleeding. Bleeding is seldom routinely measured on concrete. However, sometimes questions
                  arise about the concrete mixture when conspicuous bleeding is seen in the field. The standard
                  test method is ASTM C232, which basically directs the user to fill a standard container, pour off
                  the bleed water as if forms, and report the total. A reasonable alternative is to use a 150- by 600-
                  millimeter strength mold or similar-size container. Fill it to the rim with concrete, screed off the
                  excess and cover to prevent evaporation. Check the sample over a period of about an hour. If
                  bleeding is more than 1 to 2 millimeters, bleeding may be too high. This simple approach is
                  useful for comparing among mixtures. Excessive bleeding is also often evidenced by the mounds
                  of paste deposited around small holes in the surface of the concrete. These are bleed channels
                  that form when concrete bleeds heavily, carrying cement fines to the surface.

              Air content. The two most commonly used standard methods for measuring air content are the
                  ASTM C231 (pressure method) and ASTM C173 (volumetric method). These both require
                  specific hardware to execute. However, air can be estimated in the field without these, but a
                  number of calculations are involved that require some materials property data, a container of
                  known volume, and a device for determining weight. Knowing the density and amount of each
                  of the main components of a concrete batch (cement, SCMs, aggregate, and water), a theoretical
                  weight of a given volume of concrete can be calculated. Then, when the concrete is actually
                  made, the amount to which the weight falls below the theoretical weight indicates the amount of
                  air in the concrete.

TROUBLESHOOTING

     21-2. Common field problems and troubleshooting approaches are discussed in the following paragraphs.

LOW STRENGTH

     21-3. Low strength in concrete can derive from one thing or a combination of several things: insufficient
     cement content, too much water, poor quality cement, weak aggregate, mixing-water chemistry,
     temperature, poor curing, and unexpected admixture effects.

     21-4. Low cement content sometimes occurs in concrete due to batching errors or fraud (cement is the
     most expensive component of concrete). Prebagged materials are not immune to this problem. Cement
     content is difficult to measure without laboratory facilities; however, where the cement content is very low,
     the mortar fraction of the concrete will tend to look like wet sand rather than smooth cement paste that
     tends to dominate the appearance. The mortar is also soft and easily dug out of the concrete with a knife or
     other metal object even after several days of curing.

     21-5. The addition of mixing water beyond what is allowed in the project specifications is probably the
     major cause of low strengths. This error is difficult to determine without experienced laboratory capability.
     This problem is particularly acute with concrete batched and hauled a significant distance to the placement
     site. Concrete more than about 1.5 hours old (less in very hot weather) tends to start setting up. Workability
     can be restored by the addition of more water. The addition of more than about 10 percent more water than
     allowed in the batch water will have significant effects on strength. The only field activity that can be used
     to detect this is to observe practices surrounding the delivery of concrete. Sometimes, adding water is
     allowed because some of the water was intentionally left out of the mix with the intention of adding it later.
     However, many delivery operators are barely aware that adding water may be forbidden or the practice is
     so common that they tend to ignore the rules on it. As a result, objectionable additions of water are often
     done quite openly. If batch tickets are available, these may be useful in determining if extra water should be
     allowed.

     21-6. Cement or SCMs are sometimes of inferior quality. In theory, these have both been tested at a
     competent laboratory before delivery and the test results included on a mill certificate. Unfortunately, not
     all mill certificates accurately represent the cement or SCM being delivered. Some insight into the
     contribution of cement to a strength problem can be gained if a sample of the cement can be obtained.
     Make a mortar of two parts sand and one part cement (by volume), and add just enough water to make a
     moderately stiff mortar (no immediate bleeding). If standard molds are not available, paper cups or other
     small containers can be used. Store the container in a moist condition as close to 23°C as practical.

21-2  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                  Contingency Field Testing and Troubleshooting

     Significant strength should be evident by 3 days. Most cements reach at least 10 megapascals by 3 days,
     and most reach about 25 megapascals (3500 pounds per square inch) by this age, although there will be
     some variation if ad hoc methods are used. Reasonable strength specimens should break with a hammer,
     but require at least moderate intensity being applied. The specimens may not be sharply brittle at this
     strength (paragraph 21-1). This brittleness might not be perceptible until about 28 days. Low strength
     materials will be soft, breading with low energy hammering.

     21-7. Weak aggregate can contribute to low strength, although low strengths from this cause are not
     usually extremely low. Weak aggregate is evidenced by many particles that are easily broken by hand or
     light mechanical pressure.

     21-8. Mixing water from industrial wastewater contaminated sources and from wash water around concrete
     mixing facilities can cause setting and strength-gain problems. Water from normally acceptable drinking
     water sources is fine. Most natural waters are also fine, but sometimes industrial contaminations aren’t
     known. Mixing water can be easily tested with ad hoc methods. Make mortars as described above for
     estimating cement strength. Make one batch with test water and another with drinking water. If strengths at
     3 days and setting times are reasonably close, then the mixing water probably is not a problem.

     21-9. The temperature has strong effects on the rate of strength gain. Below about 5°C strength gain is very
     slow, about half as fast as at ideal temperatures of around 25°C. Freezing concrete before it is about 3 days
     old can cause damage that results in low strengths. High temperatures accelerate strength gain even more
     and are not a problem, except that hot concretes tend to lose significant amounts of water due to
     evaporation, particularly under windy conditions. Many agencies limit concrete placing to temperatures
     between a low of 5°C and rising about 30°C due to early stiffening and drying concerns.

     21-10. Failure to provide adequate curing water can cause low strengths, particularly of the near surface
     concrete. In extreme cases, the concrete surface is easily abraded. Concrete rarely dries to a depth of more
     that about 50 millimeters due to poor curing. Surface cracking is another symptom of this kind of poor
     curing. Curing usually needs to be continued for at least 3 days unless the weather is wet enough that
     significant evaporation does not occur.

     21-11. Occasionally admixtures interact with specific cement sources and result in extremely slow
     strength gain. These problems are more common with WRAs than other chemical admixtures. It is a more
     common problem when admixtures are used at, or higher than, the maximum recommended dosages. This
     can be investigated using testing similar to the investigation of mixing water, paragraph 21-8, except make
     one batch with and one batch without admixture (or at a lower dose). The strength problem normally
     accompanies an extremely long setting time (many hours or even days).

EARLY LOSS OF WORKABILITY

     21-12. Early loss of workability is evidenced by problems with spreading and consolidating concrete.
     Slump measurements are useful in documenting this behavior. Slump that drops from the upper range of
     specified slump to below the minimum range in less than an hour is usually a problem. If concrete can be
     placed and consolidated before this loss of workability sets in, there will be no further problem with the
     concrete. Early loss in workability is relatively common in otherwise-acceptable concrete when
     temperatures of the concrete get much above 32°C. Other causes include cement, mixing-water chemistry,
     and admixture effects.

     21-13. Some cements exhibit a tendency to show a weak setting behavior within a few minutes after
     adding mixing water (called false setting). Although relatively weak, this stiffening is sufficient to interfere
     with emptying the truck, or spreading and consolidating concrete. This rarely happens if the concrete has
     been mixed for more than 2 to 3 minutes, so it is not a problem with transit-truck delivered concrete (as
     long as it is an agitating or revolving drum truck). The problem usually occurs when concrete is mixed on
     site. Times for on-site mixing are typically about 1 minute. Increasing the mixing time by a minute or two
     will work through this problem, and it will not cause further problems in the concrete.

     21-14. Admixture effects similar to those described for low-strength concrete problems can also cause
     early loss in workability. These can be investigated using the same concept as investigating strength
     problems (compare slump losses for concrete with different dosages of admixture and with no admixture).

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  21-3


---

Chapter 21

     21-15. Mixing-water chemistry problems sometimes cause cement to hydrate more rapidly than normal.
     This can be tested by comparing slump loss between concrete or mortar made with drinking water and with
     water.

SETTING-TIME PROBLEMS

     21-16. Setting time and early stiffening are often assumed to be different extremes of the same
     phenomenon. However, early stiffening is usually caused by a different chemical reaction than setting.
     Sometimes, concrete will have early loss of workability (as indicated by slump loss), but delayed time of
     setting. True setting does not usually occur until after 2 hours.

     21-17. Even though setting-time problems are caused by different chemical reactions than like
     workability loss problems, are caused by temperature, cement, mixing water, and admixtures. Isolating
     these problems follows the same logic as the workability loss problems, but the time of setting rather than
     slump loss is used as the metric.

EARLY AGE CRACKING

     21-18. Early-age cracking, for purposes of this discussion, is cracking that occurs during the first 24 hours
     after placing. One form of early age cracking occurs, or is at least initiated, before the concrete reaches the
     initial setting. This is called plastic shrinkage cracking. Another form of cracking occurs after time of
     setting, but usually about 24 hours, although this depends on the weather. This cracking is caused by drying
     of hardened concrete and by thermal effects, or a combination of the two.

     21-19. Plastic shrinkage cracking occurs because the rate of evaporation of bleed water exceeds the rate of
     formation of bleed water. This is difficult to anticipate. Concretes made with water-cement ratios below
     about 0.45 are particularly susceptible because of the relatively low bleed rate. This includes much paving
     concrete. This problem is particularly acute when conditions are windy and concrete temperatures are high.
     Air temperature and relative humidity also contribute, but to a lesser degree. The solution to this problem is
     to keep concrete as cool as possible and protect fresh concrete from too much wind or drying, which can be
     difficult in many construction environments. Much concrete is placed at night in strongly drying
     environments.

     21-20. After the time of setting, concrete continues to dry, although usually at a slower rate than before
     setting. This drying can contribute to cracking as early as a few hours after setting when curing is
     particularly poor, but also at much later ages with moderately good curing. Thick placements of concrete
     (thick pavements, large structures) tend to generate a significant amount of heat internally due to the
     hydration of the cement. This, along with any environmental heating, can cause the concrete to reach
     temperatures above 65°C during the first 24 hours. The concrete then cools after it has become rigid due to
     setting, so it tends to shrink and crack. This is normally dealt with by sawing joints into the concrete that
     direct the cracking to locations that can then be sealed and managed. Sawing too late or at intervals that are
     too wide is the most common cause of random cracks during or soon after setting.

     21-21. The practice of curing with wet burlap (or other absorbent material) in dry windy climates
     (particularly deserts) can cause cracking. The rate of evaporation from the wet absorbent materials can be
     high enough to cause significant evaporative cooling, resulting in a significant temperature drop in the
     concrete surface. If the rest of the concrete is hot, a sufficient temperature gradient can develop over the
     thickness of the structure to result in cracking. This can be prevented by covering the absorbent material
     with plastic.

21-4  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                           PART FOUR

                    Soils Stabilization

Part Four presents criteria and tests for improving the structural quality and
workability of soils used for base courses, subbase courses, select materials, and
subgrades for pavements. It is applicable to all elements responsible for Army, Air
Force, or Navy pavement construction.

                                                Chapter 22

        Mechanics of Soil Stabilization and Modification

         Stabilized soils can often be adequate for airfields, traffic pavements, and parking and
         storage areas where an all-weather surface is required, yet traffic does not justify a
         higher-strength pavement. Surface treatments are also used to provide dust control.
         The most widely recognized form of stabilization is compaction, which improves the
         mechanical stability of virtually any soil. However, compaction alone is often not
         enough.

STABILIZATION

     22-1. Stabilization is the process of blending and mixing materials with a soil to improve the strength and
     durability of the soil. The process may include blending soils to achieve a desired gradation or mixing
     commercially available additives that may alter the gradation, change the strength and durability, or act as a
     binder to cement the soil (TM 3-34.64/MCRP 3-17.7G and TM 5-822-14/AFJMAN 32-1019).

STABILIZATION USES

     22-2. Pavement design is based on the premise that specified levels of quality will be achieved for each
     soil layer in the pavement system. Each layer must—

              Resist shearing within the layer.
              Avoid excessive elastic deflections that would result in fatigue cracking within the layer or in

                  overlying layers.
              Prevent excessive permanent deformation through densification.

     22-3. As the quality of a soil layer is increased, the ability of that layer to distribute the load over a greater
     area is generally increased enough to permit a reduction in the required thickness of the soil and surface
     layers.

Improve Soil Quality

     22-4. Stabilization is commonly used for better soil gradation, the reduction of the PI or swelling potential,
     and increased durability and strength. Stabilized pavement surfaces can be used for many common
     applications (roads, airfield facilities, construction platforms). Stabilized pavements generally do not
     provide the durability of asphalt or concrete surfacing.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034                      22-1


---

Chapter 22

Reduce Surface Pavement Thickness

     22-5. A soil layer’s tensile strength and stiffness can be improved by using additives and can, thereby,
     reduce the thickness of the stabilized layer and overlying layers within the pavement system. Procedures
     for designing pavements that use stabilized soils are presented in TM 5-822-5/AFM 88-7. Before a
     stabilized layer can be used to reduce the required thickness in the design of a pavement system, the
     stabilized material must meet the durability requirements of various types of additive stabilization and the
     minimum strength requirements. Generally, as the percent of fines and the PI increase, pulverization
     becomes more difficult and it is harder to obtain uniform distribution of the stabilizing additive. For these
     types of soils, preprocessing or pretreatment with other additives may be necessary. For example, fine-
     grained soils may be pretreated with lime to aid in their pulverization, making the mixing of a bitumen or
     cement additive more successful.

STABILIZATION METHODS

     22-6. The two general stabilization methods are mechanical and additive. The effectiveness of stabilization
     depends on the ability to obtain uniformity in blending the various materials. Mixing in a stationary or
     traveling plant is preferred. However, other means of mixing (scarifiers, plows, disks, graders, rotary
     mixers, reclaimers/stabilizers) have been satisfactory.

     22-7. The soil-stabilization method is determined by the amount of stabilizing required and the conditions
     encountered on the project. An accurate soil description and classification are essential for selecting the
     correct materials and procedures. TM 5-822-14/AFJMAN 32-1019 lists the most suitable treatments for
     various soil types to stabilize these soils for different objectives and contains detailed descriptions of proper
     construction techniques.

Mechanical

     22-8. Mechanical stabilization is accomplished by mixing or blending two or more gradations of material
     to obtain a mixture meeting the required specifications. The blending of these materials may take place at
     the construction site, at a central plant, or at a borrow area. The blended material is then spread and
     compacted to the required densities by conventional means. If, after blending these materials, the mixture
     does not meet the specifications, stabilization with an additive may be necessary. Compaction is also a
     form of mechanical stabilization.

Additive

     22-9. Additive refers to a manufactured commercial product that, when added to the soil in the proper
     quantities, will improve the quality of the soil layer. Additives may stabilize the soil by chemically,
     physically or a combination of the two (physicochemically). An example of pure chemical stabilizers are
     lime, acids, and enzymes that cause a chemical change in the soil (ion exchange, covalent reactions). An
     example of pure physical stabilizers are bitumens that bind the soil by adhesion and fibers that bind by
     mechanical friction and interpenetration. Physicochemical stabilizers (lignosulfonates, lime-cement-fly ash
     [LCF]) may bind soil particles together, but also induce chemical changes in the soil (pozzolonic reactions).
     Selecting and determining the percentage of additives depend on the soil classification and the degree of
     improvement in the soil quality desired. Smaller amounts of additives are usually required to alter soil
     properties (gradation, workability, plasticity) than to improve the strength and durability sufficiently to
     permit a thickness reduction design.

MODIFICATION

     22-10. Modification refers to the stabilization process that results in the improvement in some property of
     the soil, but does not (by design), result in a significant increase in the strength and durability of the soil.

22-2  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                            Mechanics of Soil Stabilization and Modification

22-11. Soil modification usually results in something less than a thoroughly cemented, hardened, or
semihardened material. This type of stabilization may be accomplished by—

        Compacting.
        Blending mechanically.
        Adding cement in small amounts.
        Adding chemical modifiers.

22-12. Cement and lime modifiers (cement-modified and lime-modified soils) are used in quantities too
small to provide high-strength cementing action. They reduce the plasticity of clay soils. Calcium chloride
or sodium chloride are added to the soil to retain moisture, control dust, hold fine material for better
compaction, and reduce frost heave by lowering the freezing point of water in the soil. Bituminous or
polymer emulsion may be used to waterproof the surface of the soil and to control dust.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  22-3


---

This page intentionally left blank.


---

                                                Chapter 23

                             Stabilizing Agents

         This chapter provides a method for determining the type or types of stabilizers and
         the amount of stabilizer to be used with a particular soil. It also considers the
         stabilization of soils with lime, cement, fly ash, bituminous materials, and
         nontraditional stabilizers.

STABILIZER TYPES

     23-1. To select the proper stabilizer for a particular soil, perform a sieve analysis test to determine
     gradation and fines content and an Atterberg limits test according to the procedures given in this manual.

TRADITIONAL STABILIZERS

     23-2. Traditional stabilizers are discussed in the following paragraphs.

Cement

     23-3. Portland cement can be used to modify and improve the quality of the soil or to transform the soil
     into a cemented mass with increased strength and durability.

     23-4. Cement can be used effectively as a stabilizer for a wide range of materials; however, the soil should
     have a PI less than 30. For coarse-grained soils, the amount passing the No. 4 sieve should be greater than
     45 percent. The amount of cement used depends on whether the soil is to be modified or stabilized.

Lime

     23-5. Experience shows that lime will react with many medium-, moderately fine-, and fine-grained soils
     to produce decreased plasticity, increased workability, reduced swell, and increased strength. Soils
     classified according to the USCS as CH, CL, MH, ML, OH, OL, SC, SM, GC, GM, SW-SC, SP-SC, SM-
     SC, GWGC, GP-GC, ML-CL, and GM-GC should be considered as potentially capable of being stabilized
     with lime. Lime should be considered with all soils having a PI greater than 10 if more than 25 percent of
     the soil passes the No. 200 sieve.

Fly Ash

     23-6. Fly ash, when mixed with lime, can be used effectively to stabilize most coarse- and medium-grained
     soils; however, the PI should not be greater than 25. Soils classified by the USCS as SW, SP, SP-SC, SW-
     SC, SW-SM, GW, GP, GP-GC, GW-GC, GP-GM, GW-GM, GC-GM, and SC-SM can be stabilized with
     fly ash. Some sources of fly ash may contain high amounts of sulfates. In some soils, this may lead to the
     formation of ettringite, which may swell excessively.

Bituminous

     23-7. Most bituminous soil stabilization has been performed with AC, cutback asphalt, and asphalt
     emulsions. Soils that can be stabilized effectively with bituminous materials usually contain less than 30
     percent passing the No. 200 sieve and have a PI less than 10. Soils classified by the USCS as SW, SP, SW-
     SM, SP-SM, SW-SC, SP-SC, SM, SC, SM-SC, GW, GP, SW-GM, SP-GM, SW-GC, GP-GC, GM, GC,
     and GM-GC can be effectively stabilized with bituminous materials if gradation and plasticity requirements
     are met.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  23-1


---

Chapter 23

Combination

     23-8. Combination stabilization is specifically defined as lime-cement, lime-asphalt, and LCF stabilization.
     Combinations of lime and cement are often acceptable expedient stabilizers. Lime can be added to the soil
     to increase the workability and mixing characteristics and to reduce the plasticity of the soil. Cement can
     then be mixed into the soil to provide rapid strength gain. Combinations of lime and asphalt are often
     acceptable stabilizers. The lime addition may prevent stripping at the asphalt-aggregate interface and
     increase the mixture’s stability.

NONTRADITIONAL STABILIZERS

     23-9. Nontraditional stabilizers are discussed in the following paragraphs.

Polymers

     23-10. Polymer emulsions (sometimes referred to as latex) used for soil stabilization are generally vinyl
     acetate or acrylic-based polymers suspended in water by surfactants. They typically consist of 45 to
     50 percent polymer content by weight. Analogous to latex paint, the emulsion cures by water loss. The
     polymers used for soil stabilization typically have excellent tensile and flexural strength, adhesion to soil
     particles, and resistance to water. These materials are often limited by a short shelf life (less than 2 years)
     due to bacterial growth and settling. Polymer emulsions should not be mixed with gray or salt water for
     dilution.

Fibers

     23-11. There are numerous fiber types available. For soil stabilization, a combination of availability, fiber
     properties, and cost per performance, pares the fiber type to polypropylene. These fibers are low-cost, are
     available in different lengths and diameters, and have been extensively tested. Monofilament, fibrillated,
     and tape fibers are available. The testing has shown that monofilament and tape fibers are more efficient for
     rapid stabilization. Fibrillated fibers may require extensive mixing to effectively defibrillate the fiber for
     maximum efficiency.

     23-12. The most important fiber property for soil stabilization is fiber length. Longer fibers generally
     result in improved performance ,but hamper construction. The longer fibers are more difficult to mix, and
     considerable balling may occur, diminishing efficiency. Testing of different fiber lengths has shown that
     fiber lengths from 3/4 to 1 1/2 inches result in good soil improvements with minimal balling.

     23-13. The soil type has a secondary role in fiber selection. Polypropylene fibers mixed into soil without
     adhesives (cement) rely exclusive on mechanical interlock, friction, and fiber entanglement between soil
     particles to anchor the fiber in place. Thus, a high surface area fiber (such as a tape fiber) may be more
     efficient in a fine-grained clay soil than in a coarse-grained soil (such as gravel) where fewer soil grains are
     in contact with the fiber.

Other

     23-14. There are a variety of other nontraditional soil stabilization and modification additives available
     (acids, lignin derivatives, enzymes, tree resin emulsions, silicates). These additives vary widely in their
     chemical composition and mechanism of action, may be in liquid or solid form, and are often touted to be
     applicable for most soils. Research studies in this area have demonstrated that many nontraditional soil
     additives have little to no benefit for granular soil types. Sandy and gravelly soils are often problematic for
     stabilization, requiring a binder (cement, asphalt emulsion) to provide cohesion between soil grains.
     Medium- and fine-grained soils are likely to benefit most from the other nontraditional stabilizers.
     However, these must be considered on a case-by-case basis.

Combinations

     23-15. Blends of soil with polymer emulsions and hydraulic cements and with fibers and hydraulic
     cements have shown promise in laboratory testing and field demonstrations. A synergism exists between

23-2  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                 Stabilizing Agents

emulsions and hydraulic cement as the water in the emulsion hydrates the cement and the polymer adds
flexibility, durability, and strain resistance to the soil cement, increasing the cracking resistance. The use of
fibers is also very attractive due to their ability to improve the resistance of soil to deformation and resist
the tensile stresses that lead to cracking. Fibers have been found to be more efficient than polymer
emulsion on a weight-to-weight basis of comparison. However, fibers may be difficult to use since they
tend to ball up when graded and they may be difficult to place in windy situations.

TIME REQUIREMENTS FOR TESTING

     23-16. The more thorough a testing program, the more assurance there is for the long-term success of the
     project. Time is often of primary concern to a military engineer—particularly, in a tactical situation—and
     the rapid completion of a project may override the requirement for a complete series of laboratory tests
     (table 23-1). Because of this, the method presented allows for a rapid or expedient approximation along
     with a more precise laboratory determination of the type and quantity of stabilizer.

                            Table 23-1. Estimated time required for test procedures

              Construction  Stabilizing     Time
                   Type        Agent     Required1

              Expedient     Any          None

                            Lime         30 days

                            LCF          30 days

              Nonexpedient Cement        6 to 9 days

                            Bitumen      1 day

                            Nontraditional 1 to 9 days

              1These criteria do not include time required for
              gradation.

              Legend:
              LCF lime-cement-fly ash

STABLIZER SELECTION AND CONCENTRATIONS

     23-17. When selecting a stabilizer additive, many factors must be considered. These factors, the design
     criteria, and the selection and mixing of stabilizers can be found in TM 5-822-14/AFJMAN 32-1019 and
     FM 5-430-00-2/AFJPAM 32-8013/MCRP 3-17.7B, Volume II. If lime is used as a preliminary additive to
     reduce the PI or to alter the soil gradation before adding the primary stabilizing agent (bitumen, cement),
     the design’s lime content is the minimum treatment level that will achieve the desired results. For
     nonplastic and low PI materials in which lime alone is not satisfactory for stabilization, adding fly ash may
     produce the necessary reaction. The lime used for soil stabilization is also used to determine lime
     requirements in the potential hydrogen (pH) test.

     23-18. Several factors need to be considered in the selection of a stabilizer. The environmental conditions,
     the availability and cost of stabilizers, logistical considerations, the equipment available, the soil type, and
     the strength and durability needed for the desired application. The selection of the stabilizer will be based
     on modified charts and tables from TM 5-822-14/AFJMAN 32-1019. In general, the higher the amount of
     fines in the soil, the harder it is to stabilize. Thus, clays can be difficult to stabilize. Lime may be needed to
     reduce the plasticity and increase friability of high-plasticity clay before cement is added. For sandy and
     gravelly soils, some type of binding agent (cement or emulsion) is necessary to adhere the particles
     together. The selection of a stabilizer begins with a classification of the soil type (figure 23-1, page 23-4).
     Once the soil is classified according to figure 23-1; table 23-2, page 23-4, is used to make an initial
     selection of appropriate stabilizers for the soil type and table 23-3, page 23-5, is utilized to choose an
     appropriate stabilizer dosage level.

23-19. The environmental conditions present at the stabilization site may need special consideration. In
particular, the temperature will be the most critical factor that influences the curing time. When
temperatures are below 45°F, curing times will be significantly decreased. In general, for every 20°F
temperature decrease, curing time doubles. Thus, for 3 percent Type III cement, 1 day of cure time is
needed at 75°F, 2 days would be needed at 55°F. The change in curing time may be offset by increasing the
stabilizer concentration from 3 to 6 percent for a 1 day curing time at 55°F (13°C).

23-20. The amounts of stabilizers used vary with the particular soil type and the expected durability
needed for the mission. In TM 5-822-14/AFJMAN 32-1019, stabilizer amounts are determined by specific
tests and criteria. Given the constraints of a rapid deployment scenario, there is not enough time to perform
mix design tests with various stabilizers and amounts to determine if those specific criteria are met. If time
allows, it is always best to perform a strict mix design according to TM 5-822-14/AFJMAN 32-1019.
However, the suggested stabilizer amounts given in table 23-3 should provide acceptable performance.

EQUIPMENT

     23-21. Use the following items for the pH test:
              A pH meter (the meter must be equipped with an electrode having a pH range of 14).
              150-milliliter (or larger) plastic bottles with screw-top lids.
              Distilled water, free of carbon dioxide.
              A balance.
              An oven.

23-6             TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034             3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                                             Stabilizing Agents

STEPS

     23-22. Perform the following steps to determine the pH:
              Step 1. Standardize the pH meter with a buffer solution having a pH of 12.45.
              Step 2. Weigh, to the nearest 0.01 gram, representative samples of oven-dried soil passing the
                  No. 40 sieve equal to 20.0 grams. Air-dried soil may be used if an oven is not available. In either
                  case, the soil should be dried until the weight is constant, indicating that additional water is not
                  evaporating.
              Step 3. Pour the soil samples into the 150-milliliter plastic bottles with screw-top lids.
              Step 4. Add varying percentages of lime, weighed to the nearest 0.01 gram, to the soils. (Lime
                  percentages of 0, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, and 10—based on the dry soil weight—may be used.)
              Step 5. Mix the soil and lime thoroughly.
              Step 6. Add 100 milliliters of the distilled water to the soil-lime mixtures.
              Step 7. Shake the soil-lime-water mixture for at least 30 seconds or until there is no evidence of
                  dry material on the bottom of the bottle.
              Step 8. Shake the bottles for 30 seconds every 10 minutes.
              Step 9. Wait 1 hour, transfer part of the slurry, to a plastic beaker, and measure the pH.
              Step 10. Record the pH for each of the soil-lime mixtures. The lowest percent of lime giving a
                  pH of 12.40 is the percent required to stabilize the soil. If the pH does not reach 12.40, the
                  minimum lime content giving the highest pH is required to stabilize the soil.

EXPEDIENT STABILIZATION

     23-23. In many cases, the military engineer needs soil improvement quickly in the theater of operations
     with minimal logistics and stabilization material. In this case, the best stabilizer of choice is cement.
     However, common portland cement (Type I) is not a rapid-cure material and may take up to 7 days to
     provide enough strength to supporting large loads (such as cargo aircraft). It also provides only limited
     durability and generally fails by cracking and surface raveling, which may pose a danger to aircraft due to
     foreign object debris (FOD). Cement is also extremely heavy due to its high specific gravity, placing a
     further logistical burden on the military planner. Using lower amounts of cement than typically called for
     (TM 5-822-14/AFJMAN 32-1019) reduces the logistical burden at the expense of durability. For expedient
     and temporary structures, suggested amounts of cement for common soils are given in table 23-3. Many of
     these concerns about cement can be reduced by incorporating monofilament polypropylene fibers into the
     soil cement mix and protecting the soil cement surface by waterproofing. It has been found that using
     monofilament fibers between 19 and 51 millimeters can—

              Increase the load-bearing capacity so that large loads can be handled in 3 days (sometimes less,
                  depending on temperature).

              Reduce FOD potential by bonding to the soil cement and creating a mat-like composite.
              Be used with high early strength cement (Type III) so that large loads (C-130 and C-17 aircraft)

                  can be placed on the structure within 24 hours of construction.

     23-24. Construction with fibers is simple and is conducted in similar fashion to cement stabilization.
     Simply calculate the correct spacing of the fiber bags to achieve the desired concentration of fiber in the
     same fashion as with cement. The use of a reclaimer/stabilizer for soil mixing is mandatory. Fiber
     concentrations of 0.1 to 0.5 percent based on the weight of soil have shown to be successful. For most soils,
     fiber concentrations above 0.5 percent have demonstrated decreased density compared to lower fiber
     contents, which may decrease durability. Stabilization depths of 150 millimeters are recommended and can
     be used for soils above CBR 8 to 10 percent. The stabilization of more than one lift is typically not practical
     in expedient situations. A balance between fiber length and constructability must be maintained. Longer
     fibers improve soil engineering characteristics to a larger degree, but are harder to construct with due to
     balling and tearing out of the soil during grading. It has been found that the grading of soil-fiber-cement is
     extremely difficult and should only be attempted if absolutely necessary and within an hour or two after
     compaction. If an area is recognized to need smoothing during compaction, stop compaction, grade it, and
     recompact it. Typically, the fiber balls in front of the grader blade and rips out of the soil, leaving large

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  23-7


---

Chapter 23

     scars and tears in the surface. A rule of thumb is that the surface started with is the surface ended with. In
     practice, this is best accomplished by preparing, compacting, and grading the surface to final elevation
     immediately prior to stabilization.

     23-25. The measurement of density for fiber-stabilized materials can be accomplished in the laboratory
     but is not possible in the field. Nuclear density devices tend to give false readings due to the fiber, and sand
     cone measurements give large errors due the problems excavating the fiber-stabilized soil. If laboratory
     measurements of density can be determined, the highest fiber content that produces equal density to a non-
     fiber-stabilized specimen should be chosen.

     23-26. If no laboratory data is available for optimum water content, the rules of thumb for cement
     stabilization are:

              For cement amounts less than 5 percent, use a water content 1 percent above the optimum for the
                  unstabilized soil.

              For cement contents above 5 percent, use a water content 2 percent above the optimum.

     23-27. Generally, it is best to compact cement-stabilized soil slightly on the wet-side of optimum to
     provide extra water for hydration of the cement.

     23-28. For the low-stabilizer amounts, it is absolutely necessary to cap the surface of the soil with a
     waterproofing agent (such as polymer emulsion) designed for soil stabilization. This prevents water from
     entering the stabilized soil matrix and reducing the strength. It also helps prevent fiber pull out from the
     surface and provides dust control. The minimum rate of application is suggested at 0.25 gallons per square
     yard at a 1:3 dilution ratio of polymer emulsion: water, and it must be reapplied, as necessary, depending
     on traffic. For airfield structures, application rates as high as 1 gallon per square yard may be used if the
     soil will accept that application rate. Bituminous emulsions can also be used, but polymer emulsions are
     much more durable under traffic. Synthetic fluids used for dust control should not be applied to cement-
     stabilized surfaces because they have been found to reduce the strength and durability.

     23-29. If logistics allow, stabilization according to TM 5-822-14/AFJMAN 32-1019 is recommended.
     However, if testing facilities are not available, the high dosage rates for the suggested amounts of
     stabilizers can be used if logistics allow and the structure may become a longer term use facility. Fibers can
     be used with any of the stabilizers. For any stabilized soil to be used as a pavement surface, it should be
     treated, as necessary, with polymer or bituminous emulsion to extend durability.

SOIL STABILIZATION IN FROST AREAS

     23-30. While bitumens, portland cement, lime, and combinations of LCF stabilization are the most
     common additives, other stabilizers may be used for pavement construction in areas of frost design, but
     only with approval from—

              Headquarters, Department of the Army (DAEN-MPE-D), Washington, D.C. 20314 (for Army
                  projects).

              Headquarters, Air Force Engineering and Services Center, Tyndall Air Force Base, FL 32401
                  (for Air Force projects).

              Headquarters, Naval Facilities Engineering Command, Alexandria, VA 22332 (for Navy
                  projects).

LIMITATIONS

     23-31. In frost areas, stabilized soil should be used only in a layer or layers comprising one of the upper
     elements of a pavement system and directly beneath the pavement’s surfacing layer. The structural
     advantage in reducing the required thickness of the pavement system compensates for the added cost of
     stabilization. Treatment with a lower degree of chemical stabilization should be used in frost areas with
     caution and only after intensive tests, because weakly cemented material usually has less capacity to endure
     repeated freezing and thawing than firmly cemented material. A possible exception is using a low level of
     stabilization to improve a soil that will be encapsulated within an impervious envelope as part of a
     membrane-encapsulated soil layer pavement system. A soil that is unsuitable for encapsulation due to

23-8  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                                             Stabilizing Agents

     excessive moisture migration and thaw weakening may be made suitable for such use by a moderate
     amount of a stabilizing additive. Materials that are modified by a small amount of a chemical additive to
     improve certain properties of the soil without significant cementation should also be tested to determine
     that the desired improvement is durable through repeated freeze-thaw cycles. The improvement should not
     be achieved at the expense of making the soil more susceptible to ice segregation.

CONSTRUCTION CUTOFF

     23-32. For materials stabilized with cement, lime, or LCF, whose strength increases with curing time, it is
     essential that the stabilized layer be constructed sufficiently early in the season to allow the adequate
     strength to develop before the first freezing cycle begins. The rate of strength gain is substantially lower at
     50°F than at 70 or 80°F. Chemical reactions will not occur rapidly for lime-stabilized soils when the soil
     temperature is less than 60°F and is not expected to increase for 1 month or for cement-stabilized soils
     when the soil temperature is less than 40°F and is not expected to increase for 1 month. In frost areas, it is
     not always sufficient to protect the mixture from freezing during a 7-day curing period as required by the
     applicable guide specifications. A construction cutoff date well in advance of the onset of freezing
     conditions may be essential.

WEATHER

     23-33. Hot, dry weather is preferred for all types of bituminous stabilization. When ACs are used for
     stabilization, proper compaction must be obtained. If thin lifts of asphalt-stabilized material are being
     placed, the air temperature should be 40°F and rising and compaction should be commenced immediately
     after lay down operations. Adequate compaction may not be obtained at freezing temperatures if thick lifts
     are used. When cutbacks and emulsions are used, the air and soil temperatures should be above freezing.
     Heavy rains on mixed, uncompacted material may be detrimental.

DRYING OF WET SOILS

     23-34. The military engineer who must execute earthwork under wet conditions faces one of the perennial
     challenges of engineering. The proper compaction and engineering properties of soils highly depend on
     moisture content, yet nature seldom provides natural materials at the ideal moisture content. Experience
     soon teaches the field military engineer that attempting to subvert the fundamental laws that describe the
     effect of moisture content on construction and engineering properties of soils and aggregates leads only to
     frustration and failure. These wet soils may be due to temporary weather conditions (thawing of frozen
     soils, rainfall) to permanent situations (high water table). One may tackle the drying of such soils by
     conventional methods of spreading, discing, and the like, but the results are likely to be slower and more
     weather-dependent than military operations can tolerate.

     23-35. When faced with the necessity of construction with soils that are far wetter than desired, the
     military engineer may opt for chemical drying to deal with isolated wet spots or, if necessary, more
     extensive areas of wet soils.

     23-36. The chemicals of choice for drying are oxides of calcium and magnesium, generally referred to as
     quicklime. Depending on the source, they may be further defined as high-calcium quicklime, magnesium
     quicklime, or dolomitic quicklime. All are adequate for the drying task at hand. The oxides (CaO) hydrate
     with water and chemically bind water in the soil into a new form of hydrated lime (CaO∙[OH]2). This
     reaction releases considerable heat (is exothermic) which, in turn, evaporates more water and further
     reduces the moisture content of the soil. Because this reaction is very avid and exothermic, it is effective
     for drying soils down to freezing temperatures.

     23-37. Lime is a generic term used to cover a variety of materials. Only quicklime is effective for drying
     soils. Hydrated lime is already combined with water and will not draw moisture into new chemical forms. It
     will have certain chemical reactions (cation exchange) with some clay minerals that will make them less
     plastic and easier to dry by conventional methods, but this is much different from the avid drying of
     quicklime that is not related to soil type. Calcium carbonate (CaCO3) is sometimes marketed as agricultural
     lime and has no value for engineering stabilization or drying uses.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  23-9


---

Chapter 23

     23-38. Any product that contains free quicklime (CaO) can be used as a drying agent. The more CaO it
     contains, the more effective it will be as a drying agent. Portland cement, fly ash (particularly high-calcium,
     Class C ashes), and kiln dust are examples of common industrial materials with significant CaO content
     that can be used for soil drying. Their effectiveness for drying directly depends on their free CaO content.

PICK-AND-CLICK TESTS

     23-39. Specimens covering a wide range of cement contents are molded at optimum moisture and
     maximum density. After 36 hours or more of hardening while kept moist and after a 3-hour soaking period,
     the specimens are inspected by picking with a pointed instrument (a dull ice pick, bayonet) and by sharply
     clicking each specimen against a hard object (concrete, a sound specimen) to determine their relative
     hardness when set. If the specimen cannot be penetrated more than 1/8 to 1/4 inch by picking and if it
     produces a clear or solid tone upon clicking, an adequate cement factor (CF) is indicated. When a dull thud
     or plunky sound is obtained, there is inadequate cement even though the specimen may resist picking.

     23-40. The specimen’s age is a factor, and a specimen that may not test properly at first may harden
     properly a few days later. Some satisfactory specimens require 7 days or longer to produce adequate
     hardening. The test results will indicate the proper content. If the results show that some intermediate
     content may be satisfactory, new test specimens (at the suggested content) should be prepared and tested. It
     is important to remember that too much cement is not harmful (although more expensive), but too little
     cement will not produce a satisfactory stabilization.

WET-DRY AND FREEZE-THAW TESTS

     23-41. After determining the maximum density and OMC, mold the specimens for the wet-dry and freeze-
     thaw tests. The test procedures are also given in TM 5-822-14/AFJMAN 32-1019.

     23-42. Prepare the specimens using the computed OMC and the cement contents previously described for
     the different soil classifications. Select the cement contents in 2 percent increments on each side of the
     median value. Mold two specimens for each of the three cement contents—one for the wet-dry test and one
     for the freeze-thaw test. Use the same procedure to mold the specimens as used for the OMC determination.
     Take special care to scarify the surfaces between layers to ensure a good bond. When the second layer is
     being placed, take a 750-gram sample for a moisture determination. Place the molded specimens in a
     moisture cabinet (or in an atmosphere of high humidity) for 7 days to permit cement hydration before
     testing.

WET-DRY TEST PROCEDURES

     23-43. After the 7-day curing period, submerge the specimens in tap water at room temperature for 5
     hours and remove them. Dry the specimens in an oven at 160°F for 42 hours, and then remove them. Wire-
     brush the entire surface area to remove material loosened during wetting and drying. Use two firm strokes
     on each portion of the surface. Apply these strokes the full height and width, with a 3-pound force. One
     cycle consists of 5 hours of water immersion, 42 hours of drying, and 1 hour of handling. Repeat the
     operation for a total of 12 cycles. After 12 cycles of the test, dry the specimens to a constant weight at
     230°F, and weigh them to determine the oven-dried weights.

FREEZE-THAW TEST PROCEDURES

     23-44. After the curing period, place water-saturated felt pads (about 1/4 inch thick) blotters, or similar
     absorptive materials between the specimens and specimen carriers. Place the assembly in a freezing cabinet
     with a constant temperature no warmer than -10°F for 24 hours, and then remove them. Allow the assembly
     to thaw in a moist room or in suitable covered containers with a temperature of 70°F and a relative
     humidity of 100 percent for 23 hours. Make free water available to the absorbent pads to permit the
     specimens to absorb water by capillarity during the thawing period. Give the specimens two firm strokes on
     all areas with the wire brush to remove material loosened during freezing and thawing. If necessary, use a
     sharp-pointed instrument to remove scale that has formed. One cycle consists of 24 hours of freezing, 23
     hours of thawing, and 1 hour of handling (total 48 hours). After brushing at the end of each thawing period,

23-10  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                     Stabilizing Agents

turn the specimens over, end for end, before replacing them on the water-saturated pads. Continue the test
for a total of 12 cycles, dry the specimens to a constant weight at 230°F, and weigh them to determine the
oven-dried weights.

CALCULATIONS AND CRITERIA

     23-45. The results of the wet-dry and freeze-thaw cycles are indicated as soil-cement losses. These losses
     are computed by using the original dry weights and final corrected dry weights.

Water-of-Hydration Correction

23-46. The final oven-dried weight of the specimen includes some water used for cement hydration that
cannot be at 230°F. The average amount of water retained in the specimen is based on the type of soil—
gravels, plus 1 1/2; sand, plus 2 1/2 percent; silt, plus 3 percent; and clays, plus 3 1/2 percent. This
correction is computed by using the following formula:

𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑡𝑒𝑑 𝑜𝑣𝑒𝑛 − 𝑑𝑟𝑖𝑒𝑑 𝑤𝑒𝑖𝑔ℎ𝑡 = �  𝑚𝑒𝑎𝑠𝑢𝑟𝑒𝑑 𝑜𝑣𝑒𝑛 − 𝑑𝑟𝑖𝑒𝑑 𝑠𝑝𝑒𝑐𝑖𝑚𝑒𝑛 𝑤𝑒𝑖𝑔ℎ𝑡  , 100� 100

                                   𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑐𝑒𝑛𝑡 𝑜𝑓 𝑤𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑟 𝑟𝑒𝑡𝑎𝑖𝑛𝑒𝑑

Example. A sample composed mostly of sand weighs 3.77 pounds at the end of the test. The
water-of-hydration correction is 2.5 percent.

                                                                         3.77
                         𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑡𝑒𝑑 𝑜𝑣𝑒𝑛 − 𝑑𝑟𝑖𝑒𝑑 𝑤𝑒𝑖𝑔ℎ𝑡 = � 2.5 , 100� 100

Soil-Cement Loss

23-47. The soil-cement loss can now be calculated as a percentage of the original dry weight or by using

the following formula:

𝑠𝑜𝑖𝑙 𝑐𝑒𝑚𝑒𝑛𝑡 𝑙𝑜𝑠𝑠 = �    𝑜𝑟𝑖𝑔𝑖𝑛𝑎𝑙 𝑜𝑣𝑒𝑛-𝑑𝑟𝑖𝑒𝑑 𝑤𝑒𝑖𝑔ℎ𝑡 − 𝑓𝑖𝑛𝑎𝑙 𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑡𝑒𝑑 𝑜𝑣𝑒𝑛-𝑑𝑟𝑖𝑒𝑑 𝑤𝑒𝑖𝑔ℎ𝑡  � 100

                               𝑜𝑟𝑖𝑔𝑖𝑛𝑎𝑙 𝑜𝑣𝑒𝑛-𝑑𝑟𝑖𝑒𝑑 𝑤𝑒𝑖𝑔ℎ𝑡

Example. A sample of soil has an original weight of 3.99 pounds. This is found by using the
following formula:

                                                             3.99 − 3.68
                     𝑠𝑜𝑖𝑙 𝑐𝑒𝑚𝑒𝑛𝑡 𝑙𝑜𝑠𝑠 = �𝑜𝑟𝑖𝑔𝑖𝑛𝑎𝑙 𝑜𝑣𝑒𝑛-𝑑𝑟𝑖𝑒𝑑 𝑤𝑒𝑖𝑔ℎ𝑡� 100

This value would be reported to the nearest whole number or as 8 percent.

Weight Loss Criteria

     23-48. The minimum cement content recommended for use is the one for which losses of specimen
     weight during 12 cycles of the wet-dry test or freeze-thaw test conform to the following standards:

              GW, GM, GC, SW, SM, SC, and SP soils—not over 14 percent.
              ML and MH soils—not over 10 percent.
              GL, CH, OH, and OL soils—not over 7 percent.

Strength Criteria

     23-49. The strength of soil-cement specimens tested in compression at various ages should increase with
     age and with increases in cement. The range of cement contents should produce results meeting the above
     requirements. A sample that has an unconfined compression strength of about 300 pounds per square inch
     after curing 7 days and shows increasing strength with age can be considered adequately stabilized. Fibers
     tend to reduce strength slightly, compared to a specimens with no fibers.

Cement Weight-to-Volume Conversion

23-50. The required cement content by weight can be converted to the equivalent cement content by
volume for control during construction since this may be the easier quantity to use in the field. The
following formula illustrates the calculation:

                                      𝑣𝑜𝑙𝑢𝑚𝑒 𝑜𝑓 𝑐𝑒𝑚𝑒𝑛𝑡 (𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑐𝑒𝑛𝑡) =  𝐷 − �𝐷𝐶�

where—                                                                     100
D = oven-dried density of soil-cement (pounds per cubic foot)
                                                                       94
     100 + % 𝑐𝑒𝑚𝑒𝑛𝑡 𝑏𝑦 𝑤𝑒𝑖𝑔ℎ𝑡
𝐶 = 100

23-51. The nomograph in figure 23-1, page 23-4, makes the conversion without computation. Use a
straightedge placed at the soil-cement density and at the percent by weight of cement. Read the percent of
cement by volume on the right-hand scale.

MODIFIED MIX DESIGN FOR SANDY SOILS

     23-52. Sandy soils are usually the most readily and economically stabilized because they require the least
     amount of cement for adequate hardening, and they contain a minimum amount of material that prevents
     intimate mixing of soil and cement. The following shortcut testing procedures for sandy soils will not
     always indicate the minimum cement contents required, but it is economical and the results will be close
     enough to be on the safe side. If time permits, the procedures for the freeze-thaw test are followed for
     greater design economy.

     23-53. The two procedures used are for—
              Soils with no material retained on the No. 4 sieve.
              Soils with material retained on the No. 4 sieve.

     23-54. These procedures can be used only with soils containing less than 50 percent of material smaller
     than 0.05 millimeter (silt and clay) and less than 20 percent smaller than 0.005 millimeter (clay). Dark gray
     to black, sandy soils obviously contain appreciable organic impurities together with miscellaneous granular
     materials (cinders, caliche, chat, chart, marl, red dog, scoria, shale, slag) should be tested using the full
     procedures for sandy soils versus the modified methods. When coarse-grained or sandy soils (usually GW,
     GP, GM, SW, or SM) are encountered, they may be classified for testing purposes using the first or the
     second procedure. There is one other exception: granular soils with materials retained on the No. 4 sieve
     whose bulk specific gravity is less than 2.45 cannot be tested.

     23-55. Perform the following steps to modify the mix design for sandy soils:
              Step 1. Determine the soil gradation.
              Step 2. Determine the bulk specific gravity of the material retained on the No. 4 sieve.
              Step 3. Perform the moisture density test of an estimated soil-cement mixture.
              Step 4. Locate the indicated cement requirements from the charts (figure 23-2).

                                                Appendix B

                  Unified Soil Classification System

         The adoption of the principles of soil mechanics has inspired numerous attempts to
         devise a simple classification system that identifies the properties of specific soils.
         Many classifications have been established based on texture, plasticity, strength, and
         other soil characteristics. Some classification systems have gained acceptance, but
         seldom has a system provided the entire amount of soil information that is needed by
         engineers. Almost every engineer who practices soil mechanics is required to modify
         the classification system that is being used by incorporating individual judgment and
         personal experience. When designs and plans are reviewed by persons who are
         uninvolved with the task, a common system of soil classification is necessary to
         convey the behavior and characteristics of the materials that are related to the project.
         The USCS is based on identifying soils according to their textural and plasticity
         qualities and on their grouping with respect to behavior. Soils rarely exist separately
         as sand, gravel, or any other single component. They are usually mixtures that
         contain varying proportions of particles of different sizes. Each component
         contributes its characteristics to the soil mixture. The USCS is based on the soil
         characteristics that indicate how the soil will perform as an engineering construction
         material. The properties that form the basis of soil identification include—

          • The percentage of gravel, sand, and fines that pass the No. 200 sieve.

          • The shape of the grain size distribution curve.

          • The plasticity and compressibility characteristics. (In the USCS, the soil is given a
                descriptive name and a letter symbol that indicates the principal characteristics.)

         This appendix describes various soil groups and discusses the methods of
         identification so that a uniform classification procedure can be followed. The
         placement of soils into their respective groups is accomplished by visual
         examinations and laboratory tests. The USCS may not be entirely adequate in all
         cases. It is a flexible system that should not be blindly followed, nor should it be
         regarded as being completely rigid.

SOIL COMPONENT DEFINITIONS

     B-1. The limiting boundaries between the various size ranges have been arbitrarily set at the U.S. standard
     sieve sizes that are listed. In the finest soil component (below the No. 200 sieve), silt and clay are used
     respectively to distinguish materials that exhibit lower plasticity from those with higher plasticity. The -200
     sieve material is silt if the LL and PI plot below the A line on the plasticity chart. If the LL and PI plot
     above the A line on the chart, the material is clay. (LL and PL tests are based on -40 sieve fraction of a
     soil.) The preceding definition is applicable for inorganic silts and clays and for organic silts, but it is not
     valid for organic clays (organic clays plot below the A line). The names of basic soil components can be
     used as nouns or adjectives when they are used to describe or classify soils.

     B-2. Before soils can be properly classified in a system, it is necessary to establish a basic terminology for
     the various soil components and to define the terms that are used. In the USCS, cobbles, gravel, sand, and
     fines (silt or clay) are used to designate the size ranges of soil particles. Gravel and sand ranges are further
     subdivided into groups (table B-1, page B-2).

PROCEDURES

     B-3. Classification system procedures apply to the identification of soils, regardless of the intended
     engineering uses. Table B-2 assists in identifying the symbols and soil descriptions within this system.
     Columns 1 through 5 identify the three major divisions of the classification system and the group symbols
     that distinguish the individual soil types. The names of the typical and representative soil types that are
     found in each group are shown in Column 6.

     B-4. Figure B-1, page B-5, demonstrates the schematic method of classifying soils from the results of
     laboratory tests.

SOIL GROUPS AND SYMBOLS

     B-5. Soils are primarily identified as coarse-grained, fine-grained, and highly organic. On a textural basis,
     coarse-grained soils have 50 percent or more by weight of the overall soil sample retained on the No. 200
     sieve; fine-grained soils are those that have more than 50 percent by weight passing the No. 200 sieve.
     Highly organic soils are usually easily identified by visual examination due to the presence of fibrous
     matter (wood, roots, leaves.) Coarse-grained soils are subdivided into gravel and gravelly soils and sand
     and sandy soils. Fine-grained soils are subdivided on their LL and plasticity properties. The symbol L is
     used for soils with LLs of 50 and less, and the symbol H is used for soils with LLs in excess of 50. Peat and
     other highly organic soils are designated by the symbol Pt and are not subdivided. There is typically no
     clear-cut boundary between gravelly soils and sandy soils, and as far as behavior is concerned, the exact
     point of division is relatively unimportant.
