COARSE-GRAINED SOILS

     B-6. For identification purposes, coarse-grained soils are classified as G if the greater percentage of the
     coarse fraction (that which is retained on the No. 200 sieve) is larger than the No. 4 sieve. They are classed
     as S if the greater portion of the coarse fraction is finer than the No. 4 sieve. In the following paragraphs,
     GW, GP, SW, and SP soils are defined as having less than 5 percent passing the No. 200 sieve. Soils that
     have between 5 and 12 percent passing the No. 200 sieve are considered borderline. These soils are
     subdivided as—

              GW and SW. GW and SW groups comprise well-graded gravelly and sandy soils that have little
                  or no nonplastic fines (less than 5 percent passing the No. 200 sieve). If the fines exhibit
                  plasticity, this information should be evaluated and the soil classified and discussed in laboratory
                  identification. In areas subject to frost action, the material should not contain more than 3
                  percent of soil grains smaller than 0.02 millimeters.

              GP and SP. Poorly graded gravels and sands that contain little or no nonplastic fines (less than 5
                  percent passing the No. 200 sieve) are classed in the GP and SP groups. The materials may be
                  classed as uniform gravels, uniform sands, or nonuniform mixtures of very coarse material and
                  very fine sand, with intermediate sizes lacking (sometimes called skip-graded, gap-graded, or
                  step-graded). The latter group often results from borrow excavation in which gravel and sand
                  layers are mixed. If the fine fraction exhibits plasticity, this information should be evaluated and
                  the soil classified under laboratory identification.

              GM and SM. GM and SM groups usually comprise gravels or sands with fines (more than 12
                  percent passing the No. 200 sieve) having low or no plasticity. The PI and LL of soils in the
                  group should plot below the A line on the plasticity chart. The gradation of materials (including
                  well-graded and poorly graded materials) is not considered significant. Some of the sands and
                  gravels in this group contain a binder that is composed of natural cementing agents which are
                  proportioned so that the mixture shows negligible swelling or shrinkage. The dry strength of the
                  materials is provided by a small amount of soil binder or by cementation of calcareous material
                  or iron oxide. The fine fraction of other materials in the GM and SM groups may be composed
                  of silts or rock flour that have little or no plasticity, and the mixture will exhibit no dry strength.

              GC and SC. GC and SC groups comprise gravelly or sandy soils with fines (more than 12
                  percent passing the No. 200 sieve) which have low or high plasticity. The PI and LL of soils
                  should plot above the A line on the plasticity chart. The gradation of the materials (including
                  well-graded and poorly graded materials) is not considered significant. The plasticity of the
                  binder fraction has more influence on the soil behavior than the variation in gradation. The fine
                  fraction is typically composed of clays.

              Borderline soils. Borderline soils may be classified as belonging to G and S groups. They are
                  divided into the following secondary groups:

                   Well-graded material with little or no fines (symbol W, groups GW and SW).

                   Poorly graded material with little or no fines (symbol P, groups GP and SP).

                   Coarse material with nonplastic fines or fines with low plasticity (symbol M, groups GM
                        and SM).

                   Coarse material with plastic fines (symbol C, groups GC and SC).

B-6         TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034     3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                      Unified Soil Classification System

Fine-Grained Soils

     B-7. Fine-grained soils are subdivided into groups based on whether they have a low or high LL. These
     two groups are subdivided as—

              ML and MH. The symbol M is used in ML and MH groups to designate predominantly silty
                  materials and micaceous or diatomaceous soils. The symbols L and H represent low and high
                  LLs, respectively, and an arbitrary dividing line between the two is set at an LL of 50. Soils in
                  the ML and MH groups are sandy silts, clayey silts, inorganic silts with relatively low plasticity,
                  loess soils, and rock flours. Micaceous and diatomaceous soils usually fall within the MH group,
                  but they may extend into the ML group when their LL is less than 50. The same is true for
                  certain types of kaolin clays and for some elite clays that have relatively low plasticity.

              CL and CH. The symbol C in the CL and CH groups stands for clay. L and H denote low or
                  high LL. These soils are primarily inorganic clays. Low-plasticity clays are classified as CL and
                  are usually lean, sandy, or silty clays. Medium- and high-plasticity clays are classified as CH.
                  CH clays include fat clays, gumbo clays, certain volcanic clays, and bentonite. Glacial clays of
                  the northern United States cover a wide portion of the CL and CH groups.

              OL and OH. Soils in the OL and OH groups are characterized by the presence of organic
                  matter. Organic silts and clays are classified in these groups. The materials have a plasticity
                  range that corresponds with the ML and MH groups.

              Highly organic soils. Highly organic soils are usually very compressible and have undesirable
                  construction characteristics. They are classified into one group and are designated by the symbol
                  Pt. Peat, humus, and swamp soils with a highly organic texture are typical soils of this group.
                  Particles of leaves, grass, branches, or other fibrous vegetable matter are common components of
                  the soils.

SOIL GROUP IDENTIFICATION

     B-8. The USCS is arranged so that most soils can be classified into at least the three primary groups of
     coarse-grained, fine-grained, and highly organic by visual examination and simple field tests. Classification
     into the subdivisions can also be made by visual examination with some degree of success. More positive
     identification can be made through laboratory testing. However, in many instances, a tentative
     classification determined in the field is of great benefit and may be the only identification necessary,
     depending on the purposes for which the soils are to be used. The field identification and individual
     laboratory test methods are explained in great detail in chapter 2. The two methods of identification are
     never entirely separated. Certain characteristics can only be estimated by visual examination. In borderline
     cases, it may be necessary to verify the classification by using laboratory tests. Conversely, field methods
     are practical for preliminary laboratory identification and may be used to group soils so that only a
     minimum number of laboratory tests are required.

LABORATORY IDENTIFICATION

     B-9. Soils identification in the laboratory is performed by determining the gradation and plasticity
     characteristics of the materials. The gradation is determined by a sieve analysis, and a grain-size curve is
     usually plotted as percent finer (or passing) by weight against a logarithmic scale of grain size in
     millimeters. DD Form 1207 is typically used for this purpose. Plasticity characteristics are evaluated by LL
     and PL tests on the soil fraction that is finer than the No. 40 sieve. Laboratory test procedures for the LL
     and PL determination can be found in chapter 14.

MAJOR SOIL GROUPS

     B-10. In the laboratory identification procedures that are shown in figure B-1, the first step in identifying a
     soil is to determine whether it is coarse-grained, fine-grained, or highly organic. This may be done by
     visual examination in most cases; however, in some borderline cases (those with very fine sands or coarse
     silts), it may be necessary to screen a representative dry sample over a No. 200 sieve and determine the
     percentage passing. Fifty percent or less passing the No. 200 sieve identifies the soil as being coarse-
     grained, and more than 50 percent identifies the soil as being fine-grained. The percentage limit of 50 has

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  B-7


---

Appendix B

     been arbitrarily selected for convenience in identification, because a numerical difference of 1 or 2 percent
     will produce no significant change in the soil behavior. After the major group is established, the
     identification procedure is continued according to figure B-1, page B-5.

COARSE-GRAINED SOILS

     B-11. A complete sieve analysis must be executed on coarse-grained soils, and a gradation curve must be
     plotted on a grain size chart. For some soils that contain a substantial amount of fines, it may be desirable
     to supplement the sieve analysis with a hydrometer analysis to define the gradation curve for particle sizes
     that are smaller than the No. 200 sieve size. Preliminary identification is made by determining the
     percentage of material in the gravel and sand sizes. If there is a greater percentage of gravel than sand, the
     material is classed as G. If there is a greater percentage of sand than gravel, the material is classed as S.
     Again, the distinction between these groups is purely arbitrary for convenience in following the system.
     The next step is to determine the amount of material that is passing the No. 200 sieve. The subgroups are
     the same for gravels and sands; therefore, are discussed jointly as follows:

      GW, SW, GP, and SP. These groups comprise nonplastic soils that contain less than 5 percent
          passing the No. 200 sieve and in which the fine fraction does not interfere with the free-draining
          properties of the soil. If the above criteria are met, an examination is made of the shape of the
          grain size curve. Well-graded materials are classified as GW or SW; poorly graded materials are
          classified as GP or SP. The gradation curve and curve data of a soil should meet the following
          qualifications to be classed and graded:

              Grain size distributions of well-graded materials plot as smooth and regular concave curves,
                  with no sizes lacking and no excess of material in any size range.

              The coefficient of the uniformity of well-graded gravels is greater than 4, and the coefficient
                  of the uniformity of well-graded sands is greater than 6. The coefficient of the uniformity is
                  determined by dividing the grain size diameter passing at 60 percent (D60) by the grain size
                  diameter passing at 10 percent (D10).

              The coefficient of curvature must be between 1 and 3. The coefficient of curvature is
                  determined by the following formula:

                                           (𝐷30)2
                                    𝐶c = 𝐷10 × 𝐷60

     Where—

     Cc = coefficient of curvature

     D30 = grain diameter at 30 percent passing by weight

     D60 = grain diameter at 60 percent passing by weight

     D10 = grain diameter at 10 percent passing by weight

     Note. The coefficient of curvature ensures that the grading curve will have a concave curvature
     that is within relatively narrow limits for a given grain diameter at 60 percent passing by weight
     and a grain diameter at 10 percent passing by weight combination. Gradations that do not meet
     this criteria are classed as poorly graded. Poorly graded soils (GP and SP) are those that have
     nearly straightline gradations, convex gradations, nearly vertical gradations, and hump
     gradations which are typical of skip-graded materials.

      GM, SM, GC and SC. Soils in the GM, SM, GC, and SC groups are composed of materials that
          have more than a 12 percent fraction passing the No. 200 sieve. They may or may not exhibit
          plasticity. For identification, LL and PL tests are required on fractions that are finer than the No.
          40 sieve. These tests should be run on representative samples of moist material. Drying affects
          the limits values and is explained in the discussion of fine-grained soils below. Materials in
          which the LL and PI plot below the A line on the plasticity chart are classed as GM or SM.
          Gravels and sands in which the LL and PI plot above the A line on the plasticity chart are
          classed as GC or SC. In the identification of materials in these groups, the plasticity
          characteristics overshadow the gradation characteristics; therefore, no distinction is made
          between well- and poorly graded materials.

B-8          TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                      Unified Soil Classification System

              Borderline soils. Coarse-grained soils that contain between 5 and 12 percent material passing
                  the No. 200 sieve are classed as borderline and carry a dual symbol (such as GW-GM).
                  Similarly, coarse-grained soils that have less than 5 percent passing the No. 200 sieve, but which
                  are not free-draining, or wherein the fine fraction exhibits plasticity are also classed as
                  borderline and are given a dual symbol.

Fine-Grained Soils

     B-12. When the identity of a fine-grained soil has been established, further identification is accomplished
     by the LL and PL tests in conjunction with the plasticity chart. The plasticity chart is a plot of LL versus PI
     on which a diagonal line (A line) and a vertical line are imposed at a LL of 50. The A line is defined by the
     following formula:

                                                      𝑃𝐼 = 0.73 (𝐿𝐿 − 20)

     B-13. An A line above an LL of about 29 represents an important empirical boundary between typical
     inorganic clays that are generally located above the line and plastic soils that contain organic colloids or
     inorganic silty soils. The vertical line at an LL of 50 separates silts and clays of low LL from those of high
     LL. In the low part of the chart, below an LL of about 29 and in the range of PI from 4 to 7, there is
     considerable overlapping of the properties of the clayey and silty soils. The separation between CL and OL
     or ML soils in this region is accomplished by a cross-hatched zone on the plasticity chart between 4 and 7
     PI and above the A line. The CL soils in this region have a PI above 7; OL or ML soils have a PI below 4.
     Soils that plot within the cross-hatched zone should be classed as borderline. The various soil groups are
     shown in their respective positions on the plasticity chart. Experience has proven that compressibility is
     about proportional to the LL and that soils which have the same LL possess about equal compressibility,
     assuming that other factors are essentially the same. When comparing the physical characteristics of soils
     that have the same LL, increasing the PI will increase the cohesive characteristics and decrease the
     permeability. Plots of the results of limits tests on various samples from the same fine-grained deposit show
     that the points lie on a straight line or in a narrow band that is almost parallel to the A line. The following
     bullets contain more information on soil tests:

              ML, CL, and OL. A soil that has an LL of less than 50 falls into the low LL group. A plot of
                  the LL and PI on the plasticity chart shows whether the soil falls above or below the A line and
                  cross-hatched zone. Soils that plot above the A line and cross-hatched zone are classed as CL
                  and are usually typical, inorganic clays. Soils that plot below the A line or cross-hatched zone
                  are inorganic silts, very fine sandy silts, organic silts, or organic silt-clays of low plasticity.
                  Because two groups fall below the A line or cross-hatched zone, further identification is
                  necessary. The distinguishing factor between the ML and OL groups is the absence or presence
                  of organic matter that is usually identified by color and odor. A comparison may be made
                  between the LL and PL of a moist sample and of one that has been oven-dried. An organic soil
                  will show a radical drop in plasticity after being oven- or air-dried. An inorganic soil typically
                  shows a change in the limits values of only 1 or 2 percent. This may be an increase or a
                  decrease; therefore, the classification should be based on the plot of limits values that are
                  determined before drying. Soils that contain organic matter usually have lower specific gravities
                  and may have higher water contents than inorganic soils. These properties may assist in
                  identifying organic soils. In special cases, the organic content may be determined by chemical
                  methods.

              MH, CH, and OH. Soils with an LL that is greater than 50 are classed with the symbol H. To
                  identify these soils, the LL and PI values are plotted on the plasticity chart. If the points fall
                  above the A line, the soil classifies as CH; if they fall below the A line, a determination is made
                  as to whether or not organic material is present. Inorganic materials are classed as MH, and
                  organic materials are classed as OH.

              Highly organic soils. Highly organic soils are usually identified according to color, texture, and
                  odor. Moisture determinations usually reveal a natural water content of several hundred percent,
                  which is far in excess of that found most soils. Specific gravities of solids in these soils may be
                  quite low. Some peaty soils can be remolded and tested for the LLs and PLs. These soils usually
                  have an LL of several hundred percent and fall well below the A line on the plasticity chart.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  B-9


---

Appendix B

Borderline Classifications

     B-14. Soils will be encountered that fall close to the boundaries which are established between the various
     groups. In addition, boundary zones for the amount of material passing the No. 200 sieve and for the lower
     part of the plasticity chart have been incorporated as part of the system. The accepted rule in classifying
     borderline soils is to use a double symbol. It is possible for a soil to fall into more than one borderline zone.
     If the appropriate symbols are used for each classification, the result should be a multiple designation that
     consists of three or more symbols. This approach is unnecessarily complicated; therefore, it is best to use a
     double symbol that represents the probable behavior of the soil. The symbols that represent the poorer of
     the possible groupings should be used.

Coarse-Grained Soils

     B-15. In previous discussions, coarse-grained soils were classified as GW, GP, SW, and SP if they
     contained less than 5 percent of material passing the No. 200 sieve. Similarly, soils were classified as GM,
     GC, SM, and SC if they had more than 12 percent passing the No. 200 sieve. The range between 5 and 12
     percent passing the No. 200 sieve is designated as borderline; the soils that fall within it are assigned a
     double symbol, depending on the gradation characteristics of the coarse fraction and the plasticity
     characteristics of the -40 sieve fraction. For example, a well-graded sandy soil with 8 percent passing the
     No. 200 sieve, an LL of 28, and a PI of 9 would be designated as SM-SC. Another type of borderline
     classification occurs for soils that contain appreciable amounts of fines (GM, GC, SM, and SC) and whose
     LL and PL values plot in the lower portion of the plasticity chart. The method of classifying these soils is
     the same as for fine-grained soils that plot in the same region as discussed below.

Fine-Grained Soils

     B-16. Discussion has been presented of a zone on the plasticity chart below an LL of about 29 and a PI of 4
     to 7. Several soil types exhibiting low plasticity plot in this general region on the plasticity chart, and no
     definite boundary between silty and clayey soils exists. Thus, if a fine-grained soil, groups (CL and ML) or
     the -40 sieve fraction of a coarse-grained soil (GM, GC, SM, and SC) plots within the cross-hatched zone
     on the plasticity chart, a double symbol (such as ML-CL) is used.

     B-17. In the descriptive name of the soil type as indicated in table B-2, page B-3, silty and clayey may be
     used to describe silt or clay soils. Since the definitions of these terms are now somewhat different from
     those used by many soils engineers, it is considered advisable to discuss their connotation as used in this
     system. In the USCS, the terms silt and clay are used to describe those soils with LLs and PLs plotting
     respectively below and above the A line and cross-hatched zone on the plasticity chart. As a logical
     extension of this concept, the terms silty and clayey may be used as adjectives in the soil names when the
     limits values plot close to the A line. For example, a clay soil with an LL of 40 and a PI of 16 may be called
     a silty clay. In general, the adjective silty is not applied to clay soils having an LL in excess of about 60.

Expansion of Classification

     B-18. In some cases, it may be necessary to expand the USCS by subdividing existing groups to classify
     soils for a particular use. The indiscriminate use of subdivisions is discouraged, and careful study should be
     given to any soil group before adopting such a step. In all cases, subdivisions should be designated,
     preferably by a suffix, to an existing group symbol. The suffix should be selected carefully so that there
     will be no confusion with existing letters that already have meanings in the classification system. In each
     case, where an existing group is subdivided, the basis and criteria for the subdivision should be explained
     so that anyone unfamiliar with it may properly understand the subdivision.

Descriptive Soil Classification

     B-19. At many stages in the soils investigation of a project—from the preliminary boring log to the final
     report—the engineer finds it convenient to give the soils a name rather than an impersonal classification
     symbol. This results, primarily, from the fact that people are accustomed to talking in terms of gravels,
     sands, silts, and clays and find it only logical to use these same names in presenting the data. The soil
     names have been associated with certain grain sizes in the textural classification as shown on the grain-size

B-10  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                            Unified Soil Classification System

chart. Such a division is generally feasible for coarse-grained soils; however, the use of such terms as silt
and clay may be entirely misleading on a textural basis. For this reason, the terms silt and clay have been
defined on a plasticity basis, as discussed previously. Within a given region of the country, the use of a
name classification based on texture is often feasible since the general behavior of similar soils is consistent
over the area. However, in another area, the same classification may be entirely inadequate. The descriptive
classification, if used intelligently, has a rightful place in soil mechanics, but its use should be carefully
evaluated by all concerned.

Description From Classification Sheet

     B-20. Column 6, table B-2, page B-3, lists typical names given to the soil types that are usually found
     within the various classification groups. By following the field or laboratory investigation procedure and
     determining the proper classification group in which the soil belongs, it is usually an easy matter to select
     an appropriate name from the classification sheet. Some soils may be readily identified and properly named
     by only visual inspection. A word of caution is considered appropriate on the use of the classification
     system for certain soils (marls, calyces, coral, shale) where the grain size can vary widely depending on the
     amount of mechanical breakdown of soil particles. For these soils, the group symbol and textural name
     have little significance and the locally used name may be important.

Other Descriptive Terms

     B-21. Records of field explorations in the form of boring logs can be of great benefit to engineer, if
     adequate information. In addition to the group symbol and the name of the soil, the general characteristics
     of the soils (plasticity, strength, moisture) provide information that is essential to a proper analysis of a
     particular problem. Locally accepted soil names should also be used to clarify the data to local bidders and
     to protect the government against later legal claims. For coarse-grained soils, the size of particles,
     mineralogical composition, shape of grains, and character of the binder are relevant features. For fine-
     grained soils, strength, moisture, and plasticity characteristics are important. When describing undisturbed
     soils, such characteristics as stratification, structure, consistency in the undisturbed and remolded states,
     cementation, and drainage are pertinent to the descriptive classification. Pertinent items to be used in
     describing soils are shown in Column 6, table B-2, page B-3. To achieve uniformity in estimating the
     consistency of soils, it is recommended that the Terzaghi classification based on unconfined compressive
     strength be used as a tentative standard. This classification is given in (table B-3).

Appendix B

EMBANKMENTS AND FOUNDATIONS

     B-23. The soil properties that are of concern for embankments or foundations are strength, permeability,
     and consolidation and compaction characteristics. Other features may be investigated for a specific
     problem, but in general, some or all of the properties mentioned are of primary importance in an earth
     embankment or foundation project of any magnitude. It is common practice to evaluate the properties of the
     soils in question by means of laboratory or field tests and to use the results of such tests as a basis for
     design and construction. The factors that influence strength, consolidation, and other characteristics are
     numerous; and some of them are not completely understood. Consequently, it is impractical to evaluate
     these features by means of a general soils classification. However, the soil groups in a given classification
     do have reasonably similar behavior characteristics. While such information is not sufficient for design
     purposes, it will give the engineer an indication of the behavior of a soil when used as a component in
     construction. This is especially true in the preliminary examination for a project when neither time nor
     money for a detailed soils testing program is available.

     B-24. Keep in mind that only generalized characteristics of the soil groups are included in this appendix,
     and they should be used primarily as a guide and not as the complete answer to a problem. For example, it
     is possible to design and construct an earth embankment of almost any type of soil and on practically any
     foundation. However, when a choice of materials is possible, certain soils may be better suited to the job
     than others. It is on this basis that the behavior characteristics of soils are presented in the following
     paragraphs and on the classification sheet. A structure’s use is often the principal deciding factor in
     selecting soil types and the type of protective measures that will be used. Since each structure is a special
     problem within itself, it is impossible to cover all possible considerations in the brief description of
     pertinent soil characteristics contained in this appendix.

SOIL CLASSIFICATION SHEET FEATURES

     B-25. General characteristics of the soil groups that are pertinent to embankments and foundations are
     presented in table B-2, page B-5.

     B-26. Figure B-2 provides a statistical breakdown of MDD and OMC for the principal soil classifications
     for standard proctor effort. Each bubble represents one standard deviation variation of MDD and OMC
     from a worldwide database of soils. This can assist in estimating construction properties for soils beyond
     the ranges given in table B-2. This is a lower compaction effort that is typically used in embankment and
     foundation construction and not in highway and airfield construction. For critical structures that are subject
     to high loading, the modified proctor effort (ASTM D1557) is recommended (figure B-3).

             Note. Standard proctor is a reduced compaction effort from the modified effort that was
             discussed in chapter 17. Design values are intended for long-term structures. For contingency
             design, unsoaked CBR design may be appropriate, depending on the structure design life and
             loading restrictions in inclement weather.

Appendix B

Suitability of Soils for Embankments

     B-27. There are three major factors that influence the suitability of soils for use in embankments:
     permeability, strength, and ease of compaction. Gravelly and sandy soils with little or no fines (GW, GP,
     SW, and SP) are stable, pervious, and able to attain good compaction with crawler tractors and rubber-tired
     rollers. Poorly graded materials may not be as desirable as those that are well graded, but all of the
     materials are suitable for use in the pervious sections of earth embankments. Poorly graded sands (SP) may
     be more difficult to use and should have flatter embankment slopes than SW soils. Gravels and sands with
     fines (GM, GC, SM, and SC) have variable characteristics that depend on the nature of the fine fraction and
     the gradation of the sample. These materials are often sufficiently impervious and stable and can be used
     for impervious sections of embankments. Soils in these groups should be carefully examined to ensure that
     they are properly zoned in relation to other materials in an embankment.

     B-28. Of the fine-grained soils, the CL group is best adapted for embankment construction. The soils are
     impervious, reasonably stable, and provide fair to good compaction with sheepsfoot or rubber-tired rollers.
     Although they are not as desirable for rolled-fill construction, MH soils may be used in the cores of
     hydraulic-fill structures. Soils in the ML group may or may not have good compaction characteristics and
     must be closely controlled in the field to secure the desired strength. CH soils have fair stability when they
     are used on flat slopes, but they have detrimental shrinkage characteristics that may necessitate blanketing
     or incorporating them in thin interior cores of embankments. Soils containing organic matter (OL, OH, and
     Pt) are not commonly used for embankment construction because of the detrimental effects of the organic
     matter present. These materials may often be used to advantage in blankets and stability berms where
     strength is not important.

Permeability and Seepage Control

     B-29. Permeability and the requirements for seepage control are essentially functions of the same property
     of a soil, so they will be discussed jointly. The subject of seepage in relation to embankments and
     foundations may be divided into three categories: seepage through embankments, seepage through
     foundations, and control of uplift procedures.

Seepage Through Embankments

     B-30. In the control of seepage through embankments, it is the relative permeability of adjacent materials
     that governs their use in a given location. An earth embankment is not watertight; therefore, the allowable
     quantity of seepage through it is largely governed by the use of the structure. For example, considerable
     seepage may be allowed in a flood-control project, but the structure will still fulfill the storage
     requirements. Much less seepage is allowed in an irrigation project because pool levels must be maintained.
     The more impervious soils (GM, GC, SM, SC, CL, MH, and CH) may be used in core sections or
     homogeneous embankments to slow the flow of water. More pervious materials are usually placed on the
     outer slopes of areas where it is important that seepage not emerge on the downstream slope or where the
     possibility of drawdown exists on upstream slopes. Coarse-grained, free-draining soils (GW, GP, SW, SP)
     are best-suited for this purpose. When a variety of materials is available, they are usually graded from least
     pervious to more pervious from the center of the embankment outward. Care should be used in the
     arrangement of materials in the embankment to prevent piping within the section. The foregoing statements
     do not preclude the use of other arrangements of materials in embankments. Dams have been successfully
     constructed of sand (SW, SP, and SM) or silt (ML), with the section made large enough to reduce seepage
     to an allowable value without the use of an impervious core. Coarse-grained soils are often used in drains
     and toe sections to collect seepage water in downstream sections of embankments. The soils used largely
     depend on the material that they drain. Free-draining sands (SW and SP) or gravels (GW and GP) are
     usually preferred, but silty sand (SM) can effectively drain clay (CL and CH) and may be satisfactory.

Seepage Through Foundations

     B-31. As with embankments, the use of the structures involved often determines the amount of seepage
     control in foundations. There have been cases in which the flow of water through a pervious foundation did
     not constitute an excessive water loss; therefore, seepage control measures were not necessary when
     adequate provisions were made against piping in critical areas. If seepage control is desired, the more

B-14        TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                      Unified Soil Classification System

     pervious soils are the soils in which necessary measures must be taken. Free-draining gravels (GW and GP)
     are capable of carrying considerable quantities of water, and some means of positive control (such as a
     cutoff trench) may be necessary. Clean sands (SW and SP) may be controlled by a cutoff or by an upstream
     impervious blanket. A line of relief well and a drainage trench at the downstream toe will not reduce the
     amount of seepage; however, both will control seepage and route the flow into collector systems where it
     can be led away harmlessly. Slightly less pervious material (silty gravels, silty sands, silts) may require a
     minor amount of seepage control (such as that afforded by a toe trench). If they are sufficiently impervious,
     no control may be necessary. The relatively impervious soils (GC, SC, CL, OL, MH, CH, OH) usually pass
     such a small volume of water that seepage control measures are not necessary.

Uplift Pressures Control

     B-32. The control of uplift pressures is directly associated with pervious foundation soils. Uplift pressures
     may be reduced by lengthening the path of seepage by a cutoff or upstream blanket or by measures for
     pressure relief in the form of wells, drainage trenches, drainage blankets, or pervious downstream shells.
     Free-draining gravels (GW and GP) may be treated by any of the aforementioned procedures; however, to
     obtain the desired pressure relief, a positive cutoff may be preferred. Blanket, well, or trench installations
     can be expensive. Free-draining sands (SW and SP) are usually less permeable than gravels, and the
     volume of water that must be controlled for pressure relief is usually less. A positive cutoff may not be
     required with free-draining sands, so an upstream blanket, well, or toe trench installation may be effective.
     In some cases, a combination of a blanket and a trench or well may be preferred.

     B-33. Silty soils (GM, SM, ML) do not usually require extensive treatment. A toe drainage trench or well
     system may be sufficient to reduce uplift pressures. The more impervious silty materials may not be
     permeable enough to permit dangerous uplift pressures to develop, and in such cases, no treatment is
     indicated. The more impervious soils (GC, SC, CL, OL, MH, CH, OH) do not usually require treatment for
     the control of uplift pressures; however, they assume importance when they occur as a relatively thin top
     stratum over more pervious materials. Uplift pressures in the lower layers that act on the base of the
     impervious top stratum can cause heaving and the formation of boils. Treatment of the lower layers by the
     methods mentioned above is usually indicated. The control of uplift pressures should not be applied
     indiscriminately merely because certain types of soils are encountered. The use of control measures should
     be based on a careful evaluation of conditions that do or can exist, and an economical solution should be
     reached that will accomplish the desired results.

Compaction Characteristics

     B-34. Table B-2, page B-3, shows the general compaction characteristics of the various soil groups. The
     evaluations that are given and the equipment that is listed are based on average field conditions in which
     proper moisture control and lift thickness are attained and a reasonable number of passes of the compaction
     equipment are required to secure the desired density. For lift construction of embankments, the sheepsfoot
     and rubber-tired rollers are commonly used pieces of equipment. An advantage of the sheepsfoot roller is
     that it creates a rough surface that generates a better bond between lifts. It also kneads the soil, affording
     better moisture distribution. The rubber-tired equipment that is referenced in the table includes heavily
     loaded compactors and earthmoving equipment that possess a minimum wheel load of 15,000 pounds.
     When standard, wobble-wheeled rollers are used for compaction, the thickness of the compacted lift is
     usually reduced to about 2 inches. Typical compaction characteristics are as follows:

              Steel-wheeled rollers and vibration produce good compaction on granular soils that retain little
                  or no fines. Well-graded materials (GW and SW) usually exhibit better results than poorly
                  graded soils (GP and SP).

              Sandy soils are best compacted by crawler tractors. Rubber-tired equipment and steel-wheeled
                  rollers are also effective on gravelly materials.

              Coarse-grained soils with fines of low plasticity (groups GM and SM) show good compaction
                  characteristics with steel-wheeled rollers and vibratory or rubber-tired equipment. However, the
                  range of moisture contents for effective compaction is typically very narrow, and close attention
                  must be paid to the compaction moisture content.

              Soils of the ML group may be compacted with rubber-tired equipment or with sheepsfoot rollers.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  B-15


---

Appendix B

              Gravels and sands with plastic fines (GC and SC) show fair compaction characteristics, although
                  this quality may vary with character and the amount of fines. Rubber-tired or sheepsfoot rollers
                  are ideal for these materials, especially sheepsfoot with vibration.

     B-35. Sheepsfoot rollers are typically used for compacting fine-grained soils. When larger grain sizes are
     present, vibratory sheepsfoot rollers are preferable. Compaction characteristics are variable. Lean clays and
     sandy clays (CL) are best, fat clays and lean organic clays or silts (OL and CH) are fair to poor, and organic
     or micaceous soils (MH and OH) are poor. There is little change in the overall compaction density of fine-
     grained soils over a wide range of moisture contents. Maintaining a proper moisture range is essential for
     the performance of these soils.

     B-36. For most construction projects, it is necessary to investigate the soil compaction characteristics by
     using a field test section. Table B-2 shows the ranges of unit dry weight for soils that are compacted
     according to the compaction test method that is discussed in chapter 2. Lower dry densities that have
     resulted from lesser compaction efforts in embankments and foundations are also shown in table B-2.
     These values are for guidance only. Design or construction control should be based on laboratory test
     results.

Suitability of Soils for Foundations

     B-37. The suitability of soils for foundations of embankments or structures depends primarily on the
     strength and consolidation characteristics of the subsoil. The type of structure and its use will largely
     govern the adaptability of a soil as a satisfactory foundation. For embankments, large settlements may be
     compensated for by overbuilding. The allowable settlement of structures (such as control towers) may be
     small to prevent overstressing the concrete or steel of which they are built or because of the necessity for
     adhering to established grades. Therefore, a soil may be satisfactory for one type of construction but require
     special treatment for another.

     B-38. Strength and settlement soil characteristics depend on variables (structure, in-place density, moisture
     content, cycles of loading in their geologic history) that are not readily evaluated by a classification system.
     Only general statements can be made concerning the suitability of various soil types (especially fine-
     grained soils) as foundations:

              Gravels and gravelly soils (GW, GP, GM, and GC) have good bearing capacities and experience
                  little consolidation under load.

              Well-graded sands (SW) usually have good bearing values.
              Poorly graded sands and silty sands (SP and SM) may exhibit variable bearing capacities,

                  depending on their density. This is true to some extent for all coarse-grained soils, but it is
                  especially critical for uniformly graded soils of the SP and SM groups. When saturated, these
                  soils may present an additional construction problem.
              Soils in the ML group may be subject to liquefaction and may have poor bearing capacities,
                  particularly where heavy structure loads are involved.
              Soils in the CL group are probably the best from a foundation standpoint. But in some cases, the
                  soils may be soft and wet and exhibit poor bearing capacities and fairly large settlements under
                  loads.
              Consolidated CH soils and soils in the MH groups may normally show poor bearing capacities
                  and large settlements.
              Organic soils (OL and OH) have poor bearing capacities and usually exhibit large settlements
                  under loads.

     B-39. For most fine-grained soils, the structure foundation is governed by factors (the bearing capacity of
     the soil and the magnitude of the load. It is possible that simple spread footings might adequately carry the
     load without excessive settlement. If the soils are poor and the structure loads are relatively heavy, alternate
     methods are indicated. Pile foundations may be necessary in some cases. In special instances (particularly
     in cases that involve CH and OH soils), it may be desirable and economically feasible to remove certain
     soils from the foundation. Highly organic soils are usually very poor foundation materials. They are
     capable of carrying light loads, but they are unsuited for most construction purposes. If highly organic soils
     occur in the foundation, they may be removed (if limited in extent) or displaced (by dumping firmer soils

B-16  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                      Unified Soil Classification System

     on top) or piling may be driven through them to form a stronger layer. Proper treatment will depend on the
     structure involved.

GRAPHICAL PRESENTATION OF SOILS DATA

     B-40. It is customary to use various symbols to present the results of soils explorations as schematic
     representations of borings and test pits. Commonly used hatching symbols include small, irregular round
     symbols for gravel; dots for sand; vertical lines for silt; and diagonal lines for clay. Combinations of these
     symbols represent the various combinations of materials found in the explorations. This system has been
     adapted to the various soil groups in the USCS. The appropriate symbols are shown in table B-2, page B-3.
     Hatching symbols may be omitted and the appropriate group letter symbol written in the boring log. In
     addition to the symbols on logs of borings, the effective size of coarse-grained soils and the natural water
     content of fine-grained soils should be shown by the side of the log. Other descriptive abbreviations may be
     used. In certain instances, the use of color to delineate soil types on maps and drawings is desirable. A
     suggested color scheme to show the major soil groups is described in table B-2.

ROADS AND AIRFIELDS

     B-41. The properties desired in soils for foundations under roads and airfields and for base courses under
     flexible pavements are adequate strength, good compaction, sufficient drainage, frost resistance, and
     acceptable compression and expansion characteristics. Some of these properties may be supplied by proper
     construction methods. For instance, adequate drainage may be obtained by installing a properly designed
     water-collecting system. Strength requirements for base course materials to be used immediately under the
     pavement of a flexible pavement structure are high and only good-quality materials are acceptable.
     However, low strengths in subgrade materials may be compensated for by increasing the thickness of
     overlying concrete pavement or of base materials in flexible pavement construction. The proper design of
     roads and airfield pavements may require a more detailed evaluation of soil properties than is possible by
     using the general soils classification system. However, the grouping of soils in the classification system is
     such that a general indication of their behavior in road and airfield construction may be obtained.

SOILS CLASSIFICATION SHEET FEATURES

     B-42. General characteristics of the soil groups that are pertinent to roads and airfields are presented in
     table B-4, page B-18.

     B-43. Table B-4 provides a statistical breakdown of maximum dry density (MDD) and optimum moisture
     content (OMC) for the principal soil classifications for modified proctor effort (ASTM D1557). Each
     bubble represents one standard deviation variation of MDD and OMC from a worldwide database of soils.
     This can assist in estimating construction properties for soils that are beyond the ranges given in table B-4.

          Note. The dry densities that are listed are for the modified effort that is discussed in chapter 17.
          Design values are intended for long-term structures. For the contingency design, the unsoaked
          CBR design may be appropriate, depending on the structure design life and on the loading
          restrictions during inclement weather.

Coarse-Grained Soil Group Subdivision

     B-44. In table B-4, page B-18, the basic soil groups (GM and SM) have been subdivided into two groups
     that are designated by the suffixes d and u. These suffixes have been chosen to represent desirable and less
     desirable (undesirable) base materials, respectively. This subdivision applies to roads and airfields only and
     is based on field observations and laboratory tests of the behavior of the soils in these groups. The basis for
     the subdivision is the LL and PI of the fraction of the soil passing the Number 40 sieve. The suffix d is used
     when the LL is 25 or less and the PI is 5 or less; otherwise, the suffix u is used. Typical symbols for soils in
     these groups are GMd and SMu.

Appendix B

Values of Soils as Subgrade, Subbase, or Base Materials

     B-45. The descriptions in Columns 7, 8, and 9 of table B-4 show the suitability of the soil groups for use as
     subgrade, subbase, or base materials, provided that they are not subject to frost. In areas where frost
     heaving is a problem, the value of materials as subgrades or subbases will be reduced, depending on the
     potential frost action of the material. Proper design procedures should be used as follows:

              Coarse-grained soils are usually the best subgrade, subbase, and base materials. The GW group
                  has excellent qualities as subgrades and subbases and is good as a base material. “Excellent” is
                  not used for any of the soils for base courses and should only be used in reference to high-
                  quality, processed crushed stones.

              Poorly graded gravels and some silty gravels (GP and GM) are usually only slightly less
                  desirable as subgrade or subbase materials and may be used as base materials under favorable
                  conditions. However, poor gradation and other factors sometimes reduce the value of soils to the
                  extent that they offer only moderate strength, and their value as a base material is less.

              GM, GC, and SW groups are reasonably good subgrade materials, but they are usually not
                  suitable as bases.

              SP and SMd soils are usually considered fair to good subgrade and subbase materials, but they
                  are unsuitable for base materials.

              SMu and SC soils are fair to poor subgrade and subbase materials and are not suitable for base
                  materials.

              Fine-grained soils range from fair to very poor subgrade materials:
                   Silts and lean clays (ML and CL): Fair to poor.
                   Organic silts, lean organic clays, and micaceous or diatomaceous soils (OL and MH): Poor.
                   Fat clays and fat organic clays (CH and OH): Poor to very poor.

     B-46. The above qualities are compensated for in flexible pavement design by increasing the thickness of
     overlying base materials. They are compensated for in rigid pavement design by increasing the pavement
     thickness or by adding a base course layer. Fine-grained soils are unsuitable as subbase and base materials.
     The fibrous organic soils (group Pt) are very poor subgrade materials and should be removed wherever
     possible; otherwise, special construction measures should be adopted. They are not suitable as subbase and
     base materials. The CBR values shown in table B-4 give a relative indication of the strength of the various
     soil groups as used in flexible pavement design. Similarly, values of subgrade modulus (k) in are relative
     indications of strengths from plate-bearing tests that are used in rigid pavement design. These tests are used
     for the design of pavements; therefore, actual test values should be used instead of the approximate values
     that are shown in the table.

     B-47. For wearing surfaces on unsurfaced roads, sand-clay-gravel mixtures (GC) are generally considered
     the most satisfactory. However, the percentage of fines should not be too large of a percentage of fines, and
     the PI should be in the range of 5 to 15.

Potential Frost Action

     B-48. The relative effects of frost action on the various soil groups is shown in table B-4. Regardless of the
     frost susceptibility of the various soil groups, two conditions must be present, simultaneously, before frost
     action will be a major consideration—a source of water during the freezing period and a sufficient period
     for the freezing temperature to penetrate the ground. Water that is necessary for the formation of ice lenses
     may become available from a high groundwater table, from a capillary supply, from within the soil voids,
     or through infiltration. The degree of ice formation that will occur is influenced by environmental factors

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034                                 B-21


---

Appendix B

     (topographic positions, stratifications of the parent soil, transitions into cut sections, the lateral flow of
     water from side cuts, localized pockets of perched groundwater, drainage conditions). Silts, fines, silty
     sands are the worst offenders. Coarse-grained materials with little or no fines are only slightly affected.
     Clays (CL and CH) are subject to frost action, but the loss of strength may not be as great for silty soils.
     Inorganic soils that contain less than 3 percent of grains finer than 0.02 millimeter in diameter by weight
     are not typically susceptible to frost. When frost-susceptible soils are encountered in subgrades and frost is
     a definite problem, two acceptable methods of design of pavements are available—a sufficient depth of
     acceptable granular material is placed over the soils to prevent freezing in the subgrade (preventing the
     detrimental effects of frost action) or a reduced depth of granular material is used (allowing freezing in the
     subgrade). The design is based on the reduced strength of the subgrade during the frost melting period. In
     many cases, appropriate drainage measures to prevent the accumulation of water in the soil pores will help
     diminish ice segregation in the subgrade and subbase.

Compressibility and Expansion

     B-49. Two types of soil characteristics are applicable to road and runway design—
              The relatively long-term compression or consolidation under the dead weight of the structure.
              The short-term compression and rebound under moving wheel loads.

     B-50. The long-term consolidation of soils becomes a factor in design when heavy fills are made on
     compressible soils. If adequate provisions are made for this type of settlement during construction, it will
     have little influence on the load-carrying capacity of the pavement. However, when elastic soils that are
     subject to compression and rebound under wheel loads are encountered, adequate protection must be
     provided. Even small movements of soil may be detrimental to the base and wearing course of pavements.

     B-51. It is fortunate that the free-draining, coarse-grained soils (GW, GP, SW, and SP) that make the best
     subgrade and subbase materials exhibit almost no tendency toward high compressibility or expansion. The
     compressibility of soils increases with an increasing LL. Compressibility is also influenced by soil
     structure, grain shape, previous loading history, and other factors that are not evaluated in the classification
     system. Undesirable compressibility or expansion characteristics may be reduced by distributing the load
     through a greater thickness of overlying material. This is adequately handled by the CBR method of design
     for flexible pavements; however, rigid pavements may require the addition of an acceptable base course
     under the pavement.

Drainage Characteristics

     B-52. The drainage characteristics of soils are a direct reflection of their permeability. The evaluation of
     drainage characteristics for use in roads and runways is shown in table B-4, page B-18. The presence of
     moisture in base, subbase, and subgrade materials (except for free-draining, coarse-grained soils) may
     cause pour water pressures and loss of strength. The moisture may stem from the infiltration of rainwater or
     by the capillary rise from an underlying water table. While free-draining materials permit the rapid draining
     of water, they also permit the rapid ingress of water. If they are adjacent to less pervious materials and have
     free access to water, they may serve as reservoirs to saturate the less pervious materials. In most instances
     adequate drainage systems should be provided. Gravelly and sandy soils with little or no fines (groups GW,
     GP, SW, and SP) have excellent drainage characteristics; GMd and SMd soils have fair to poor drainage
     characteristics; and GMu, GC, SMu, and SC soils may be practically impervious. ML, MH, and Pt soils
     have fair to poor drainage characteristics. The other groups have poor drainage characteristics or are
     practically impervious.

Compaction Equipment

     B-53. The compaction of soils for roads and runways requires that a high degree of density be attained at
     the time of construction so that detrimental consolidation will not take place under traffic. In addition, the
     detrimental effects of water are lessened when saturation or near saturation takes place. Processed materials
     (such as crushed rock) are often used as base course and require special treatment in compaction.
     Compaction equipment that usually produces the desired densities is shown in table B-4, page B-18.
     Several types of equipment are listed for some of the soil groups because variations in soil type within a

B-22  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  3 April 2015


---

                                                                                                      Unified Soil Classification System

     given group may require the use of different equipment. In some cases, more than one type of equipment
     may be necessary to produce the desired densities.

     B-54. Steel-wheeled rollers are recommended for angular materials that possess limited amounts of fines,
     crawler tractors and rubber-tired rollers are recommended for gravels and sands, and sheepsfoot rollers are
     recommended for coarse-grained or fine-grained soils that have some cohesive qualities. Rubber-tired
     rollers are also recommended for final compaction operations for most soils, except for those with a high
     LL. The suggested minimum weights for the various types of equipment are shown in table B-4, page B-18.
     It also shows ranges of unit dry weight for soils that are compacted according to the compaction test
     method that is described in chapter 2. These values are included primarily for guidance, the design or
     control of construction should be based on actual test results.

GRAPHICAL PRESENTATION OF SOILS DATA

     B-55. It is customary to present the results of soils explorations on drawings as schematic representations
     of the borings or test pits or on soil profiles with the various soils encountered shown by appropriate
     symbols. As one approach, the group’s letter symbol may be written in the appropriate section of the log.
     As an alternative, the hatching symbols shown in table B-2, page B-3, or table B-4 may be used. In
     addition, the natural-water content of fine-grained soils should be shown along the side of the log. Other
     descriptive abbreviations may be used as deemed appropriate. In certain instances, the use of color to
     delineate soil types on maps and drawings is desirable. A suggested color scheme to show the major soil
     groups is indicated in table B-2.

3 April 2015  TM 3-34.43/MCRP 3-17.7H/NAVFAC MO 330/AFH 32-1034  B-23

                                           Appendix C

                     Rapid Soils Analysis Kit

C-1. The RSAK addresses the need for rapid onsite soil characterization and provides soil classification
and construction design parameters within 1 hour. The RSAK includes a small field laboratory that consists
of a microwave, electric balance, sieve shaker, grinder, PL tool, sieves, bowls, spatulas, and scoops (figure
C-1.) The kit is packaged into two large pelican cases that weigh approximately 70 to 75 pounds each
(figure C-2.) Soil moisture, grain size distribution, and PL are determined from a small soil sample. The
numerical data that are generated from soils tests are entered into regression software to determine the soil
classification. The software uses multiple-linear regression that is based on soil property databases to
estimate the OMC and MDD. Built-in, higher order regression equations compute complete design curves
for Proctor density, as-built CBR, and soaked CBR for the constructed condition of the soil of interest
(figure C-3, page C-2).

                                     Figure C-1. RSAK contents

Legend:       identification

                    Figure C-2. RSAK packaged contents

CBR         joint rapid airfield construction
JRAC        maximum dry density
MDD         optimum moisture content
OMC         pound per cubic foot
pcf
                            Figure C-3. RSAK software output

PROBLEM

     C-2. An accurate and expedient method of determining soil classification is essential to establish the soil
     design criteria for contingency airfield and lines of communication construction using in situ soil. During a
     contingency design and construction scenario, only a few hours are available to accumulate the ground
     truth data. Currently, only subjective field analysis techniques are available. The results fail to provide
     tangible numerical data that can be used to establish construction criteria.

BENEFIT AND SAVINGS

     C-3. The RSAK dramatically reduces the logistics of performing in-theater soils analyses. The RSAK is
     easily transported as a two-man carry and can be used on site in forward operations with a setup time of
     only a few minutes. A traditional soil laboratory must be stationed at a rear operating base. Collected field
     samples must be sent for an analysis that requires days of testing, but an RSAK analysis is complete in 1
     hour. The RSAK employs microwave drying that enables rapid in situ moisture content determination
     without the need for nuclear test devices.

DISTRIBUTION SOURCES

     C-4. RSAK software is available from ERDC. Hardware is commercially available from worldwide soil
     equipment distributors. Current hardware costs for a single kit to the government from commercial vendors
     is approximately $5,000. The hardware and the RSAK, as a whole, is currently not an NSN product and
     must be assembled by the users or by ERDC.

TRAINING

     C-5. Current training requires a 4-hour time block with a technical engineer.

CONTENTS

     C-6. The contents of the commercially-available RSAK are inserted into two large 1660 pelican cases.
     Some of the equipment is standard and is included in supplied military soils kits. Table C-1 lists the
     equipment packed into each case. Specific model numbers vary depending on the vendor and can be
     requested from the ERDC-WES at the contact given.

                                                Appendix D

             United States Marine Corps Soil Test Set

         The soil test set is a portable system that consists of several major testing components
         packed inside a series of three deployable cases. It also includes various sieves,
         containers, tarps, tools, and items that are designed to aid in testing soils. Some
         components of the set require power and can be operated by batteries or optional
         alternating current power adapters. The soil test set is an organic table of equipment
         item in the engineer support battalions, Marine logistics groups and wing support
         squadrons, Marine aircraft wings. It allows Marines to separate soils, assess the in
         situ bearing capacity, determine the CBR values, determine the soil moisture content,
         and determine the field identification classification.

CASE 1

     D-1. Case 1 (figure D-1) contains laboratory equipment. It is used to determine soil classification (grain
     size, clay content, and soil weight), electrical soil moisture content, and soil trafficability. Table D-1, page
     D-2, shows the contents of Case 1.
